@@@ mn-066-polynomial-maps.md
---
title: Polynomial maps between matrix spaces are continuous
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: polynomial maps, continuity, determinant, adjugate
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that every map $M_n\to M_m$ whose entries are polynomials in the entries of the input is continuous. Deduce that $\det$, $\operatorname{tr}$, $A\mapsto A^T$, $A\mapsto A^k$ and $A\mapsto\operatorname{adj}A$ are continuous.

## Hint

Sums and products of continuous functions are continuous.

Identify $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ with $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$ and look at coordinate functions.

## Solution

**Idea.** All norms on $M_n(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$ are equivalent, so "continuous" means the usual continuity on $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$. A polynomial in the coordinates is continuous, and a map into $M_m$ is continuous iff each of its $m^2$ entries is.

**Step 1: coordinate functions.** The map $\pi_{ij}:A\mapsto a_{ij}$ is linear, and $|a_{ij}|\le\|A\|_F$, so $|\pi_{ij}(A)-\pi_{ij}(B)|\le\|A-B\|_F$: it is $1$-Lipschitz, hence continuous.

**Step 2: polynomials.** Constants and the $\pi_{ij}$ are continuous. Sums and products of real continuous functions are continuous (limit laws). Every polynomial in the $a_{ij}$ is obtained from these by finitely many sums and products, so it is continuous.

**Step 3: vector-valued maps.** If $F:M_n\to M_m$ has entries $F_{kl}$ that are polynomials, then $\|F(A)-F(B)\|_F^2=\sum_{k,l}|F_{kl}(A)-F_{kl}(B)|^2$ tends to $0$ as $B\to A$, since each term does.

**Step 4: the examples.**
- $\operatorname{tr}A=\sum_i a_{ii}$ is linear.
- $A\mapsto A^T$ only permutes the entries.
- $A\mapsto A^k$: by induction, the entries of $A^k=A\cdot A^{k-1}$ are $\sum_l a_{il}(A^{k-1})_{lj}$, polynomials in the $a_{ij}$.
- $\det A=\sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_i a_{i\sigma(i)}$ is a polynomial (Leibniz formula).
- $\operatorname{adj}A$ has entries $(-1)^{i+j}\det A^{(j,i)}$, where $A^{(j,i)}$ is $A$ with row $j$ and column $i$ removed: these are determinants of smaller matrices whose entries are entries of $A$, hence polynomials.

**Remark.** Polynomial maps are even *locally Lipschitz*, with $C^\infty$ regularity. The converse fails: $A\mapsto\|A\|$ and $A\mapsto\sqrt{\ }$ are continuous without being polynomial. The next problems treat the first genuinely non-polynomial map: the inverse.
@@@ mn-067-multiplication.md
---
title: Matrix multiplication is jointly continuous
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: multiplication, bilinear map, continuity, submultiplicativity
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that matrix multiplication $M_n\times M_n\to M_n$, $(A,B)\mapsto AB$, is continuous.

## Hint

The entries of $AB$ are bilinear expressions in the entries of $A$ and $B$.

For a quantitative version, write $A'B'-AB=(A'-A)B'+A(B'-B)$ and use $\|XY\|\le\|X\|\|Y\|$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Product topology on $M_n\times M_n\cong\mathbb{R}^{2n^2}$ and polynomial dependence of the entries give continuity at once. A direct estimate gives more: local Lipschitz control.

**Step 1 (qualitative).** $(AB)_{ij}=\sum_{k}a_{ik}b_{kj}$ is a polynomial in the $2n^2$ coordinates $(a_{ik},b_{kj})$, so it is continuous (see Problem 66). All $n^2$ entries are continuous, hence so is the map.

**Step 2 (quantitative).** Use the operator norm, which is submultiplicative. For $(A',B')$ near $(A,B)$:
$$A'B'-AB=(A'-A)B'+A(B'-B),$$
check: $(A'-A)B'+A(B'-B)=A'B'-AB'+AB'-AB=A'B'-AB$. Hence
$$\|A'B'-AB\|\le\|A'-A\|\,\|B'\|+\|A\|\,\|B'-B\|.$$
If $\|B'-B\|\le1$, then $\|B'\|\le\|B\|+1$, so
$$\|A'B'-AB\|\le(\|B\|+1)\|A'-A\|+\|A\|\,\|B'-B\|\xrightarrow[(A',B')\to(A,B)]{}0.$$

**Remark.** The map is *not* uniformly continuous on $M_n\times M_n$: take $A=B=tI$ and $A'=B'=(t+\frac1t)I$; the distance is $\frac{2\sqrt n}{t}\to0$ but $\|A'B'-AB\|=\|(2+t^{-2})I\|\ge2$. This is the same phenomenon as for $(x,y)\mapsto xy$ on $\mathbb{R}^2$. Together with the next problems it shows that $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is a topological group.
@@@ mn-068-inverse.md
---
title: Inversion is a homeomorphism of GL_n
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: inverse, Cramer's rule, homeomorphism, adjugate
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $A\mapsto A^{-1}$ is continuous on $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ and is a homeomorphism of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ onto itself.

## Hint

Cramer's rule: $A^{-1}=\frac1{\det A}\operatorname{adj}A$.

For the homeomorphism part, compute the composition of the inversion with itself.

## Solution

**Idea.** The formula $A^{-1}=\frac{1}{\det A}\operatorname{adj}A$ expresses the inverse as a polynomial map divided by a nonvanishing polynomial.

**Step 1: continuity.** On $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ the function $\det$ is continuous and nonzero (Problem 66), so $A\mapsto\frac1{\det A}$ is continuous as the composition of $\det$ with $t\mapsto1/t$ on $\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$. The map $A\mapsto\operatorname{adj}A$ is continuous (polynomial entries). The product of a continuous scalar function with a continuous matrix-valued map is continuous. Hence $\iota:A\mapsto A^{-1}$ is continuous.

**Step 2: homeomorphism.** $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is closed under inversion, so $\iota:GL_n\to GL_n$ is well defined. Moreover $\iota(\iota(A))=(A^{-1})^{-1}=A$, so $\iota\circ\iota=\mathrm{id}$. Thus $\iota$ is a continuous bijection whose inverse is itself, hence continuous: $\iota$ is a homeomorphism (an *involution*).

**Example.** For $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$, $A^{-1}=\frac1{ad-bc}\begin{pmatrix}d&-b\\-c&a\end{pmatrix}$, visibly continuous where $ad-bc\ne0$.

**Remark.**
- Inversion is not defined on all of $M_n$, and $\|A^{-1}\|\to\infty$ as $A$ approaches a singular matrix (Problem 64 in the previous part). So it does not extend continuously to the closure.
- Inversion exchanges the two connected components of $GL_n$ only in the sense that it preserves each: $\det(A^{-1})=1/\det A$ has the same sign.
@@@ mn-069-classification.md
---
title: Which natural functions of a matrix are continuous?
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: continuity, rank, kernel, eigenvalues, counterexamples
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Which of these are continuous on their natural domain: $\operatorname{rank}A$, $\dim\ker A$, $1/\det A$ on $GL_n$, the number of distinct real eigenvalues of $A$, $\operatorname{tr}A$?

## Hint

Test on $\frac1kI\to0$.

For the eigenvalue count with $n=2$, find a family of matrices with no real eigenvalues that converges to a matrix with one.

## Solution

**Idea.** A function with values in $\mathbb{Z}$ is continuous iff it is locally constant. So it suffices to find, for each integer-valued function, a convergent sequence along which the value jumps.

**Answer.** Only $1/\det A$ and $\operatorname{tr}A$ are continuous.

**$\operatorname{tr}$ and $1/\det$.** $\operatorname{tr}$ is linear. $\det$ is a polynomial, nonzero on $GL_n$, so $1/\det$ is continuous on $GL_n$.

**Rank.** $\frac1kI\to0$ and $\operatorname{rank}\frac1kI=n$ for all $k$, but $\operatorname{rank}0=0\neq n$. Not continuous (it is only lower semicontinuous: Problem 83).

**$\dim\ker$.** By rankâ€“nullity $\dim\ker A=n-\operatorname{rank}A$, so the same sequence gives $\dim\ker(\frac1kI)=0\not\to n=\dim\ker0$.

**Number of distinct real eigenvalues.** Take $n=2$ and
$$A_\varepsilon=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\varepsilon\\\varepsilon&0\end{pmatrix}.$$
Its characteristic polynomial is $t^2+\varepsilon^2$, with no real root for $\varepsilon\ne0$, so the count is $0$. As $\varepsilon\to0$, $A_\varepsilon\to0$, which has the real eigenvalue $0$: count $1$. The count jumps. (For $n\ge2$ use block diagonal matrices.)

**The jump can also go down.** $\operatorname{diag}(1,1+\varepsilon)\to I$ has $2$ distinct real eigenvalues for $\varepsilon\neq0$ but $I$ has $1$. So the count is neither upper nor lower semicontinuous.

**Remark.** Counting eigenvalues *with multiplicity in $\mathbb{C}$* gives the constant $n$, and the multiset of complex eigenvalues does depend continuously on $A$ (Problem 81).
@@@ mn-070-topological-group.md
---
title: GL_n is a topological group
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: topological group, determinant, homomorphism, SL_n
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is a topological group and $\det:GL_n(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}^\times$ is a continuous homomorphism with kernel $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

A topological group is a group with continuous multiplication and inversion. Use the previous two problems and $\det(AB)=\det A\det B$.

## Solution

**Idea.** There is nothing new to prove except to check the definitions: the group structure is the usual one and the topology is the subspace topology from $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Step 1: group.** $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is a group under multiplication (Problem 67 gives closure: $\det(AB)\neq0$).

**Step 2: topology.** The subspace topology on $GL_n$ is Hausdorff (subspace of $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$). Multiplication $GL_n\times GL_n\to GL_n$ is the restriction of the continuous multiplication $M_n\times M_n\to M_n$ (Problem 67), and inversion is continuous (Problem 68). Hence $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is a topological group; in fact a *Lie group*, since $GL_n$ is an open subset of $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$ and both operations are polynomial or rational.

**Step 3: the determinant.** $\det(AB)=\det A\det B$ says $\det:GL_n\to\mathbb{R}^\times=\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$ is a group homomorphism for multiplication on $\mathbb{R}^\times$; it is continuous (Problem 66). Its kernel is $\{A:\det A=1\}=SL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Step 4: consequences.**
- $SL_n(\mathbb{R})=\det^{-1}(\{1\})$ is a *closed* normal subgroup of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$, being the preimage of a closed set by a continuous map.
- The cosets of $SL_n$ are the level sets $\{\det=c\}$. The map $A\mapsto\det A$ is surjective onto $\mathbb{R}^\times$ (use $\operatorname{diag}(c,1,\dots,1)$), so $GL_n/SL_n\cong\mathbb{R}^\times$.

**Remark.** $\mathbb{R}^\times$ has two connected components, and $\det$ is surjective and continuous. So $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ has *at least* two components (the signs of $\det$); Part 5 shows that it has exactly two.
@@@ mn-071-conjugation.md
---
title: Conjugation is continuous
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: conjugation, composition of continuous maps, similarity
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show $(P,A)\mapsto PAP^{-1}$ is continuous on $GL_n(\mathbb{R})\times M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Compose continuous maps: inversion on the first factor, then multiplication.

## Solution

**Idea.** Write the map as a composite of maps that are already known to be continuous.

**Step 1.** The map $(P,A)\mapsto(P,A,P^{-1})$ from $GL_n\times M_n$ to $M_n\times M_n\times M_n$ is continuous: its components are the two projections and the inversion $P\mapsto P^{-1}$ (Problem 68). (A map into a product is continuous iff its components are.)

**Step 2.** Multiplication $M_n\times M_n\to M_n$ is continuous (Problem 67), so $(P,A,P^{-1})\mapsto PA$ and then $(PA,P^{-1})\mapsto(PA)P^{-1}$ are continuous.

**Step 3.** Composing, $(P,A)\mapsto PAP^{-1}$ is continuous.

**Consequences.**
- For fixed $P$, $A\mapsto PAP^{-1}$ is a linear automorphism of $M_n$, hence a homeomorphism that preserves trace, determinant, rank, characteristic polynomial and spectrum.
- The *similarity class* (orbit) $\{PAP^{-1}\}$ is the image of $GL_n$ under the continuous map $P\mapsto PAP^{-1}$.

**Remark.** The orbits are generally not closed: the orbit of $\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ contains $\begin{pmatrix}1&t\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ for all $t\ne0$ (conjugate by $\operatorname{diag}(t,1)$), and $t\to0$ gives $I$, which is not in the orbit. Compare the analysis of closures of similarity classes in Part 6.
@@@ mn-072-lipschitz.md
---
title: Lipschitz estimates for the norm and for inversion
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: Lipschitz, inversion, resolvent identity, operator norm
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $\|\cdot\|$ be the operator norm. Show that $A\mapsto\|A\|$ is $1$-Lipschitz, and that inversion is $M^2$-Lipschitz on $\{A\in GL_n:\|A^{-1}\|\le M\}$.

## Hint

For the first part, use the triangle inequality twice.

For the second, use $A^{-1}-B^{-1}=A^{-1}(B-A)B^{-1}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The norm is Lipschitz by the triangle inequality; the inverse is Lipschitz thanks to an algebraic identity expressing $A^{-1}-B^{-1}$ through $B-A$.

**Part 1: the norm.**
- $\|A\|=\|(A-B)+B\|\le\|A-B\|+\|B\|$, so $\|A\|-\|B\|\le\|A-B\|$.
- Exchanging $A$ and $B$ gives $\|B\|-\|A\|\le\|B-A\|=\|A-B\|$.

Hence $\big|\|A\|-\|B\|\big|\le\|A-B\|$.

**Part 2: inversion.**
- *The identity.* For invertible $A,B$:
$$A^{-1}(B-A)B^{-1}=A^{-1}BB^{-1}-A^{-1}AB^{-1}=A^{-1}-B^{-1}.$$
- *The estimate.* By submultiplicativity,
$$\|A^{-1}-B^{-1}\|\le\|A^{-1}\|\,\|B-A\|\,\|B^{-1}\|\le M\cdot\|A-B\|\cdot M=M^2\|A-B\|.$$

Here both $A$ and $B$ must lie in the set $\{\|\,\cdot^{-1}\|\le M\}$.

**Sharpness.** For $A=\operatorname{diag}(\frac1M,1,\dots,1)$ and $B=\operatorname{diag}(\frac2M,1,\dots,1)$ we have $\|A^{-1}\|=M$, $\|B^{-1}\|=\frac M2\le M$, $\|A-B\|=\frac1M$ and $\|A^{-1}-B^{-1}\|=\left|M-\frac M2\right|=\frac M2$. The ratio is $\frac{M/2}{1/M}=\frac{M^2}2$: the constant $M^2$ cannot be improved beyond a factor $2$.

**Remark.** The set $\{\|A^{-1}\|\le M\}$ is exactly $\{\sigma_{\min}(A)\ge1/M\}$, the matrices at distance $\ge1/M$ from the singular matrices (Problem 63). So inversion is Lipschitz *away from the singular set*, with a constant blowing up like $\operatorname{dist}^{-2}$ â€” and Problem 73 shows that it fails to be uniformly continuous globally.
@@@ mn-073-no-uniform-continuity.md
---
title: Inversion is not uniformly continuous
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: uniform continuity, inversion, singular matrices, counterexample
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show inversion is not uniformly continuous on $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ (for the operator norm, hence for any norm).

## Hint

Take two diagonal matrices very close to the singular set and to each other.

Compare $\operatorname{diag}(\varepsilon,1,\dots,1)$ with $\operatorname{diag}(2\varepsilon,1,\dots,1)$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Uniform continuity means: for every $\eta>0$ there is $\delta>0$ with $\|A-B\|<\delta\Rightarrow\|A^{-1}-B^{-1}\|<\eta$ for *all* $A,B\in GL_n$. To refute it, exhibit pairs at distance tending to $0$ whose inverses stay far apart.

**Step 1: the pairs.** For $0<\varepsilon\le1$ let
$$A_\varepsilon=\operatorname{diag}(\varepsilon,1,\dots,1),\qquad B_\varepsilon=\operatorname{diag}(2\varepsilon,1,\dots,1).$$
Both are invertible.

**Step 2: distances.** $A_\varepsilon-B_\varepsilon=\operatorname{diag}(-\varepsilon,0,\dots,0)$, whose operator norm is $\varepsilon$. And
$$A_\varepsilon^{-1}-B_\varepsilon^{-1}=\operatorname{diag}\left(\tfrac1\varepsilon-\tfrac1{2\varepsilon},0,\dots,0\right)=\operatorname{diag}\left(\tfrac1{2\varepsilon},0,\dots,0\right),$$
with norm $\frac1{2\varepsilon}$.

**Step 3: conclusion.** As $\varepsilon\to0$, $\|A_\varepsilon-B_\varepsilon\|=\varepsilon\to0$ while $\|A_\varepsilon^{-1}-B_\varepsilon^{-1}\|=\frac1{2\varepsilon}\to\infty$. For $\eta=1$ no $\delta>0$ works.

**Numeric check.** $\varepsilon=10^{-2}$: distance $0.01$, inverse difference $50$. $\varepsilon=10^{-4}$: $10^{-4}$ and $5000$.

**Remark.**
- The ratio $\frac{\|A^{-1}-B^{-1}\|}{\|A-B\|}=\frac1{2\varepsilon^2}$ matches the Lipschitz constant $M^2=\varepsilon^{-2}$ of Problem 72 (up to the factor $2$).
- Inversion *is* uniformly continuous on each set $\{\sigma_{\min}\ge c\}$ with $c>0$, e.g. on the compact group $O(n)$ or on any bounded set at distance $\ge c$ from the singular matrices.
- Compare with $x\mapsto1/x$ on $(0,1)$ â€” the case $n=1$.
@@@ mn-074-squares.md
---
title: Matrices of negative determinant are not squares
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: squares, determinant sign, surjectivity
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that a real matrix with $\det<0$ is not the square of a real matrix (for every $n\ge1$); hence the continuous map $A\mapsto A^2$ is not onto $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Look at the sign of $\det$ of a square.

## Solution

**Idea.** The determinant is multiplicative, and a real number squared is nonnegative.

**Step 1.** If $A=B^2$ with $B\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$, then
$$\det A=\det(B^2)=(\det B)^2\ge0,$$
because $\det B$ is a *real* number.

**Step 2: not onto.** Take $A=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$, with $\det A=-1<0$. By Step 1 it is not a square. So $A\mapsto A^2$ does not map $GL_n$ onto $GL_n$. More precisely, the image lies in $GL_n^+=\{\det>0\}$.

**Complex squares exist.** Over $\mathbb{C}$ every invertible matrix has a square root, so the obstruction is genuinely about real matrices.

**Warning: $\det>0$ is not sufficient.** For $n=2$ the matrix
$$A=\begin{pmatrix}-1&1\\0&-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad\det A=1>0,$$
is not the square of any real matrix. Indeed suppose $A=B^2$; the eigenvalues $\mu$ of $B$ satisfy $\mu^2=-1$, so they are $\pm i$ (for a real $2\times2$ matrix they are conjugate), distinct. Then $B$ is diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$, so $B^2$ is too, with both eigenvalues $-1$, which forces $B^2=-I\ne A$. Contradiction. (Whereas $-I_2=R_{\pi/2}^2$ is a square.)

**Remark.** So the image of squaring is a proper subset of $GL_n^+$, and *determining* it is a classical problem: a real invertible matrix is a square iff its Jordan blocks for negative eigenvalues come in pairs of equal size.
@@@ mn-075-symmetrisation.md
---
title: The nearest symmetric matrix is the symmetric part
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: symmetric matrices, skew-symmetric, orthogonal projection, Frobenius norm
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $A\mapsto\frac{A+A^T}2$ is a continuous linear projection of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ onto $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ with kernel the skew-symmetric matrices, and that $\frac{A+A^T}2$ is the symmetric matrix nearest to $A$ in the Frobenius norm. Compute the distance.

## Hint

Show that symmetric and skew-symmetric matrices are orthogonal for the Frobenius inner product.

Then use Pythagoras.

## Solution

**Idea.** The decomposition $A=\frac{A+A^T}2+\frac{A-A^T}2$ is an orthogonal decomposition of $M_n$ for $\langle X,Y\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(X^TY)$, so the symmetric part is an orthogonal projection, and orthogonal projections give the best approximation.

**Step 1: the projection.** Let $\pi(A)=\frac{A+A^T}2$. It is linear, and $\pi(A)^T=\pi(A)$ so it takes values in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$. If $A$ is symmetric then $\pi(A)=A$. Hence $\pi\circ\pi=\pi$, and $\pi$ is onto $\mathrm{Sym}_n$. $\pi(A)=0$ iff $A^T=-A$: the kernel is the space $\mathrm{Skew}_n$ of skew matrices. It is continuous as a linear map between finite-dimensional spaces (explicitly $\|\pi(A)\|_F\le\|A\|_F$).

**Step 2: orthogonality.** For $S$ symmetric and $K$ skew,
$$\langle S,K\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(S^TK)=\operatorname{tr}(SK)=\operatorname{tr}\big((SK)^T\big)=\operatorname{tr}(K^TS^T)=-\operatorname{tr}(KS)=-\operatorname{tr}(SK),$$
(using $\operatorname{tr}(X)=\operatorname{tr}(X^T)$ and $\operatorname{tr}(KS)=\operatorname{tr}(SK)$), so $\operatorname{tr}(SK)=0$. Thus $\mathrm{Sym}_n\perp\mathrm{Skew}_n$ and $M_n=\mathrm{Sym}_n\oplus\mathrm{Skew}_n$, an orthogonal direct sum.

**Step 3: best approximation.** Write $A=S_0+K_0$ with $S_0=\frac{A+A^T}2$, $K_0=\frac{A-A^T}2$. For any $S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n$, the vector $S_0-S$ is symmetric, so is orthogonal to $K_0$ and
$$\|A-S\|_F^2=\|K_0\|_F^2+\|S_0-S\|_F^2\ge\|K_0\|_F^2,$$
with equality iff $S=S_0$. So $S_0$ is the unique nearest symmetric matrix and
$$\operatorname{dist}_F(A,\mathrm{Sym}_n)=\left\|\frac{A-A^T}2\right\|_F.$$

**Example.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&3\end{pmatrix}$: $S_0=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&3\end{pmatrix}$, $K_0=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}$, distance $\sqrt2$.

**Remark.** The Frobenius norm is essential: for the operator norm the nearest symmetric matrix is in general *not* unique and not equal to $S_0$ (the operator-norm unit ball is not strictly convex).
@@@ mn-076-characteristic-polynomial.md
---
title: The characteristic polynomial map and its section
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: characteristic polynomial, companion matrix, continuous section
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

For $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ write $\det(tI-A)=t^n+c_{n-1}(A)t^{n-1}+\dots+c_0(A)$ and let $\chi:M_n\to\mathbb{R}^n$, $A\mapsto(c_{n-1}(A),\dots,c_0(A))$. Show that $\chi$ is continuous and surjective, and has a continuous right inverse.

## Hint

The coefficients are polynomials in the entries of $A$.

For the right inverse, use companion matrices.

## Solution

**Idea.** Continuity follows from the polynomial dependence. A right inverse is a continuous choice of a matrix with prescribed characteristic polynomial; the companion matrix does it.

**Step 1: continuity.** $\det(tI-A)$ is a polynomial in $t$ whose coefficients are polynomial in the entries $a_{ij}$ (expand the determinant by Leibniz). So each $c_k$ is a polynomial map, hence continuous (Problem 66). Explicitly $c_{n-1}=-\operatorname{tr}A$ and $c_0=(-1)^n\det A$.

**Step 2: the companion matrix.** For $c=(c_{n-1},\dots,c_0)\in\mathbb{R}^n$ let
$$C(c)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\cdots&0&-c_0\\1&0&\cdots&0&-c_1\\0&1&\cdots&0&-c_2\\\vdots&&\ddots&&\vdots\\0&0&\cdots&1&-c_{n-1}\end{pmatrix}.$$
The entries of $C(c)$ are $0$, $1$ or $-c_k$, so $c\mapsto C(c)$ is continuous (it is even affine).

**Step 3: $\chi(C(c))=c$.** Expanding $\det(tI-C(c))$ along the last column gives $t^n+c_{n-1}t^{n-1}+\dots+c_0$. (Check $n=2$: $C=\begin{pmatrix}0&-c_0\\1&-c_1\end{pmatrix}$, $\det(tI-C)=t(t+c_1)+c_0=t^2+c_1t+c_0$ âœ“.)

**Step 4: conclusion.** $\chi\circ C=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}^n}$. So $\chi$ is surjective, and $C$ is a continuous right inverse (a continuous *section*).

**Remark.** There is no *linear* right inverse for $n\ge2$ in general, but there is a continuous one. Also, a continuous *left* inverse is impossible, because $\chi$ is far from injective (all conjugates of $A$ share the same characteristic polynomial). This map identifies the quotient "$M_n$ modulo conjugation" with the space of polynomials, at least on the open set of matrices with distinct eigenvalues.
@@@ mn-077-nilpotent-cone.md
---
title: The nilpotent cone is closed but not compact
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: nilpotent matrices, fibre, Cayley-Hamilton, unbounded set
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

With $\chi$ as in the previous problem, show that the fibre $\chi^{-1}(0)=\{A:\det(tI-A)=t^n\}$ is exactly the set $\mathcal N$ of nilpotent matrices ("nilpotent cone"), that it is closed, and that it is unbounded for $n\ge2$.

## Hint

Use the Cayleyâ€“Hamilton theorem in one direction and the eigenvalues in the other.

For unboundedness look at $n=2$ and a single nonzero entry above the diagonal.

## Solution

**Idea.** Characteristic polynomial $t^n$ means that all complex eigenvalues are $0$, which is the same as nilpotency.

**Step 1: $\chi_A=t^n\Rightarrow A$ nilpotent.** By the Cayleyâ€“Hamilton theorem, $\chi_A(A)=0$, i.e. $A^n=0$.

**Step 2: $A$ nilpotent $\Rightarrow\chi_A=t^n$.** If $A^m=0$ and $Av=\lambda v$ with $v\ne0$ ($\lambda\in\mathbb{C}$), then $0=A^mv=\lambda^mv$ gives $\lambda=0$. So all complex roots of $\chi_A$ vanish and, $\chi_A$ being monic of degree $n$, $\chi_A=t^n$.

**Step 3: closed.** $\mathcal N=\chi^{-1}(\{0\})$ is the preimage of a point under a continuous map (Problem 76), hence closed. Equivalently $\mathcal N=\{A:A^n=0\}$ is the zero set of polynomial maps.

**Step 4: unbounded.** For $s\in\mathbb{R}$ let
$$N_s=\begin{pmatrix}0&s\\0&0\end{pmatrix}\oplus 0_{n-2}.$$
Then $N_s^2=0$, so $N_s\in\mathcal N$, and $\|N_s\|=|s|$ (it maps $e_2\mapsto se_1$ and kills the other basis vectors). Letting $s\to\infty$ shows $\mathcal N$ is unbounded; hence it is not compact, although it is closed.

**Remark.** The nilpotent cone is a *cone*: $tN\in\mathcal N$ for all $t\in\mathbb{R}$ if $N\in\mathcal N$. It has empty interior (Part 2) and is singular at $0$. For $n=1$ the fibre is $\{0\}$ (a point), which explains the hypothesis $n\ge2$.
@@@ mn-078-intermediate-value.md
---
title: A path from positive to negative determinant hits a singular matrix
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: intermediate value theorem, determinant, paths, connectedness
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

If $t\mapsto A(t)$ is a continuous path $[0,1]\to M_n(\mathbb{R})$ with $\det A(0)>0>\det A(1)$, show that some $A(t)$ is singular.

## Hint

Compose with $\det$ and use the intermediate value theorem.

## Solution

**Idea.** The function $f(t)=\det A(t)$ is a continuous real function on an interval, so it must vanish between a positive and a negative value.

**Step 1.** $\det:M_n\to\mathbb{R}$ is continuous (Problem 66) and $t\mapsto A(t)$ is continuous, so $f=\det\circ A:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}$ is continuous.

**Step 2.** $f(0)>0>f(1)$. By the intermediate value theorem there exists $t_0\in(0,1)$ with $f(t_0)=0$, i.e. $A(t_0)$ is singular.

**Example.** The path $A(t)=\operatorname{diag}(1-2t,1)$ joins $I$ ($\det=1$) to $\operatorname{diag}(-1,1)$ ($\det=-1$) and passes through the singular matrix $\operatorname{diag}(0,1)$ at $t=\frac12$. The rotation path $R_{\pi t}$ in $SO(2)$ never leaves the determinant-$1$ level set.

**Consequence.** Any path in $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ keeps a constant sign of $\det$. Hence $I$ and $\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$ lie in *different path-components* of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$: $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is not connected (see Part 5).

**Remark.** The statement uses that a path is defined on a *connected* interval; it is the one-dimensional case of "the continuous image of a connected set is connected".
@@@ mn-079-derivative-of-det.md
---
title: The derivative of the determinant (Jacobi's formula)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: derivative of determinant, Jacobi formula, adjugate, density
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\frac d{dt}\det(I+tB)\big|_{t=0}=\operatorname{tr}B$, and more generally $\frac d{dt}\det(A+tB)\big|_{t=0}=\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{adj}(A)\,B)$ for all $A,B\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Expand $\det(I+tB)$ with the Leibniz formula and keep only the terms of order $\le1$ in $t$.

For general $A$, first treat the invertible case, then use density of $GL_n$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The determinant is a polynomial in $t$; its linear coefficient is read off from the identity permutation. Jacobi's formula follows by factoring out $A$, and extends to singular $A$ because both sides are continuous in $A$.

**Step 1: $A=I$.** By Leibniz,
$$\det(I+tB)=\sum_\sigma\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^n(\delta_{i\sigma(i)}+tb_{i\sigma(i)}).$$
For $\sigma\ne\mathrm{id}$, at least two indices have $\sigma(i)\ne i$, so the product contains at least two factors $tb_{i\sigma(i)}$: the term is $O(t^2)$. For $\sigma=\mathrm{id}$, $\prod_i(1+tb_{ii})=1+t\sum_ib_{ii}+O(t^2)$. Hence
$$\det(I+tB)=1+t\operatorname{tr}B+O(t^2),$$
and the derivative at $0$ is $\operatorname{tr}B$.

**Step 2: $A$ invertible.** $A+tB=A(I+tA^{-1}B)$, so
$$\det(A+tB)=\det A\cdot\det(I+tA^{-1}B)=\det A\,\big(1+t\operatorname{tr}(A^{-1}B)+O(t^2)\big).$$
The derivative at $0$ is $\det A\operatorname{tr}(A^{-1}B)=\operatorname{tr}\big(\det A\cdot A^{-1}B\big)=\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{adj}(A)B)$, because $\operatorname{adj}A=\det A\cdot A^{-1}$.

**Step 3: all $A$.** Fix $B$. Both sides $D(A):=\frac d{dt}\det(A+tB)|_{0}$ and $\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{adj}(A)B)$ are polynomial in the entries of $A$ (the first because $\det(A+tB)$ is a polynomial in $t$ and the entries, and its $t$-coefficient is then polynomial in $A$). They agree on the dense set $GL_n$ (Part 2) and are continuous, so they agree everywhere.

**Numeric check.** For random $3\times3$ matrices, the difference quotient at $t=10^{-6}$ gave $3.24429$ against $\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{adj}(A)B)=3.24429$.

**Remark.**
- Equivalent form: $\frac{d}{dt}\det A(t)=\det A\operatorname{tr}(A^{-1}A')$, the basis of *Liouville's formula* for linear ODE.
- Taking $B=I$: $\frac{d}{dt}\det(A+tI)|_0=\operatorname{tr}\operatorname{adj}A$ is the coefficient $c_1$ of the characteristic polynomial up to sign.
@@@ mn-080-derivative-of-inversion.md
---
title: The derivative of matrix inversion
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: derivative, inversion, Neumann series, FrÃ©chet derivative
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the derivative of $\iota:A\mapsto A^{-1}$ at $A\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ in the direction $H\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is $D\iota_A(H)=-A^{-1}HA^{-1}$.

## Hint

Write $A+tH=A(I+tA^{-1}H)$ and invert with a geometric series.

## Solution

**Idea.** Reduce to inverting a matrix close to $I$, where the Neumann series gives an explicit expansion.

**Step 1: factorisation.** For $t$ small, $A+tH=A(I+tA^{-1}H)$. We have $\|tA^{-1}H\|\le|t|\|A^{-1}\|\|H\|<1$ for $|t|<\frac1{\|A^{-1}\|\|H\|}$.

**Step 2: Neumann series.** For $\|X\|<1$, $(I+X)^{-1}=\sum_{k\ge0}(-X)^k=I-X+X^2(I+X)^{-1}$, hence
$$\|(I+X)^{-1}-I+X\|\le\frac{\|X\|^2}{1-\|X\|}.$$
(Indeed $(I+X)^{-1}-I+X=X^2(I+X)^{-1}$, as one checks by multiplying by $I+X$.) With $X=tA^{-1}H$ this is $O(t^2)$.

**Step 3: expansion.**
$$(A+tH)^{-1}=(I+tA^{-1}H)^{-1}A^{-1}=\big(I-tA^{-1}H+O(t^2)\big)A^{-1}=A^{-1}-tA^{-1}HA^{-1}+O(t^2).$$

**Step 4: conclusion.** So $\frac{d}{dt}(A+tH)^{-1}|_{t=0}=-A^{-1}HA^{-1}$. Since $H\mapsto-A^{-1}HA^{-1}$ is linear and the remainder is $O(\|H\|^2)$ uniformly, this is the FrÃ©chet derivative.

**Consistency check.** Differentiating $AA^{-1}=I$ gives $HA^{-1}+A\,D=0$, i.e. $D=-A^{-1}HA^{-1}$. For $n=1$: $\frac{d}{da}\frac1a=-\frac1{a^2}$ âœ“.

**Remark.** Matrices do not commute, so one must keep the order $A^{-1}\,H\,A^{-1}$; for $n\ge2$ it is *not* $-HA^{-2}$ in general. The map $\iota$ is $C^\infty$ (even analytic) on $GL_n$: iterating, the $k$-th derivative involves products with $k+1$ copies of $A^{-1}$.
@@@ mn-081-roots-depend-continuously.md
---
title: Roots of a polynomial depend continuously on its coefficients
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: roots of polynomials, Cauchy bound, HÃ¶lder continuity, characteristic polynomial
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $p(z)=z^n+c_{n-1}z^{n-1}+\dots+c_0$ be a monic polynomial with complex roots $r_1,\dots,r_n$ (with multiplicity), and let $q(z)=z^n+d_{n-1}z^{n-1}+\dots+d_0$ be monic with $|d_k-c_k|\le\eta\le1$ for all $k$. Show that there is a constant $C$ depending only on $n$ and $\max_k|c_k|$ such that every root of $q$ is within $C\eta^{1/n}$ of some root of $p$, and vice versa.

## Hint

At a root $z$ of $q$ we have $p(z)=p(z)-q(z)$, which is small. And $p(z)=\prod_i(z-r_i)$.

Use the Cauchy bound to control $|z|$ and hence the size of $p(z)-q(z)$.

## Solution

**Idea.** If $|p(z)|\le\delta$ then the product of the $n$ distances $|z-r_i|$ is at most $\delta$, so one of them is at most $\delta^{1/n}$. Smallness of $p(z)$ at a root of $q$ comes from the closeness of the coefficients.

**Step 1: Cauchy bound.** Every root $z$ of a monic $z^n+a_{n-1}z^{n-1}+\dots+a_0$ satisfies $|z|\le1+\max_k|a_k|$. (If $|z|>1$ then $|z|^n=|\sum a_kz^k|\le\max|a_k|\frac{|z|^n-1}{|z|-1}<\max|a_k|\frac{|z|^n}{|z|-1}$, so $|z|-1<\max|a_k|$.) Put $m=\max_k|c_k|$ and $R=2+m$. Since $\eta\le1$, $\max|d_k|\le m+1$, so all roots of $p$ and of $q$ lie in $\{|z|\le R\}$, and $R\ge2\ge1$.

**Step 2: $p(z)$ is small at a root of $q$.** Let $q(z_0)=0$, $|z_0|\le R$. Then
$$|p(z_0)|=|p(z_0)-q(z_0)|=\Big|\sum_{k<n}(c_k-d_k)z_0^k\Big|\le\eta\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}R^k\le\eta\,nR^{n-1}=:\delta.$$

**Step 3: some root is close.** $|p(z_0)|=\prod_{i=1}^n|z_0-r_i|\le\delta$, so $\min_i|z_0-r_i|^n\le\prod_i|z_0-r_i|\le\delta$? Careful: this needs *every* factor to be $\ge\min$, so $\min_i|z_0-r_i|^n\le\prod_i|z_0-r_i|$. Hence
$$\min_i|z_0-r_i|\le\delta^{1/n}=(nR^{n-1})^{1/n}\eta^{1/n}=:C\eta^{1/n}.$$

**Step 4: converse.** Let $r_i$ be a root of $p$; $|r_i|\le R$. Then $|q(r_i)|=|q(r_i)-p(r_i)|\le\delta$ by the same computation, and $q(r_i)=\prod_j(r_i-s_j)$ with $s_j$ the roots of $q$, so some $s_j$ satisfies $|r_i-s_j|\le\delta^{1/n}$.

**Sharpness of the exponent $\frac1n$.** For $p=z^n$ and $q=z^n-\eta$ the roots of $q$ have modulus $\eta^{1/n}$: with $n=3$, $\eta=10^{-6}$, the roots have modulus $0.01$ (checked numerically). So one can't replace $\eta^{1/n}$ by $\eta$ in general; with *simple* roots one can (implicit function theorem).

**Remark.** This is a "quantitative" form of the theorem that roots are continuous functions of coefficients. It applies to characteristic polynomials, since the coefficients of $\chi_A$ are polynomial in the entries of $A$.
@@@ mn-082-spectral-radius.md
---
title: The spectral radius is continuous
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: spectral radius, continuity of eigenvalues, HÃ¶lder, characteristic polynomial
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Deduce from the previous problem that $\rho(A)=\max_i|\lambda_i(A)|$ (the maximum of the moduli of the complex eigenvalues) is a continuous function on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$. Show that it need not be Lipschitz.

## Hint

Apply Problem 81 to the characteristic polynomials of $A_k$ and $A$.

For the failure of Lipschitz, look at $\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\\varepsilon&0\end{pmatrix}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** $\rho(A)$ is the largest modulus of a root of $\chi_A$. Moving the coefficients a little moves each root a little, and so moves the largest modulus a little.

**Step 1: set-up.** Let $A_k\to A$. The coefficients of $\chi_{A_k}$ converge to those of $\chi_A$ (they are polynomial in the entries), so $\eta_k:=\max_j|c_j(A_k)-c_j(A)|\to0$; discard finitely many terms so that $\eta_k\le1$. Let $C$ be the constant of Problem 81 for $p=\chi_A$.

**Step 2: upper bound.** Let $\lambda$ be an eigenvalue of $A_k$ with $|\lambda|=\rho(A_k)$. By Problem 81, there is an eigenvalue $\mu$ of $A$ with $|\lambda-\mu|\le C\eta_k^{1/n}$, so
$$\rho(A_k)=|\lambda|\le|\mu|+C\eta_k^{1/n}\le\rho(A)+C\eta_k^{1/n}.$$

**Step 3: lower bound.** Let $\mu$ be an eigenvalue of $A$ with $|\mu|=\rho(A)$. By the converse part there is an eigenvalue $\lambda$ of $A_k$ with $|\lambda-\mu|\le C\eta_k^{1/n}$, so
$$\rho(A_k)\ge|\lambda|\ge|\mu|-C\eta_k^{1/n}=\rho(A)-C\eta_k^{1/n}.$$

**Step 4: conclusion.** $|\rho(A_k)-\rho(A)|\le C\eta_k^{1/n}\to0$.

**Not Lipschitz.** $A_\varepsilon=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\\varepsilon&0\end{pmatrix}$ has characteristic polynomial $t^2-\varepsilon$, so $\rho(A_\varepsilon)=\sqrt\varepsilon$ while $\rho(A_0)=0$ and $\|A_\varepsilon-A_0\|=\varepsilon$. The ratio $\sqrt\varepsilon/\varepsilon\to\infty$. (Numerically: $\varepsilon=10^{-4}$ gives $\rho=0.01$.) For the $n\times n$ Jordan block with a corner entry $\varepsilon$, $\rho=\varepsilon^{1/n}$.

**Remark.** On the other hand $\rho$ *is* Lipschitz on the set of **symmetric** (more generally normal) matrices, where $\rho(A)=\|A\|$ (Weyl, Problem 88).
@@@ mn-083-rank.md
---
title: Rank is lower but not upper semicontinuous
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: rank, semicontinuity, minors, open sets
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that rank is lower semicontinuous (for every $A$ there is a neighbourhood on which $\operatorname{rank}\ge\operatorname{rank}A$) but not upper semicontinuous.

## Hint

The rank is the size of the largest nonvanishing minor.

For the failure of upper semicontinuity, test on $\frac1kI\to0$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Having rank $\ge r$ is an *open* condition (some $r\times r$ minor is nonzero), while having rank $\le r$ is *closed*.

**Step 1: minors.** $\operatorname{rank}A\ge r$ iff some $r\times r$ minor of $A$ is nonzero. (If some minor is nonzero, the corresponding $r$ rows are independent. Conversely, if the rank is $\ge r$, one finds $r$ independent rows, and among the columns $r$ independent ones of the resulting $r\times n$ matrix.)

**Step 2: lower semicontinuity.** Let $r=\operatorname{rank}A$ and choose an $r\times r$ minor $m(A)\ne0$. Minors are polynomials in the entries, hence continuous, so $m(B)\ne0$ for all $B$ in a neighbourhood $U$ of $A$. For $B\in U$: $\operatorname{rank}B\ge r=\operatorname{rank}A$. Equivalently, $\{\operatorname{rank}\ge r\}$ is open for every $r$.

**Step 3: not upper semicontinuous.** Upper semicontinuity at $A$ would mean that $\operatorname{rank}B\le\operatorname{rank}A$ for all $B$ near $A$. At $A=0$, $B=\frac1kI\to0$ has rank $n>0$. So it fails at $0$. More generally it fails at every singular $A$, since $A+\varepsilon I$ is invertible for small $\varepsilon\ne0$ (Part 2).

**Consequences.**
- The *maximal* rank $n$ is attained on an open dense set, which is why "generic" matrices are invertible.
- $\{\operatorname{rank}\le r\}$ is closed, and by Step 3 has empty interior for $r<n$.

**Remark.** Rank is continuous exactly on $GL_n$ (the set where it is constant on a neighbourhood, being maximal). More precisely, the points of continuity of $\operatorname{rank}$ are the matrices of rank $n$: at a matrix of rank $r<n$, perturbation by $\varepsilon I$ raises the rank.
@@@ mn-084-adjugate.md
---
title: The rank of the adjugate matrix
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: adjugate, rank, minors, kernel
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge2$. Show that $\operatorname{rank}(\operatorname{adj}A)$ equals $n$, $1$ or $0$ according as $\operatorname{rank}A=n$, $\operatorname{rank}A=n-1$, or $\operatorname{rank}A\le n-2$.

## Hint

Use $A\operatorname{adj}A=\operatorname{adj}A\,A=(\det A)I$.

For the middle case, locate the columns and the rows of $\operatorname{adj}A$ in $\ker A$ and $\ker A^T$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The entries of $\operatorname{adj}A$ are the $(n-1)\times(n-1)$ minors of $A$ (up to sign), so the pattern of vanishing depends on the rank. The identity $A\operatorname{adj}A=(\det A)I$ controls the kernel.

**Case 1: $\operatorname{rank}A=n$.** Then $\det A\ne0$, $\operatorname{adj}A=\det A\cdot A^{-1}$ is invertible: rank $n$.

**Case 2: $\operatorname{rank}A\le n-2$.** Every $(n-1)\times(n-1)$ minor has size larger than the rank, hence vanishes (Problem 83, Step 1). All entries of $\operatorname{adj}A$ vanish: $\operatorname{adj}A=0$, rank $0$.

**Case 3: $\operatorname{rank}A=n-1$.**
- *Nonzero.* Some $(n-1)\times(n-1)$ minor is nonzero (Problem 83), so $\operatorname{adj}A\ne0$: rank $\ge1$.
- *Columns.* $\det A=0$, so $A\operatorname{adj}A=0$: every column of $\operatorname{adj}A$ lies in $\ker A$, which has dimension $n-\operatorname{rank}A=1$. So the column space of $\operatorname{adj}A$ has dimension $\le1$, i.e. $\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{adj}A\le1$.

Hence $\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{adj}A=1$. (By symmetry, $\operatorname{adj}A\,A=0$ puts the rows into $\ker A^T$, also of dimension $1$: $\operatorname{adj}A=c\,uv^T$ with $u\in\ker A$, $v\in\ker A^T$.)

**Example.** $n=2$, $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\2&4\end{pmatrix}$ (rank $1=n-1$): $\operatorname{adj}A=\begin{pmatrix}4&-2\\-2&1\end{pmatrix}$, rank $1$ âœ“. For $n=4$, random matrices of rank $4,3,2$ gave adjugate ranks $4,1,0$ numerically.

**Remark.** The case $n=1$ is excluded since $\operatorname{adj}A=(1)$ always. The result implies that $\operatorname{adj}$ is continuous but its rank pattern jumps. Also $\det(\operatorname{adj}A)=(\det A)^{n-1}$.
@@@ mn-085-squaring.md
---
title: The sign of the determinant under squaring
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: squaring, determinant sign, open sets, GL_n^+
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $GL_n^-=\{A:\det A<0\}$ and $GL_n^+=\{A:\det A>0\}$ are open in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$, and that $A\mapsto A^2$ maps $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ into $GL_n^+$ but is not onto $GL_n^+$ when $n=2$.

## Hint

$GL_n^\pm=\det^{-1}$ of an open half-line.

For non-surjectivity, find a $2\times2$ matrix of determinant $1$ which is not a square (see Problem 74).

## Solution

**Idea.** $\det$ is continuous, so preimages of open sets are open; multiplicativity of $\det$ controls the sign of a square.

**Step 1: openness.** $GL_n^+=\det^{-1}((0,\infty))$ and $GL_n^-=\det^{-1}((-\infty,0))$ are preimages of open sets by the continuous map $\det$.

**Step 2: squares have positive determinant.** For $A\in GL_n$, $\det(A^2)=(\det A)^2>0$, so $A^2\in GL_n^+$.

**Step 3: not onto for $n=2$.** The matrix $N=\begin{pmatrix}-1&1\\0&-1\end{pmatrix}$ has $\det N=1$, so $N\in GL_2^+$. If $N=B^2$ with $B$ real, the eigenvalues of $B$ are two conjugate non-real numbers $\pm i$ or two real numbers; real $\mu$ would have $\mu^2=-1$, impossible, so they are $i,-i$, distinct. Then $B$ is diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$, so is $B^2$, with eigenvalues $-1,-1$: $B^2=-I\neq N$. Contradiction.

**Remark.**
- Combined with Problem 74: the image of squaring in $GL_n$ is a *proper* subset of $GL_n^+$ ($n\ge2$) â€” it contains the open set of matrices with no negative eigenvalue (which have a real square root by the principal logarithm/power series), but it is not open-and-closed in $GL_n^+$ in an obvious way.
- Squaring on $GL_n^+$ is far from injective: $B$ and $-B$ have the same square, and for $n\ge2$ also $\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)$ squares to $I$.
@@@ mn-086-open-mapping.md
---
title: The determinant is an open map
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: determinant, open map, SVD, singular matrices
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\det:M_n(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}$ is an open map: it sends open sets to open sets ($n\ge1$).

## Hint

It suffices to show that every neighbourhood of every $A$ contains matrices whose determinants fill a neighbourhood of $\det A$.

If $A$ is singular, write $A=P\Sigma Q^T$ (SVD) and modify the zero singular values.

## Solution

**Idea.** For an open $U\ni A$ we must find an interval around $\det A$ contained in $\det(U)$. We move $A$ along a curve inside $U$ along which $\det$ varies over an interval around $\det A$.

**Case 1: $\det A\ne0$.** Let $A_s=A\operatorname{diag}(s,1,\dots,1)$. Then $\det A_s=s\det A$ and $A_s\to A$ as $s\to1$, so $A_s\in U$ for $s\in(1-\tau,1+\tau)$ for some $\tau>0$. As $s$ runs over $(1-\tau,1+\tau)$, $\det A_s$ runs over the interval $\big(\det A(1-\tau),\det A(1+\tau)\big)$ (or reversed), which is a neighbourhood of $\det A$.

**Case 2: $\det A=0$.** Write the SVD $A=P\Sigma Q^T$ with $P,Q$ orthogonal and $\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_r,0,\dots,0)$, $\sigma_i>0$, $r<n$. Let $k=n-r\ge1$. For $\tau>0$ small and $s\in[-\tau,\tau]$ put
$$\Sigma_s=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_r,\,s,\,\tau,\dots,\tau)\quad(\text{with }k-1\text{ entries equal to }\tau),$$
and $A_s=P\Sigma_sQ^T$. Then
- $\|A_s-A\|=\|\Sigma_s-\Sigma\|\le\tau$ (operator norm is invariant under orthogonal maps), so $A_s\in U$ when $\tau$ is small enough;
- $\det A_s=\det P\det Q\cdot\sigma_1\cdots\sigma_r\cdot\tau^{k-1}\cdot s=c\,s$ where $c=\pm\sigma_1\cdots\sigma_r\tau^{k-1}\ne0$.

As $s$ ranges over $[-\tau,\tau]$, $\det A_s=cs$ covers the interval $[-|c|\tau,|c|\tau]$, a neighbourhood of $0=\det A$.

**Conclusion.** For every open $U$ and $A\in U$, $\det(U)$ contains a neighbourhood of $\det A$, so $\det(U)$ is open.

**Remark.** The determinant is a *submersion* at every matrix of rank $\ge n-1$ (its derivative $\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{adj}(A)\,\cdot)$ is nonzero there, Problem 79), so openness is easy near them; the interest is at matrices of rank $\le n-2$ where $d\det=0$: $\det$ is still open though not a submersion. For $n=1$ it is the identity.
@@@ mn-087-weyl-inequalities-for-singular-values.md
---
title: Weyl's inequality for singular values
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: singular values, Courant-Fischer, perturbation, Lipschitz
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $\sigma_1(A)\ge\sigma_2(A)\ge\dots\ge\sigma_n(A)\ge0$ be the singular values of $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$. Prove that for every $k$,
$$|\sigma_k(A)-\sigma_k(B)|\le\|A-B\|.$$

## Hint

Courantâ€“Fischer (minâ€“max) for singular values: $\sigma_k(A)=\max_{\dim V=k}\ \min_{x\in V,\ |x|=1}|Ax|$.

Compare $|Ax|$ and $|Bx|$ for unit $x$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Each $\sigma_k$ is given by a minâ€“max of the quantity $|Ax|$ over unit vectors, and $x\mapsto|Ax|$ moves by at most $\|A-B\|$ when $A$ is replaced by $B$.

**Step 0: the minâ€“max formula.** $\sigma_k(A)^2=\lambda_k(A^TA)$, the $k$-th largest eigenvalue of the symmetric matrix $A^TA$. By the Courantâ€“Fischer theorem (quoted without proof),
$$\lambda_k(A^TA)=\max_{\dim V=k}\ \min_{x\in V,|x|=1}x^TA^TAx=\max_{\dim V=k}\min_{x\in V,|x|=1}|Ax|^2.$$
Taking square roots gives the displayed formula (square root is increasing).

**Step 1: pointwise comparison.** For every unit vector $x$,
$$|Ax|\ge|Bx|-|(A-B)x|\ge|Bx|-\|A-B\|.$$

**Step 2: min over $V$.** For any $k$-dimensional subspace $V$,
$$\min_{x\in V,|x|=1}|Ax|\ge\min_{x\in V,|x|=1}|Bx|-\|A-B\|.$$

**Step 3: max over $V$.** Taking the maximum over all $k$-dimensional $V$ on both sides,
$$\sigma_k(A)\ge\sigma_k(B)-\|A-B\|.$$

**Step 4: symmetry.** Exchanging $A$ and $B$: $\sigma_k(B)\ge\sigma_k(A)-\|A-B\|$. Together: $|\sigma_k(A)-\sigma_k(B)|\le\|A-B\|$.

**Numeric check.** For a random $5\times5$ matrix $A$ and $B=A+0.1\,\mathrm{noise}$ the largest deviation $\max_k|\sigma_k(A)-\sigma_k(B)|=0.229$ is below $\|A-B\|=0.322$.

**Remark.**
- Consequences: each $\sigma_k$ is $1$-Lipschitz, hence continuous; with $k=n$ one recovers that $\sigma_{\min}$ is the distance to the singular matrices (Problem 63) and with $k=1$ that $\|\cdot\|$ is $1$-Lipschitz (Problem 72).
- In contrast with the eigenvalues of a non-normal matrix (Problem 82), singular values are *Lipschitz* functions of the matrix: this is why numerical analysts prefer them.
@@@ mn-088-weyl-inequalities-for-eigenvalues.md
---
title: Weyl's inequality for eigenvalues of symmetric matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: eigenvalues, symmetric matrices, Courant-Fischer, perturbation
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

For symmetric $A,B\in\mathrm{Sym}_n$ with ordered eigenvalues $\lambda_1\ge\dots\ge\lambda_n$, prove that $|\lambda_k(A)-\lambda_k(B)|\le\|A-B\|$ for every $k$.

## Hint

Courantâ€“Fischer for the Rayleigh quotient: $\lambda_k(A)=\max_{\dim V=k}\min_{x\in V,|x|=1}x^TAx$.

Bound $|x^TAx-x^TBx|$ for unit $x$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Same as for singular values: the quantities in the minâ€“max formula move by at most $\|A-B\|$.

**Step 0.** By Courantâ€“Fischer, for symmetric $A$,
$$\lambda_k(A)=\max_{\dim V=k}\ \min_{x\in V,|x|=1}x^TAx.$$

**Step 1: pointwise.** For every unit $x$,
$$x^TAx=x^TBx+x^T(A-B)x\ge x^TBx-|x^T(A-B)x|\ge x^TBx-\|A-B\|,$$
since $|x^TCx|\le|x|\,|Cx|\le\|C\|$ by Cauchyâ€“Schwarz.

**Step 2: min over $V$, then max over $V$.** For each $k$-dimensional $V$,
$$\min_{V,|x|=1}x^TAx\ge\min_{V,|x|=1}x^TBx-\|A-B\|,$$
and taking $\max_V$ gives $\lambda_k(A)\ge\lambda_k(B)-\|A-B\|$.

**Step 3: symmetry.** Exchange $A$ and $B$ to get the other inequality.

**Numeric check.** For two random PSD matrices $S=AA^T$, $T=BB^T$ with $\|S-T\|=0.709$: $\max_k|\lambda_k(S)-\lambda_k(T)|=0.412\le0.709$ âœ“.

**Remark.**
- In particular $\rho(A)=\max_k|\lambda_k(A)|$ is $1$-Lipschitz on $\mathrm{Sym}_n$, contrasting with the HÃ¶lder exponent $\frac1n$ of Problem 82 for general matrices.
- The inequality fails for non-symmetric matrices with the ordered *real parts* replaced by anything: e.g. Jordan-type perturbations give $\varepsilon^{1/n}$ shifts.
- Weyl's inequality is the core of the Hoffmanâ€“Wielandt theorem $\sum(\lambda_k(A)-\lambda_k(B))^2\le\|A-B\|_F^2$.
@@@ mn-089-continuity-of-the-matrix-square-root.md
---
title: The PSD square root is continuous
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: matrix square root, compactness, uniqueness, PSD cone
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $S\mapsto\sqrt S$ is continuous on the cone of symmetric positive semidefinite matrices, where $\sqrt S$ is the unique PSD matrix whose square is $S$.

## Hint

Take $S_k\to S$ and show every subsequence of $T_k=\sqrt{S_k}$ has a further subsequence converging to $\sqrt S$.

Boundedness: $\|T_k\|^2=\|S_k\|$.

## Solution

**Idea.** A compactness-plus-uniqueness argument: bounded sequences have convergent subsequences, and the limit must satisfy the defining property of $\sqrt S$, which determines it uniquely.

**Step 0: the definition.** For $S$ PSD, the spectral theorem gives $S=Q\operatorname{diag}(\mu_i)Q^T$ with $\mu_i\ge0$, and $\sqrt S=Q\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{\mu_i})Q^T$ is PSD with $(\sqrt S)^2=S$. *Uniqueness* of the PSD square root is a standard fact (used here without proof): if $T\ge0$ and $T^2=S$ then $T=\sqrt S$.

**Step 1: boundedness.** $T_k=\sqrt{S_k}$ is symmetric, so $\|T_k\|^2=\|T_k^2\|=\|S_k\|$ (for a symmetric matrix, $\|T\|=\rho(T)$ and $\rho(T^2)=\rho(T)^2$). Since $S_k\to S$ is convergent, $\|S_k\|$ is bounded, hence so is $(T_k)$.

**Step 2: limit points.** By Bolzanoâ€“Weierstrass, any subsequence of $(T_k)$ has a further subsequence $T_{k_j}\to T$. The cone of PSD matrices is closed, so $T$ is PSD. By continuity of multiplication (Problem 67), $T^2=\lim T_{k_j}^2=\lim S_{k_j}=S$. By uniqueness, $T=\sqrt S$.

**Step 3: convergence.** Every subsequence of $(T_k)$ has a sub-subsequence converging to the same limit $\sqrt S$; hence $T_k\to\sqrt S$ (if not, some subsequence would stay at distance $\ge\epsilon$ from $\sqrt S$, contradicting Step 2).

**Remark.**
- *Quantitative version.* The map is HÃ¶lder: $\|\sqrt A-\sqrt B\|\le\sqrt{\|A-B\|}$ for PSD $A,B$ (known; compare $n=1$, $\sqrt x$ at $0$). It is not Lipschitz at singular matrices: $\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ vs $\varepsilon$.
- On $\{A\ge cI\}$ with $c>0$ one has, in the Frobenius norm, $\|\sqrt A-\sqrt B\|_F\le\frac{\|A-B\|_F}{2\sqrt c}$. (With $X=\sqrt A-\sqrt B$ one has $\sqrt A\,X+X\sqrt B=A-B$, and in the eigenbases the coefficient of $x_{ij}$ is $\alpha_i+\beta_j\ge2\sqrt c$.) A numeric test confirmed the bound ($0.388\le1.551$).
- Used in Problem 90 to show the polar decomposition is continuous.
@@@ mn-090-polar-decomposition.md
---
title: Polar decomposition is a homeomorphism
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: polar decomposition, homeomorphism, orthogonal group, positive definite
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the map $GL_n(\mathbb{R})\to O(n)\times\mathrm{PD}$, $A\mapsto(U,P)$ with $A=UP$, is a homeomorphism, where $\mathrm{PD}$ is the set of symmetric positive definite matrices.

## Hint

$P=\sqrt{A^TA}$ and $U=AP^{-1}$.

Prove first that the pair $(U,P)$ is unique, then continuity in both directions.

## Solution

**Idea.** Existence and uniqueness give a bijection; the formulas for the two directions are compositions of continuous maps (square root, inverse, product).

**Step 1: uniqueness.** If $A=UP$ with $U$ orthogonal and $P$ symmetric positive definite, then
$$A^TA=P^TU^TUP=P^2.$$
$P$ is a PSD matrix whose square is $A^TA$, so by uniqueness of the PSD square root $P=\sqrt{A^TA}$, and $U=AP^{-1}$.

**Step 2: existence.** For $A$ invertible, $A^TA$ is symmetric and positive definite ($x^TA^TAx=|Ax|^2>0$ for $x\ne0$). Put $P=\sqrt{A^TA}$: it is positive definite (its eigenvalues are the positive square roots), hence invertible. Put $U=AP^{-1}$. Then
$$U^TU=P^{-1}A^TAP^{-1}=P^{-1}P^2P^{-1}=I,$$
so $U\in O(n)$ and $A=UP$.

**Step 3: continuity of $\Phi:A\mapsto(U,P)$.** $A\mapsto A^TA$ is polynomial, $S\mapsto\sqrt S$ is continuous (Problem 89), $P\mapsto P^{-1}$ is continuous (Problem 68), and the product is continuous (Problem 67). So $P=\sqrt{A^TA}$ and $U=AP^{-1}$ are continuous functions of $A$.

**Step 4: continuity of the inverse.** $\Psi:(U,P)\mapsto UP$ is the restriction of the multiplication, continuous, and takes values in $GL_n$. By Steps 1â€“2, $\Psi\circ\Phi=\mathrm{id}$ and $\Phi\circ\Psi=\mathrm{id}$.

**Consequences.**
- $GL_n(\mathbb{R})\cong O(n)\times\mathrm{PD}$, and $\mathrm{PD}$ is a convex cone, hence contractible. So $GL_n$ deformation retracts onto $O(n)$ via $A\mapsto U$; likewise $GL_n^+\simeq SO(n)$.
- $O(n)$ has two components, so $GL_n$ has exactly two (Part 5).

**Remark.** The analogue for $n=1$ is $\mathbb{R}^\times=\{\pm1\}\times(0,\infty)$, the polar form $x=\operatorname{sgn}(x)|x|$ of a real number; for complex numbers it is $z=e^{i\theta}r$.
@@@ mn-091-qr-decomposition-and-the-iwasawa-homeomorphism.md
---
title: GL_n is homeomorphic to O(n) Ã— upper triangular
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: QR decomposition, Gram-Schmidt, Iwasawa, homeomorphism
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $T_+$ be the set of upper triangular real matrices with positive diagonal entries. Show that $GL_n(\mathbb{R})\to O(n)\times T_+$, $A\mapsto(Q,R)$ with $A=QR$, is a homeomorphism.

## Hint

Gramâ€“Schmidt on the columns of $A$ is built from sums, products, norms and divisions by nonzero numbers.

Uniqueness: an orthogonal upper triangular matrix is diagonal.

## Solution

**Idea.** Existence is Gramâ€“Schmidt; the same recipe is a continuous formula; uniqueness makes the maps inverse to each other.

**Step 1: existence by Gramâ€“Schmidt.** Let $a_1,\dots,a_n$ be the columns of $A$ (a basis). Define
$$u_j=a_j-\sum_{i<j}\langle a_j,q_i\rangle q_i,\qquad q_j=\frac{u_j}{|u_j|},$$
where $u_j\ne0$ because $a_j\notin\operatorname{span}(a_1,\dots,a_{j-1})$. Then $(q_j)$ is orthonormal and $a_j=\sum_{i\le j}R_{ij}q_i$ with $R_{ij}=\langle a_j,q_i\rangle$ for $i<j$ and $R_{jj}=|u_j|>0$. So $A=QR$ with $Q=(q_1|\dots|q_n)$ orthogonal and $R\in T_+$.

**Step 2: uniqueness.** If $QR=Q'R'$ then $M:=R'R^{-1}=Q'^TQ$ is orthogonal and upper triangular with positive diagonal. An orthogonal matrix satisfies $M^{-1}=M^T$; but $M^{-1}$ is upper triangular, and $M^T$ is lower triangular, so $M$ is diagonal. A diagonal orthogonal matrix has entries $\pm1$, and positive ones force $M=I$. Hence $R'=R$, $Q'=Q$.

**Step 3: continuity of $A\mapsto(Q,R)$.** Each step of Gramâ€“Schmidt uses sums, products, inner products, and $u\mapsto u/|u|$, which is continuous on $u\neq0$. By induction on $j$, the vectors $q_j$ and the numbers $R_{ij}$ are continuous functions of $A$ on $GL_n$, as $u_j\ne0$ throughout.

**Step 4: the inverse.** $(Q,R)\mapsto QR$ is continuous (Problem 67) with values in $GL_n$, and by uniqueness and existence it is the inverse of $A\mapsto(Q,R)$.

**Remark.**
- $T_+\cong(0,\infty)^n\times\mathbb{R}^{n(n-1)/2}$ is contractible, so this decomposition again shows that $GL_n$ deformation retracts onto $O(n)$ (compare Problem 90).
- It is the $GL_n(\mathbb{R})=K\cdot A\cdot N$ **Iwasawa decomposition**, with $K=O(n)$, $A$ the positive diagonal matrices, and $N$ the unipotent upper triangular matrices.
- Numerically, `numpy.linalg.qr` does not enforce $R_{jj}>0$; one must multiply by the signs of the diagonal (checked).
@@@ mn-092-cholesky-decomposition-homeomorphism.md
---
title: Cholesky: a homeomorphism from T_+ onto PD
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Cholesky decomposition, positive definite matrices, QR, homeomorphism
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $R\mapsto R^TR$ is a homeomorphism from $T_+$ (upper triangular matrices with positive diagonal) onto the cone $\mathrm{PD}$ of symmetric positive definite matrices.

## Hint

For the inverse, combine the square root $A\mapsto\sqrt A$ with the QR decomposition of Problem 91.

Injectivity: if $R^TR=R'^TR'$ then $R'R^{-1}$ is orthogonal and upper triangular.

## Solution

**Idea.** The map $R\mapsto R^TR$ is polynomial; its inverse is constructed by composing already-known continuous maps.

**Step 1: well-defined.** For $R\in T_+$, $R$ is invertible, $R^TR$ is symmetric and $x^TR^TRx=|Rx|^2>0$ for $x\ne0$: $R^TR\in\mathrm{PD}$. The map is polynomial in the entries, hence continuous.

**Step 2: injective.** If $R^TR=R'^TR'$ with $R,R'\in T_+$, put $M=R'R^{-1}$, upper triangular with positive diagonal (products and inverses of such matrices are such). Then
$$M^TM=R^{-T}R'^TR'R^{-1}=R^{-T}R^TRR^{-1}=I,$$
so $M$ is orthogonal and upper triangular with positive diagonal; as in Problem 91, $M=I$, i.e. $R'=R$.

**Step 3: surjective, with a continuous inverse.** Let $A\in\mathrm{PD}$, $S=\sqrt A$ (positive definite, symmetric, hence in $GL_n$). By Problem 91, $S=QR$ with $Q$ orthogonal and $R\in T_+$. Then
$$A=S^2=S^TS=R^TQ^TQR=R^TR.$$
The assignment $A\mapsto R$ is the composite of $A\mapsto\sqrt A$ (continuous, Problem 89) and $S\mapsto R$ from the QR decomposition (continuous, Problem 91). It is a right and left inverse of $R\mapsto R^TR$ by the injectivity just proved.

**Dimension count.** $T_+\cong(0,\infty)^n\times\mathbb{R}^{n(n-1)/2}$, and $\dim\mathrm{Sym}_n=\frac{n(n+1)}2=n+\frac{n(n-1)}2$ âœ“.

**Numeric check.** `numpy.linalg.cholesky` returns the lower factor $L=R^T$ with positive diagonal, and $LL^T=P$ held to $9\cdot10^{-16}$.

**Remark.** Cholesky's factorisation is the standard way to test positive definiteness and solve linear systems; the topological content here is that the factor depends *continuously* on the matrix, so it is stable under small perturbations. As a by-product, $\mathrm{PD}$ is homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^{n(n+1)/2}$.
@@@ mn-093-nearest-orthogonal-matrix.md
---
title: The orthogonal matrix nearest to a given matrix
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: orthogonal Procrustes, SVD, Frobenius norm, polar decomposition
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ have singular values $\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_n$ and SVD $A=U\Sigma V^T$. Show that
$$\min_{Q\in O(n)}\|A-Q\|_F^2=\sum_{i=1}^n(\sigma_i-1)^2,$$
attained at $Q=UV^T$, which is the orthogonal factor of a polar decomposition of $A$. If $A$ is invertible, this minimiser is unique.

## Hint

Expand $\|A-Q\|_F^2=\|A\|_F^2-2\operatorname{tr}(Q^TA)+\|Q\|_F^2$ and note $\|Q\|_F^2=n$.

Insert the SVD in the trace and set $W=V^TQ^TU$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Minimising the distance is the same as maximising $\operatorname{tr}(Q^TA)$, which becomes $\sum\sigma_iW_{ii}$ for an orthogonal matrix $W$.

**Step 1: expansion.** With $\langle X,Y\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(X^TY)$,
$$\|A-Q\|_F^2=\|A\|_F^2-2\operatorname{tr}(Q^TA)+\|Q\|_F^2=\sum_i\sigma_i^2-2\operatorname{tr}(Q^TA)+n,$$
using $\|A\|_F^2=\sum\sigma_i^2$ and $\|Q\|_F^2=\operatorname{tr}(Q^TQ)=n$.

**Step 2: the trace.** By cyclicity of the trace,
$$\operatorname{tr}(Q^TA)=\operatorname{tr}(Q^TU\Sigma V^T)=\operatorname{tr}(V^TQ^TU\,\Sigma)=\operatorname{tr}(W\Sigma)=\sum_i\sigma_iW_{ii},\qquad W:=V^TQ^TU\in O(n).$$

**Step 3: bound.** Every entry of an orthogonal matrix satisfies $|W_{ii}|\le1$ (columns are unit vectors). Since $\sigma_i\ge0$,
$$\operatorname{tr}(Q^TA)\le\sum_i\sigma_i,$$
with equality when $W=I$, that is $V^TQ^TU=I\iff Q=UV^T$.

**Step 4: the minimum.**
$$\min_Q\|A-Q\|_F^2=\sum\sigma_i^2-2\sum\sigma_i+n=\sum(\sigma_i-1)^2.$$

**Step 5: polar factor.** $A=U\Sigma V^T=(UV^T)(V\Sigma V^T)$, with $V\Sigma V^T$ symmetric PSD: the orthogonal factor is $UV^T$.

**Step 6: uniqueness for invertible $A$.** All $\sigma_i>0$, so equality forces $W_{ii}=1$ for every $i$. A unit column with a $1$ in position $i$ is $e_i$, so $W=I$ and $Q=UV^T$ is the only minimiser.

**Numeric check.** For a random $4\times4$ matrix, $\|A-UV^T\|_F^2=2.2953=\sum(\sigma_i-1)^2$, and the best of 2000 random orthogonal matrices gave $3.41>2.295$ âœ“.

**Remark.** This is the *orthogonal Procrustes problem*. For a singular $A$ the minimiser may be non-unique (e.g. $A=0$: every $Q$ is at distance $\sqrt n$). Consequently the nearest-orthogonal map is continuous on $GL_n$ but not on all of $M_n$.
@@@ mn-094-the-cayley-transform.md
---
title: The Cayley transform of a skew-symmetric matrix
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Cayley transform, skew-symmetric, special orthogonal group, chart
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $C(K)=(I-K)(I+K)^{-1}$ is a homeomorphism from the space $\mathrm{Skew}_n$ of skew-symmetric matrices onto $\{U\in SO(n):-1\notin\operatorname{spec}U\}$.

## Hint

For skew $K$, compute $v^T(I+K)v$ to show that $I+K$ is invertible.

The inverse is given by the same formula: $K=(I-U)(I+U)^{-1}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The matrices $I+K$ and $I-K$ commute and are transposes of each other, which gives orthogonality; the formula is an involution.

**Step 1: $I+K$ is invertible.** If $(I+K)v=0$ then $0=v^T(I+K)v=|v|^2+v^TKv=|v|^2$, since $v^TKv=0$ for skew $K$ ($v^TKv=(v^TKv)^T=-v^TKv$). So $v=0$.

**Step 2: $C(K)$ is orthogonal.** Using $K^T=-K$: $(I+K)^T=I-K$, so
$$C(K)^T=\big((I+K)^{-1}\big)^T(I-K)^T=(I-K)^{-1}(I+K).$$
Since $I+K$ and $I-K$ commute,
$$C^TC=(I-K)^{-1}(I+K)(I-K)(I+K)^{-1}=(I-K)^{-1}(I-K)(I+K)(I+K)^{-1}=I.$$
Moreover $\det C=\frac{\det(I-K)}{\det(I+K)}=1$, because $\det(I-K)=\det((I+K)^T)=\det(I+K)$. So $C(K)\in SO(n)$.

**Step 3: $-1$ is not an eigenvalue.** If $C(K)v=-v$, let $w=(I+K)^{-1}v$. Then $(I-K)w=-(I+K)w$, so $w-Kw=-w-Kw$, hence $2w=0$, $v=0$.

**Step 4: the inverse map.** For $U\in SO(n)$ with $I+U$ invertible, define $K=(I-U)(I+U)^{-1}$.
- *Skew:* with $U^T=U^{-1}$, $K^T=(I+U^T)^{-1}(I-U^T)=\big(U^{-1}(U+I)\big)^{-1}U^{-1}(U-I)=(I+U)^{-1}(U-I)=-K$ (as $I\pm U$ commute).
- *$C(K)=U$:* $I+K=2(I+U)^{-1}$ and $I-K=2U(I+U)^{-1}$, so $C(K)=2U(I+U)^{-1}\cdot\frac12(I+U)=U$.
- Similarly the inverse formula applied to $C(K)$ returns $K$ (the roles of the two maps are symmetric: the Cayley formula is an involution).

**Step 5: continuity.** Both maps are composites of inversion (continuous, Problem 68), sums and products, hence continuous.

**Example.** $n=2$, $K=t\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$: $C(K)$ is the rotation by angle $-2\arctan t$. Numerically $t=0.7$ gives $\arctan2(C_{10},C_{00})=-1.22145=-2\arctan0.7$ âœ“. The only rotation not reached is the one with $-1$ in its spectrum, namely $R_\pi=-I$ ($t\to\infty$).

**Remark.**
- For even $n$, orthogonal matrices with $\det=-1$ always have $-1$ as an eigenvalue, and for odd $n$ too; so the condition "$\in SO(n)$" is automatic.
- This shows $SO(n)$ minus a closed nowhere dense set is homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^{n(n-1)/2}$: a rational (algebraic) chart, an alternative to the exponential map.
@@@ mn-095-no-continuous-choice-of-eigenvector.md
---
title: No continuous choice of a unit eigenvector
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: eigenvectors, MÃ¶bius band, connectedness, no continuous section
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $A(\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\\sin\theta&-\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$ for $\theta\in[0,2\pi]$ (so $A(0)=A(2\pi)$). Show that there is no continuous map $v:[0,2\pi]\to\mathbb{R}^2$ of unit eigenvectors for the largest eigenvalue of $A(\theta)$ with $v(0)=v(2\pi)$.

## Hint

Find the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of $A(\theta)$ explicitly.

Show that any continuous choice must coincide, up to a global sign, with $(\cos\frac\theta2,\sin\frac\theta2)$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The eigenvector of the largest eigenvalue is determined up to sign, and following it continuously around the loop returns it with the opposite sign: a "holonomy" of $-1$, like a MÃ¶bius band.

**Step 1: eigenvalues.** $A(\theta)$ is symmetric with $\operatorname{tr}A=0$ and $\det A=-\cos^2\theta-\sin^2\theta=-1$, so the eigenvalues are $\pm1$ and the top eigenvalue is $1$, always simple.

**Step 2: eigenvectors.** Let $w(\theta)=(\cos\frac\theta2,\sin\frac\theta2)$. Then
$$A(\theta)w=\big(\cos\theta\cos\tfrac\theta2+\sin\theta\sin\tfrac\theta2,\ \sin\theta\cos\tfrac\theta2-\cos\theta\sin\tfrac\theta2\big)=\big(\cos\tfrac\theta2,\ \sin\tfrac\theta2\big)=w(\theta),$$
by the subtraction formulas. So $w(\theta)$ is a unit eigenvector for $1$, and since the eigenspace is a line, the unit eigenvectors are exactly $\pm w(\theta)$.

**Step 3: any continuous choice is $\pm w$.** Let $v$ be a continuous choice. Then $v(\theta)=\varepsilon(\theta)w(\theta)$ with $\varepsilon(\theta)=\langle v(\theta),w(\theta)\rangle\in\{\pm1\}$. The function $\varepsilon$ is continuous on the connected interval $[0,2\pi]$ with values in $\{\pm1\}$, hence constant.

**Step 4: contradiction.** If $v(0)=v(2\pi)$ then $\varepsilon w(0)=\varepsilon w(2\pi)$, i.e. $w(0)=w(2\pi)$. But $w(0)=(1,0)$ while $w(2\pi)=(\cos\pi,\sin\pi)=(-1,0)\ne w(0)$. Contradiction.

**Remark.**
- The *eigenvalue* $1$ and the *eigenprojection* $v v^T=w w^T$ do depend continuously (and periodically) on $\theta$; only the *eigenvector* does not. This is why eigenvectors must be defined up to sign when matrices vary along loops.
- Geometrically: the set $\{(A,v):Av=v,|v|=1\}$ is a double cover of the circle $\theta\in S^1$, and it is connected (a single circle traversed twice); a double cover of a circle with a continuous section would be trivial.
- The same obstruction shows there is no continuous orientation of eigenlines of $2\times2$ symmetric matrices with distinct eigenvalues.
@@@ mn-096-no-continuous-square-root-on-so2.md
---
title: SO(2) has no continuous square root
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-continuity
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: path lifting, SO(2), square root, covering space
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that there is no continuous map $S:SO(2)\to SO(2)$ with $S(U)^2=U$ for all $U\in SO(2)$.

## Hint

Write $R_\theta=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$ and lift $\theta\mapsto S(R_\theta)$ to a continuous angle function.

Compare $\varphi(2\pi)-\varphi(0)$ computed from $S(U)^2=U$ and from $R_0=R_{2\pi}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** A square root on $SO(2)\cong S^1$ would be a continuous section of the doubling map $z\mapsto z^2$; going once around the circle would give half a turn for the square root, which is not a closed loop.

**Step 1: lifting.** The map $\theta\mapsto S(R_\theta)$ is a continuous path $[0,2\pi]\to SO(2)$. The map $\mathbb{R}\to SO(2)$, $\varphi\mapsto R_\varphi$ is a covering map, so by the path-lifting theorem there is a *continuous* function $\varphi:[0,2\pi]\to\mathbb{R}$ with
$$S(R_\theta)=R_{\varphi(\theta)}\quad\text{for all }\theta\in[0,2\pi].$$
(Concretely: $\varphi(\theta)$ is the polar angle of the first column of $S(R_\theta)$, followed continuously.)

**Step 2: the equation.** Since $R_a R_b=R_{a+b}$, $S(R_\theta)^2=R_{2\varphi(\theta)}$ and the equation $S(U)^2=U$ reads $R_{2\varphi(\theta)}=R_\theta$, i.e.
$$2\varphi(\theta)-\theta\in2\pi\mathbb{Z}\qquad\text{for all }\theta\in[0,2\pi].$$

**Step 3: constancy.** The function $\theta\mapsto\frac{2\varphi(\theta)-\theta}{2\pi}$ is continuous and integer valued on the interval $[0,2\pi]$, hence constant, equal to some $k\in\mathbb{Z}$. So $\varphi(\theta)=\frac\theta2+k\pi$ and
$$\varphi(2\pi)-\varphi(0)=\pi.$$

**Step 4: contradiction.** $R_{2\pi}=R_0=I$, so $S(R_{2\pi})=S(R_0)$, i.e. $R_{\varphi(2\pi)}=R_{\varphi(0)}$, which forces $\varphi(2\pi)-\varphi(0)\in2\pi\mathbb{Z}$. But $\pi\notin2\pi\mathbb{Z}$. Contradiction.

**Remark.**
- It is the same fact as the non-existence of a continuous branch of $\sqrt z$ on $\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}$ or on the unit circle. Locally a continuous square root exists: e.g. on $SO(2)\setminus\{-I\}$ the Cayley transform of Problem 94 gives $U=R_\theta\mapsto R_{\theta/2}$ for $\theta\in(-\pi,\pi)$.
- The obstruction is topological: $SO(2)$ is not simply connected ($\pi_1=\mathbb{Z}$). On the simply connected $SU(2)$ or on the contractible cone $\mathrm{PD}$ (Problem 89) square roots do exist continuously.
- Combined with the principal square root on the PSD cone (Problem 89): the existence of a continuous square root depends on the domain.

@@@ mn-035-basic-sets.md
---
title: The groups GL_n and SL_n: open and closed
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: open sets, closed sets, determinant, GL_n, SL_n
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is open and $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is closed in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Write each set as the preimage of a simple subset of $\mathbb{R}$ under the map $\det$. Then use: the preimage of an open (resp. closed) set under a continuous map is open (resp. closed).

## Solution

**Idea.** Both sets are defined by a condition on the single number $\det A$, and $\det$ is continuous. Topology then does all the work.

**Step 1: $\det$ is continuous.** By the Leibniz formula
$$\det A=\sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)\,a_{1\sigma(1)}\cdots a_{n\sigma(n)}$$
$\det A$ is a polynomial in the $n^2$ entries of $A$. The coordinate functions $A\mapsto a_{ij}$ are continuous (for any norm on $M_n(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$), and sums and products of continuous functions are continuous. Hence $\det:M_n(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}$ is continuous.

**Step 2: $GL_n$ is open.** A matrix is invertible iff $\det A\neq0$, so
$$GL_n(\mathbb{R})=\det{}^{-1}\big(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\big).$$
The set $\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$ is open in $\mathbb{R}$, so its preimage under the continuous map $\det$ is open.

**Step 3: $SL_n$ is closed.** By definition
$$SL_n(\mathbb{R})=\det{}^{-1}(\{1\}),$$
and $\{1\}$ is closed in $\mathbb{R}$, so the preimage is closed.

**Remark.**
- Concretely, the openness of $GL_n$ says: if $A$ is invertible and $B$ is close enough to $A$, then $B$ is invertible. A quantitative version is in the "Inverse stability" problem of Part 1: it suffices that $\|B-A\|<1/\|A^{-1}\|$.
- $SL_n$ is closed but not open (it has empty interior, since $\det(tA)=t^n$ leaves the value $1$ as soon as $t>0$, $t\ne1$, and $tA\to A$ as $t\to1$).
- $SL_n$ is also unbounded for $n\ge2$: $\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\0&1/t\end{pmatrix}\in SL_2$ for all $t>0$. Closed does not mean compact.

@@@ mn-036-subspaces.md
---
title: Proper subspaces are closed with empty interior
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: subspaces, interior, closed sets, finite dimension
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that every proper linear subspace $V\subsetneq M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is closed and has empty interior.

## Hint

Closedness: a finite-dimensional subspace of a normed space is complete. Empty interior: if $V$ contained a ball, use $\pm$ small multiples of any vector $X$ to show $X\in V$.

## Solution

**Idea.** A subspace that contains even a tiny ball contains every direction, hence is everything. And finite-dimensional subspaces cannot have "holes" in their closure.

**Step 1: $V$ is closed.** $V$ is a finite-dimensional normed space, hence complete (it is isometric to some $\mathbb{R}^k$ after choosing an orthonormal basis for the Frobenius inner product). A complete subset of a metric space is closed: if $A_k\in V$ and $A_k\to A$, the sequence is Cauchy in $V$, so it converges to some $A'\in V$, and by uniqueness of limits $A=A'\in V$.

(Another argument: $V$ is the common kernel of finitely many linear functionals $\varphi_1,\dots,\varphi_m$, which are continuous, so $V=\bigcap\varphi_i^{-1}(\{0\})$ is closed.)

**Step 2: empty interior.** Suppose $V$ contained an open ball $B(A,r)$ with $A\in V$ (a point of the interior may be taken to be the centre of a ball inside $V$; since the ball is inside $V$, its centre is in $V$). Take any $X\neq0$ in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and put
$$t=\frac r{2\|X\|}.$$
Then $\|(A\pm tX)-A\|=r/2<r$, so $A+tX$ and $A-tX$ both lie in $B(A,r)\subset V$. Since $V$ is a subspace,
$$tX=\tfrac12\big((A+tX)-(A-tX)\big)\in V,\qquad\text{so}\qquad X=\tfrac1t(tX)\in V.$$
As $X$ was arbitrary, $V=M_n(\mathbb{R})$, contradicting properness.

**Remark.**
- The same proof works in any normed space: a proper subspace has empty interior. Closedness, however, genuinely needs finite dimension (the polynomials form a dense proper subspace of $C[0,1]$).
- This statement is used constantly: for instance the symmetric matrices, the trace-zero matrices and the commutant of a non-scalar matrix are all closed nowhere dense sets.

@@@ mn-037-examples.md
---
title: Closed or open? Three classical subspaces
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: subspaces, symmetric matrices, trace, triangular matrices
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge2$. Which of these sets are closed, open, or neither in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$: the symmetric matrices, the trace-zero matrices, the upper triangular matrices?

## Hint

Each is a linear subspace. Is it a proper subspace? (Give a matrix outside each one.) Then apply the previous problem.

## Solution

**Idea.** Show each set is a *proper* linear subspace; then the previous problem says it is closed with empty interior, in particular not open.

**Step 1: they are subspaces.**
- $\mathrm{Sym}_n=\{A:A^T=A\}$ is the kernel of the linear map $A\mapsto A^T-A$.
- $\{\operatorname{tr}A=0\}$ is the kernel of the linear form $\operatorname{tr}$.
- Upper triangular matrices are those with $a_{ij}=0$ for $i>j$: the common kernel of the coordinate functionals $A\mapsto a_{ij}$ with $i>j$.

**Step 2: they are proper (here $n\ge2$ is used).**
- $E_{12}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}\oplus0$ is not symmetric (its transpose is $E_{21}\ne E_{12}$).
- $I_n$ has trace $n\neq0$.
- $E_{21}$ is not upper triangular.

**Step 3: conclusion.** By the previous problem each of the three sets is **closed**, has **empty interior**, and is therefore **not open** (a non-empty open set has non-empty interior; each set contains $0$, so it is non-empty).

**Remark.**
- For $n=1$ the situation is different: $M_1(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}$, symmetric and upper triangular matrices are *everything* (hence open and closed), while $\{\operatorname{tr}=0\}=\{0\}$ is closed and not open.
- Dimensions: $\dim\mathrm{Sym}_n=\frac{n(n+1)}2$, $\dim\{\operatorname{tr}=0\}=n^2-1$, $\dim\{\text{upper triangular}\}=\frac{n(n+1)}2$, all $<n^2$.

@@@ mn-038-integer-matrices.md
---
title: Integer matrices are closed and discrete, rational ones dense
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: integer matrices, discrete sets, density, rationals
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $M_n(\mathbb{Z})$ is closed and discrete in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$, while $M_n(\mathbb{Q})$ is dense in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Use the max-entry norm. For two distinct integer matrices, look at an entry where they differ. For density, approximate each entry by a rational number.

## Solution

**Idea.** The topology of $M_n(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$ is the product topology, so everything can be checked entry by entry, where it reduces to the facts that $\mathbb{Z}$ is discrete in $\mathbb{R}$ and $\mathbb{Q}$ is dense.

**Step 1: discreteness.** Use the norm $\|A\|_{\max}=\max_{i,j}|a_{ij}|$ (all norms are equivalent). If $A\neq B$ in $M_n(\mathbb{Z})$, some entries satisfy $a_{ij}\neq b_{ij}$ and both are integers, so $|a_{ij}-b_{ij}|\ge1$. Hence
$$\|A-B\|_{\max}\ge1.$$
So the open ball $B(A,1)$ contains no other integer matrix: every point of $M_n(\mathbb{Z})$ is isolated in $M_n(\mathbb{Z})$.

**Step 2: closedness.** Let $A_k\in M_n(\mathbb{Z})$ converge to $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$. Then $(A_k)$ is Cauchy, so for $k,l$ large $\|A_k-A_l\|_{\max}<1$, which by Step 1 forces $A_k=A_l$. The sequence is eventually constant, so its limit $A$ equals that constant and is an integer matrix. Hence $M_n(\mathbb{Z})$ contains all its limit points.

**Step 3: density of $M_n(\mathbb{Q})$.** Let $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and $\varepsilon>0$. For each entry $a_{ij}$ choose $q_{ij}\in\mathbb{Q}$ with $|a_{ij}-q_{ij}|<\varepsilon$ (density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$). Then $Q=(q_{ij})\in M_n(\mathbb{Q})$ and $\|A-Q\|_{\max}<\varepsilon$.

**Remark.**
- A closed discrete set has no accumulation points: any bounded part of it is finite. In particular $M_n(\mathbb{Z})\cap\{\|A\|\le R\}$ is finite for each $R$.
- $M_n(\mathbb{Q})$ is dense and countable, so $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is separable. It also has empty interior (it is countable, and a non-empty open set of $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$ is uncountable).

@@@ mn-039-balls.md
---
title: Open ball, closed ball and sphere in the operator norm
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: operator norm, ball, closure, boundary
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

For the operator norm, show that $\{\|A\|<1\}$ is open, that its closure is $\{\|A\|\le1\}$, and that its boundary is $\{\|A\|=1\}$.

## Hint

Continuity of $\|\cdot\|$ gives openness and closedness. For the closure, push a point of the closed ball slightly towards $0$ by scaling.

## Solution

**Idea.** In any normed space the open unit ball is the interior of the closed unit ball, and the closed ball is the closure of the open one. The proof is the "radial scaling" trick.

**Step 1: openness and closedness.** The norm is $1$-Lipschitz: $|\,\|A\|-\|B\|\,|\le\|A-B\|$, hence continuous. So $U=\{\|A\|<1\}=\|\cdot\|^{-1}((-\infty,1))$ is open and $F=\{\|A\|\le1\}=\|\cdot\|^{-1}((-\infty,1])$ is closed.

**Step 2: $\overline U=F$.**
- Since $U\subset F$ and $F$ is closed, $\overline U\subset F$.
- Conversely let $\|A\|\le1$. For $t\in(0,1)$, $\|tA\|=t\|A\|<1$, so $tA\in U$, and
$$\|tA-A\|=(1-t)\|A\|\le1-t\longrightarrow0\quad(t\to1^-).$$
Hence $A\in\overline U$.

**Step 3: the boundary.** For an open set $U$ the boundary is $\partial U=\overline U\setminus U$ (because $U$ equals its interior). Thus
$$\partial U=F\setminus U=\{\|A\|=1\}.$$

**Remark.**
- Nothing here uses that the norm is the operator norm: the argument works for *any* norm on *any* real normed space (with $U\ne\emptyset$).
