- In the metric space $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ one should not confuse "closed ball is closure of the open ball" with the general metric-space statement, which is false for discrete metrics. Here it holds because of the linear structure.

@@@ mn-040-cones.md
---
title: Positive definite matrices: open cone with closure PSD
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: positive definite, cones, eigenvalues, interior
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

In the space $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ of real symmetric matrices, show that the set PD of positive definite matrices is open, the set PSD of positive semidefinite matrices is closed, $\operatorname{int}(\mathrm{PSD})=\mathrm{PD}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{PD}}=\mathrm{PSD}$ (interior and closure taken in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$).

## Hint

$A\in\mathrm{PD}$ iff $\lambda_{\min}(A)>0$ iff $x^TAx>0$ for all unit $x$. Show that $\lambda_{\min}(A)=\min_{|x|=1}x^TAx$ is a continuous function of $A$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Everything is governed by the continuous function $\lambda_{\min}$. PD is $\{\lambda_{\min}>0\}$ and PSD is $\{\lambda_{\min}\ge0\}$; and shifting by $\pm\varepsilon I$ moves $\lambda_{\min}$ by exactly $\pm\varepsilon$.

**Step 1: $\lambda_{\min}$ is continuous.** For symmetric $A$ the Rayleigh formula gives $\lambda_{\min}(A)=\min_{|x|=1}x^TAx$. If $A,B$ are symmetric and $|x|=1$,
$$x^TAx=x^TBx+x^T(A-B)x\ge\lambda_{\min}(B)-\|A-B\|,$$
so $\lambda_{\min}(A)\ge\lambda_{\min}(B)-\|A-B\|$; exchanging $A,B$ gives
$$|\lambda_{\min}(A)-\lambda_{\min}(B)|\le\|A-B\|.$$
So $\lambda_{\min}$ is $1$-Lipschitz.

**Step 2: open / closed.** $\mathrm{PD}=\lambda_{\min}^{-1}((0,\infty))$ is open and $\mathrm{PSD}=\lambda_{\min}^{-1}([0,\infty))$ is closed in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$.

**Step 3: $\operatorname{int}\mathrm{PSD}=\mathrm{PD}$.** PD is open and contained in PSD, so $\mathrm{PD}\subset\operatorname{int}\mathrm{PSD}$. Conversely let $A\in\mathrm{PSD}$ with $\lambda_{\min}(A)=0$. For every $\varepsilon>0$ the matrix $A-\varepsilon I\in\mathrm{Sym}_n$ has $\lambda_{\min}=-\varepsilon<0$, so it is not PSD, while $\|(A-\varepsilon I)-A\|=\varepsilon$. So no neighbourhood of $A$ lies in PSD: $A\notin\operatorname{int}\mathrm{PSD}$.

**Step 4: $\overline{\mathrm{PD}}=\mathrm{PSD}$.** PSD is closed and contains PD, so $\overline{\mathrm{PD}}\subset\mathrm{PSD}$. If $A\in\mathrm{PSD}$, then $A+\varepsilon I$ has $\lambda_{\min}=\lambda_{\min}(A)+\varepsilon>0$ for $\varepsilon>0$, so it is in PD, and it tends to $A$ as $\varepsilon\to0$.

**Remark.**
- Example ($n=2$): $\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ is PSD but not PD; $\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&-\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}$ is arbitrarily close and not PSD.
- PD is a convex cone, and the identities $\overline{\mathrm{PD}}=\mathrm{PSD}$, $\operatorname{int}\mathrm{PSD}=\mathrm{PD}$ are the standard picture behind semidefinite programming.

@@@ mn-041-cones.md
---
title: PSD matrices have empty interior in all of M_n
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: positive semidefinite, symmetric matrices, interior
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge2$. Show that the set PSD of symmetric positive semidefinite matrices has empty interior in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ (not just in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$).

## Hint

Perturb a PSD matrix by a small *skew-symmetric* matrix, or note that PSD is contained in a proper subspace.

## Solution

**Idea.** PSD matrices are symmetric by definition, and $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ is a proper subspace of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ when $n\ge2$. A subset of a set with empty interior has empty interior.

**Step 1: PSD $\subset\mathrm{Sym}_n$ and $\mathrm{Sym}_n\ne M_n$.** For $n\ge2$ the matrix $E_{12}$ is not symmetric, so $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ is a *proper* linear subspace.

**Step 2: $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ has empty interior.** This is the earlier result on proper subspaces. Explicitly (the skew-symmetric perturbation of the hint): let $A\in\mathrm{Sym}_n$ and let $K=E_{12}-E_{21}$ (skew-symmetric, $\|K\|=1$). Then $A+\varepsilon K\notin\mathrm{Sym}_n$ for every $\varepsilon\neq0$ (its transpose is $A-\varepsilon K$), and $\|A+\varepsilon K-A\|=\varepsilon$. So every ball around $A$ contains a non-symmetric matrix.

**Step 3: conclusion.** Any ball around a PSD matrix contains a non-symmetric matrix, hence a matrix outside PSD. So $\operatorname{int}_{M_n}(\mathrm{PSD})=\emptyset$.

**Remark.**
- For $n=1$ the claim is false: $M_1=\mathbb{R}$ and $\mathrm{PSD}=[0,\infty)$ has interior $(0,\infty)$.
- Be careful with definitions. If "positive semidefinite" is defined for *non-symmetric* real matrices by the condition $x^TAx\ge0$ for all $x$, the set is $\mathrm{PSD}+\mathrm{Skew}_n$ and does have non-empty interior (e.g. $I+K$ for small skew $K$). In this book PSD means *symmetric* with $x^TAx\ge0$.
- This is why one always says "interior in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$": the ambient space matters for interior and boundary.

@@@ mn-042-algebraic-sets.md
---
title: Nilpotent matrices and idempotents are closed
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: nilpotent, idempotent, algebraic sets, nowhere dense
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the set of nilpotent matrices is closed with empty interior, and that the set of idempotent matrices ($P^2=P$) is closed.

## Hint

Each set is the zero set of polynomial equations in the entries. For the empty interior of the nilpotent set, add a small multiple of the identity.

## Solution

**Idea.** Sets defined by polynomial equations are closed (preimages of $\{0\}$ under polynomial maps). Emptiness of the interior needs an actual perturbation argument.

**Step 1: nilpotents are closed.** $A$ is nilpotent iff $A^n=0$ (a nilpotent $n\times n$ matrix satisfies $A^n=0$ by Cayleyâ€“Hamilton, since its characteristic polynomial is $t^n$). The map $A\mapsto A^n$ is polynomial in the entries (hence continuous), so
$$\mathcal N=\{A:A^n=0\}=(A\mapsto A^n)^{-1}(\{0\})$$
is closed.

**Step 2: nilpotents have empty interior.** Let $N\in\mathcal N$ and $\varepsilon>0$. The eigenvalues of $N$ are all $0$, so those of $N+\varepsilon I$ are all $\varepsilon\neq0$: the matrix $N+\varepsilon I$ is invertible. A nilpotent matrix is singular, so $N+\varepsilon I\notin\mathcal N$, though $\|N+\varepsilon I-N\|=\varepsilon$. Hence no ball around $N$ lies in $\mathcal N$.

**Step 3: idempotents are closed.** $\mathcal P=\{P:P^2-P=0\}$ is the preimage of $\{0\}$ under the continuous map $P\mapsto P^2-P$.

**Example.** For $N=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$: $N^2=0$, while $(N+\varepsilon I)^2=\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon^2&2\varepsilon\\0&\varepsilon^2\end{pmatrix}\ne0$.

**Remark.**
- Idempotents also have empty interior: if $P^2=P$ then all eigenvalues of $P$ lie in $\{0,1\}$, whereas $P+\varepsilon I$ has eigenvalues in $\{\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon\}$, so $P+\varepsilon I$ is not idempotent for $\varepsilon\ne0$ small, though it is at distance $\varepsilon$ from $P$. They are also *unbounded* for $n\ge2$: $\begin{pmatrix}1&t\\0&0\end{pmatrix}^2=\begin{pmatrix}1&t\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ for all $t$ (oblique projections).
- The nilpotent cone is the fibre $\chi^{-1}(t^n)$ of the characteristic polynomial map (see Part 3).

@@@ mn-043-density.md
---
title: GL_n is dense and its boundary is the singular set
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: invertible matrices, density, boundary, determinant
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is dense in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$, and that its boundary is the set of singular matrices.

## Hint

For a fixed $A$, $\det(A+tI)$ is a polynomial in $t$; it has finitely many roots.

## Solution

**Idea.** A matrix can be made invertible by adding a tiny multiple of the identity: this only fails for finitely many values of the multiple.

**Step 1: density.** Fix $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$. The function $p(t)=\det(tI+A)$ is a *monic* polynomial of degree $n$ in $t$ (it equals $(-1)^n\chi_A(-t)$, where $\chi_A$ is the characteristic polynomial). A non-zero polynomial has finitely many roots, so there is $\delta>0$ such that $p(t)\neq0$ for $0<|t|<\delta$. For such $t$, $A+tI\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ and $\|A+tI-A\|=|t|\to0$. Hence $A\in\overline{GL_n(\mathbb{R})}$.

(Equivalently: $A+tI$ is singular iff $-t$ is an eigenvalue of $A$, and $A$ has at most $n$ eigenvalues.)

**Step 2: the boundary.** For any set $E$, $\partial E=\overline E\setminus\operatorname{int}E$. Here $E=GL_n$ is open (previous problem), so $\operatorname{int}E=E$, and by Step 1 $\overline E=M_n(\mathbb{R})$. Therefore
$$\partial GL_n(\mathbb{R})=M_n(\mathbb{R})\setminus GL_n(\mathbb{R})=\{A:\det A=0\}.$$

**Remark.**
- Equivalently, the singular matrices form a closed set with empty interior (they are the zero set of a non-zero polynomial). This is the key reason "generic matrices are invertible".
- A typical use: to prove an identity between continuous functions of $A$ (such as $\det(AB)=\det(A)\det(B)$ or Cayleyâ€“Hamilton) it suffices to prove it on the dense set $GL_n$, or even on the dense set of matrices with distinct eigenvalues.

@@@ mn-044-entries.md
---
title: Matrices with no zero entry: open and dense
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: nowhere dense, hyperplanes, open dense sets
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the set of matrices all of whose entries are non-zero is open and dense in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$, and that the set of matrices with at least one zero entry is closed with empty interior.

## Hint

The second set is a finite union of coordinate hyperplanes $\{a_{ij}=0\}$. Each is closed with empty interior; a finite union of such sets is still nowhere dense.

## Solution

**Idea.** The two sets in the statement are complements of each other. It is enough to show that $Z=\{A:\exists(i,j),\,a_{ij}=0\}$ is closed with empty interior; then its complement is open and dense.

**Step 1: $Z$ is closed.** $Z=\bigcup_{i,j}H_{ij}$ with $H_{ij}=\{a_{ij}=0\}$, a finite union of closed sets (each is the kernel of a continuous linear functional).

**Step 2: each $H_{ij}$ has empty interior.** $H_{ij}$ is a proper linear subspace, so it has empty interior (earlier problem on subspaces). Concretely, $A+\varepsilon E_{ij}\notin H_{ij}$ if $a_{ij}=0$ and $\varepsilon\neq0$.

**Step 3: $Z$ has empty interior.** We prove: *the union of two closed sets with empty interior has empty interior* (then induct on the number of sets). Let $F,G$ be closed with empty interior and suppose an open ball $B\subset F\cup G$. Since $F$ has empty interior, $B\not\subset F$, so $B\setminus F$ is a non-empty open set contained in $G$, so $G$ has non-empty interior: contradiction.

**Step 4: conclusion.** $Z$ is closed with empty interior, so $M_n(\mathbb{R})\setminus Z$ is open, and it is dense (a set is dense iff its complement has empty interior).

**Remark.**
- This is a model for "generic" statements: a property that holds outside a finite union of proper algebraic subsets (zero sets of non-zero polynomials) holds on an open dense set. Examples: invertible, distinct eigenvalues, all entries non-zero.
- For *countable* unions the argument must be replaced by the Baire theorem, and one only gets a dense $G_\delta$ complement, not an open one (see the Baire problem below).

@@@ mn-045-cones.md
---
title: Entrywise nonnegative matrices: a closed cone with interior
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: cones, convexity, interior, nonnegative matrices
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the set $N$ of entrywise nonnegative matrices is a closed convex cone, and that its interior is the set of entrywise positive matrices.

## Hint

$N=\bigcap_{i,j}\{a_{ij}\ge0\}$. For the interior, an entry equal to $0$ can be decreased slightly.

## Solution

**Idea.** The conditions are coordinatewise, so everything reduces to the half-line $[0,\infty)\subset\mathbb{R}$.

**Step 1: closed, convex cone.** Each $\{a_{ij}\ge0\}$ is a closed half-space (preimage of $[0,\infty)$ under a continuous linear functional); an intersection of closed convex cones is a closed convex cone. In more detail: if $A,B\in N$ and $s,t\ge0$ then $sA+tB$ has entries $sa_{ij}+tb_{ij}\ge0$.

**Step 2: positive matrices are interior.** Let $A$ have $a_{ij}>0$ for all $i,j$ and put $m=\min_{i,j}a_{ij}>0$. If $\|B-A\|_F<m$ then for every entry $|b_{ij}-a_{ij}|\le\|B-A\|_F<m\le a_{ij}$, so $b_{ij}>0$. Hence $B(A,m)\subset N$ and $A\in\operatorname{int}N$.

**Step 3: matrices with a zero entry are not interior.** Let $A\in N$ with $a_{ij}=0$ for some $(i,j)$. For $\varepsilon>0$, $A-\varepsilon E_{ij}$ has entry $-\varepsilon<0$ at $(i,j)$, so it lies outside $N$, while $\|A-\varepsilon E_{ij}-A\|=\varepsilon$. So $A\notin\operatorname{int}N$.

**Conclusion.** $\operatorname{int}N=\{A:a_{ij}>0\ \forall i,j\}$.

**Remark.**
- This is the same picture as for PSD matrices: closed cone, interior = "strict" version, closure of the interior = the cone (here every nonnegative $A$ is the limit of $A+\varepsilon J$, with $J$ the all-ones matrix).
- Positive matrices are exactly the setting of the Perronâ€“Frobenius theorem.

@@@ mn-046-closure-of.md
---
title: The closure of {det > 0} is {det â‰¥ 0}
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: determinant, singular value decomposition, closure, boundary
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $U=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\det A>0\}$. Show that $\overline U=\{\det\ge0\}$ and $\partial U=\{\det=0\}$.

## Hint

Take a singular matrix $A$, write it with a singular value decomposition $A=P\Sigma Q^T$, and replace the zero singular values by small non-zero numbers. If the sign of the determinant is wrong, put a minus sign on one of them.

## Solution

**Idea.** A small perturbation of a singular matrix is invertible, but its determinant could be negative; we must control the *sign*. The SVD makes the sign explicit.

**Step 1: $U$ is open and $\overline U\subset\{\det\ge0\}$.** $\det$ is continuous, so $U=\det^{-1}((0,\infty))$ is open and $\{\det\ge0\}=\det^{-1}([0,\infty))$ is closed. Since $U\subset\{\det\ge0\}$, taking closures gives $\overline U\subset\{\det\ge0\}$.

**Step 2: every matrix with $\det A>0$ is in $U$; remains $\det A=0$.** Let $\det A=0$. Write the SVD $A=P\Sigma Q^T$ with $P,Q$ orthogonal and $\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_n)$, $\sigma_i\ge0$. Since $A$ is singular, the set $Z=\{i:\sigma_i=0\}$ is non-empty. Let $\delta=\det P\cdot\det Q\in\{\pm1\}$ and let $i_0\in Z$.

For $\varepsilon>0$ define $\Sigma_\varepsilon=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma^{(\varepsilon)}_i)$ by
$$\sigma^{(\varepsilon)}_i=\begin{cases}\sigma_i&i\notin Z,\\ \varepsilon&i\in Z,\ i\ne i_0,\\ \delta\,\varepsilon&i=i_0,\end{cases}$$
and $A_\varepsilon=P\Sigma_\varepsilon Q^T$.

**Step 3: the sign.** $\det A_\varepsilon=\det P\,\det Q\,\det\Sigma_\varepsilon=\delta\cdot\delta\,\varepsilon^{|Z|}\prod_{i\notin Z}\sigma_i=\varepsilon^{|Z|}\prod_{i\notin Z}\sigma_i>0$ because $\delta^2=1$. So $A_\varepsilon\in U$.

**Step 4: closeness.** $A_\varepsilon-A=P(\Sigma_\varepsilon-\Sigma)Q^T$ and the operator norm is invariant under orthogonal multiplication, so $\|A_\varepsilon-A\|=\|\Sigma_\varepsilon-\Sigma\|=\varepsilon\to0$. Therefore $A\in\overline U$.

**Step 5: the boundary.** $U$ is open so $\partial U=\overline U\setminus U=\{\det\ge0\}\setminus\{\det>0\}=\{\det=0\}$.

**Numeric check.** For $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ one may take $P=I$, $Q=I$ ($\delta=1$) and $A_\varepsilon=\operatorname{diag}(1,\varepsilon)$ with $\det=\varepsilon>0$. For $A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$, an SVD has $\delta=-1$, and the sign flip is indeed needed.

**Remark.**
- Symmetrically $\overline{\{\det<0\}}=\{\det\le0\}$, so $\{\det>0\}$ and $\{\det<0\}$ share the same boundary $\{\det=0\}$. Thus $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is cut by the singular matrices into two open pieces, the first hint at its two connected components (Part 5).
- The naive perturbation $A+\varepsilon I$ may have negative determinant for both signs of $\varepsilon$: for $A=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,0,0)$ one gets $\det(A+\varepsilon I)=\varepsilon^2(1+\varepsilon)(\varepsilon-1)<0$ for all small $\varepsilon\ne0$. The SVD argument avoids this issue altogether.

@@@ mn-047-rank.md
---
title: Lower-rank matrices form a closed set, higher-rank an open one
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: rank, minors, semicontinuity, nowhere dense
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

For an integer $r$ with $0\le r<n$, show that $\{\operatorname{rank}\le r\}$ is closed with empty interior, and that $\{\operatorname{rank}\ge r\}$ is open (this holds for all $r\ge0$).

## Hint

$\operatorname{rank}A\ge r$ iff some $r\times r$ minor of $A$ is non-zero.

## Solution

**Idea.** Rank is characterised by the vanishing of minors, which are polynomials in the entries.

**Step 1: minors and rank.** $\operatorname{rank}A$ is the largest $k$ such that some $k\times k$ minor (determinant of a $k\times k$ submatrix) is non-zero. Hence
$$\operatorname{rank}A\le r\iff\text{all }(r+1)\times(r+1)\text{ minors of }A\text{ vanish.}$$

**Step 2: $\{\operatorname{rank}\le r\}$ is closed.** It is a finite intersection of zero sets of the continuous functions "minor", hence closed.

**Step 3: $\{\operatorname{rank}\ge r\}$ is open.** If $\operatorname{rank}A\ge r$, some $r\times r$ minor $m(A)\ne0$ (for $r=0$ the set is everything). Since $m$ is continuous, $m(B)\neq0$ for all $B$ in a neighbourhood of $A$, so $\operatorname{rank}B\ge r$ there. (This is the *lower semicontinuity* of rank: it can jump up, never down, under small perturbation.)

(Note the complement of $\{\operatorname{rank}\ge r\}$ is $\{\operatorname{rank}\le r-1\}$, consistent with Step 2 for $r-1$.)

**Step 4: empty interior when $r<n$.** Let $\operatorname{rank}A\le r<n$. Then $A$ is singular and by the density of $GL_n$ (earlier problem) $A+\varepsilon I\in GL_n$ for small $\varepsilon\ne0$, so $\operatorname{rank}(A+\varepsilon I)=n>r$. Thus every neighbourhood of $A$ contains matrices not in $\{\operatorname{rank}\le r\}$.

**Remark.**
- For $r\ge n$ the set $\{\operatorname{rank}\le r\}$ is all of $M_n$, which has non-empty interior: the restriction $r<n$ is needed.
- In words: *rank can only go up in a neighbourhood*. The set of rank-exactly-$r$ matrices is neither open nor closed (next problem).

@@@ mn-048-rank.md
---
title: Matrices of rank exactly r: neither open nor closed
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: rank, closure, SVD, semicontinuity
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

For $0<r<n$ show that $\{\operatorname{rank}=r\}$ is neither open nor closed, and that its closure is $\{\operatorname{rank}\le r\}$.

## Hint

Perturb up (to rank $r+1$) to see non-openness, and let a rank-$r$ sequence shrink to $0$ for non-closedness. For the closure, use the SVD and replace zero singular values by $\varepsilon$.

## Solution

**Idea.** By the previous problem $\{\operatorname{rank}\le r\}$ is closed and $\{\operatorname{rank}\ge r+1\}$ is open. The set $\{\operatorname{rank}=r\}$ is their "difference" and sits in between.

Let $D_r=\operatorname{diag}(1,\dots,1,0,\dots,0)$ with $r$ ones; it has rank $r$.

**Step 1: not open.** $D_r+\varepsilon E_{nn}$ has diagonal $(1,\dots,1,0,\dots,0,\varepsilon)$ with $r+1$ non-zero entries (this uses $r<n$ so that position $n$ is a zero of $D_r$), so rank $r+1$. It tends to $D_r$ as $\varepsilon\to0$. So $D_r$ has no neighbourhood inside $\{\operatorname{rank}=r\}$.

**Step 2: not closed.** $\frac1kD_r$ has rank $r$ for every $k\ge1$ and converges to $0$, which has rank $0\neq r$ (here $r>0$ is used).

**Step 3: the closure is $\{\operatorname{rank}\le r\}$.**
- ($\subset$) $\{\operatorname{rank}=r\}\subset\{\operatorname{rank}\le r\}$ and the latter is closed.
- ($\supset$) Let $\operatorname{rank}A=s\le r$. Write the SVD $A=P\Sigma Q^T$ with singular values $\sigma_1\ge\dots\ge\sigma_s>0=\sigma_{s+1}=\dots=\sigma_n$. Define $A_\varepsilon=P\Sigma_\varepsilon Q^T$ where $\Sigma_\varepsilon$ replaces $\sigma_{s+1},\dots,\sigma_r$ by $\varepsilon>0$ (and keeps the rest). Then $\operatorname{rank}A_\varepsilon=r$ and $\|A_\varepsilon-A\|=\varepsilon\to0$.

**Remark.**
- Matrices of rank exactly $r$ form a *manifold* of dimension $r(2n-r)$, but not a closed one: its boundary consists of the lower-rank matrices.
- For $r=n$ the set $\{\operatorname{rank}=n\}=GL_n$ is open; for $r=0$ it is $\{0\}$, which is closed. Both are excluded by $0<r<n$.

@@@ mn-049-real-eigenvalues.md
---
title: Matrices with a real eigenvalue: closed set, interior for n = 2
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: real eigenvalues, closed sets, discriminant, 2x2 matrices
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $\mathcal R$ be the set of real $n\times n$ matrices having at least one real eigenvalue. Show that $\mathcal R$ is closed. For $n=2$, find its interior.

## Hint

A real eigenvalue is bounded by the norm, so a sequence of eigenvalues has a convergent subsequence. For $n=2$, $A\in\mathcal R$ iff $(\operatorname{tr}A)^2-4\det A\ge0$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Closedness is a compactness argument (eigenvalues cannot escape to infinity). For $n=2$ the condition becomes a single inequality on $(\operatorname{tr},\det)$.

**Step 1: $\mathcal R$ is closed.** Let $A_k\to A$ with $A_k\in\mathcal R$ and real eigenvalues $\lambda_k$. If $A_kv=\lambda_kv$ with $|v|=1$, then $|\lambda_k|\le\|A_k\|$. As $(A_k)$ converges, $\|A_k\|$ is bounded, so $(\lambda_k)$ is bounded and has a convergent subsequence $\lambda_{k_j}\to\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$. By continuity of $\det$,
$$\det(A-\lambda I)=\lim_j\det(A_{k_j}-\lambda_{k_j}I)=0,$$
so $\lambda$ is a real eigenvalue of $A$ and $A\in\mathcal R$.

**Step 2: the case $n=2$.** The characteristic polynomial is $t^2-(\operatorname{tr}A)t+\det A$, with discriminant $\Delta(A)=(\operatorname{tr}A)^2-4\det A$. It has a real root iff $\Delta\ge0$:
$$\mathcal R=\{\Delta\ge0\}.$$

**Step 3: interior.** We claim $\operatorname{int}\mathcal R=\{\Delta>0\}$.
- $\{\Delta>0\}$ is open (as $\Delta$ is continuous) and contained in $\mathcal R$.
- Let $\Delta(A)=0$ with $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$ and let $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$. Then $A+\varepsilon J=\begin{pmatrix}a&b-\varepsilon\\c+\varepsilon&d\end{pmatrix}$ has the same trace and $\det(A+\varepsilon J)=\det A-\varepsilon(b-c)+\varepsilon^2$, so
$$\Delta(A+\varepsilon J)=\Delta(A)+4\varepsilon(b-c)-4\varepsilon^2=4\varepsilon(b-c)-4\varepsilon^2.$$
If $b=c$ this is $-4\varepsilon^2<0$ for any $\varepsilon\neq0$; if $b\neq c$ choose the sign of $\varepsilon$ so that $\varepsilon(b-c)<0$. In both cases $\Delta(A+\varepsilon J)<0$ for small $\varepsilon\ne0$, so $A+\varepsilon J\notin\mathcal R$ and $A\notin\operatorname{int}\mathcal R$.

**Check.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ ($\Delta=0$). For $\varepsilon=-0.01$ the eigenvalues of $A+\varepsilon J$ are $1\pm0.1005\,i$ (non-real), while for $\varepsilon=+0.01$ they are $1\pm0.0995$ (real). So $A$ is a boundary point.

**Remark.**
- For odd $n$ the characteristic polynomial has odd degree, so every real matrix has a real eigenvalue and $\mathcal R=M_n(\mathbb{R})$ trivially.
- For $n=2$ the boundary of $\mathcal R$ is $\{\Delta=0\}$: matrices with a double real eigenvalue. These are the places where a pair of real eigenvalues collides and leaves the real line.

@@@ mn-050-diagonalisable.md
---
title: Real-diagonalisable matrices: neither open nor closed
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: diagonalisable, Jordan blocks, counterexamples
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge2$. Show that the set of $\mathbb{R}$-diagonalisable matrices is neither open nor closed in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Perturb the identity into a Jordan block (not open); approximate a Jordan block by matrices with distinct eigenvalues (not closed). Treat $n=2$, then add an identity block.

## Solution

**Idea.** Diagonalisable = no non-trivial Jordan block; a Jordan block can be created by an arbitrarily small perturbation (not open) and is a limit of diagonalisable matrices with distinct eigenvalues (not closed).

**Step 1: not open ($n=2$).** $I_2$ is diagonalisable. The matrices $B_\varepsilon=\begin{pmatrix}1&\varepsilon\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ ($\varepsilon\ne0$) satisfy $B_\varepsilon-I=\varepsilon E_{12}\ne0$ but $(B_\varepsilon-I)^2=0$; the minimal polynomial is $(t-1)^2$, which has a repeated root, so $B_\varepsilon$ is not diagonalisable. Yet $B_\varepsilon\to I$.

**Step 2: not closed ($n=2$).** For $\varepsilon>0$, $C_\varepsilon=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1+\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}$ has two distinct real eigenvalues $1,1+\varepsilon$ and is therefore diagonalisable. As $\varepsilon\to0$, $C_\varepsilon\to C_0=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$, which is a non-trivial Jordan block (minimal polynomial $(t-1)^2$) and is *not* diagonalisable.

**Step 3: all $n\ge2$.** Put $B_\varepsilon\oplus I_{n-2}$, $C_\varepsilon\oplus I_{n-2}$ (block diagonal). A block-diagonal matrix is diagonalisable iff each block is, so the same sequences show non-openness and non-closedness. (If one prefers eigenvalues to stay distinct in Step 2, use $C_\varepsilon\oplus\operatorname{diag}(2,3,\dots,n-1)$; this is not needed.)

**Remark.**
- In Part 7 we shall see the correct statements: the *interior* is the set of matrices with $n$ distinct real eigenvalues and the *closure* is the set of matrices whose characteristic polynomial splits over $\mathbb{R}$ (two problems below).
- Over $\mathbb{C}$ the situation is better: the set of matrices with distinct eigenvalues is open and dense, so diagonalisable matrices are dense (but still not open).

@@@ mn-051-distinct-eigenvalues.md
---
title: Matrices with n distinct eigenvalues form an open set
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: discriminant, distinct eigenvalues, open sets, characteristic polynomial
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the set of real matrices having $n$ distinct (complex) eigenvalues is open in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

A polynomial has distinct roots iff its discriminant is non-zero. The discriminant of $\chi_A$ is a polynomial in the entries of $A$.

## Solution

**Idea.** "Distinct eigenvalues" is a non-vanishing condition for a polynomial expression in the entries, hence open.

**Step 1: the discriminant.** For a monic polynomial $p(t)=t^n+c_{n-1}t^{n-1}+\dots+c_0$ the discriminant $\operatorname{disc}(p)=\pm\operatorname{Res}(p,p')$ (resultant, the determinant of the Sylvester matrix) is a polynomial in the coefficients $c_0,\dots,c_{n-1}$, and $\operatorname{disc}(p)\neq0$ iff $p$ has $n$ distinct roots in $\mathbb{C}$ (equivalently $\gcd(p,p')=1$).

**Step 2: it is polynomial in the entries.** The coefficients of $\chi_A(t)=\det(tI-A)$ are polynomials in the entries $a_{ij}$ (sums of principal minors, up to sign). Hence $\Delta(A)=\operatorname{disc}(\chi_A)$ is a polynomial in the entries, thus continuous.

**Step 3: conclusion.** The set in question is
$$\{A:\Delta(A)\neq0\}=\Delta^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}),$$
open as the preimage of an open set under a continuous map.

**Example ($n=2$).** $\Delta(A)=(\operatorname{tr}A)^2-4\det A$, and eigenvalues are distinct iff $\Delta\neq0$.

**Remark.**
- The complementary set $\{\Delta=0\}$ (a repeated eigenvalue) is closed with empty interior (see the problem on repeated eigenvalues below), so *generic matrices have distinct eigenvalues* and are therefore diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$.
- Equivalent proof: roots of a polynomial depend continuously on its coefficients (Part 3), so $n$ distinct roots stay distinct under small perturbations.

@@@ mn-052-orthogonal-group.md
---
title: The orthogonal group is closed and nowhere dense
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: orthogonal group, spheres, nowhere dense, Frobenius norm
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $O(n)$ is closed and nowhere dense in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

$O(n)=\{A:A^TA=I\}$. It lies in the Frobenius sphere of radius $\sqrt n$; a sphere has empty interior.

## Solution

**Idea.** $O(n)$ is cut out by polynomial equations, so it is closed. It is "thin" because it sits inside a sphere, and spheres are thin.

**Step 1: closed.** The map $\Phi:A\mapsto A^TA-I$ is polynomial in the entries, hence continuous, and $O(n)=\Phi^{-1}(\{0\})$.

**Step 2: $O(n)\subset S$, a sphere.** If $A^TA=I$ then $\|A\|_F^2=\operatorname{tr}(A^TA)=\operatorname{tr}I=n$. So $O(n)\subset S=\{\|A\|_F=\sqrt n\}$.

**Step 3: a sphere has empty interior.** Let $B\in S$ and $\varepsilon>0$. Then $(1+\varepsilon)B$ satisfies $\|(1+\varepsilon)B\|_F=(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt n>\sqrt n$, so $(1+\varepsilon)B\notin S$, while $\|(1+\varepsilon)B-B\|_F=\varepsilon\sqrt n\to0$. So no ball around $B$ lies in $S$.

**Step 4: nowhere dense.** A subset of a set with empty interior has empty interior, and $O(n)$ is closed, so $\overline{O(n)}=O(n)$ has empty interior: $O(n)$ is nowhere dense.

**Remark.**
- $O(n)$ is in fact compact (closed and bounded) and is a manifold of dimension $\frac{n(n-1)}2$, much smaller than $n^2$.
- A sharper statement is that the orthogonal group is the set of extreme points of the operator-norm unit ball (Part 1).

@@@ mn-053-involutions.md
---
title: Involutions form a closed unbounded set
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: involutions, closed sets, unbounded, counterexample
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge2$. Show that $\mathcal I=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):A^2=I\}$ is closed but unbounded.

## Hint

Closedness: polynomial equation. Unboundedness: find a one-parameter family of $2\times2$ involutions with growing norm.

## Solution

**Idea.** The set is defined by $A^2=I$, so it is closed; but a non-orthogonal reflection can be sheared arbitrarily far.

**Step 1: closed.** $\mathcal I=\{A:A^2-I=0\}$ is the preimage of $\{0\}$ under the continuous map $A\mapsto A^2-I$.

**Step 2: unbounded.** For $t\in\mathbb{R}$ let
$$S_t=\begin{pmatrix}1&t\\0&-1\end{pmatrix}.$$
Then
$$S_t^2=\begin{pmatrix}1\cdot1+t\cdot0&1\cdot t+t\cdot(-1)\\0&(-1)(-1)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}.$$
So $S_t\in\mathcal I$ for all $t$, and $\|S_t\|\ge|t|$ (the entry $t$ is at most the norm), which tends to $\infty$. For $n>2$ use $S_t\oplus I_{n-2}$.

**Remark.**
- The *orthogonal* involutions ($A^2=I$ and $A^T=A$, equivalently $A\in O(n)$ symmetric) form a compact set; the unboundedness comes from non-normal examples. The set $\mathcal I$ is the union of the conjugacy classes of the matrices $\operatorname{diag}(1,\dots,1,-1,\dots,-1)$; the classes containing both signs are unbounded.
- $\mathcal I$ has empty interior too: the eigenvalues of an involution lie in $\{1,-1\}$, while $A+\varepsilon I$ has eigenvalues in $\{1+\varepsilon,-1+\varepsilon\}$, so it is not an involution for small $\varepsilon\ne0$.

@@@ mn-054-positive-definite-boundary.md
---
title: The boundary of the positive definite cone
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: positive definite, boundary, symmetric matrices, determinant
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that, in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$, $\partial\,\mathrm{PD}=\{A\in\mathrm{Sym}_n:A\ge0,\ \det A=0\}$.

## Hint

Use $\partial E=\overline E\setminus\operatorname{int}E$ together with the results $\overline{\mathrm{PD}}=\mathrm{PSD}$ and PD open.

## Solution

**Idea.** The boundary is "closure minus interior". We already know both for PD.

**Step 1: closure and interior.** In $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ the set PD is open (so $\operatorname{int}\mathrm{PD}=\mathrm{PD}$) and $\overline{\mathrm{PD}}=\mathrm{PSD}$ (problem on cones above). Hence
$$\partial\,\mathrm{PD}=\overline{\mathrm{PD}}\setminus\operatorname{int}\mathrm{PD}=\mathrm{PSD}\setminus\mathrm{PD}.$$

**Step 2: identify $\mathrm{PSD}\setminus\mathrm{PD}$.** A symmetric matrix has real eigenvalues $\lambda_1\le\dots\le\lambda_n$ and
- $A\in\mathrm{PSD}\iff\lambda_1\ge0$,
- $A\in\mathrm{PD}\iff\lambda_1>0$.
So $A\in\mathrm{PSD}\setminus\mathrm{PD}$ iff $\lambda_1=0$. For a PSD matrix all eigenvalues are $\ge0$ and $\det A=\prod\lambda_i$, so $\det A=0$ iff some $\lambda_i=0$ iff $\lambda_1=0$. Thus
$$\mathrm{PSD}\setminus\mathrm{PD}=\{A\ge0:\det A=0\}.$$

**Example ($n=2$).** The boundary of PD is the set of $\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\b&c\end{pmatrix}$ with $a,c\ge0$, $ac=b^2$: one nappe of a quadratic cone in the three-dimensional space $\mathrm{Sym}_2\cong\mathbb{R}^3$: the boundary of the Lorentz cone $\{a+c\ge\sqrt{(a-c)^2+4b^2}\}$.

**Remark.**
- Note the condition $A\ge0$ cannot be dropped: $\operatorname{diag}(1,0,-1)$ has $\det=0$ but is *not* a boundary point of PD, since it is not even in $\mathrm{PSD}$.
- The boundary consists of PSD matrices of rank $<n$: the faces of the cone PSD, parametrised by their ranges.

@@@ mn-055-projections.md
---
title: Orthogonal projections: compact with empty interior in Sym_n
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: projections, compactness, symmetric matrices, nowhere dense
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the set $\mathcal P$ of orthogonal projections ($P=P^T=P^2$) is compact, and that it has empty interior in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$.

## Hint

Eigenvalues of a projection are $0$ and $1$, so $\|P\|\le1$. For the interior, scale $P$ slightly.

## Solution

**Idea.** The defining equations make $\mathcal P$ closed; boundedness comes from the norm of a projection. For the empty interior one finds symmetric matrices arbitrarily near $P$ whose eigenvalues are not in $\{0,1\}$.

**Step 1: closed.** $\mathcal P=\{P:P^T-P=0\}\cap\{P:P^2-P=0\}$, an intersection of zero sets of continuous maps.

**Step 2: bounded.** If $P=P^T=P^2$ and $|x|=1$, then
$$|Px|^2=x^TP^TPx=x^TP^2x=x^TPx=\langle Px,x\rangle\le|Px||x|,$$
so $|Px|\le1$. Thus $\|P\|\le1$ and $\mathcal P$ lies in the closed ball of radius $1$.

**Step 3: compact.** By the Heineâ€“Borel theorem in $M_n(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$, a closed and bounded set is compact.

**Step 4: empty interior in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$.** Let $P\in\mathcal P$ and $\varepsilon>0$ small.
- If $P\neq0$: $(1+\varepsilon)P$ is symmetric, and $P$ has eigenvalue $1$, so $(1+\varepsilon)P$ has eigenvalue $1+\varepsilon\notin\{0,1\}$. A projection has all eigenvalues in $\{0,1\}$ (from $P^2=P$), so $(1+\varepsilon)P\notin\mathcal P$.
- If $P=0$: $\varepsilon I$ is symmetric, has eigenvalue $\varepsilon\notin\{0,1\}$, so is not a projection.
In both cases there are symmetric matrices at distance $\varepsilon\|P\|$ (resp. $\varepsilon$) from $P$ outside $\mathcal P$. So $P$ is not an interior point (relative to $\mathrm{Sym}_n$).

**Remark.**
- $\mathcal P$ is the disjoint union $\bigsqcup_{r=0}^n\mathcal P_r$ where $\mathcal P_r$ is the set of rank-$r$ projections, each compact and connected (a Grassmannian). So $\mathcal P$ has $n+1$ connected components, and the trace $\operatorname{tr}P=\operatorname{rank}P$ is locally constant on it.
- For $n=1$, $\mathcal P=\{0,1\}\subset\mathbb{R}$, which is finite and nowhere dense.

@@@ mn-056-subgroups.md
---
title: An open subgroup of GL_n is also closed
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: topological groups, open subgroup, cosets, GL_n
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that an open subgroup $H$ of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is also closed in $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Write the complement of $H$ as a union of cosets $gH$ and show these are open.

## Solution

**Idea.** The cosets of $H$ partition the group. If $H$ is open, so are all its cosets (translations are homeomorphisms), and the complement of $H$ is a union of other cosets.

**Step 1: translations are homeomorphisms.** For $g\in GL_n$ the left translation $L_g:X\mapsto gX$ is continuous (matrix multiplication is polynomial in the entries) with continuous inverse $L_{g^{-1}}$. So $L_g$ maps open sets to open sets.

**Step 2: cosets are open.** $gH=L_g(H)$ is open if $H$ is.

**Step 3: the complement is a union of cosets.** The left cosets $gH$ partition $GL_n$, with $H=1\cdot H$ one of them. Two cosets are equal or disjoint. Therefore
$$GL_n\setminus H=\bigcup_{g\notin H}gH$$
(a coset $gH$ with $g\notin H$ is disjoint from $H$: if $gh\in H$ then $g\in H$). This is a union of open sets, hence open. So $H$ is closed in $GL_n$.

**Example.** $GL_n^+=\{\det>0\}$ is an open subgroup (it is open, closed under products and inverses since $\det(AB)=\det A\det B$), and its complement $\{\det<0\}$ is the open coset $gGL_n^+$ for any $g$ with $\det g<0$.

**Remark.**
- The argument works in every topological group. A consequence: if $G$ is connected, an open subgroup $H$ is open and closed and non-empty, hence $H=G$. We use it in Part 5 to show that $GL_n^+$ is the identity component.
- "Closed in $GL_n$" is *not* the same as closed in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$: the set $GL_n^+$ is not closed in $M_n$, since its closure contains singular matrices.

@@@ mn-057-level-sets.md
---
title: Interior and boundary of {det â‰¥ 1}
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: determinant, interior, boundary, level sets
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\operatorname{int}\{\det\ge1\}=\{\det>1\}$ and $\partial\{\det\ge1\}=SL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

$\det(tA)=t^n\det A$. If $\det A=1$ and $t$ is slightly less than $1$, what happens to $\det(tA)$?

## Solution

**Idea.** The level $\det=1$ is an actual "edge" of the set, because scaling by a factor $t<1$ moves the determinant strictly down.

Let $E=\{\det\ge1\}$ and $O=\{\det>1\}$.

**Step 1: $E$ is closed, $O$ is open.** Preimages of $[1,\infty)$ and $(1,\infty)$ under the continuous map $\det$.

**Step 2: $O\subset\operatorname{int}E$.** $O$ is open and $O\subset E$.

**Step 3: $\operatorname{int}E\subset O$.** Let $A\in E\setminus O$, i.e. $\det A=1$. For $t\in(0,1)$, $\det(tA)=t^n\det A=t^n<1$, so $tA\notin E$; and $\|tA-A\|=(1-t)\|A\|\to0$ as $t\to1^-$. Thus every neighbourhood of $A$ contains a point outside $E$, and $A\notin\operatorname{int}E$. Hence $\operatorname{int}E=O$.

**Step 4: the boundary.** $E$ is closed, so $\overline E=E$ and
$$\partial E=\overline E\setminus\operatorname{int}E=\{\det\ge1\}\setminus\{\det>1\}=\{\det=1\}=SL_n(\mathbb{R}).$$

**Remark.**
- The same proof shows $\partial\{\det\ge c\}=\{\det=c\}$ for every $c>0$. For $c=0$ one needs the more delicate SVD argument ($\overline{\{\det>0\}}=\{\det\ge0\}$, problem above), since scaling does not move $\det=0$.
- For the set $\{\det\ge-1\}$ and a point with $\det A=-1$ the scaling must go the other way: $\det(tA)=-t^n>-1$ for $t<1$ stays inside, but for $t>1$ one gets $-t^n<-1$, outside, so such $A$ is a boundary point. Always check the sign.

@@@ mn-058-commutants.md
---
title: The commutant of a non-scalar matrix is a thin subspace
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: commutant, subspaces, scalar matrices, nowhere dense
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

For $B\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ let $C(B)=\{A:AB=BA\}$. Show that $C(B)$ is closed, and that it has empty interior unless $B$ is a scalar matrix.

## Hint

$C(B)$ is a linear subspace. It is the whole space iff $B$ commutes with every $E_{ij}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** $C(B)$ is a subspace, so by the subspace problem it is closed, and it has empty interior as soon as it is proper. It remains to see that it is proper unless $B$ is scalar.

**Step 1: $C(B)$ is a closed subspace.** $C(B)=\ker(A\mapsto AB-BA)$, the kernel of a linear (hence continuous) map: a closed linear subspace.

**Step 2: if $C(B)=M_n$ then $B$ is scalar.** Suppose $B$ commutes with every matrix. Take $A=E_{ij}$. The matrix $BE_{ij}$ has $j$-th column equal to the $i$-th column of $B$ and zeros elsewhere; $E_{ij}B$ has $i$-th row equal to the $j$-th row of $B$ and zeros elsewhere. Equality of the two matrices gives:
- for $k\neq i$, comparing entries $(k,j)$: $b_{ki}=0$ (off-diagonal entries of $B$ vanish);
- comparing entries $(i,j)$: $b_{ii}=b_{jj}$.
So $B=bI$ is scalar. Conversely if $B$ is scalar, $C(B)=M_n$.

**Step 3: conclusion.** If $B$ is not scalar, $C(B)$ is a *proper* closed subspace, hence has empty interior by the subspace problem. (If $B$ is scalar, $C(B)=M_n$ is open and has full interior, so the hypothesis cannot be dropped.)

**Remark.**
- Dimension count: $\dim C(B)\ge n$ always, with equality iff $B$ is *non-derogatory* (minimal polynomial = characteristic polynomial), which holds for a generic $B$ (these are classical facts, not needed for the problem; $C(B)$ contains the span of $I,B,B^2,\dots$). Numerically, for a random $3\times3$ matrix the commutant has dimension $3$.
- Hence for generic $B$ the commutant is a thin set of dimension $n$ in a space of dimension $n^2$.

@@@ mn-059-traces.md
---
title: Matrices with rational trace: dense with empty interior
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: trace, density, rationals, open maps
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\operatorname{tr}A\in\mathbb{Q}\}$ is dense in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ but has empty interior.

## Hint

A non-zero linear functional is an open map. The image of a ball under $\operatorname{tr}$ is a non-degenerate interval.

## Solution

**Idea.** The trace sends a neighbourhood of any matrix onto an interval; an interval contains both rationals and irrationals.

Let $T=\{A:\operatorname{tr}A\in\mathbb{Q}\}$.

**Step 1: images of balls.** Fix $A$ and $r>0$. For $\|H\|_F<r$ and $s\in(-r/\sqrt n,r/\sqrt n)$ take $H_s=\frac s{\sqrt n}I$: $\|H_s\|_F=|s|<r$ and $\operatorname{tr}(A+H_s)=\operatorname{tr}A+s\sqrt n$. Therefore
$$\operatorname{tr}\big(B(A,r)\big)\supset\big(\operatorname{tr}A-r\sqrt n,\ \operatorname{tr}A+r\sqrt n\big),$$
a non-empty open interval.

**Step 2: dense.** The interval above contains a rational number $q$. Choose $s$ with $\operatorname{tr}A+s\sqrt n=q$; then $A+H_s\in T$ and $\|A+H_s-A\|_F=|s|<r$. So every ball meets $T$.

**Step 3: empty interior.** The same interval contains an irrational number $x$ (the irrationals are dense), and choosing $s$ with $\operatorname{tr}A+s\sqrt n=x$ gives $A+H_s\in B(A,r)\setminus T$. So no ball is contained in $T$.

**Remark.**
- $T=\bigcup_{q\in\mathbb{Q}}\{\operatorname{tr}=q\}$ is a *countable union of closed hyperplanes*, each nowhere dense. This is an example of a dense *meagre* set: the Baire theorem (next problem) forbids a countable union of nowhere dense closed sets from being the whole space, consistent with $T\neq M_n$.
- The set $T$ is neither open nor closed; its complement (irrational trace) is a dense $G_\delta$.

@@@ mn-060-baire.md
---
title: Countably many proper subspaces never cover M_n
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: Baire category theorem, subspaces, covering
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ cannot be written as a union of finitely many proper subspaces, nor as a union of countably many proper subspaces.

## Hint

Each proper subspace is closed with empty interior. Use the Baire category theorem (a non-empty complete metric space is not a countable union of closed nowhere dense sets).

## Solution

**Idea.** Proper subspaces are "small" in the topological sense (closed nowhere dense); a complete space is "large" (Baire).

**Step 1: proper subspaces are closed nowhere dense.** By the earlier problem on subspaces, a proper subspace $V\subsetneq M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is closed with empty interior, i.e. nowhere dense.

**Step 2: $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is a complete metric space.** It is isometric to $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$ with the Frobenius norm.

**Step 3: Baire.** Suppose $M_n(\mathbb{R})=\bigcup_{k\ge1}V_k$ with each $V_k$ a proper subspace. This is a countable union of closed nowhere dense sets covering a non-empty complete metric space, contradicting the Baire category theorem. The finite case is a special case (repeat the subspaces).

**Elementary proof for finitely many.** Suppose $V_1,\dots,V_m$ are proper subspaces covering $M=M_n(\mathbb{R})$, with $m$ minimal. Pick $x\in V_1$ not in $V_2\cup\dots\cup V_m$ (it exists by minimality) and $y\notin V_1$. For $t\in\mathbb{R}$, the points $y+tx$ are distinct for distinct $t$; none lies in $V_1$ (else $y\in V_1$ as $tx\in V_1$). Each of the infinitely many points $y+tx$ lies in some $V_j$ with $j\ge2$, so one $V_j$ contains two of them, $y+tx$ and $y+t'x$, hence $(t-t')x\in V_j$ and $x\in V_j$: contradiction.

**Remark.**
- The finite statement is a property of *infinite* fields: over $\mathbb{F}_2$ the space $\mathbb{F}_2^2$ is the union of the three lines through the origin.
- The countable statement is false in a non-complete space: $\mathbb{Q}^{n^2}$ is a countable union of points.
- The example $T=\{\operatorname{tr}\in\mathbb{Q}\}$ from the previous problem shows that a union of *countably many hyperplanes (affine ones)* can be dense without being everything.

@@@ mn-061-spectral-radius.md
---
title: The set {Ï(A) < 1}: open, with boundary {Ï = 1}
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: spectral radius, continuity of eigenvalues, closure, boundary
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $\rho(A)$ be the spectral radius of $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ (the largest modulus of a complex eigenvalue). Show that $\{\rho<1\}$ is open, its closure is $\{\rho\le1\}$, and its boundary is $\{\rho=1\}$.

## Hint

$\rho$ is continuous because the roots of a polynomial depend continuously on its coefficients. For the closure, note $\rho(tA)=t\rho(A)$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Once $\rho$ is known to be continuous, the topology follows from the homogeneity $\rho(tA)=|t|\rho(A)$, exactly as for a norm (even though $\rho$ is not a norm).

**Step 1: $\rho$ is continuous.** Let $A_k\to A$. The characteristic polynomials $\chi_{A_k}$ have coefficients converging to those of $\chi_A$. By the continuity of roots (Part 3: every root of $\chi_{A_k}$ is within $C\eta_k^{1/n}$ of a root of $\chi_A$ and vice versa, with $\eta_k\to0$), we get $|\rho(A_k)-\rho(A)|\to0$. (The estimate behind this: if $p,q$ are monic of degree $n$ with $|\mu|\le R$ a root of $q$ and coefficients differing by at most $\eta$, then $\prod_i|\mu-\lambda_i|=|p(\mu)|=|p(\mu)-q(\mu)|\le\eta\,n(1+R)^{n-1}$, so some root $\lambda_i$ of $p$ is within $(\eta n(1+R)^{n-1})^{1/n}$ of $\mu$; the coefficients of $\chi_{A_k}$ are bounded since $A_k$ is, and so are the roots.) Concretely: if $\mu$ is a root of $\chi_{A_k}$ of maximal modulus, some root $\lambda$ of $\chi_A$ has $|\mu-\lambda|\le C\eta_k^{1/n}$, so $\rho(A_k)\le\rho(A)+C\eta_k^{1/n}$; exchanging the roles gives the reverse inequality.

**Step 2: open and closed sets.** $\{\rho<1\}=\rho^{-1}((-\infty,1))$ is open and $\{\rho\le1\}=\rho^{-1}((-\infty,1])$ is closed.

**Step 3: closure.** Since $\{\rho<1\}\subset\{\rho\le1\}$ and the latter is closed, $\overline{\{\rho<1\}}\subset\{\rho\le1\}$. Conversely if $\rho(A)\le1$ then for $t\in(0,1)$: $\rho(tA)=t\rho(A)<1$ (eigenvalues of $tA$ are $t$ times those of $A$; if $\rho(A)=0$ then $\rho(tA)=0<1$), and $\|tA-A\|=(1-t)\|A\|\to0$. So $A\in\overline{\{\rho<1\}}$.

**Step 4: boundary.** The set $\{\rho<1\}$ is open, so $\partial\{\rho<1\}=\overline{\{\rho<1\}}\setminus\{\rho<1\}=\{\rho=1\}$.

**Example.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&5\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ has $\rho=1$ (boundary) although $\|A\|\approx5.19>1$; $tA$ has $\rho=t<1$.

**Remark.**
- $\rho(A)<1$ iff $A^k\to0$ iff some norm satisfies $\|A\|<1$; but no single norm works for all of $\{\rho<1\}$: the set is **not bounded** and **not convex**. For the non-convexity take $N=\begin{pmatrix}0&2\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ and $N^T$: $\rho(N)=\rho(N^T)=0$ but $\frac12(N+N^T)=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ has $\rho=1$.
- The interior of $\{\rho\le1\}$ is $\{\rho<1\}$ (if $\rho(A)=1$ then $\rho(tA)=t>1$ for $t>1$ arbitrarily close).

@@@ mn-062-diagonalisable-matrices.md
---
title: The interior of the real-diagonalisable matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: diagonalisable, interior, distinct eigenvalues, Jordan blocks
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}$ be the set of $\mathbb{R}$-diagonalisable matrices. Show that
$$\operatorname{int}\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}=\{A:A\text{ has }n\text{ distinct real eigenvalues}\}.$$

## Hint

Distinct real eigenvalues is an open condition (use sign changes of the characteristic polynomial). If an eigenvalue is repeated, add a small nilpotent term inside its eigenspace to create a Jordan block.

## Solution

**Idea.** ($\supset$) simple real eigenvalues persist under perturbation. ($\subset$) a diagonalisable matrix with a repeated eigenvalue can be perturbed into a non-diagonalisable one.

Let $\mathcal S$ be the set of matrices with $n$ distinct real eigenvalues. Matrices in $\mathcal S$ are diagonalisable, so $\mathcal S\subset\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}$.

**Step 1: $\mathcal S$ is open.** Let $A\in\mathcal S$ with eigenvalues $r_1<\dots<r_n$. Choose reals $s_0<r_1<s_1<r_2<\dots<r_n<s_n$. The values $\chi_A(s_0),\dots,\chi_A(s_n)$ are non-zero and alternate in sign (a monic polynomial with simple roots changes sign at each root). Each $B\mapsto\chi_B(s_i)$ is continuous, so for $B$ close to $A$ the signs are the same. By the intermediate value theorem $\chi_B$ has a root in each interval $(s_{i-1},s_i)$, giving $n$ distinct real roots; as $\deg\chi_B=n$, these are all of them. So $B\in\mathcal S$. Hence $\mathcal S\subset\operatorname{int}\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}$.

**Step 2: a diagonalisable matrix with a repeated eigenvalue is not interior.** Let $A\in\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}$ with $A=PDP^{-1}$, $D=\operatorname{diag}(d_1,\dots,d_n)$, and suppose some value $d$ occurs $m\ge2$ times; reorder so that $d_1=d_2=d$. Put
$$A_\varepsilon=P(D+\varepsilon E_{12})P^{-1}.$$
Then $A_\varepsilon\to A$ as $\varepsilon\to0$ (conjugation is continuous). We show $A_\varepsilon$ is not diagonalisable. Let $M=D+\varepsilon E_{12}$, so $A_\varepsilon=PMP^{-1}$. The matrix $M$ is upper triangular (the extra entry lies above the diagonal) with diagonal $d_1,\dots,d_n$, so $\chi_M=\prod(t-d_i)$ and the eigenvalue $d$ has algebraic multiplicity $m$. The geometric multiplicity is $n-\operatorname{rank}(M-dI)$. We have $\operatorname{rank}(D-dI)=n-m$ (it has $n-m$ non-zero diagonal entries), and $M-dI=(D-dI)+\varepsilon E_{12}$ where column $2$ of $D-dI$ is zero (as $d_2=d$) and the new entry $\varepsilon$ lies in column $2$ and in row $1$, a row where $D-dI$ is zero. So the rank increases by $1$: $\operatorname{rank}(M-dI)=n-m+1$ and the geometric multiplicity is $m-1<m$. Hence $M$, and therefore $A_\varepsilon$, is not diagonalisable.

So $A\notin\operatorname{int}\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}$.

**Step 3: conclusion.** If $A\in\operatorname{int}\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}$ then $A\in\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}$ has all eigenvalues real, and by Step 2 no repeated eigenvalue, so $A\in\mathcal S$. Together with Step 1, $\operatorname{int}\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathcal S$.

**Numeric check.** $D=\operatorname{diag}(2,2,2,5)$, $M=D+0.1E_{12}$: $\operatorname{rank}(M-2I)=2=n-m+1$ with $n=4$, $m=3$ âœ“.

**Remark.**
- Combined with the problem on closures (next), we see $\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}$ is "squeezed" between an open set $\mathcal S$ and a closed set $\overline{\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}}\supsetneq\mathcal S$.
- Over $\mathbb{C}$ the analogous interior is the set of matrices with $n$ distinct complex eigenvalues.

@@@ mn-063-diagonalisable-matrices.md
---
title: The closure of the real-diagonalisable matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: diagonalisable, closure, triangularisation, continuity of roots
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the closure of the set of $\mathbb{R}$-diagonalisable matrices is $\{A:\chi_A\text{ splits over }\mathbb{R}\}$, i.e. the set of matrices all of whose eigenvalues are real.

## Hint

($\subset$) the roots of a limit of real-rooted polynomials are real. ($\supset$) triangularise over $\mathbb{R}$ and perturb the diagonal to make the eigenvalues distinct.

## Solution

**Idea.** The only obstruction to being a limit of diagonalisable matrices is having non-real eigenvalues. Real-rooted matrices can always be nudged to have distinct real eigenvalues.

Let $\mathcal T=\{A:\chi_A\text{ splits over }\mathbb{R}\}$ (all roots real, with multiplicity).

**Step 1: $\overline{\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}}\subset\mathcal T$.** If $A$ is diagonalisable over $\mathbb{R}$ then $A\in\mathcal T$, so $\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}\subset\mathcal T$. It suffices to show that $\mathcal T$ is closed. Let $A_k\in\mathcal T$, $A_k\to A$, and let $z$ be a root of $\chi_A$. By continuity of roots (Part 3), for $k$ large there is a root $z_k$ of $\chi_{A_k}$ with $|z-z_k|\to0$. The roots $z_k$ are real, and the limit of real numbers is real, so $z\in\mathbb{R}$. Hence all roots of $\chi_A$ are real: $A\in\mathcal T$.

**Step 2: $\mathcal T\subset\overline{\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}}$.** Let $A\in\mathcal T$. Since all eigenvalues are real, $A$ is triangularisable over $\mathbb{R}$ (Schur: $A=PTP^{-1}$ with $P$ invertible, $T$ upper triangular with the eigenvalues on the diagonal; one can take $P$ orthogonal). Let $\Delta_\varepsilon=\varepsilon\operatorname{diag}(1,2,\dots,n)$. The matrix $T_\varepsilon=T+\Delta_\varepsilon$ is upper triangular with diagonal entries $t_{ii}+i\varepsilon$. These are pairwise distinct for all small $\varepsilon\ne0$: $t_{ii}+i\varepsilon=t_{jj}+j\varepsilon$ forces $\varepsilon=\frac{t_{ii}-t_{jj}}{j-i}$ for some pair $i\neq j$, which excludes only finitely many values of $\varepsilon$. For such $\varepsilon$ the matrix $T_\varepsilon$ has $n$ distinct real eigenvalues, so is diagonalisable over $\mathbb{R}$, and so is $A_\varepsilon=PT_\varepsilon P^{-1}$. Finally $\|A_\varepsilon-A\|=\|P\Delta_\varepsilon P^{-1}\|\le\varepsilon n\|P\|\|P^{-1}\|\to0$.

**Remark.**
- Example: $\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal T$ is the limit of $\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1+\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}$; but the rotation $\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}\notin\mathcal T$ and is at positive distance from $\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}$.
- Thus the three sets satisfy $\mathcal S=\operatorname{int}\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}\subset\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}\subset\overline{\mathcal D_{\mathbb{R}}}=\mathcal T$, with both inclusions strict for $n\ge2$.

@@@ mn-064-repeated-eigenvalues.md
---
title: Matrices with a repeated eigenvalue are rare
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: discriminant, algebraic sets, polynomials, nowhere dense
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge2$. Show that $\mathcal D=\{A:\chi_A\text{ has a repeated root}\}$ is closed with empty interior.

## Hint

$\mathcal D=\{\operatorname{disc}(\chi_A)=0\}$ is the zero set of a polynomial in the entries. Check that this polynomial is not identically $0$, and that a non-zero polynomial cannot vanish on a ball.

## Solution

**Idea.** $\mathcal D$ is an *algebraic hypersurface*: the zero set of a single non-zero polynomial, hence closed and nowhere dense.

**Step 1: $\mathcal D$ is the zero set of a polynomial.** As in the problem on distinct eigenvalues, $\Delta(A)=\operatorname{disc}(\chi_A)$ is a polynomial in the entries of $A$, and $\chi_A$ has a repeated root iff $\Delta(A)=0$. So $\mathcal D=\Delta^{-1}(\{0\})$ is closed.

**Step 2: $\Delta\not\equiv0$.** For $A_0=\operatorname{diag}(1,2,\dots,n)$ the roots of $\chi_{A_0}$ are $1,\dots,n$, distinct, so $\Delta(A_0)\neq0$.

**Step 3: a non-zero polynomial does not vanish on a ball.** Suppose a polynomial $p$ in $N=n^2$ variables vanished on a ball $B(a,r)$. Then all partial derivatives of $p$ at $a$ vanish (they are limits of difference quotients of the zero function inside the ball). A polynomial is equal to its Taylor expansion at $a$, which has all coefficients $\frac{\partial^\alpha p(a)}{\alpha!}=0$. So $p\equiv0$.

**Step 4: conclusion.** By Steps 2 and 3, $\Delta$ does not vanish identically on any ball, so $\mathcal D$ contains no ball: $\operatorname{int}\mathcal D=\emptyset$.

**Remark.**
- For $n=1$ there are no repeated roots, so $\mathcal D=\emptyset$.
- The argument is a template: *the zero set of a non-zero polynomial on $\mathbb{R}^N$ is closed and nowhere dense.* It covers the set of singular matrices ($\det=0$), the set $\{\operatorname{rank}\le r\}$, the set of matrices with a repeated eigenvalue, and so on.
- For $n=2$, $\mathcal D=\{\Delta=(\operatorname{tr})^2-4\det=0\}$ is the boundary of the set of matrices with a real eigenvalue (see the problem on real eigenvalues).

@@@ mn-065-symmetric-matrices.md
---
title: Symmetric matrices with distinct eigenvalues are open and dense
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-open-closed
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: symmetric matrices, spectral theorem, genericity, distinct eigenvalues
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the symmetric matrices with $n$ distinct eigenvalues form an open dense subset of $\mathrm{Sym}_n$.

## Hint

Open: the discriminant is non-zero. Dense: diagonalise orthogonally, and perturb the eigenvalues in the diagonal factor.

## Solution

**Idea.** The spectral theorem lets us change the eigenvalues of a symmetric matrix at will without leaving $\mathrm{Sym}_n$: we keep the orthogonal factor and move the diagonal one.

Let $\mathcal G=\{S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n:S\text{ has }n\text{ distinct eigenvalues}\}$.

**Step 1: $\mathcal G$ is open in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$.** $\mathcal G=\{S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n:\Delta(S)\ne0\}$ where $\Delta(S)=\operatorname{disc}(\chi_S)$ is a polynomial in the entries (previous problems). It is the preimage of $\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$ under a continuous map restricted to $\mathrm{Sym}_n$, hence open in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$.

**Step 2: $\mathcal G$ is dense in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$.** Let $S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n$. By the spectral theorem $S=Q\Lambda Q^T$ with $Q$ orthogonal and $\Lambda=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n)$. For $\varepsilon\ne0$ put
$$S_\varepsilon=Q\big(\Lambda+\varepsilon\operatorname{diag}(1,2,\dots,n)\big)Q^T.$$
This is symmetric (it is $Q\,\mathrm{diag}\,Q^T$), and its eigenvalues are $\lambda_i+i\varepsilon$. These are pairwise distinct unless $\lambda_i+i\varepsilon=\lambda_j+j\varepsilon$ for some $i<j$, i.e. $\varepsilon=\frac{\lambda_i-\lambda_j}{j-i}$: finitely many values of $\varepsilon$ to avoid. So for all small $\varepsilon\ne0$, $S_\varepsilon\in\mathcal G$. Finally, orthogonal invariance of the operator norm gives
$$\|S_\varepsilon-S\|=\varepsilon\,\|\operatorname{diag}(1,\dots,n)\|=n\varepsilon\to0.$$

**Remark.**
- Both statements together say that *a generic symmetric matrix has simple spectrum*, which is why proofs for symmetric matrices can usually assume distinct eigenvalues and conclude by continuity.
- The complement of $\mathcal G$ in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ is closed with empty interior, but unlike in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ it has codimension $2$, not $1$: two eigenvalues of a symmetric matrix collide in codimension $2$ (von Neumannâ€“Wigner "level repulsion"), e.g. in $\mathrm{Sym}_2\cong\mathbb{R}^3$ the repeated-eigenvalue matrices are the *line* of scalar matrices $\{aI\}$.

@@@ mn-001-frobenius-norm.md
---
title: The Frobenius norm comes from an inner product
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: Frobenius norm, inner product, trace, identification with R^(nÂ²)
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\langle A,B\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(A^TB)$ is an inner product on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and that its associated norm is $\|A\|_F=\big(\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}^2\big)^{1/2}$.

## Hint

Compute $\operatorname{tr}(A^TB)$ entry by entry.

Write the $i$-th diagonal entry of $A^TB$ as a sum over the index $k$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The trace of $A^TB$ turns out to be the ordinary dot product of the two matrices read as vectors of length $n^2$. All the axioms of an inner product are then inherited from $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$.

**Step 1: the formula.** The $(i,i)$ entry of $A^TB$ is $\sum_k (A^T)_{ik}B_{ki}=\sum_k a_{ki}b_{ki}$. Summing over $i$,
$$\operatorname{tr}(A^TB)=\sum_{i,k}a_{ki}b_{ki}=\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}b_{ij}.$$

**Step 2: the axioms.** Using this formula:
- *Bilinearity:* the right-hand side is linear in $A$ for fixed $B$, and linear in $B$ for fixed $A$.
- *Symmetry:* $\sum a_{ij}b_{ij}=\sum b_{ij}a_{ij}$.
- *Positivity:* $\langle A,A\rangle=\sum a_{ij}^2\ge0$, with equality iff every $a_{ij}=0$, i.e. $A=0$.

**Step 3: the norm.** $\sqrt{\langle A,A\rangle}=\big(\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}^2\big)^{1/2}=\|A\|_F$.

**Consequence.** The map $A\mapsto(a_{11},a_{12},\dots,a_{nn})$ is a linear bijection $M_n(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$ which preserves inner products, hence distances. So $(M_n(\mathbb{R}),\|\cdot\|_F)$ **is** Euclidean space $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$, and every notion of "open", "closed", "compact", "convergent" in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is the usual one of $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$.

**Remark.** Because the Frobenius norm comes from an inner product, the Cauchyâ€“Schwarz inequality $|\operatorname{tr}(A^TB)|\le\|A\|_F\|B\|_F$ and the parallelogram law hold in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$. We will use both repeatedly (for instance in the problems on submultiplicativity and strict convexity below).
@@@ mn-002-frobenius-norm.md
---
title: Two norms of the identity matrix
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: Frobenius norm, operator norm, identity matrix
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Compute $\|I_n\|_F$ and the operator norm $\|I_n\|=\sup_{|x|=1}|I_nx|$.

## Hint

Apply the definitions directly.

For the Frobenius norm, count how many entries of $I_n$ are non-zero.

## Solution

**Idea.** The two norms measure different things: the Frobenius norm adds up all the entries, whereas the operator norm only measures the largest stretching factor.

**Step 1: Frobenius.** The identity has $n$ entries equal to $1$ (the diagonal) and all the others $0$. Therefore
$$\|I_n\|_F^2=\sum_{i,j}\delta_{ij}^2=n,\qquad \|I_n\|_F=\sqrt n.$$

**Step 2: operator norm.** For every unit vector $x$ we have $|I_nx|=|x|=1$, so the supremum is $\|I_n\|=1$.

**Remark.** This shows that the two norms are really different when $n\ge2$: $\|I_n\|_F/\|I_n\|=\sqrt n$. This ratio is the largest possible one (see the problem on the best equivalence constants): the identity, and more generally every orthogonal matrix, is the extremal case where the Frobenius norm is $\sqrt n$ times the operator norm. A common mistake is to say "$\|I\|=1$ for every norm"; this is true for operator norms but false for the Frobenius norm.
@@@ mn-003-frobenius-norm.md
---
title: Entries versus Frobenius norm
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: Frobenius norm, max norm, equivalence of norms
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\max_{i,j}|a_{ij}|\le\|A\|_F\le n\max_{i,j}|a_{ij}|$ for every $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Compare a single term $a_{ij}^2$ with the sum of all $n^2$ terms.

For the right inequality, bound every $a_{ij}^2$ by the same number.

## Solution

**Idea.** A sum of $n^2$ non-negative numbers is at least its largest term and at most $n^2$ times its largest term.

**Step 1: left inequality.** Fix $(k,l)$. Since all terms are non-negative,
$$a_{kl}^2\le\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}^2=\|A\|_F^2.$$
Taking square roots, $|a_{kl}|\le\|A\|_F$, and then take the maximum over $(k,l)$.

**Step 2: right inequality.** Let $m=\max_{i,j}|a_{ij}|$. Then each $a_{ij}^2\le m^2$ and there are $n^2$ terms, so
$$\|A\|_F^2\le n^2m^2,\qquad\|A\|_F\le nm.$$

**Equality cases.** The left inequality is an equality iff $A$ has at most one non-zero entry (e.g. $A=E_{11}$). The right one is an equality iff $|a_{ij}|=m$ for all $i,j$ (e.g. the all-ones matrix, for which $\|J\|_F=n$ and $m=1$).

**Remark.** These inequalities show directly that the Frobenius norm and the max-entry norm are equivalent. They are the simplest instance of the fact (proved later) that all norms on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ are equivalent. In particular a sequence of matrices converges iff each entry converges.
@@@ mn-004-completeness.md
---
title: Every norm on M_n(R) is complete
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: completeness, finite dimension, equivalence of norms
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ equipped with any norm is complete.

## Hint

Reduce to $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$ using the Frobenius norm.

You will need the fact (proved in the problem "Equivalence of norms" below) that all norms on a finite-dimensional space are equivalent.

## Solution

**Idea.** Completeness is a property of the *uniform structure*, so it is the same for two equivalent norms. For one particular norm, the Frobenius norm, the space is just $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$, which is complete.

**Step 1: the Frobenius norm.** A Cauchy sequence $(A_k)$ for $\|\cdot\|_F$ has Cauchy entries: $|a^{(k)}_{ij}-a^{(l)}_{ij}|\le\|A_k-A_l\|_F$ (previous problem). Each entry sequence converges in $\mathbb{R}$, say to $a_{ij}$. Let $A=(a_{ij})$; then $\|A_k-A\|_F^2=\sum_{i,j}(a^{(k)}_{ij}-a_{ij})^2\to0$, a finite sum of terms tending to $0$.

**Step 2: any other norm.** Let $\|\cdot\|'$ be any norm. By equivalence of norms there are $c,C>0$ with $c\|A\|_F\le\|A\|'\le C\|A\|_F$. A sequence is $\|\cdot\|'$-Cauchy iff it is $\|\cdot\|_F$-Cauchy, and it converges for one norm iff it converges for the other (to the same limit). Hence completeness transfers.

**Remark.** This is specific to finite dimension. In infinite dimension, different norms on the same vector space can give a complete and a non-complete space (e.g. continuous functions with the sup norm versus the $L^1$ norm).
@@@ mn-005-computation.md
---
title: Column-sum and row-sum norms of a 2Ã—2 matrix
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: matrix norms, column sum, row sum, computation
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Compute the maximum column-sum norm $\|A\|_1=\max_j\sum_i|a_{ij}|$ and the maximum row-sum norm $\|A\|_\infty=\max_i\sum_j|a_{ij}|$ of $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&-2\\3&4\end{pmatrix}$.

## Hint

Take absolute values of the entries first, then sum along columns (resp. rows).

## Solution

**Idea.** These are the operator norms induced by the $\ell^1$ and $\ell^\infty$ norms on $\mathbb{R}^n$; both have simple closed formulas.

**Step 1: absolute values.** $|A|=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\3&4\end{pmatrix}$.

**Step 2: columns.** Column sums: $1+3=4$ and $2+4=6$. Hence $\|A\|_1=\max(4,6)=6$.

**Step 3: rows.** Row sums: $1+2=3$ and $3+4=7$. Hence $\|A\|_\infty=\max(3,7)=7$.

**Check with a test vector.** For $x=(0,1)$, $|x|_1=1$ and $Ax=(-2,4)$ with $|Ax|_1=6$: the bound $\|A\|_1=6$ is attained. For $x=(1,1)$, $|x|_\infty=1$ and $Ax=(-1,7)$ with $|Ax|_\infty=7$: the bound $\|A\|_\infty=7$ is attained.

**Remark.** Note that $\|A^T\|_1=\|A\|_\infty$ in general. For comparison, the Frobenius norm is $\sqrt{1+4+9+16}=\sqrt{30}\approx5.48$ and the operator (spectral) norm is about $5.12$. The inequality $\|A\|^2\le\|A\|_1\|A\|_\infty$ ($26.2\le42$) is proved in the problem "Schur test" below.
@@@ mn-006-submultiplicativity.md
---
title: The Frobenius norm is submultiplicative
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: Frobenius norm, Cauchyâ€“Schwarz, submultiplicativity
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\|AB\|_F\le\|A\|_F\|B\|_F$ for all $A,B\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Apply the Cauchyâ€“Schwarz inequality to each entry of $AB$.

Think of $(AB)_{ij}$ as a dot product of the $i$-th row of $A$ with the $j$-th column of $B$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Every entry of $AB$ is a dot product; Cauchyâ€“Schwarz controls it by the lengths of the row and the column involved. Summing over all entries reassembles the Frobenius norms.

**Step 1: one entry.** Let $r_i$ be the $i$-th row of $A$ and $c_j$ the $j$-th column of $B$, both viewed in $\mathbb{R}^n$. Then $(AB)_{ij}=\sum_ka_{ik}b_{kj}=\langle r_i,c_j\rangle$, and by Cauchyâ€“Schwarz
$$(AB)_{ij}^2\le|r_i|^2|c_j|^2.$$

**Step 2: sum over $i,j$.**
$$\|AB\|_F^2\le\sum_{i,j}|r_i|^2|c_j|^2=\Big(\sum_i|r_i|^2\Big)\Big(\sum_j|c_j|^2\Big)=\|A\|_F^2\|B\|_F^2.$$
The two sums factor because $i$ and $j$ vary independently, and $\sum_i|r_i|^2=\sum_{i,k}a_{ik}^2=\|A\|_F^2$ (similarly for $B$).

**Conclusion.** Taking square roots gives $\|AB\|_F\le\|A\|_F\|B\|_F$.

**Remark.** Together with completeness this makes $(M_n(\mathbb{R}),\|\cdot\|_F)$ a Banach algebra. Compare with the max-entry norm, which is *not* submultiplicative (next problems). A useful corollary is $\|A^k\|_F\le\|A\|_F^k$.
@@@ mn-007-operator-norm.md
---
title: Operator norm: submultiplicativity and comparison with Frobenius
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: operator norm, submultiplicativity, Frobenius norm
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $\|A\|=\sup_{|x|=1}|Ax|$ be the operator norm. Show that $\|AB\|\le\|A\|\|B\|$ and that
$$\|A\|\le\|A\|_F\le\sqrt n\,\|A\|.$$

## Hint

Use the basic inequality $|Ax|\le\|A\||x|$ for all $x$.

For the two comparisons, write $|Ax|^2$ using the rows of $A$, and $\|A\|_F^2$ using the columns of $A$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Submultiplicativity follows from applying the definition twice. The comparison with $\|\cdot\|_F$ uses Cauchyâ€“Schwarz in one direction and a sum over the $n$ unit vectors $e_j$ in the other.

**Step 1: the basic inequality.** By definition of the supremum, for $x\ne0$ we have $|Ax|=|x|\,|A(x/|x|)|\le\|A\||x|$ (and it is trivial for $x=0$).

**Step 2: submultiplicativity.** For every $x$, $|ABx|\le\|A\||Bx|\le\|A\|\|B\||x|$. Taking the supremum over unit $x$ gives $\|AB\|\le\|A\|\|B\|$.

**Step 3: $\|A\|\le\|A\|_F$.** Let $r_i$ be the rows of $A$. Then $(Ax)_i=\langle r_i,x\rangle$ and by Cauchyâ€“Schwarz
$$|Ax|^2=\sum_i\langle r_i,x\rangle^2\le\sum_i|r_i|^2|x|^2=\|A\|_F^2|x|^2.$$
Take $|x|=1$ and the supremum.

**Step 4: $\|A\|_F\le\sqrt n\|A\|$.** The columns of $A$ are the vectors $Ae_j$. Hence
$$\|A\|_F^2=\sum_{j=1}^n|Ae_j|^2\le\sum_{j=1}^n\|A\|^2|e_j|^2=n\|A\|^2.$$

**Remark.** Both bounds are sharp: the first is an equality for rank-one matrices, the second for orthogonal matrices (see the problem on the best constants). One consequence, used constantly: $\|A\|$ is controlled by the entries, e.g. $\|A\|\le\|A\|_F\le n\max|a_{ij}|$.
@@@ mn-008-examples.md
---
title: Operator norms of three basic matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: operator norm, nilpotent, diagonal, rotation
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Find the operator norm (for the Euclidean norm on $\mathbb{R}^2$) of $\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$, of $\operatorname{diag}(1,-3)$ and of a rotation matrix $R_\theta=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$.

## Hint

The operator norm is the largest factor by which $A$ can stretch a unit vector.

## Solution

**Idea.** In each case we find the largest value of $|Ax|$ on the unit circle, by an upper bound plus an example attaining it.

**Case 1: $N=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$.** For $x=(x_1,x_2)$, $Nx=(x_2,0)$, so $|Nx|=|x_2|\le|x|$. Equality holds for $x=e_2$, where $Ne_2=e_1$. Hence $\|N\|=1$. (Note $Ne_1=0$: the matrix kills a direction but still has norm $1$, and $\rho(N)=0<\|N\|$.)

**Case 2: $D=\operatorname{diag}(1,-3)$.** $|Dx|^2=x_1^2+9x_2^2\le9(x_1^2+x_2^2)=9|x|^2$, with equality at $x=e_2$. So $\|D\|=3$, the largest absolute value of a diagonal entry.

**Case 3: rotation.** $|R_\theta x|=|x|$ for all $x$ since a rotation preserves lengths (indeed $R_\theta^TR_\theta=I$, so $|R_\theta x|^2=x^TR_\theta^TR_\theta x=|x|^2$). So $\|R_\theta\|=1$.

**Remark.** Case 1 shows that the operator norm can be positive for a nilpotent matrix, so $\|A\|$ is not the spectral radius. This phenomenon is the reason for the Gelfand-type bound $\rho(A)\le\|A^k\|^{1/k}$ in a later problem. In Case 3 the Frobenius norm is $\sqrt2$, so $\|R_\theta\|_F=\sqrt2\|R_\theta\|$: the extremal case of $\|A\|_F\le\sqrt n\|A\|$.
@@@ mn-009-non-submultiplicative.md
---
title: The max-entry norm is not submultiplicative
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: max norm, submultiplicativity, counterexample
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge2$. Show that $\|A\|_{\max}=\max_{i,j}|a_{ij}|$ is a norm on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ which is not submultiplicative.

## Hint

Check the norm axioms entry by entry.

For the counterexample, try the all-ones matrix $J$ and compute $J^2$.

## Solution

**Idea.** $\|\cdot\|_{\max}$ is the sup-norm on $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$, so it is automatically a norm; but multiplication of matrices adds up $n$ products, which can exceed the product of the maxima.

**Step 1: it is a norm.**
- Definiteness: $\max|a_{ij}|=0$ iff all entries vanish.
- Homogeneity: $\max|\lambda a_{ij}|=|\lambda|\max|a_{ij}|$.
- Triangle inequality: $|a_{ij}+b_{ij}|\le|a_{ij}|+|b_{ij}|\le\|A\|_{\max}+\|B\|_{\max}$ for every $(i,j)$.

**Step 2: the counterexample.** Let $J$ be the matrix whose entries are all equal to $1$. Then $\|J\|_{\max}=1$ and
$$(J^2)_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^n1\cdot1=n,\quad\text{so}\quad J^2=nJ,\quad\|J^2\|_{\max}=n.$$
Since $n\ge2$, $\|J^2\|_{\max}=n>1=\|J\|_{\max}^2$.

**Fix.** The rescaled norm $n\|A\|_{\max}$ is submultiplicative: $n|(AB)_{ij}|\le n\sum_k|a_{ik}||b_{kj}|\le n\cdot n\|A\|_{\max}\|B\|_{\max}$, i.e. $\|AB\|'\le\|A\|'\|B\|'$ for $\|A\|'=n\|A\|_{\max}$.

**Remark.** For $n=1$ the statement fails (the norm is $|a|$, which is multiplicative). Being submultiplicative is exactly what is needed to write $\|A^k\|\le\|A\|^k$ and to use geometric series.
@@@ mn-010-isometries.md
---
title: Orthogonal invariance of the Frobenius and operator norms
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: orthogonal matrices, isometry, trace cyclicity
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\|UAV\|_F=\|A\|_F$ and $\|UAV\|=\|A\|$ for all orthogonal matrices $U,V\in O(n)$ and $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Use $\|A\|_F^2=\operatorname{tr}(A^TA)$ and the cyclicity of the trace.

For the operator norm use that orthogonal maps preserve Euclidean lengths and are bijections of the unit sphere.

## Solution

**Idea.** Orthogonal matrices are the isometries of $\mathbb{R}^n$; multiplying on the left or on the right by one cannot change any norm built from lengths.

**Step 1: Frobenius.** Using $\|M\|_F^2=\operatorname{tr}(M^TM)$,
$$\|UAV\|_F^2=\operatorname{tr}(V^TA^TU^TUAV)=\operatorname{tr}(V^TA^TAV)=\operatorname{tr}(A^TAVV^T)=\operatorname{tr}(A^TA)=\|A\|_F^2.$$
We used $U^TU=I$, then the cyclicity $\operatorname{tr}(XY)=\operatorname{tr}(YX)$ with $X=V^T$, $Y=A^TAV$, and finally $VV^T=I$.

**Step 2: operator norm.** For every $x$, $|UAVx|=|AVx|$ because $U$ preserves lengths. Moreover $V$ maps the unit sphere onto itself bijectively, so
$$\|UAV\|=\sup_{|x|=1}|AVx|=\sup_{|y|=1}|Ay|=\|A\|.$$

**Consequence.** Both norms of $A$ depend only on the singular values of $A$ (since $A=P\Sigma Q^T$ gives $\|A\|=\|\Sigma\|$ and $\|A\|_F=\|\Sigma\|_F$). In particular the distances $\|A-B\|$ are unchanged by $A\mapsto UAV$: the maps $A\mapsto UAV$ are isometries of $(M_n,\|\cdot\|)$.

**Remark.** These norms are not invariant under general conjugation $A\mapsto PAP^{-1}$ with $P\in GL_n$ (for instance $\|\cdot\|$ of a nilpotent matrix can be made arbitrarily large by conjugating with $\operatorname{diag}(1,t)$), only under *orthogonal* conjugation.
@@@ mn-011-symmetric-matrices.md
---
title: Operator norm of a symmetric matrix
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: symmetric matrices, spectral theorem, operator norm
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that for a symmetric matrix $A$, $\|A\|=\max_i|\lambda_i|$, where $\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n$ are its eigenvalues.

## Hint

Apply the spectral theorem: $A=Q\Lambda Q^T$ with $Q$ orthogonal and $\Lambda$ diagonal.

Then use the invariance of the operator norm under orthogonal multiplication, and compute the norm of a diagonal matrix.

## Solution

**Idea.** A symmetric matrix is a diagonal matrix in a well-chosen orthonormal basis, and orthonormal changes of basis do not change the operator norm.

**Step 1: reduction.** By the spectral theorem, $A=Q\Lambda Q^T$ with $Q\in O(n)$ and $\Lambda=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n)$. By the previous problem, $\|A\|=\|Q\Lambda Q^T\|=\|\Lambda\|$.

**Step 2: diagonal matrices.** For $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$,
$$|\Lambda x|^2=\sum_i\lambda_i^2x_i^2\le\max_i\lambda_i^2\sum_ix_i^2=\max_i\lambda_i^2\,|x|^2,$$
with equality for $x=e_{i_0}$, where $i_0$ attains the max. So $\|\Lambda\|=\max_i|\lambda_i|$.

**Conclusion.** $\|A\|=\max_i|\lambda_i|=\rho(A)$.

**Remark.** This is false for non-symmetric matrices: $\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ has $\rho=0$ but $\|\cdot\|=1$. The equality $\|A\|=\rho(A)$ holds more generally for *normal* matrices (those with $AA^T=A^TA$). Note also that $\max|\lambda_i|$ may be attained at a *negative* eigenvalue, e.g. $\|\operatorname{diag}(1,-3)\|=3$.
@@@ mn-012-diameter-of.md
---
title: The diameter of the orthogonal group
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: orthogonal group, diameter, triangle inequality
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the diameter of $O(n)$ is $2$ for the operator norm and $2\sqrt n$ for the Frobenius norm, i.e. $\sup_{U,V\in O(n)}\|U-V\|=2$ and $\sup_{U,V\in O(n)}\|U-V\|_F=2\sqrt n$.

## Hint

Use the triangle inequality for the upper bound, and compare $I$ with $-I$ for the lower bound.

Remember that $\|U\|=1$ and $\|U\|_F=\sqrt n$ for every $U\in O(n)$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Two points of a "sphere" of radius $r$ in a normed space are at distance at most $2r$, with equality for antipodal points. $O(n)$ lies on the operator-norm sphere of radius $1$ and on the Frobenius sphere of radius $\sqrt n$, and contains the antipodal pair $\pm I$.
