**Step 1: norms of orthogonal matrices.** For $U\in O(n)$, $|Ux|=|x|$ for all $x$, so $\|U\|=1$. And $\|U\|_F^2=\operatorname{tr}(U^TU)=\operatorname{tr}I=n$.

**Step 2: upper bounds.** For $U,V\in O(n)$,
$$\|U-V\|\le\|U\|+\|V\|=2,\qquad\|U-V\|_F\le\|U\|_F+\|V\|_F=2\sqrt n.$$

**Step 3: attainment.** $-I$ is orthogonal since $(-I)^T(-I)=I$. Then $\|I-(-I)\|=\|2I\|=2$ and $\|I-(-I)\|_F=2\|I\|_F=2\sqrt n$.

**Conclusion.** The two diameters are exactly $2$ and $2\sqrt n$.

**Remark.** For $n=1$, $O(1)=\{\pm1\}$ and the diameter is $2$. Note that $-I$ lies in $SO(n)$ iff $n$ is even. For odd $n\ge3$ the operator-norm diameter of $SO(n)$ is still $2$: the matrices $I$ and $D=\operatorname{diag}(-1,-1,1,\dots,1)\in SO(n)$ satisfy $\|I-D\|=\|\operatorname{diag}(2,2,0,\dots,0)\|=2$, and the upper bound $2$ is the same as above. (For the Frobenius norm the value $2\sqrt n$ is no longer attained when $n$ is odd; we do not need this.)
@@@ mn-013-spectral-norm.md
---
title: The operator norm via the largest eigenvalue of Aáµ€A
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: spectral norm, singular values, Rayleigh quotient
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\|A\|^2$ is the largest eigenvalue of $A^TA$, and that $\|A\|_F^2=\sum_i\sigma_i^2$, where $\sigma_1\ge\dots\ge\sigma_n\ge0$ are the singular values of $A$ (the square roots of the eigenvalues of $A^TA$).

## Hint

Write $|Ax|^2=x^T(A^TA)x$ and note that $A^TA$ is symmetric positive semi-definite.

Diagonalise $A^TA$ in an orthonormal basis to compute the maximum of $x^TA^TAx$ over unit vectors.

## Solution

**Idea.** The quantity $|Ax|^2$ is a quadratic form in $x$ with matrix $S=A^TA$; the maximum of a quadratic form on the unit sphere is the largest eigenvalue of its (symmetric) matrix.

**Step 1: $S=A^TA$ is symmetric PSD.** $S^T=A^T(A^T)^T=S$, and $x^TSx=|Ax|^2\ge0$. So the spectral theorem gives an orthonormal eigenbasis $u_1,\dots,u_n$ with eigenvalues $\mu_1\ge\dots\ge\mu_n\ge0$ (non-negative since $\mu_i=u_i^TSu_i=|Au_i|^2$).

**Step 2: maximum of the quadratic form.** Write $x=\sum c_iu_i$ with $\sum c_i^2=|x|^2=1$. Then
$$|Ax|^2=x^TSx=\sum_i\mu_ic_i^2\le\mu_1\sum_ic_i^2=\mu_1,$$
with equality for $x=u_1$. Hence $\|A\|^2=\sup_{|x|=1}|Ax|^2=\mu_1$, so $\|A\|=\sigma_1=\sqrt{\mu_1}$.

**Step 3: Frobenius norm.** $\|A\|_F^2=\operatorname{tr}(A^TA)=\operatorname{tr}(S)$ and the trace of a symmetric matrix is the sum of its eigenvalues. So $\|A\|_F^2=\sum_i\mu_i=\sum_i\sigma_i^2$.

**Remark.** Consequently $\|A\|=\sigma_1\le\big(\sum\sigma_i^2\big)^{1/2}=\|A\|_F\le\sqrt n\,\sigma_1=\sqrt n\|A\|$, which reproves the comparison of the two norms, with equality cases visible: $\|A\|=\|A\|_F$ iff only one singular value is non-zero (rank $\le1$), and $\|A\|_F=\sqrt n\|A\|$ iff all singular values are equal (multiples of orthogonal matrices).
@@@ mn-014-computation.md
---
title: The operator norm of a shear matrix
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: operator norm, golden ratio, singular values
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Compute the operator norm of $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$.

## Hint

By the previous problem, $\|A\|^2$ is the largest eigenvalue of $A^TA$.

The characteristic polynomial of a $2\times2$ matrix is $t^2-(\operatorname{tr})t+\det$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Compute $A^TA$, find its largest eigenvalue, and take a square root; then simplify the nested radical.

**Step 1: the matrix $A^TA$.**
$$A^TA=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}.$$

**Step 2: eigenvalues.** Its trace is $3$ and its determinant is $1$, so the characteristic polynomial is $t^2-3t+1$, with roots $\mu=\dfrac{3\pm\sqrt5}2$. The largest is $\mu_1=\dfrac{3+\sqrt5}2$.

**Step 3: simplify the square root.** Since
$$\left(\frac{1+\sqrt5}2\right)^2=\frac{1+2\sqrt5+5}4=\frac{3+\sqrt5}2,$$
we get $\|A\|=\sqrt{\mu_1}=\dfrac{1+\sqrt5}2\approx1.618$, the golden ratio.

**Check.** The singular values are $\varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt5}2$ and $\varphi^{-1}=\frac{\sqrt5-1}2$, with product $1=|\det A|$, and $\sigma_1^2+\sigma_2^2=3=\|A\|_F^2$ âœ“. A direct numerical computation of the largest value of $|Ax|$ on the unit circle also gives $1.618\ldots$

**Remark.** The shear has $\rho(A)=1$ (both eigenvalues $1$) but $\|A\|=\varphi>1$; and $\|A^k\|$ grows linearly in $k$ (the matrix $A^k=\begin{pmatrix}1&k\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$), although $\|A^k\|^{1/k}\to1=\rho(A)$.
@@@ mn-015-transpose.md
---
title: The operator norm is invariant under transposition
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: transpose, operator norm, duality
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\|A^T\|=\|A\|$ for every $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Show that $\|A\|=\sup_{|x|=|y|=1}y^TAx$.

Then transpose the scalar $y^TAx$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Express the norm as a supremum of a *bilinear* expression in which $x$ and $y$ play symmetric roles, then notice that transposing $A$ simply exchanges them.

**Step 1: the bilinear formula.** For a vector $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$, $|v|=\sup_{|y|=1}y^Tv$: indeed $y^Tv\le|y||v|=|v|$ by Cauchyâ€“Schwarz, and equality holds for $y=v/|v|$ (any $y$ if $v=0$). Applying this to $v=Ax$,
$$\|A\|=\sup_{|x|=1}|Ax|=\sup_{|x|=1}\sup_{|y|=1}y^TAx=\sup_{|x|=|y|=1}y^TAx.$$

**Step 2: transposing.** The number $y^TAx$ is a scalar, so it equals its own transpose: $y^TAx=(y^TAx)^T=x^TA^Ty$. Hence
$$\|A\|=\sup_{|x|=|y|=1}x^TA^Ty=\sup_{|y|=|x|=1}x^T A^Ty=\|A^T\|,$$
where in the last step we applied the formula of Step 1 to $A^T$ (with the roles of $x$ and $y$ exchanged).

**Remark.** An alternative proof: $\|A^T\|^2=\lambda_{\max}(AA^T)$ and $AA^T$, $A^TA$ have the same non-zero eigenvalues (equal to $\sigma_i^2$), so $\|A^T\|=\sigma_1=\|A\|$. The same equality *fails* for the induced norms $\|\cdot\|_1,\|\cdot\|_\infty$: there $\|A^T\|_1=\|A\|_\infty$, which differs in general from $\|A\|_1$.
@@@ mn-016-spectral-radius.md
---
title: Eigenvalues are bounded by the operator norm
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: spectral radius, complex eigenvalue, operator norm
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $|\lambda|\le\|A\|$ for every complex eigenvalue $\lambda$ of a real matrix $A$. In other words, $\rho(A)\le\|A\|$.

## Hint

If $\lambda$ is non-real, take a complex eigenvector $v=x+iy$ with $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^n$.

Because $A$ is real, $Av=Ax+iAy$ with $Ax,Ay$ real; compute $|Av|^2$.

## Solution

**Idea.** For a real eigenvalue the claim is immediate from $|Ax|\le\|A\||x|$. For a non-real one, we cannot apply the definition of $\|A\|$ (a supremum over *real* vectors) directly, so we split the complex eigenvector into its real and imaginary parts.

**Step 1: real eigenvalues.** If $Ax=\lambda x$ with $x\in\mathbb{R}^n\setminus\{0\}$, then $|\lambda||x|=|Ax|\le\|A\||x|$, so $|\lambda|\le\|A\|$.

**Step 2: general case.** Let $v=x+iy\ne0$ with $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^n$ and $Av=\lambda v$. The Hermitian norm is $|v|^2=|x|^2+|y|^2$. Since $A$ is real, $Av=Ax+iAy$ with $Ax,Ay\in\mathbb{R}^n$, so
$$|\lambda|^2(|x|^2+|y|^2)=|Av|^2=|Ax|^2+|Ay|^2\le\|A\|^2|x|^2+\|A\|^2|y|^2=\|A\|^2(|x|^2+|y|^2).$$
Dividing by $|x|^2+|y|^2>0$ gives $|\lambda|\le\|A\|$.

**Example.** For the rotation $R_\theta$, the eigenvalues are $e^{\pm i\theta}$ with $|\lambda|=1=\|R_\theta\|$: the bound is sharp. For $A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$, $\rho=0<1=\|A\|$.

**Remark.** The same proof works for every induced norm provided it is extended to complex vectors; the Euclidean case is the easiest because the Hermitian norm splits as above. This inequality is the basic step towards the Gelfand bound $\rho(A)\le\|A^k\|^{1/k}$.
@@@ mn-017-neumann-series.md
---
title: The Neumann series
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: Neumann series, invertibility, geometric series, Banach algebra
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

If $\|A\|<1$ (operator norm), show that $I-A$ is invertible, that $(I-A)^{-1}=\sum_{k\ge0}A^k$, and that $\|(I-A)^{-1}\|\le\dfrac1{1-\|A\|}$.

## Hint

Use the geometric series, with the submultiplicativity $\|A^k\|\le\|A\|^k$ to prove convergence.

Telescope: compute $(I-A)(I+A+\dots+A^N)$.

## Solution

**Idea.** This is the geometric series $\frac1{1-a}=\sum a^k$ for $|a|<1$, transported to matrices; the matrix norm plays the role of $|a|$.

**Step 1: absolute convergence.** By submultiplicativity, $\|A^k\|\le\|A\|^k$ so $\sum_k\|A^k\|\le\sum_k\|A\|^k=\frac1{1-\|A\|}<\infty$. Since $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is complete, an absolutely convergent series converges: let $S=\sum_{k\ge0}A^k$.

**Step 2: $S$ is the inverse.** Let $S_N=\sum_{k=0}^NA^k$. Then
$$(I-A)S_N=S_N(I-A)=I-A^{N+1}.$$
As $N\to\infty$, $\|A^{N+1}\|\le\|A\|^{N+1}\to0$, and $S_N\to S$. Multiplication is continuous, so $(I-A)S=S(I-A)=I$. Hence $I-A$ is invertible with inverse $S$.

**Step 3: the bound.** By the triangle inequality for series,
$$\|S\|\le\sum_{k\ge0}\|A^k\|\le\sum_{k\ge0}\|A\|^k=\frac1{1-\|A\|}.$$

**Example.** For $A=\tfrac12\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$, $A^2=0$, so $(I-A)^{-1}=I+A=\begin{pmatrix}1&1/2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ and indeed $\|I+A\|\approx1.28\le\frac1{1-1/2}=2$.

**Remark.** The result uses only that the norm is submultiplicative and complete (a *Banach algebra*), so it is valid for any induced norm and for the Frobenius norm. It also shows that the open unit ball around $I$ is contained in $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$; the next problem generalises this to a ball around any invertible matrix.
@@@ mn-018-inverse-stability.md
---
title: Invertibility is stable under small perturbations
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: invertible matrices, perturbation, Neumann series, open set
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be invertible and $\|B-A\|<1/\|A^{-1}\|$. Show that $B$ is invertible. Deduce that $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is open and contains the open ball of radius $1/\|A^{-1}\|$ around each of its elements $A$.

## Hint

Factor $B=A\big(I-A^{-1}(A-B)\big)$.

Apply the Neumann series to $A^{-1}(A-B)$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Factor out the invertible matrix $A$, so that what remains is a small perturbation of the identity, for which we have the Neumann series.

**Step 1: the factorisation.** Let $E=A^{-1}(A-B)$. Then
$$A(I-E)=A-AA^{-1}(A-B)=A-(A-B)=B.$$

**Step 2: $E$ is small.** By submultiplicativity,
$$\|E\|\le\|A^{-1}\|\|A-B\|<\|A^{-1}\|\cdot\frac1{\|A^{-1}\|}=1.$$

**Step 3: conclusion.** By the Neumann series, $I-E$ is invertible. Hence $B=A(I-E)$ is a product of two invertible matrices, so it is invertible, with $B^{-1}=(I-E)^{-1}A^{-1}$.

**Consequence.** Every $A\in GL_n$ has a ball $B(A,1/\|A^{-1}\|)\subset GL_n$, so $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is open. Moreover this gives the quantitative bound $\|B^{-1}\|\le\dfrac{\|A^{-1}\|}{1-\|A^{-1}\|\|A-B\|}$.

**Example.** $A=\operatorname{diag}(1,\varepsilon)$ has $1/\|A^{-1}\|=\varepsilon$ and $B=\operatorname{diag}(1,0)$ is at distance $\varepsilon$ from $A$ and singular: the radius $1/\|A^{-1}\|$ cannot be improved (see the next problems).

**Remark.** This is the first of several ways to see that $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is open; another is that $\det$ is continuous and $GL_n=\det^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})$. The Neumann argument is more quantitative and works in any Banach algebra, including infinite-dimensional ones, where $\det$ is not available.
@@@ mn-019-trace-bound.md
---
title: The trace is bounded by the Frobenius norm
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: trace, Cauchyâ€“Schwarz, Frobenius norm
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $|\operatorname{tr}A|\le\sqrt n\,\|A\|_F$ for every $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$. When is there equality?

## Hint

The trace is the sum of the $n$ diagonal entries: apply Cauchyâ€“Schwarz in $\mathbb{R}^n$.

Alternatively, write $\operatorname{tr}A=\langle I,A\rangle_F$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The trace only sees the $n$ diagonal entries, while the Frobenius norm sees all $n^2$ entries; Cauchyâ€“Schwarz compares the sum of $n$ numbers with the length of the vector they form.

**Step 1: Cauchyâ€“Schwarz.** Using $1\cdot a_{ii}$ in $\mathbb{R}^n$,
$$|\operatorname{tr}A|=\Big|\sum_{i=1}^n1\cdot a_{ii}\Big|\le\Big(\sum_i1^2\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\sum_ia_{ii}^2\Big)^{1/2}=\sqrt n\Big(\sum_ia_{ii}^2\Big)^{1/2}.$$

**Step 2: add the off-diagonal terms.** Since $\sum_ia_{ii}^2\le\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}^2=\|A\|_F^2$, we get $|\operatorname{tr}A|\le\sqrt n\|A\|_F$.

**Equality.** Both steps must be equalities: the off-diagonal entries vanish and the diagonal vector $(a_{ii})$ is proportional to $(1,\dots,1)$. So equality holds iff $A=cI$ is a scalar matrix.

**Second proof.** $\operatorname{tr}A=\langle I,A\rangle_F$, and by Cauchyâ€“Schwarz in $(M_n,\langle,\rangle_F)$, $|\langle I,A\rangle_F|\le\|I\|_F\|A\|_F=\sqrt n\|A\|_F$.

**Remark.** The trace is a linear functional, hence continuous, and this computation gives its norm: $\|\operatorname{tr}\|=\sqrt n$ with respect to $\|\cdot\|_F$ (attained at $A=I$). With respect to the operator norm, $|\operatorname{tr}A|\le n\|A\|$ (also attained at $I$).
@@@ mn-020-schur-test.md
---
title: The Schur test: â€–Aâ€–Â² â‰¤ â€–Aâ€–â‚â€–Aâ€–âˆž
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: Schur test, spectral radius, induced norms
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Prove that $\|A\|^2\le\|A\|_1\|A\|_\infty$ for every $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$, where $\|\cdot\|$ is the Euclidean operator norm, $\|A\|_1=\max_j\sum_i|a_{ij}|$ and $\|A\|_\infty=\max_i\sum_j|a_{ij}|$.

## Hint

Use $\|A\|^2=\rho(A^TA)$ and that the spectral radius is at most any induced norm.

Note that $\|A^T\|_1=\|A\|_\infty$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The spectral radius of the symmetric matrix $A^TA$ equals $\|A\|^2$; bounding it by the *easily computable* norm $\|\cdot\|_1$ and then using submultiplicativity yields a bound in terms of row and column sums.

**Step 1: $\|A\|^2=\rho(A^TA)$.** By the problem on the spectral norm, $\|A\|^2=\lambda_{\max}(A^TA)$, and $A^TA$ is symmetric PSD so $\lambda_{\max}=\rho$.

**Step 2: spectral radius versus an induced norm.** $\|\cdot\|_1$ is the operator norm induced by the $\ell^1$-norm of $\mathbb{R}^n$, so it is submultiplicative, and the argument of the problem "Eigenvalues are bounded by the operator norm" (applied to a real eigenvector, since $A^TA$ is symmetric and has a real eigenvector $x$ for its largest eigenvalue $\mu$) gives $\mu|x|_1=|A^TAx|_1\le\|A^TA\|_1|x|_1$. So $\rho(A^TA)\le\|A^TA\|_1$.

**Step 3: submultiplicativity.**
$$\|A^TA\|_1\le\|A^T\|_1\|A\|_1=\|A\|_\infty\|A\|_1,$$
because the maximum column sum of $A^T$ is the maximum row sum of $A$.

**Conclusion.** $\|A\|^2\le\|A\|_1\|A\|_\infty$.

**Example.** For $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&-2\\3&4\end{pmatrix}$: $\|A\|^2\approx26.2$, while $\|A\|_1\|A\|_\infty=6\cdot7=42$ âœ“. For a symmetric matrix $\|A\|_1=\|A\|_\infty$ and the inequality becomes $\|A\|\le\|A\|_1$.

**Remark.** This is the discrete form of the Schur test for boundedness of integral operators on $L^2$. It gives a cheap upper bound for $\|A\|$ with no eigenvalue computation. It can be quite far from sharp, e.g. for the all-ones matrix $J$ it gives $\|J\|^2\le n^2$ and in fact $\|J\|=n$ (sharp), but for a matrix with one large entry and many small ones it overestimates.
@@@ mn-021-convexity.md
---
title: Strict convexity of the Frobenius ball but not of the operator ball
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: strict convexity, unit ball, parallelogram law, operator norm
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge2$. Show that the unit sphere of the operator norm contains a non-trivial line segment (so the unit ball is not strictly convex), whereas the Frobenius unit sphere contains none.

## Hint

For the operator norm, look for two non-equal matrices of norm $1$ whose segment stays in the sphere; try $I$ and a diagonal matrix.

For the Frobenius norm use the parallelogram law.

## Solution

**Idea.** A ball is strictly convex if the midpoint of two distinct points of the sphere lies strictly inside the ball. Euclidean balls satisfy this; the operator-norm ball looks like a "cube" in the diagonal directions and has flat faces.

**Step 1: the operator norm.** Let $D=\operatorname{diag}(1,0,\dots,0)$, so $D\ne I$ as $n\ge2$. For $t\in[0,1]$,
$$tI+(1-t)D=\operatorname{diag}(1,t,\dots,t),$$
whose operator norm is $\max(1,t)=1$ (norm of a diagonal matrix = largest absolute value of an entry). So the whole segment $[D,I]$ lies on the sphere $\{\|A\|=1\}$ and has positive length $\|I-D\|=1$.

**Step 2: the Frobenius norm.** Let $X\ne Y$ with $\|X\|_F=\|Y\|_F=1$. By the parallelogram law, which holds because $\|\cdot\|_F$ comes from an inner product,
$$\Big\|\frac{X+Y}2\Big\|_F^2=\frac{\|X\|_F^2+\|Y\|_F^2}2-\Big\|\frac{X-Y}2\Big\|_F^2=1-\frac{\|X-Y\|_F^2}4<1.$$
So the midpoint lies strictly inside the ball; no segment of positive length lies in the sphere (its midpoint would be on it).

**Remark.** For $n=1$ both norms coincide with $|a|$ and the unit ball is $[-1,1]$, whose sphere $\{\pm1\}$ contains no segment, which is why $n\ge2$ is needed. The lack of strict convexity of the operator ball is linked to its extreme points being just $O(n)$ (see "Extreme points"): the flat faces consist of non-extreme points.
@@@ mn-022-rank-metric.md
---
title: The rank metric
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: rank, discrete topology, metric, continuity
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $d(A,B)=\operatorname{rank}(A-B)$ is a metric on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and that its topology is discrete. Is the identity map $(M_n,d)\to(M_n,\|\cdot\|)$ continuous? And its inverse?

## Hint

Use the subadditivity $\operatorname{rank}(X+Y)\le\operatorname{rank}X+\operatorname{rank}Y$.

For the continuity questions, find a sequence converging for the norm but not for $d$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Rank takes only integer values, so a distance defined by the rank has a gap: distinct points are at distance at least $1$.

**Step 1: $d$ is a metric.**
- $d(A,B)=\operatorname{rank}(A-B)=0$ iff $A-B=0$ iff $A=B$.
- Symmetry: $\operatorname{rank}(-X)=\operatorname{rank}X$.
- Triangle inequality: $A-C=(A-B)+(B-C)$ and the column space of a sum is contained in the sum of the column spaces, so $\operatorname{rank}(A-C)\le\operatorname{rank}(A-B)+\operatorname{rank}(B-C)$.

**Step 2: discrete.** If $A\ne B$ then $d(A,B)\ge1$. Hence the open ball of radius $\frac12$ around $A$ is $\{A\}$: every singleton is open, which is the discrete topology.

**Step 3: identity to the norm topology.** From a discrete space every map is continuous. So $\mathrm{id}:(M_n,d)\to(M_n,\|\cdot\|)$ is continuous.

**Step 4: the inverse.** The sequence $A_k=\frac1kE_{11}$ tends to $0$ in the operator norm ($\|A_k\|=\frac1k$). But $d(A_k,0)=\operatorname{rank}(A_k)=1$ does not tend to $0$, so $A_k\not\to0$ in $(M_n,d)$. Thus $\mathrm{id}:(M_n,\|\cdot\|)\to(M_n,d)$ is not continuous.

**Remark.** The rank is only *lower* semi-continuous for the norm topology: the set $\{\operatorname{rank}\ge r\}$ is open. This is why rank "can only jump down" in the limit, as in $A_k\to0$. The discrete topology is strictly finer than the norm topology, which is why only one of the two identity maps is continuous.
@@@ mn-023-linear-functionals.md
---
title: Linear functionals on M_n(R) are traces against a matrix
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: linear functionals, Riesz representation, continuity
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that every linear functional $\varphi:M_n(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}$ is of the form $\varphi(A)=\operatorname{tr}(B^TA)$ for a unique $B\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$, and that every such functional is continuous.

## Hint

Use the Frobenius inner product $\langle B,A\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(B^TA)$.

Or directly: expand $A=\sum a_{ij}E_{ij}$ in the basis of matrix units.

## Solution

**Idea.** In a finite-dimensional inner product space, every linear functional is "taking the inner product with a fixed vector" (Riesz representation). Here the inner product is $\operatorname{tr}(B^TA)$.

**Step 1: existence, by hand.** Write $A=\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}E_{ij}$ where $E_{ij}$ is the matrix with a $1$ in position $(i,j)$ and zeros elsewhere. By linearity,
$$\varphi(A)=\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}\varphi(E_{ij})=\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}b_{ij}=\operatorname{tr}(B^TA),\qquad b_{ij}:=\varphi(E_{ij}).$$

**Step 2: uniqueness.** If $\operatorname{tr}(B^TA)=\operatorname{tr}(B'^TA)$ for all $A$, then $\langle B-B',A\rangle=0$ for all $A$, in particular for $A=B-B'$, which gives $\|B-B'\|_F^2=0$.

**Step 3: continuity.** By Cauchyâ€“Schwarz,
$$|\varphi(A)|=|\langle B,A\rangle_F|\le\|B\|_F\|A\|_F,$$
so $\varphi$ is Lipschitz with constant $\|B\|_F$, hence continuous (for $\|\cdot\|_F$, and therefore for every norm by equivalence of norms).

**Examples.** $\operatorname{tr}$ corresponds to $B=I$; the $(i,j)$ entry corresponds to $B=E_{ij}$; $A\mapsto x^TAy$ corresponds to $B=xy^T$.

**Remark.** The same argument shows that every linear map between finite-dimensional normed spaces is continuous. The kernel of a non-zero functional is a hyperplane, which is therefore closed (and has empty interior), a fact we use in the topology of subspaces.
@@@ mn-024-banach-algebra.md
---
title: Induced operator norms make M_n(R) a Banach algebra
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: Banach algebra, induced norm, equivalent norms
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $|\cdot|'$ be any norm on $\mathbb{R}^n$ and define, for $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$, $\|A\|'=\sup_{|x|'=1}|Ax|'$. Show that:
1. $\|\cdot\|'$ is a norm on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ which is submultiplicative, $\|AB\|'\le\|A\|'\|B\|'$, and $\|I\|'=1$;
2. $(M_n(\mathbb{R}),\|\cdot\|')$ is complete, i.e. a Banach algebra;
3. $\|\cdot\|'$ is equivalent to the Euclidean operator norm $\|\cdot\|$.

## Hint

For parts 1 and 2, repeat the arguments for the Euclidean case; use that all norms on $\mathbb{R}^n$ (and on $M_n$) are equivalent.

For part 3, compare $|x|'$ with $|x|$ using constants $c,C$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The proofs for the Euclidean operator norm used only the definition as a supremum; they carry over verbatim. Equivalence of all norms in finite dimension gives finiteness and the comparison.

**Step 0: finiteness.** By equivalence of norms on $\mathbb{R}^n$, there are $0<c\le C$ with $c|x|\le|x|'\le C|x|$ for all $x$. Then $|Ax|'\le C|Ax|\le C\|A\||x|\le\frac Cc\|A\||x|'$, so $\|A\|'\le\frac Cc\|A\|<\infty$.

**Step 1: norm axioms.**
- Homogeneity and triangle inequality: the supremum of the seminorms $A\mapsto|Ax|'$ is a seminorm.
- Definiteness: if $\|A\|'=0$ then $Ax=0$ for all $x$, so $A=0$.
- $\|I\|'=\sup|x|'=1$.
- Submultiplicativity: as for the Euclidean case, $|ABx|'\le\|A\|'|Bx|'\le\|A\|'\|B\|'|x|'$.

**Step 2: comparison and completeness.** By Step 0, $\|A\|'\le\frac Cc\|A\|$. Conversely, $|Ax|\le\frac1c|Ax|'\le\frac1c\|A\|'|x|'\le\frac Cc\|A\|'|x|$, so $\|A\|\le\frac Cc\|A\|'$. This is part 3. Since $\|\cdot\|'$ is equivalent to $\|\cdot\|$ and $M_n$ is complete for $\|\cdot\|$, it is complete for $\|\cdot\|'$ (part 2).

**Examples.** $\|\cdot\|_1$ (max column sum) and $\|\cdot\|_\infty$ (max row sum) are induced by the $\ell^1$ and $\ell^\infty$ norms. The Frobenius norm is *not* an induced norm for $n\ge2$ since $\|I\|_F=\sqrt n\ne1$, though it is submultiplicative.

**Remark.** Part 3 says that every norm on $\mathbb{R}^n$ gives an operator norm equivalent to the Euclidean one. The same proof shows that in a Banach algebra the Neumann series and the openness of the group of invertible elements hold for any induced norm.
@@@ mn-025-equivalence-of-norms.md
---
title: All norms on M_n(R) are equivalent
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: equivalence of norms, compactness, finite dimension
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Prove that any two norms on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ are equivalent: if $\|\cdot\|'$ is a norm, there exist $0<c\le C$ with $c\|A\|_F\le\|A\|'\le C\|A\|_F$ for all $A$.

## Hint

Show first that $\|\cdot\|'$ is bounded above by a multiple of $\|\cdot\|_F$, hence continuous for $\|\cdot\|_F$.

Then use compactness of the Frobenius unit sphere.

## Solution

**Idea.** The Frobenius unit sphere is compact (closed and bounded in $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$) and any norm is continuous and positive on it, so it has a positive minimum and a finite maximum. Homogeneity extends the bounds to all matrices.

**Step 1: upper bound.** Write $A=\sum a_{ij}E_{ij}$. By the triangle inequality and Cauchyâ€“Schwarz,
$$\|A\|'\le\sum_{i,j}|a_{ij}|\|E_{ij}\|'\le\Big(\sum_{i,j}\|E_{ij}\|'^2\Big)^{1/2}\|A\|_F=:C\|A\|_F.$$

**Step 2: continuity.** By the reverse triangle inequality, $\big|\|A\|'-\|B\|'\big|\le\|A-B\|'\le C\|A-B\|_F$: the function $\|\cdot\|'$ is continuous for the Frobenius topology.

**Step 3: lower bound.** The Frobenius unit sphere $S=\{\|A\|_F=1\}$ is compact. The continuous function $\|\cdot\|'$ attains its minimum $c$ on $S$ at some $A_0\in S$; and $c=\|A_0\|'>0$ since $A_0\ne0$.

**Step 4: homogeneity.** For $A\ne0$, $A/\|A\|_F\in S$, hence $c\le\|A/\|A\|_F\|'=\|A\|'/\|A\|_F$, i.e. $c\|A\|_F\le\|A\|'$. Together with Step 1 we get $c\|A\|_F\le\|A\|'\le C\|A\|_F$, and $c\le C$ is automatic.

**Remark.** The compactness of the unit sphere is where finite dimension enters; in infinite dimension the unit sphere is not compact and the statement fails. The result implies that all the topological notions (open, closed, compact, convergence, continuity of maps between $M_n$'s) do not depend on the chosen norm, which is why we freely use whichever norm is most convenient.
@@@ mn-026-distance-to-singular.md
---
title: Distance from an invertible matrix to the singular matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: distance to singular matrices, singular values, Eckartâ€“Young, SVD
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be invertible. Prove that the distance from $A$ to the set of singular matrices equals $1/\|A^{-1}\|=\sigma_{\min}(A)$, both for the operator norm and for the Frobenius norm.

## Hint

The lower bound is the stability problem (invertibility of nearby matrices).

For the upper bound, use the singular value decomposition and delete the smallest singular value.

## Solution

**Idea.** The matrix closest to $A$ among the singular ones is obtained by "killing" the weakest direction of $A$, namely the one with the smallest singular value.

**Step 1: $\sigma_{\min}=1/\|A^{-1}\|$.** If $A=P\Sigma Q^T$ is an SVD ($P,Q$ orthogonal, $\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_1\ge\dots\ge\sigma_n>0)$), then $A^{-1}=Q\Sigma^{-1}P^T$ has operator norm $\max_i\sigma_i^{-1}=1/\sigma_n$ by the orthogonal invariance of the norm.

**Step 2: lower bound.** Let $B$ be singular. If $\|A-B\|<1/\|A^{-1}\|$, the stability problem would make $B$ invertible. Hence $\|A-B\|\ge1/\|A^{-1}\|=\sigma_n$. Since $\|X\|_F\ge\|X\|$ we also have $\|A-B\|_F\ge\sigma_n$.

**Step 3: attainment.** Write $A=\sum_i\sigma_iu_iv_i^T$ with $u_i,v_i$ the columns of $P,Q$, and set
$$B=A-\sigma_nu_nv_n^T=\sum_{i<n}\sigma_iu_iv_i^T.$$
Then $B$ has rank $n-1$, so it is singular, and $A-B=\sigma_nu_nv_n^T$ is rank one with
$$\|A-B\|=\|A-B\|_F=\sigma_n\,|u_n||v_n|=\sigma_n.$$
(For a rank-one matrix $uv^T$: $\|uv^T\|_F^2=\sum_{i,j}u_i^2v_j^2=|u|^2|v|^2$, and $|uv^Tx|=|u||v^Tx|\le|u||v||x|$ with equality at $x=v/|v|$, so both norms equal $|u||v|$.)

**Conclusion.** The distance is exactly $\sigma_n=1/\|A^{-1}\|$ in both norms.

**Example.** For $A=\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&\tfrac12\end{pmatrix}$, $1/\|A^{-1}\|=\frac12$ and $B=\operatorname{diag}(2,0)$ is the nearest singular matrix. In particular a matrix can have a large norm ($\|A\|=2$) and be extremely close to a singular matrix.

**Remark.** This is a special case of the Eckartâ€“Young theorem: the best rank-$r$ approximation of $A$ (in operator or Frobenius norm) is obtained by truncating the SVD. The quantity $\kappa(A)=\|A\|\|A^{-1}\|$ measures, relative to $\|A\|$, how close $A$ is to being singular.
@@@ mn-027-blow-up.md
---
title: The inverse blows up near singular matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: inverse, blow-up, distance to singular matrices
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that if $A\to A_0$ with $A_0$ singular and $A$ invertible, then $\|A^{-1}\|\to\infty$.

## Hint

Use the formula $\|A^{-1}\|=1/\operatorname{dist}(A,\{\text{singular matrices}\})$ from the previous problem.

## Solution

**Idea.** A matrix very close to a singular one is very close to the singular set, and the inverse's norm is the reciprocal of that distance.

**Step 1: the distance is small.** Since $A_0$ is singular, $\operatorname{dist}(A,\text{singular})\le\|A-A_0\|$.

**Step 2: apply the formula.** By the previous problem,
$$\|A^{-1}\|=\frac1{\operatorname{dist}(A,\text{singular})}\ge\frac1{\|A-A_0\|}\xrightarrow[A\to A_0]{}+\infty.$$

**Consequence.** Inversion $A\mapsto A^{-1}$ is continuous on $GL_n$ but it cannot be extended continuously to any singular matrix, and it is unbounded near the boundary of $GL_n$. In particular $\{A\in GL_n:\|A^{-1}\|\le M\}$ is closed in $M_n$ (not just in $GL_n$): if $A_k$ lie in it and $A_k\to A_0$, then $\operatorname{dist}(A_k,\text{singular})\ge1/M$ and the distance function is $1$-Lipschitz, so $\operatorname{dist}(A_0,\text{singular})\ge1/M>0$; thus $A_0$ is invertible and $\|A_0^{-1}\|\le M$.

**Example.** $A_\varepsilon=\operatorname{diag}(1,\varepsilon)\to A_0=\operatorname{diag}(1,0)$ and $\|A_\varepsilon^{-1}\|=1/\varepsilon$, which matches the bound $\ge1/\|A_\varepsilon-A_0\|=1/\varepsilon$ exactly.

**Remark.** The numerical consequence is the ill-conditioning of linear systems $Ax=b$ for nearly singular $A$: a tiny perturbation of the data produces a perturbation of the solution of size up to $\|A^{-1}\|$ times larger.
@@@ mn-028-hadamard.md
---
title: Hadamard's inequality for determinants
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Hadamard inequality, QR decomposition, Gramâ€“Schmidt, determinant
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Prove that $|\det A|\le\prod_{j=1}^n|a_j|$, where $a_1,\dots,a_n\in\mathbb{R}^n$ are the columns of $A$. Also determine when equality holds.

## Hint

If the columns are dependent, $\det A=0$. Otherwise apply Gramâ€“Schmidt to the columns to write $A=QR$.

The diagonal entries of $R$ are distances from $a_j$ to the span of $a_1,\dots,a_{j-1}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The determinant is the signed volume of the parallelepiped spanned by the columns. This volume is at most the product of the edge lengths, with equality for a rectangular box. The proof makes this precise by orthogonalising.

**Step 1: trivial case.** If $a_1,\dots,a_n$ are linearly dependent, then $\det A=0\le\prod|a_j|$.

**Step 2: Gramâ€“Schmidt.** Otherwise define orthonormal vectors $q_1,\dots,q_n$ by Gramâ€“Schmidt: $q_j$ is the normalised component of $a_j$ orthogonal to $\operatorname{span}(a_1,\dots,a_{j-1})$. Then $a_j=\sum_{i\le j}R_{ij}q_i$, i.e. $A=QR$ with $Q=(q_1|\dots|q_n)\in O(n)$ and $R$ upper triangular with
$$R_{jj}=\langle q_j,a_j\rangle,\qquad|R_{jj}|=\operatorname{dist}\big(a_j,\operatorname{span}(a_1,\dots,a_{j-1})\big)>0.$$

**Step 3: bound the diagonal.** The distance from $a_j$ to a subspace is at most the distance to $0$ (which belongs to the subspace), so $|R_{jj}|\le|a_j|$.

**Step 4: determinant.** $\det Q=\pm1$ and the determinant of the triangular matrix $R$ is $\prod R_{jj}$, so
$$|\det A|=\prod_{j=1}^n|R_{jj}|\le\prod_{j=1}^n|a_j|.$$

**Equality case.** If $\det A\ne0$, equality holds iff $|R_{jj}|=|a_j|$ for every $j$, i.e. iff $a_j\perp\operatorname{span}(a_1,\dots,a_{j-1})$ for every $j$: the columns are pairwise orthogonal. If $\det A=0$, equality holds iff some column is zero.

**Example.** For $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$, $\det A=1\le\sqrt1\cdot\sqrt2=1.41$, strict since the columns are not orthogonal.

**Remark.** Applying the inequality to $A^T$ gives the same bound with the rows. It is the key tool in the next two problems (an upper bound on $|\det A|$ in terms of the Frobenius norm, and the Lipschitz estimate for $\det$ on bounded sets).
@@@ mn-029-determinant-bound.md
---
title: The determinant is at most (â€–Aâ€–_F/âˆšn)â¿
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Hadamard inequality, AMâ€“GM, equality case, determinant
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $|\det A|\le\left(\dfrac{\|A\|_F}{\sqrt n}\right)^n$ for every $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$, with equality iff $A$ is a multiple of an orthogonal matrix (including $A=0$).

## Hint

Combine Hadamard's inequality with the AMâ€“GM inequality applied to the numbers $|a_j|^2$.

Study the equality cases of both inequalities.

## Solution

**Idea.** Hadamard bounds the determinant by the product of column lengths; AMâ€“GM then bounds this product by the arithmetic mean of the squared lengths, which is $\|A\|_F^2/n$.

**Step 1: Hadamard.** $|\det A|^2\le\prod_{j=1}^n|a_j|^2$.

**Step 2: AMâ€“GM.** For the non-negative numbers $|a_j|^2$,
$$\prod_{j=1}^n|a_j|^2\le\Big(\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n|a_j|^2\Big)^n=\Big(\frac{\|A\|_F^2}n\Big)^n,$$
since $\sum_j|a_j|^2=\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}^2=\|A\|_F^2$. Taking square roots of the combined inequality gives the claim.

**Equality, if $\det A\ne0$.** We need equality in both steps. Equality in Hadamard means the columns are pairwise orthogonal; equality in AMâ€“GM means all $|a_j|^2$ are equal, say to $s^2>0$. Then $A^TA=s^2I$, so $A/s$ is orthogonal and $A=sU$ with $U\in O(n)$.

**Equality, if $\det A=0$.** The left side is $0$, so the right side must vanish: $\|A\|_F=0$, i.e. $A=0$.

**Converse.** If $A=sU$ then $|\det A|=|s|^n$ and $\|A\|_F=|s|\sqrt n$, so both sides equal $|s|^n$.

**Remark.** Writing the inequality as $|\det A|^{2/n}\le\frac1n\|A\|_F^2=\frac1n\operatorname{tr}(A^TA)$ shows it is the AMâ€“GM inequality for the eigenvalues of the PSD matrix $A^TA$ (whose determinant is $\det(A)^2$ and whose trace is $\|A\|_F^2$): $\det(S)^{1/n}\le\operatorname{tr}(S)/n$.
@@@ mn-030-lipschitz-determinant.md
---
title: The determinant is Lipschitz on bounded sets
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: determinant, Lipschitz, telescoping, Hadamard inequality
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $|\det A-\det B|\le nR^{n-1}\|A-B\|$ whenever $\|A\|\le R$ and $\|B\|\le R$ (operator norm).

## Hint

Replace the columns of $A$ by those of $B$ one at a time, and use the multilinearity of $\det$ in the columns.

Bound each resulting determinant with Hadamard's inequality.

## Solution

**Idea.** Change one column at a time. Each change costs a determinant with one column equal to a difference $a_k-b_k$ and the other columns bounded by $R$; Hadamard bounds it by $R^{n-1}|a_k-b_k|$.

**Step 1: the hybrid matrices.** Let $H_k$ be the matrix with columns $(b_1,\dots,b_k,a_{k+1},\dots,a_n)$, for $k=0,\dots,n$. Then $H_0=A$ and $H_n=B$, so
$$\det A-\det B=\sum_{k=1}^n\big(\det H_{k-1}-\det H_k\big).$$

**Step 2: multilinearity.** $H_{k-1}$ and $H_k$ differ only in column $k$, namely $a_k$ versus $b_k$. By linearity of $\det$ in that column,
$$\det H_{k-1}-\det H_k=\det(b_1,\dots,b_{k-1},\,a_k-b_k,\,a_{k+1},\dots,a_n).$$

**Step 3: Hadamard.** The columns of this matrix have lengths $|b_j|\le\|B\|\le R$ (for $j<k$), $|a_j|\le\|A\|\le R$ (for $j>k$), and $|a_k-b_k|=|(A-B)e_k|\le\|A-B\|$. Here we used $|Me_j|\le\|M\|$. Hence
$$|\det H_{k-1}-\det H_k|\le R^{n-1}\|A-B\|.$$

**Step 4: sum.** Adding the $n$ terms, $|\det A-\det B|\le nR^{n-1}\|A-B\|$.

**Sanity check.** For $n=1$ the bound reads $|a-b|\le|a-b|$ âœ“. For $A=\operatorname{diag}(R,\dots,R)$, $B=(R-\delta)I$ the exact difference is $R^n-(R-\delta)^n\approx nR^{n-1}\delta$ and $\|A-B\|=\delta$: the constant is asymptotically sharp.

**Remark.** This gives a quantitative proof of the continuity of $\det$ and shows that $\det$ is *locally Lipschitz*, with Lipschitz constant on the ball $\{\|A\|\le R\}$ at most $nR^{n-1}$. The derivative $\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{adj}(A)H)$ computed later in the book has norm at most $nR^{n-1}$ on that ball, consistent with this bound.
@@@ mn-031-extreme-points.md
---
title: The extreme points of the operator-norm unit ball
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: extreme points, orthogonal group, singular value decomposition, convexity
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Prove that the extreme points of the closed unit ball $\mathcal B=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\|A\|\le1\}$ for the operator norm are exactly the orthogonal matrices. (A point $E\in\mathcal B$ is extreme if $E=\frac{A+B}2$ with $A,B\in\mathcal B$ forces $A=B$.)

## Hint

For $U\in O(n)$, use that the Euclidean unit ball of $\mathbb{R}^n$ is strictly convex.

For the converse, use the SVD and split a singular value $\sigma_i<1$ as the midpoint of two distinct numbers in $[-1,1]$.

## Solution

**Idea.** An orthogonal matrix sends every unit vector to a *unit* vector, i.e. to an extreme point of the Euclidean ball, which leaves no room for a non-trivial midpoint decomposition. A matrix with a singular value below $1$ has slack in that direction.

**Step 1: orthogonal matrices are extreme.** Let $U\in O(n)$ and $U=\frac{A+B}2$ with $\|A\|,\|B\|\le1$. For a unit vector $x$,
$$1=|Ux|=\Big|\frac{Ax+Bx}2\Big|\le\frac{|Ax|+|Bx|}2\le1.$$
Hence $|Ax|=|Bx|=1$. By the parallelogram law,
$$\Big|\frac{Ax+Bx}2\Big|^2=\frac{|Ax|^2+|Bx|^2}2-\Big|\frac{Ax-Bx}2\Big|^2=1-\frac{|Ax-Bx|^2}4,$$
and the left side is $|Ux|^2=1$, so $Ax=Bx$. This holds for all unit $x$, hence $A=B$ (and $=U$).

**Step 2: other points of $\mathcal B$ are not extreme.** Let $A\in\mathcal B$ be non-orthogonal, with SVD $A=P\Sigma Q^T$, $\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_i)$, $0\le\sigma_i\le1$. If all $\sigma_i=1$ then $A=PQ^T$ is orthogonal; so some $\sigma_i<1$. Choose real numbers $s\ne t$ in $[-1,1]$ with $\frac{s+t}2=\sigma_i$:
- if $0<\sigma_i<1$, take $\delta=\min(\sigma_i,1-\sigma_i)>0$ and $s=\sigma_i+\delta$, $t=\sigma_i-\delta$ (then $0\le t<s\le1$);
- if $\sigma_i=0$, take $s=1$, $t=-1$.

Let $\Sigma_s$, $\Sigma_t$ be $\Sigma$ with $\sigma_i$ replaced by $s$, resp. $t$. Both diagonal matrices have entries of absolute value $\le1$, so $A_s=P\Sigma_sQ^T$ and $A_t=P\Sigma_tQ^T$ belong to $\mathcal B$. They are distinct, and $\frac{A_s+A_t}2=A$. So $A$ is not extreme.

**Remark.** Compare with the Frobenius ball, which is strictly convex: *all* of its boundary points are extreme. The operator-norm ball has far fewer extreme points ($O(n)$, a manifold of dimension $n(n-1)/2$, inside a boundary of dimension $n^2-1$). The next problem shows these extreme points generate the whole ball by convex combinations (a Kreinâ€“Milman-type statement).
@@@ mn-032-convex-hull.md
---
title: The operator-norm ball is the convex hull of O(n)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: convex hull, orthogonal group, SVD, Kreinâ€“Milman
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Prove that the closed operator-norm unit ball $\mathcal B=\{\|A\|\le1\}$ equals the convex hull of the orthogonal group $O(n)$.

## Hint

Show $\operatorname{conv}O(n)\subseteq\mathcal B$ because $\mathcal B$ is convex and contains $O(n)$.

For the other inclusion, write each singular value $\sigma_i\in[0,1]$ as $\lambda_i\cdot1+(1-\lambda_i)\cdot(-1)$ and expand the product over $i$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The singular value decomposition reduces the problem to diagonal matrices with entries in $[-1,1]$, and the cube $[-1,1]^n$ is the convex hull of its vertices $\{\pm1\}^n$.

**Step 1: $\operatorname{conv}O(n)\subseteq\mathcal B$.** Every orthogonal matrix has norm $1$, so $O(n)\subset\mathcal B$. The unit ball of any norm is convex, hence contains the convex hull of $O(n)$.

**Step 2: diagonal matrices.** Let $\sigma\in[0,1]^n$. Put $\lambda_i=\frac{1+\sigma_i}2\in[\frac12,1]$, so that $\sigma_i=\lambda_i\cdot(+1)+(1-\lambda_i)\cdot(-1)$. For each sign vector $\varepsilon\in\{\pm1\}^n$ define the weight
$$w_\varepsilon=\prod_{i=1}^n\begin{cases}\lambda_i&\text{if }\varepsilon_i=+1\\1-\lambda_i&\text{if }\varepsilon_i=-1.\end{cases}$$
Then $w_\varepsilon\ge0$, and $\sum_\varepsilon w_\varepsilon=\prod_i(\lambda_i+1-\lambda_i)=1$. Moreover the $i$-th diagonal entry of $\sum_\varepsilon w_\varepsilon\operatorname{diag}(\varepsilon)$ is $\sum_\varepsilon w_\varepsilon\varepsilon_i=\lambda_i-(1-\lambda_i)=\sigma_i$ (the weights of the other coordinates sum to $1$). So
$$\operatorname{diag}(\sigma)=\sum_{\varepsilon}w_\varepsilon\operatorname{diag}(\varepsilon).$$

**Step 3: general case.** Let $A\in\mathcal B$ with SVD $A=P\operatorname{diag}(\sigma)Q^T$, $\sigma_i\in[0,1]$. Then
$$A=\sum_\varepsilon w_\varepsilon\,P\operatorname{diag}(\varepsilon)Q^T,$$
a convex combination of the orthogonal matrices $P\operatorname{diag}(\varepsilon)Q^T$.

**Probabilistic reading.** If $\varepsilon_i$ are independent random signs with $\mathbb{P}(\varepsilon_i=1)=\lambda_i$, then $\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{diag}(\varepsilon)]=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma)$.

**Remark.** The hull of $O(n)$ is compact (the convex hull of a compact set in finite dimension is compact), so it is automatically *closed*, consistent with the statement. Note that the convex hull of $SO(n)$ is strictly smaller (every orthogonal matrix of determinant $-1$ is an extreme point of $\mathcal B$ by the previous problem, so it cannot be a convex combination of elements of $SO(n)$ other than itself), and the representation above uses $2^n$ orthogonal matrices; by CarathÃ©odory's theorem a representation with at most $n^2+1$ matrices exists.
@@@ mn-033-gelfand-type-bound.md
---
title: The spectral radius is at most â€–Aáµâ€–^(1/k)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: spectral radius, Gelfand formula, symmetric matrices, powers
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\rho(A)\le\|A^k\|^{1/k}$ for all $k\ge1$, and that $\|A^k\|^{1/k}=\rho(A)$ for every symmetric matrix $A$ and every $k\ge1$ (so in particular $\|A^k\|^{1/k}\to\rho(A)$).

## Hint

The eigenvalues of $A^k$ are the $k$-th powers of the eigenvalues of $A$.

Use $\rho(B)\le\|B\|$ from an earlier problem, and for symmetric $A$ the formula $\|S\|=\rho(S)$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The spectral radius is controlled by the norm of *every* power: $\rho(A)\le\|A^k\|^{1/k}$. For symmetric matrices the inequality is an equality, because the operator norm of a symmetric matrix is its spectral radius.

**Step 1: eigenvalues of $A^k$.** Triangularise $A$ over $\mathbb{C}$: $A=PTP^{-1}$ with $T$ upper triangular with diagonal $\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n$. Then $A^k=PT^kP^{-1}$ and $T^k$ is upper triangular with diagonal $\lambda_1^k,\dots,\lambda_n^k$. Hence the eigenvalues of $A^k$ are $\lambda_i^k$ and $\rho(A^k)=\rho(A)^k$.

**Step 2: the inequality.** By the problem "Eigenvalues are bounded by the operator norm" applied to $A^k$,
$$\rho(A)^k=\rho(A^k)\le\|A^k\|,$$
so $\rho(A)\le\|A^k\|^{1/k}$.

**Step 3: symmetric matrices.** If $A$ is symmetric, so is $A^k$, with eigenvalues $\lambda_i^k$. For symmetric matrices the operator norm is the largest absolute eigenvalue, so
$$\|A^k\|=\max_i|\lambda_i|^k=\rho(A)^k.$$
Hence $\|A^k\|^{1/k}=\rho(A)$ for every $k$, and the limit is $\rho(A)$ trivially.

**Counter-example for non-symmetric matrices.** For $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$, $\rho(A)=1$, and $\|A^k\|^{1/k}$ equals $2.41,1.59,1.10,1.014$ for $k=1,5,50,500$: it decreases to $1$ but is $>1$ for each finite $k$.

**Remark.** The limit statement $\|A^k\|^{1/k}\to\rho(A)$ holds for *every* matrix (Gelfand's formula), not only symmetric ones; the proof for general $A$ requires the Jordan form or complex analysis (the resolvent). In particular, $\rho(A)<1$ iff $A^k\to0$.
@@@ mn-034-equivalence-constants.md
---
title: The best constants comparing operator and Frobenius norms
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-norms
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: equivalence constants, singular values, rank one, orthogonal
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Determine the largest constant $c$ and the smallest constant $C$ such that
$$c\,\|A\|_F\le\|A\|\le C\,\|A\|_F\quad\text{for all }A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}).$$

## Hint

Use the singular values: $\|A\|=\sigma_1$ and $\|A\|_F^2=\sum\sigma_i^2$.

Look at rank-one matrices and at orthogonal matrices for the equality cases.

## Solution

**Idea.** Both norms are functions of the singular values: $\sigma_1$ against $\big(\sum\sigma_i^2\big)^{1/2}$. The ratio $\sigma_1/\big(\sum\sigma_i^2\big)^{1/2}$ is largest when only one singular value is non-zero, and smallest when all are equal.

**Step 1: the upper constant.** Since $\sigma_1^2\le\sum_i\sigma_i^2$, we have $\|A\|\le\|A\|_F$. Equality holds for the rank-one matrix $E_{11}$ (where $\|E_{11}\|=\|E_{11}\|_F=1$). So the smallest possible $C$ is $C=1$.

**Step 2: the lower constant.** Since $\sum_i\sigma_i^2\le n\sigma_1^2$, we have $\|A\|_F\le\sqrt n\|A\|$, i.e. $\frac1{\sqrt n}\|A\|_F\le\|A\|$. Equality holds for $A=I$, where $\|I\|=1$ and $\|I\|_F=\sqrt n$. So the largest possible $c$ is $c=\frac1{\sqrt n}$.

**Conclusion.**
$$\frac1{\sqrt n}\|A\|_F\le\|A\|\le\|A\|_F.$$

**Remark.** The constants are sharp for every $n$, but each of them is attained only on a very small set: the right one at rank-one matrices, the left one at multiples of orthogonal matrices (all singular values equal). For a "typical" random matrix the ratio $\|A\|_F/\|A\|$ is of order $\sqrt n/2$, closer to the lower extreme. As $n\to\infty$ these constants degenerate, which is why quantitative estimates in high dimension must keep track of the dimension.

@@@ mn-conn-convex-sets.md
---
title: Convex sets of matrices are path connected
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: connectedness, convex sets, path connected, matrices
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

1. Show that every convex subset of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is path connected.
2. Give four examples of convex subsets of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and say why each one is convex.

## Hint

A set is convex if it contains the whole segment between any two of its points. Is a segment a path?

## Solution

**Idea.** Convexity is *exactly* the statement that straight-line paths do not leave the set.

**1. Proof.** Let $C\subset M_n(\mathbb{R})$ be convex and $A,B\in C$. Define
$$\gamma:[0,1]\to M_n(\mathbb{R}),\qquad \gamma(t)=(1-t)A+tB.$$
- $\gamma$ is continuous: each entry $(1-t)a_{ij}+tb_{ij}$ is an affine function of $t$.
- $\gamma(0)=A$ and $\gamma(1)=B$.
- $\gamma(t)\in C$ for every $t\in[0,1]$, because $C$ is convex.

So any two points of $C$ are joined by a path inside $C$: $C$ is path connected (the empty set and singletons are trivially path connected).

**2. Four examples.**
- *The whole space $M_n(\mathbb{R})$*: it is a vector space.
- *Any open or closed ball* $\{A:\|A-A_0\|<r\}$ for a norm $\|\cdot\|$: if $\|A-A_0\|<r$ and $\|B-A_0\|<r$, then by the triangle inequality $\|(1-t)A+tB-A_0\|\le(1-t)\|A-A_0\|+t\|B-A_0\|<r$.
- *Positive definite symmetric matrices*: if $x^TAx>0$ and $x^TBx>0$ for all $x\neq0$, then $x^T((1-t)A+tB)x=(1-t)x^TAx+tx^TBx>0$ (the sum is a combination with weights $1-t,t\ge0$ not both zero).
- *Stochastic matrices* (nonnegative entries, every row sums to $1$): the two conditions are preserved by convex combinations, since they are linear equalities and inequalities.

**Remark.** Path connectedness is strictly weaker than convexity. A circle (for instance $SO(2)$) is path connected but not convex. Most of this chapter is about sets that are path connected for *non*-obvious reasons.
@@@ mn-conn-components-of-gl-n.md
---
title: Components of GLâ‚™(â„)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: connected components, general linear group, determinant, local connectedness
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

1. Show that every connected component of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is both open and closed in $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$.
2. Show that $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is not connected.

## Hint

For part 1, a ball in $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is connected. For part 2, look at the sign of the determinant.

## Solution

**Idea.** Components are always closed. They are open as soon as the space is *locally connected*, and $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is an open subset of a normed space, so it is.

**1. Components are clopen.**
- *Closed.* The closure of a connected set is connected, so the closure of a component $K$ inside $GL_n$ is a connected set containing $K$; by maximality it equals $K$.
- *Open.* $GL_n(\mathbb{R})=\det^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})$ is open in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ because $\det$ is continuous (it is a polynomial in the entries). So for $A\in K$ there is an open ball $B(A,r)\subset GL_n(\mathbb{R})$. A ball is convex, hence connected (Problem 1). Therefore $B(A,r)\cup K$ is connected (two connected sets sharing the point $A$), so $B(A,r)\subset K$ by maximality. Thus $K$ is open.

**2. Disconnectedness.** The map $\det:GL_n(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$ is continuous and onto: $\det(\operatorname{diag}(c,1,\dots,1))=c$ for any $c\ne0$. Hence
$$GL_n(\mathbb{R})=\{\det>0\}\sqcup\{\det<0\},$$
a union of two disjoint non-empty open sets. So $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is not connected, and each component lies entirely in $\{\det>0\}$ or in $\{\det<0\}$.

**Remark.** The next problems show that there are *exactly two* components, $\{\det>0\}$ and $\{\det<0\}$. Note the contrast with $GL_n(\mathbb{C})$, which is connected: the determinant takes values in $\mathbb{C}^\times$, which is connected.
@@@ mn-conn-singular-matrices.md
---
title: Singular matrices and the nilpotent cone are star-shaped
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: connectedness, singular matrices, nilpotent matrices, star-shaped
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the set of singular matrices $\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\det A=0\}$ and the set of nilpotent matrices are path connected.

## Hint

Both sets contain $0$. What happens to a singular (nilpotent) matrix when you multiply it by a scalar?

## Solution

**Idea.** Show that each set is *star-shaped about $0$*: if $A$ is in the set, so is $tA$ for $t\in[0,1]$.

**Singular matrices.** If $\det A=0$, then $\det(tA)=t^n\det A=0$ for every $t$. So $t\mapsto tA$, $t\in[0,1]$, is a continuous path inside the set joining $A$ (at $t=1$) to $0$ (at $t=0$).

**Nilpotent matrices.** If $A^k=0$, then $(tA)^k=t^kA^k=0$. The same path works.

**Conclusion.** Every point is joined to $0$ inside the set, so by concatenating two such paths (and reversing one) any two points are joined. Both sets are path connected.

**Remark.** The set of singular matrices is the hypersurface $\{\det=0\}$, which is *not* a manifold at $0$ (it is a cone with a singular vertex), but it is connected. By contrast, its complement $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is disconnected (Problem 2).
@@@ mn-conn-sl-n-z-is-discrete.md
---
title: SLâ‚™(â„¤) is totally disconnected
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: totally disconnected, discrete subspace, integer matrices
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $SL_n(\mathbb{Z})$, viewed as a subset of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$, is totally disconnected: its connected components are single points.

## Hint

Two different integer matrices cannot be arbitrarily close.

## Solution

**Idea.** $SL_n(\mathbb{Z})$ is a *discrete* subspace, so every subset is open in it, and a connected subset of a discrete space has at most one point.

**Step 1: the subspace is discrete.** Take the maximum norm $\|A\|_\infty=\max_{i,j}|a_{ij}|$ (all norms on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ are equivalent, so the topology does not depend on this choice). If $A\neq B$ are integer matrices, some entry differs by an integer different from $0$, so
$$\|A-B\|_\infty\ge1.$$
Hence the open ball $B(A,1)$ contains no other integer matrix, and $\{A\}=B(A,1)\cap SL_n(\mathbb{Z})$ is open in $SL_n(\mathbb{Z})$.

**Step 2: connected subsets are points.** Let $S\subset SL_n(\mathbb{Z})$ be connected with two distinct points $A,B$. The set $\{A\}$ is open in $S$ (by step 1) and also closed in $S$ (singletons are closed), and it is neither empty nor equal to $S$. That disconnects $S$, a contradiction. So $S$ has at most one point.

**Remark.** The same proof shows that any subset of $M_n(\mathbb{Z})$ is totally disconnected. This is a first example of a *discrete subgroup* of a Lie group. The quotient $SL_n(\mathbb{R})/SL_n(\mathbb{Z})$ is the space of unimodular lattices, a central object of number theory.
@@@ mn-conn-the-sign-of-the-trace.md
---
title: Components of the set where the trace is non-zero
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: connected components, trace, half-spaces
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

How many connected components does $\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\operatorname{tr}A\neq0\}$ have?

## Hint

The trace is a continuous *linear* map to $\mathbb{R}$.

## Solution

**Answer.** Exactly two.

**Idea.** The set is the union of two open half-spaces, each convex, hence connected.

**Step 1: the two pieces.** Let $H_+=\{\operatorname{tr}>0\}$ and $H_-=\{\operatorname{tr}<0\}$. They are open (preimages of open sets under the continuous map $\operatorname{tr}$), disjoint, non-empty (they contain $I$ and $-I$ since $\operatorname{tr}(\pm I)=\pm n$), and their union is the set in question.

**Step 2: each piece is connected.** $\operatorname{tr}$ is linear, so if $\operatorname{tr}A>0$ and $\operatorname{tr}B>0$ then $\operatorname{tr}((1-t)A+tB)=(1-t)\operatorname{tr}A+t\operatorname{tr}B>0$. So $H_+$ is convex, hence path connected (Problem 1); likewise $H_-$.

**Step 3: conclusion.** The set is a disjoint union of two non-empty open connected sets, so these are precisely its two components.

**Remark.** The same argument applies to the complement of any hyperplane: $\{\varphi\ne0\}$ for a non-zero linear form $\varphi$ always has two components. The complement of a subspace of codimension $\ge2$, on the other hand, is connected (Problem 13).
@@@ mn-conn-the-circle-so-2.md
---
title: SO(2) is a circle, O(2) has two components
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: SO(2), O(2), rotations, reflections, path components
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $R_\theta=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\ \sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$. Show that $SO(2)=\{R_\theta:\theta\in\mathbb{R}\}$ is path connected, and that $O(2)$ has exactly two path components.

## Hint

First prove that every element of $SO(2)$ is some $R_\theta$. Then look at the determinant.

## Solution

**Step 1: $SO(2)=\{R_\theta\}$.** Let $U=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}\in SO(2)$. The first column $(a,c)^T$ is a unit vector, so $(a,c)=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)$ for some $\theta$. The second column is a unit vector orthogonal to the first, so it is $\pm(-\sin\theta,\cos\theta)$, and $\det U=ad-bc=1$ selects the sign $+$. So $U=R_\theta$. Conversely $R_\theta^TR_\theta=I$ and $\det R_\theta=\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta=1$.

**Step 2: $SO(2)$ is path connected.** $\theta\mapsto R_\theta$ is continuous from $[0,2\pi]$ onto $SO(2)$, and the continuous image of an interval is path connected.

**Step 3: the other half of $O(2)$.** Let $S=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)$. If $V\in O(2)$ with $\det V=-1$, then $\det(VS)=1$ so $VS=R_\theta$ for some $\theta$, and $V=R_\theta S^{-1}=R_\theta S$. Hence
$$O(2)\setminus SO(2)=\{R_\theta S:\theta\in\mathbb{R}\},$$
the image of $[0,2\pi]$ under the continuous map $\theta\mapsto R_\theta S$, hence path connected.

**Step 4: exactly two components.** $\det:O(2)\to\{\pm1\}$ is continuous. The two pieces $SO(2)=\det^{-1}(1)$ and $O(2)\setminus SO(2)=\det^{-1}(-1)$ are disjoint, non-empty, and both open and closed in $O(2)$. Each is path connected, so they are the two path components.

**Remark.** The map $\theta\mapsto R_\theta$ identifies $SO(2)$ with the circle $\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}$, a *compact* connected group. This is the basic example for the study of $SO(n)$ in the following problems.
@@@ mn-conn-reflections-and-o-n.md
---
title: O(n) has exactly two components
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: orthogonal group, components, reflections, homeomorphism
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Assuming that $SO(n)$ is connected, show that $O(n)$ has exactly two connected components: $SO(n)$ and $O(n)\setminus SO(n)$.

## Hint

Compose with a fixed reflection to map $SO(n)$ homeomorphically onto the other half.

## Solution

**Idea.** The determinant splits $O(n)$ into two pieces; a reflection shows that the two pieces have the same shape.

**Step 1: $O(n)$ is the disjoint union of two clopen sets.** For $U\in O(n)$, $\det(U)^2=\det(U^TU)=1$, so $\det U=\pm1$. Since $\det$ is continuous, $SO(n)=\det^{-1}(1)$ and $O(n)\setminus SO(n)=\det^{-1}(-1)$ are closed and open in $O(n)$ and disjoint. The second is non-empty: it contains $D=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$. So $O(n)$ is disconnected.

**Step 2: the second piece is connected.** Consider $L:O(n)\to O(n)$, $L(U)=DU$ (note $D\in O(n)$, so the product stays in $O(n)$). Then $\det(DU)=-\det U$, so $L$ maps $SO(n)$ into $O(n)\setminus SO(n)$. It is onto that set: if $\det V=-1$ then $V=D(DV)$ with $\det(DV)=1$. So $O(n)\setminus SO(n)=L(SO(n))$ is the continuous image of a connected set, hence connected. (In fact $L$ is a homeomorphism, its inverse being $L$ itself.)

**Step 3: conclusion.** $O(n)$ is the disjoint union of two non-empty connected clopen sets, so these are exactly its two components.

**Remark.** The hypothesis is proved later (Problems 16 and 17). In the language of Lie groups, $SO(n)$ is the *identity component* of $O(n)$, and $O(n)/SO(n)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2$.
@@@ mn-conn-positive-determinant.md
---
title: GLâ‚™âº(â„) is path connected
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: polar decomposition, general linear group, path connected
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Assuming that $SO(n)$ is path connected, show that $GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})=\{A:\det A>0\}$ is path connected.

## Hint

Use the polar decomposition $A=UP$ with $U$ orthogonal and $P$ symmetric positive definite.

## Solution

**Idea.** Every invertible matrix factors as "rotation $\times$ stretch". The rotation part lives in the connected group $SO(n)$ and the stretch part lives in a convex cone.

**Step 1: polar decomposition.** For $A\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$, the matrix $A^TA$ is symmetric positive definite (since $x^TA^TAx=\|Ax\|^2>0$ for $x\ne0$). Let $P=(A^TA)^{1/2}$ be its positive definite square root (obtained from the spectral theorem by taking square roots of the eigenvalues), and $U=AP^{-1}$. Then
$$U^TU=P^{-1}A^TAP^{-1}=P^{-1}P^2P^{-1}=I,$$
so $U\in O(n)$ and $A=UP$.

**Step 2: sign.** $\det P>0$ (product of positive eigenvalues), so $\det A=\det U\det P$ has the sign of $\det U$. If $A\in GL_n^+$, then $\det U=1$: $U\in SO(n)$.

**Step 3: a path to $I$.** Let $U_t$ be a path in $SO(n)$ from $I$ to $U$ (it exists by assumption) and $P_t=(1-t)I+tP$, $t\in[0,1]$. Each $P_t$ is symmetric positive definite (convexity of the cone of positive definite matrices, Problem 1). Then
$$A_t=U_tP_t$$
is continuous, lies in $GL_n^+$ (as $\det U_t=1$ and $\det P_t>0$), and goes from $A_0=I$ to $A_1=UP=A$.

**Conclusion.** Every $A\in GL_n^+$ is joined to $I$ inside $GL_n^+$, so $GL_n^+$ is path connected. Together with Problem 2 and the map $A\mapsto DA$ ($D=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$), this shows that $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ has exactly two components.

**Remark.** The decomposition $GL_n^+\cong SO(n)\times\{\text{positive definite matrices}\}$ is a homeomorphism (polar decomposition depends continuously on $A$). It is the source of the deformation retraction of Problem 25.
@@@ mn-conn-level-sets-of-the-determinant.md
---
title: Level sets of the determinant
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: determinant, level sets, SL_n, scaling
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Assume that $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is path connected (Problems 18 and 19). For $n\ge2$ and $c\neq0$, show that the level set $\det^{-1}(c)=\{A:\det A=c\}$ is path connected.

## Hint

Multiply $SL_n$ by the scalar $|c|^{1/n}$, and by a reflection if $c<0$.

## Solution

**Idea.** All the non-zero level sets are copies of $SL_n$ obtained by a scaling and perhaps a reflection, hence they are all homeomorphic to $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Case $c>0$.** Let $s=c^{1/n}>0$ and define $\Phi(A)=sA$. Since $\det(sA)=s^n\det A=c\det A$, $\Phi$ maps $SL_n$ into $\det^{-1}(c)$. It is a bijection onto it: its inverse is $B\mapsto s^{-1}B$, because $\det(s^{-1}B)=c^{-1}\cdot c=1$. Both maps are continuous, so $\det^{-1}(c)\cong SL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Case $c<0$.** Let $D=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$ and $s=|c|^{1/n}$. Define $\Psi(A)=sDA$. Then $\det(sDA)=s^n\cdot(-1)\cdot\det A=-|c|=c$ for $A\in SL_n$. Again $\Psi$ is continuous with continuous inverse $B\mapsto s^{-1}DB$ (note $D^{-1}=D$), so $\det^{-1}(c)\cong SL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Conclusion.** $\det^{-1}(c)$ is the continuous image of the path connected set $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$, hence path connected.

**Remark.** For $c=0$ the level set is the set of singular matrices, which is also path connected (Problem 3), but for a very different reason (it is a cone, not a group). The hypothesis $n\ge2$ is not even necessary here: for $n=1$ each level set is a single point.
@@@ mn-conn-star-shaped-sets.md
---
title: Spectral radius balls and star-shaped sets
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: spectral radius, star-shaped, norm ball
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $\rho(A)$ be the spectral radius (the largest modulus of an eigenvalue of $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$).

1. Show that $\{A:\rho(A)<1\}$ and $\{A:\|A\|<1\}$ are path connected.
2. Show that $\{A:\rho(A)>1\}$ is not star-shaped about $0$.

## Hint

How do $\rho(tA)$ and $\|tA\|$ depend on $t\ge0$?

## Solution

**Idea.** Scaling by $t\in[0,1]$ shrinks both $\rho$ and $\|\cdot\|$, so the sets are star-shaped about $0$.

**1.** The eigenvalues of $tA$ are $t\lambda$ for the eigenvalues $\lambda$ of $A$, so $\rho(tA)=t\rho(A)$ for $t\ge0$. Also $\|tA\|=t\|A\|$. If $\rho(A)<1$ then $\rho(tA)=t\rho(A)<1$ for every $t\in[0,1]$, so the segment from $A$ to $0$ stays in $\{\rho<1\}$. Same for $\{\|A\|<1\}$. Every point is joined to $0$, so both sets are path connected.

**2.** Suppose $\rho(A)>1$. Then $\rho(tA)=t\rho(A)$ is $<1$ as soon as $t<1/\rho(A)$. In particular $0\in$ the segment $[0,A]$ is *not* in $\{\rho>1\}$ (indeed $\rho(0)=0$), and neither are the points $tA$ with small $t>0$. So the segment from $0$ to $A$ leaves the set $\{\rho>1\}$: it is not star-shaped about $0$ (and $0$ doesn't even belong to it).

**Remark.** For $n=1$, $\{|a|>1\}=(-\infty,-1)\cup(1,\infty)$ is not even connected. Also note the inclusion $\{\|A\|<1\}\subset\{\rho(A)<1\}$ (since $\rho(A)\le\|A\|$), strict as soon as $n\ge2$ (for example $\begin{pmatrix}0&2\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ is nilpotent, so $\rho=0$, but has norm $2$).
@@@ mn-conn-hurwitz-matrices.md
---
title: Hurwitz (stable) matrices form a connected set
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: Hurwitz matrices, stability, eigenvalues, linear deformation
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

A matrix $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is *Hurwitz* if every eigenvalue $\lambda\in\mathbb{C}$ of $A$ satisfies $\operatorname{Re}\lambda<0$. Show that the set of Hurwitz matrices is path connected.

## Hint

Deform $A$ linearly towards $-I$. What are the eigenvalues of $(1-t)A-tI$?

## Solution

**Idea.** The set is star-shaped about $-I$.

**Step 1: eigenvalues of the deformation.** For $t\in[0,1]$ let $A_t=(1-t)A-tI$. If $Av=\lambda v$ with $v\in\mathbb{C}^n\setminus\{0\}$, then
$$A_tv=\big((1-t)\lambda-t\big)v,$$
so $(1-t)\lambda-t$ is an eigenvalue of $A_t$. Conversely every eigenvalue of $A_t$ has this form: for $t<1$, $A=\frac1{1-t}(A_t+tI)$ so the eigenvalues of $A$ are $\frac{\mu+t}{1-t}$ for $\mu\in\operatorname{Spec}(A_t)$, i.e. $\mu=(1-t)\lambda-t$. For $t=1$, $A_1=-I$ with unique eigenvalue $-1$.

**Step 2: stability is preserved.** For $t\in[0,1)$,
$$\operatorname{Re}\big((1-t)\lambda-t\big)=(1-t)\operatorname{Re}\lambda-t<0$$
since $1-t>0$, $\operatorname{Re}\lambda<0$ and $t\ge0$. For $t=1$ the eigenvalue is $-1<0$. So $A_t$ is Hurwitz for all $t\in[0,1]$.

**Step 3: conclusion.** $t\mapsto A_t$ is a continuous (affine) path in the set of Hurwitz matrices from $A$ to $-I$. Every Hurwitz matrix is joined to $-I$, so the set is path connected.

**Remark.** The Hurwitz set is open (eigenvalues depend continuously on the matrix) and *convex?* No: it is star-shaped about $-I$ but not convex in general for $n\ge2$. A convex combination of two Hurwitz matrices need not be Hurwitz, a well-known fact in control theory.
@@@ mn-conn-positive-spectrum.md
---
title: Matrices with real positive spectrum
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: eigenvalues, linear deformation, path connected
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the set $\mathcal{S}$ of matrices $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ all of whose eigenvalues are real and positive is path connected.

## Hint

Deform $A$ linearly towards $I$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Same trick as for Hurwitz matrices: $\mathcal{S}$ is star-shaped about $I$.

**Step 1: eigenvalues along the deformation.** For $A\in\mathcal{S}$ and $t\in[0,1]$ let $A_t=(1-t)A+tI$. If $\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n$ are the eigenvalues of $A$ with multiplicity (all real and positive), then $A_t$ has eigenvalues $(1-t)\lambda_i+t$. (To see this, triangularise $A$ over $\mathbb{C}$: $A=PTP^{-1}$ with $T$ upper triangular with diagonal $\lambda_i$; then $A_t=P((1-t)T+tI)P^{-1}$ and $(1-t)T+tI$ is upper triangular with diagonal $(1-t)\lambda_i+t$.)

**Step 2: they are real and positive.** For $t\in[0,1]$, $(1-t)\lambda_i+t$ is a convex combination of the positive numbers $\lambda_i$ and $1$, hence real and $>0$. So $A_t\in\mathcal{S}$.

**Step 3: conclusion.** $A_0=A$, $A_1=I$, and $t\mapsto A_t$ is continuous. Every element of $\mathcal{S}$ is joined to $I$ inside $\mathcal{S}$, so $\mathcal{S}$ is path connected.

**Remark.** The same argument shows that $\{A:\text{all eigenvalues real and} \ge0\}$ and $\{A:\text{all eigenvalues in an interval }J\}$ (for $J$ convex and $1\in J$) are star-shaped. The set of matrices with all eigenvalues real is *not* obviously connected: its connectedness (Problem 26 handles the simple-spectrum part) needs more work.
@@@ mn-conn-complement-of-a-subspace.md
---
title: Complement of a subspace
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: hyperplane, codimension, connectedness, linear subspace
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $E$ be a real vector space of finite dimension $N\ge1$ and $V\subsetneq E$ a linear subspace.

1. If $V$ is a hyperplane (dimension $N-1$), show that $E\setminus V$ has exactly two connected components.
2. If $\operatorname{codim}V\ge2$, show that $E\setminus V$ is path connected.

## Hint

In part 2, given $a,b\notin V$, look for a third point $c$ such that the segments $[a,c]$ and $[c,b]$ do not meet $V$.

## Solution

**1. Hyperplane.** Write $V=\ker\varphi$ with $\varphi$ a non-zero linear form. Then $E\setminus V=\{\varphi>0\}\sqcup\{\varphi<0\}$. Both pieces are open, disjoint, non-empty (replace $x$ by $-x$), and convex (preimages of intervals under a linear map), hence path connected. So there are exactly two components.

**2. Codimension $\ge2$.** Let $a,b\in E\setminus V$.

*Which points $c$ are bad for $a$?* Suppose $(1-t)a+tc\in V$ for some $t\in[0,1]$. Then $t\ne0$ (because $a\notin V$), and so $c=\frac1t\big(v-(1-t)a\big)$ with $v\in V$, i.e.
$$c\in V+\mathbb{R}a.$$
$V+\mathbb{R}a$ is a linear subspace of dimension $\dim V+1$ (since $a\notin V$), which is $\le N-1<N$. Likewise define $W_b=V+\mathbb{R}b$. If $c\notin W_a$ the whole segment $[a,c]$ avoids $V$ and similarly for $[c,b]$ and $W_b$.

*Choice of $c$.* $E$ is not the union of two proper subspaces $W_a$, $W_b$ (a vector space over an infinite field is never a union of finitely many proper subspaces). Pick $c\notin W_a\cup W_b$. Then $c\notin V$ and the path $[a,c]\cup[c,b]$ (two segments) lies in $E\setminus V$.

**Conclusion.** $E\setminus V$ is path connected.

**Remark.** In $M_n(\mathbb{R})$, the complement of the hyperplane $\{\operatorname{tr}=0\}$ has two components (Problem 5), whereas the complement of the subspace of scalar matrices $\mathbb{R}I$ (codimension $n^2-1\ge2$ for $n\ge2$) is connected. The result is false for *non-linear* sets of codimension $1$ and may fail even for codimension $2$ non-linear sets that are not "nice".
@@@ mn-conn-complement-of-o-n.md
---
title: The complement of O(n) is path connected
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: orthogonal group, complement, singular values, path connected
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $M_n(\mathbb{R})\setminus O(n)$ is path connected for $n\ge2$. What happens for $n=1$?

## Hint

Join every matrix to $0$ along a ray $t\mapsto tA$. The ray hits $O(n)$ only when all singular values of $A$ are equal. If they are, perturb $A$ first.

## Solution

**Idea.** The origin is outside $O(n)$, and almost every ray from the origin avoids $O(n)$ altogether. The few bad rays can be avoided by a small preliminary detour.

**Step 0: when is a matrix orthogonal in terms of singular values?** $B\in O(n)$ iff $B^TB=I$ iff all singular values of $B$ equal $1$.

**Step 1: rays.** For $t>0$ the singular values of $tA$ are $t\sigma_i(A)$. So $tA\in O(n)$ iff $\sigma_1(A)=\dots=\sigma_n(A)=1/t$. Consequently:
- if the singular values of $A$ are *not all equal*, then $tA\notin O(n)$ for all $t\in[0,1]$ (for $t=0$ we get $0\notin O(n)$);
- so $t\mapsto tA$ joins $A$ to $0$ inside $M_n(\mathbb{R})\setminus O(n)$.

**Step 2: the bad case.** Suppose all singular values of $A$ are equal to $c\ge0$. Then $A^TA=c^2I$. If $c=0$ then $A=0$. Otherwise $U=A/c\in O(n)$, so $A=cU$ with $c\ne1$ (since $A\notin O(n)$).
- If $0<c<1$: for $t\in[0,1]$, $tA=tcU$ has all singular values equal to $tc<1$, so $tA\notin O(n)$. The ray works again.
- If $c>1$: consider $A_s=U\operatorname{diag}(c+s,c,\dots,c)$, $s\in[0,1]$. Because $n\ge2$ and $U$ is orthogonal, the singular values of $A_s$ are $c+s,c,\dots,c$: for $s>0$ they are not all equal, and for $s=0$ they are all $c>1$. In both cases $A_s\notin O(n)$ (at least one singular value is $>1$... precisely, the *smallest* singular value is $c>1$ so none equals $1$). So $s\mapsto A_s$ is a path in the complement from $A$ to $A_1$, whose singular values are *not all equal*. Then apply Step 1 to $A_1$ to go to $0$.

**Conclusion.** Every point of the complement is joined to $0$ inside the complement, so it is path connected.

**The case $n=1$.** $O(1)=\{\pm1\}$ and $\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\pm1\}=(-\infty,-1)\cup(-1,1)\cup(1,\infty)$ has three components. For $n=1$ Step 2 fails because $\operatorname{diag}(c+s)$ again has all singular values equal.

**Remark.** The result expresses that $O(n)$ has codimension $\frac{n(n+1)}{2}\ge3$ in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$, which is enough to prevent it from separating space. The general principle: a closed submanifold of codimension $\ge2$ never disconnects a connected manifold.
@@@ mn-conn-the-nilpotent-cone-and-idempotents.md
---
title: Nilpotent matrices and idempotents of fixed rank
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: nilpotent matrices, idempotents, conjugacy orbits, projections
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that:
1. the set of nilpotent matrices is path connected;
2. for $0<k<n$, the set $\mathcal{P}_k=\{P\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):P^2=P,\ \operatorname{rank}P=k\}$ is path connected.

You may use that $GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$ is path connected.

## Hint

For part 2, every idempotent of rank $k$ is conjugate to $E_k=\operatorname{diag}(I_k,0)$. The reflection $\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$ commutes with $E_k$.

## Solution

**1. Nilpotent matrices.** Done in Problem 3: $(tA)^m=t^mA^m=0$ when $A^m=0$, so $t\mapsto tA$ joins $A$ to $0$.

**2. Idempotents of rank $k$.**

*Step 1: conjugacy.* If $P^2=P$ then $\mathbb{R}^n=\operatorname{Im}P\oplus\ker P$ (every $x=Px+(x-Px)$, and $P$ is the identity on $\operatorname{Im}P$). In a basis adapted to this decomposition, $P$ becomes $E_k=\operatorname{diag}(I_k,0)$ with $k=\operatorname{rank}P$. So
$$\mathcal{P}_k=\{QE_kQ^{-1}:Q\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})\}.$$

*Step 2: we may take $\det Q>0$.* Let $D=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$. It is diagonal, so $DE_k=E_kD$. If $\det Q<0$, put $Q'=QD$. Then $\det Q'>0$ and
$$Q'E_kQ'^{-1}=QDE_kD^{-1}Q^{-1}=QE_kQ^{-1}.$$
So $\mathcal{P}_k=\{QE_kQ^{-1}:Q\in GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})\}$.

*Step 3.* The map $GL_n^+\to M_n$, $Q\mapsto QE_kQ^{-1}$ is continuous (the inverse is a rational function of the entries with non-vanishing denominator $\det Q$). Its image is $\mathcal{P}_k$, so $\mathcal{P}_k$ is the continuous image of a path connected set, hence path connected.

**Remark.** Why is $0<k<n$ needed? For $k=0$ and $k=n$ the set is a single point ($\{0\}$ or $\{I\}$), trivially connected. The role of $D$ in step 2 is the crucial subtlety: without a negative-determinant matrix in the centraliser of $E_k$, an orbit of $GL_n$ would split in two (see the case of $J$ in Problem 29).
@@@ mn-conn-so-n-is-connected.md
---
title: SO(n) is path connected (normal form)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 3
tags: special orthogonal group, normal form, rotations, path connected
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Prove that $SO(n)$ is path connected.

## Hint

Use the real normal form of an orthogonal matrix: in a suitable orthonormal basis it is block diagonal with $2\times2$ rotation blocks and $\pm1$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Any rotation is a product of commuting rotations in orthogonal planes, and each can be "turned back" to the identity continuously.

**Step 1: normal form.** Let $U\in O(n)$. Then there are $Q\in O(n)$ and angles $\theta_1,\dots,\theta_p\in(0,\pi)$ such that
$$U=Q\,\operatorname{diag}\big(R(\theta_1),\dots,R(\theta_p),\,I_a,\,-I_b\big)\,Q^T,\qquad R(\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\ \sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix},$$
with $2p+a+b=n$. (Derivation: $U$ is a normal matrix, so it is unitarily diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$ with eigenvalues of modulus $1$. Real eigenvalues are $\pm1$ and non-real ones come in conjugate pairs $e^{\pm i\theta}$. For an eigenvector $v=x+iy$ of $e^{i\theta}$, the real vectors $x,y$ span a $U$-invariant plane on which $U$ acts as a rotation by $\theta$, in an orthonormal basis of that plane. Eigenspaces for distinct eigenvalues are orthogonal, which gives an orthonormal basis of $\mathbb{R}^n$.)

**Step 2: for $U\in SO(n)$, the multiplicity $b$ of $-1$ is even.** $\det U=\det R(\theta_1)\cdots\det R(\theta_p)\cdot1^a(-1)^b=(-1)^b$, because each $\det R(\theta_j)=1$. So $\det U=1$ forces $b$ even, say $b=2m$.

**Step 3: write $-I_{2m}$ as rotations.** $-I_2=R(\pi)$, so $-I_{2m}=\operatorname{diag}(R(\pi),\dots,R(\pi))$. We obtain $U=Q\,B\,Q^T$ with
$$B=\operatorname{diag}\big(R(\theta_1),\dots,R(\theta_p),R(\pi),\dots,R(\pi),I_a\big)=\operatorname{diag}\big(R(\varphi_1),\dots,R(\varphi_q),I_a\big),\quad q=p+m.$$

**Step 4: the path.** Let $B_t=\operatorname{diag}\big(R(t\varphi_1),\dots,R(t\varphi_q),I_a\big)$, $t\in[0,1]$. It is continuous in $t$, $B_t$ is orthogonal with $\det B_t=1$, $B_0=I$ and $B_1=B$. Then
$$\gamma(t)=QB_tQ^T$$
is a continuous path from $I$ to $U$. It stays in $SO(n)$: $\gamma(t)$ is orthogonal and $\det\gamma(t)=\det B_t=1$. (Note that $Q\in O(n)$ may have determinant $-1$; conjugation does not change the determinant.)

**Conclusion.** Every element of $SO(n)$ is joined to $I$ in $SO(n)$, so $SO(n)$ is path connected.

**Remark.** The proof also shows that $\exp:\mathfrak{so}(n)\to SO(n)$ is onto: $\gamma(t)=\exp(tX)$ with $X=Q\operatorname{diag}(\varphi_1J,\dots,\varphi_qJ,0)Q^T$, $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ (Problem 8 of the exponential chapter). Compare with $SO(n)$ being compact and connected.

---
title: SO(n) is connected by Givens rotations
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 3
tags: Givens rotations, induction, special orthogonal group
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Give a second proof that $SO(n)$ is path connected, using rotations in coordinate planes and induction on $n$.

## Hint

Rotate the first column $Ue_1$ of $U$ onto $e_1$ with at most $n-1$ plane rotations. What does the matrix look like afterwards?

## Solution

**Notation.** For $1\le i<j\le n$ and $\theta\in\mathbb{R}$, the *Givens rotation* $G_{ij}(\theta)$ is the identity matrix except for the four entries $(i,i),(i,j),(j,i),(j,j)$ which form the block $R(\theta)$. It lies in $SO(n)$ and $\theta\mapsto G_{ij}(\theta)$ is a path from $G_{ij}(0)=I$.

**Induction.** For $n=1$, $SO(1)=\{1\}$. Assume $SO(n-1)$ is path connected and let $U\in SO(n)$, $n\ge2$.

**Step 1: rotate $v=Ue_1$ onto $e_1$.** $v$ is a unit vector. For $j=n,n-1,\dots,2$ choose an angle $\theta_j$ such that the rotation $G_{j-1,j}(\theta_j)$ turns the vector obtained so far into a vector whose $j$-th coordinate is $0$: it acts only on coordinates $j-1$ and $j$, and a plane rotation can bring any vector $(x,y)$ to $(\sqrt{x^2+y^2},0)$. After these $n-1$ rotations, we get the vector $(r,0,\dots,0)$ with $r=\pm1$ (the norm is preserved), and for the last rotation we can choose the angle so that $r=+1$ (a rotation in the plane $(1,2)$ can send $(x,y)$ to $(\sqrt{x^2+y^2},0)$ and we are done). Thus $G\,Ue_1=e_1$ for $G=G_{12}(\theta_2)\cdots G_{n-1,n}(\theta_n)$.

**Step 2: the remaining matrix.** $GU\in SO(n)$ has first column $e_1$. Since its columns are orthonormal, its first row is $e_1^T$ too (the first row has norm $1$ and its first entry is $1$). So
