$$GU=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&U'\end{pmatrix},\quad U'\in O(n-1),\ \det U'=\det(GU)=1,$$
so $U'\in SO(n-1)$.

**Step 3: paths.** By induction there is a path $U'_t$ from $I_{n-1}$ to $U'$ in $SO(n-1)$, hence a path $1\oplus U'_t$ from $I$ to $GU$ in $SO(n)$. Also $G^{-1}=G_{n-1,n}(-\theta_n)\cdots G_{12}(-\theta_2)$ is joined to $I$ by the path $G_{n-1,n}(-s\theta_n)\cdots G_{12}(-s\theta_2)$, $s\in[0,1]$. Hence
$$s,t\longmapsto G(s)^{-1}\,(1\oplus U'_t)$$
(first run $t$, then $s$) is a path inside $SO(n)$ from $I$ to $G^{-1}(GU)=U$.

**Conclusion.** $SO(n)$ is path connected.

**Remark.** The proof is the same as the QR-decomposition by Givens rotations used in numerical linear algebra, and it shows that $SO(n)$ is generated by plane rotations.
@@@ mn-conn-sl-n-via-polar-decomposition.md
---
title: SLâ‚™(â„) is path connected (polar decomposition)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 3
tags: special linear group, polar decomposition, path connected
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is path connected, using the polar decomposition and a path inside the positive definite matrices of determinant $1$.

## Hint

Write $A=UP$ with $U\in SO(n)$ and $P$ positive definite of determinant $1$. Then diagonalise $P$ and take powers $p_i^{1-t}$ of its eigenvalues.

## Solution

**Idea.** The rotation part is handled by the connectedness of $SO(n)$ (Problems 16, 17); the stretch part is contracted to $I$ without leaving $\{\det=1\}$ by raising the eigenvalues to the power $1-t$.

**Step 1: polar decomposition.** Let $A\in SL_n(\mathbb{R})$. Write $A=UP$ with $P=(A^TA)^{1/2}$ positive definite and $U\in O(n)$ (Problem 8). Then $1=\det A=\det U\det P$ with $\det P>0$, so $\det P=1$ and $\det U=1$.

**Step 2: path for $P$.** By the spectral theorem $P=Q\operatorname{diag}(p_1,\dots,p_n)Q^T$ with $Q\in O(n)$ and $p_i>0$, $\prod p_i=\det P=1$. Define
$$P_t=Q\operatorname{diag}(p_1^{1-t},\dots,p_n^{1-t})Q^T,\qquad t\in[0,1].$$
- $t\mapsto P_t$ is continuous;
- $P_t$ is symmetric positive definite;
- $\det P_t=\prod p_i^{1-t}=\big(\prod p_i\big)^{1-t}=1$;
- $P_0=P$, $P_1=I$.

**Step 3: path for $U$.** Since $SO(n)$ is path connected, choose a path $U_s$ in $SO(n)$ from $U$ to $I$.

**Step 4: concatenate.** First run $t\mapsto UP_t$ (from $A=UP$ to $U$, staying in $SL_n$ because $\det(UP_t)=1$), then $s\mapsto U_s$ (from $U$ to $I$, inside $SO(n)\subset SL_n$). This is a path in $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ from $A$ to $I$.

**Conclusion.** $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is path connected.

**Remark.** Note that we cannot use the *linear* path $(1-t)P+tI$ here: its determinant is not $1$ in general (for $P=\operatorname{diag}(4,\tfrac14)$ the midpoint $\operatorname{diag}(\tfrac52,\tfrac58)$ has determinant $\tfrac{25}{16}$). The multiplicative path $p_i^{1-t}$ is the natural "geodesic" in the group.
@@@ mn-conn-sl-n-by-transvections.md
---
title: SLâ‚™(â„) is path connected (transvections)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 3
tags: special linear group, elementary matrices, transvections, row reduction
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Prove that $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is path connected using elementary matrices $T_{ij}(t)=I+tE_{ij}$, $i\ne j$.

## Hint

Prove first that $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is generated by the matrices $T_{ij}(t)$, by Gaussian elimination.

## Solution

**Idea.** Each generator lies on an explicit path through $I$; a product of generators can be shrunk to $I$ by shrinking all the parameters simultaneously.

**Step 1: $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is generated by transvections.** Let $A\in SL_n(\mathbb{R})$. Left multiplication by $T_{ij}(t)$ adds $t$ times row $j$ to row $i$. Using these operations we reduce $A$ to the identity:
- *Making the $(1,1)$ entry equal to $1$.* Some entry of the first column is non-zero (else $\det A=0$). If $a_{11}=0$, add that row to row $1$ so that $a_{11}\ne0$. If $a_{11}\ne1$ we want to make it $1$: if $a_{11}\ne 0$, add row $2$ times $\frac{1-a_{11}}{a_{21}}$ if $a_{21}\ne0$. If $a_{21}=0$ first add row $2$ to... (some entry $a_{k1}\neq0$ with $k\ge2$ exists unless the first column is $(a_{11},0,\dots,0)^T$; in that case add row $2$ to row $1$ after having made $a_{22}\ne 0$ or move on). In all cases one can reach $a_{11}=1$ using only transvections whenever $n\ge2$ (a standard fact).
- *Clearing the first column and first row.* Subtract multiples of row $1$ from the others to clear the first column; then use column operations (right multiplication by transvections) to clear the first row.
- *Induction.* We are left with $1\oplus A'$ with $\det A'=1$; repeat with $A'$ in $SL_{n-1}$. At the end we reach a diagonal matrix with determinant $1$ and last entry $1$, hence $I$ (the diagonal entries equal to $1$ except possibly the last; the determinant being $1$, it is $1$).

So $A$ is a product of transvections: $A=T_1T_2\cdots T_m$ with $T_k=T_{i_kj_k}(t_k)$.

**Step 2: the path.** Define $\gamma(s)=T_{i_1j_1}(st_1)\cdots T_{i_mj_m}(st_m)$, $s\in[0,1]$. Each factor has determinant $1$ ($T_{ij}(t)$ is unipotent triangular after permutation), so $\gamma(s)\in SL_n$. It is continuous with $\gamma(0)=I$ and $\gamma(1)=A$.

**Conclusion.** Every $A\in SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is joined to $I$ in $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Remark.** This proof works over any topological field in which $SL_n$ is generated by transvections; it proves for instance that $SL_n(\mathbb{C})$ is connected. It does not by itself prove that $GL_n^+$ is connected: that needs one more step, scaling by $|\det|^{1/n}$.

**Note.** The source solution only states the generation fact; the elimination argument above was written out in more detail. For a complete treatment of the first bullet, see any proof that $SL_n(F)=E_n(F)$ for a field $F$.
@@@ mn-conn-rank-at-least-n-minus-1.md
---
title: Matrices of rank at least n âˆ’ 1
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 3
tags: rank, singular values, determinant sign change, path connected
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\mathcal{R}=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\operatorname{rank}A\ge n-1\}$ is path connected for $n\ge2$.

## Hint

$\mathcal{R}$ is the union of $GL_n^+$, $GL_n^-$ and the set of rank $n-1$ matrices. Join these three pieces through the matrices $\operatorname{diag}(1,\dots,1,s)$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The two components of $GL_n$ are connected to each other *through* the rank $n-1$ matrices.

**Step 1: the three pieces.** $\mathcal{R}=GL_n^+\cup GL_n^-\cup\mathcal{M}_{n-1}$ with $\mathcal{M}_{n-1}$ the set of rank $n-1$ matrices. We know:
- $GL_n^+$ is path connected (Problem 8).
- $GL_n^-=D\cdot GL_n^+$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$ is path connected too (image of $GL_n^+$ under the continuous map $A\mapsto DA$).
- $\mathcal{M}_{n-1}$ is path connected (Problem 21, case $r=n-1<n$, whose proof does not use the present problem).

**Step 2: joining them.** Consider $E(s)=\operatorname{diag}(1,\dots,1,s)$, $s\in[-1,1]$. For $s>0$, $E(s)\in GL_n^+$; for $s<0$, $E(s)\in GL_n^-$; and $E(0)$ has rank $n-1$. So the path $s\mapsto E(s)$ runs inside $\mathcal{R}$ from a point of $GL_n^-$ ($s=-1$) through a point of $\mathcal{M}_{n-1}$ ($s=0$) to a point of $GL_n^+$ ($s=1$).

**Step 3: union of connected sets.** Let $X_1=GL_n^+$, $X_2=\mathcal{M}_{n-1}$, $X_3=GL_n^-$. Each is path connected, $X_1\cup X_2$ is path connected because the path $E(s)$, $s\in[0,1]$, links a point of $X_2$ to a point of $X_1$, and similarly $X_2\cup X_3$. Hence $\mathcal{R}=X_1\cup X_2\cup X_3$ is path connected.

**Remark.** The condition rank $\ge n-1$ is sharp: the set of matrices of rank $\ge n-2$ is also connected, but the argument for rank $\ge n-1$ is what allows the determinant to change sign continuously. In particular, a continuous path from $GL_n^+$ to $GL_n^-$ in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ *must* pass through singular matrices (intermediate value theorem), but need not pass through a matrix of rank $<n-1$.
@@@ mn-conn-fixed-rank.md
---
title: Matrices of fixed rank
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 3
tags: rank, singular value decomposition, path connected
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\mathcal{M}_r=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\operatorname{rank}A=r\}$ is path connected for $1\le r<n$, and has exactly two components for $r=n$.

## Hint

Use the singular value decomposition $A=P\Sigma Q^T$ and check that $P$ and $Q$ can be taken in $SO(n)$ when $r<n$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The singular value decomposition parametrises $\mathcal{M}_r$ by rotations and positive numbers, all of which are connected.

**Step 1: SVD.** Every $A$ of rank $r$ can be written $A=P\Sigma Q^T$ with $P,Q\in O(n)$ and
$$\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_r,0,\dots,0),\quad\sigma_i>0.$$

**Step 2: for $r<n$, we may assume $P,Q\in SO(n)$.** Let $D=\operatorname{diag}(1,\dots,1,-1)$. Since $r<n$, the last diagonal entry of $\Sigma$ is $0$, so $D\Sigma D=\Sigma$. Therefore
$$(PD)\,\Sigma\,(QD)^T=PD\Sigma DQ^T=P\Sigma Q^T=A.$$
If $\det P=-1$ replace $P$ by $PD\in SO(n)$; if $\det Q=-1$ replace $Q$ by $QD\in SO(n)$ (independently).

**Step 3: the path ($r<n$).** Choose paths $P_t,Q_t$ in $SO(n)$ from $P,Q$ to $I$ (Problem 16) and let $\Sigma_t=\operatorname{diag}((1-t)\sigma_1+t,\dots,(1-t)\sigma_r+t,0,\dots,0)$. Then
$$A_t=P_t\Sigma_tQ_t^T$$
is continuous, has rank exactly $r$ for all $t$ (the first $r$ diagonal entries of $\Sigma_t$ stay positive, and rank is preserved by multiplication by invertible matrices), and goes from $A$ to $E_r=\operatorname{diag}(I_r,0)$. Every matrix of rank $r$ is joined to $E_r$ inside $\mathcal{M}_r$, so $\mathcal{M}_r$ is path connected.

**Step 4: the case $r=n$.** $\mathcal{M}_n=GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ has exactly two components $GL_n^+$ and $GL_n^-$ (Problems 2 and 8).

**Remark.** The argument fails for $r=n$ precisely because $D\Sigma D\ne\Sigma$ is not a symmetry any more: the sign of $\det P\det Q$ cannot be changed without changing $A$. Topologically, $\mathcal{M}_r$ is a connected manifold of dimension $r(2n-r)$.
@@@ mn-conn-complement-of-sl-n.md
---
title: The complement of SLâ‚™(â„)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 3
tags: determinant, level sets, components, SL_n
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that for $n\ge2$ the set $\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\det A\ne1\}$ has exactly two connected components, $\{\det<1\}$ and $\{\det>1\}$.

## Hint

Write $A=|d|^{1/n}A_1$ with $d=\det A$ and $\det A_1=\pm1$, and move $A_1$ inside its (connected) level set.

## Solution

**Idea.** Show that $\{\det>1\}$ and $\{\det<1\}$ are each connected; they are clearly disjoint open sets covering the set.

**Step 0: ingredients.** $\{\det>1\}$ and $\{\det<1\}$ are open (preimages of open intervals under the continuous determinant), disjoint, and non-empty ($2^{1/n}I$ and $0$). By Problem 9, $\det^{-1}(1)=SL_n$ and $\det^{-1}(-1)$ are path connected.

**Step 1: $\{\det>1\}$ is path connected.** Let $\det A=d>1$ and $A=d^{1/n}A_1$ with $A_1\in SL_n$.
- Take a path $A_1(s)$ in $SL_n$ from $A_1$ to $I$. Then $d^{1/n}A_1(s)$ has determinant $d$ for all $s$, and runs from $A$ to $d^{1/n}I$.
- Then move along $c\,I$, $c$ between $d^{1/n}$ and $2^{1/n}$ (monotonically). Its determinant $c^n$ lies between $d$ and $2$, hence is $>1$.

So $A$ is joined within $\{\det>1\}$ to the fixed matrix $2^{1/n}I$.

**Step 2: $\{\det<1\}$ is path connected.** We show that every $A$ with $\det A<1$ is joined to $0$ inside $\{\det<1\}$.
- *$\det A=0$.* The ray $tA$ has $\det=0<1$.
- *$\det A=d\in(0,1)$.* As in step 1, $A$ is joined, at constant determinant $d$, to $d^{1/n}I$; then $c\mapsto cI$ with $c$ decreasing from $d^{1/n}$ to $0$ has $\det=c^n\le d<1$.
- *$\det A=d<0$.* $A=|d|^{1/n}A_1$ with $\det A_1=-1$; by connectedness of $\det^{-1}(-1)$, $A$ is joined at constant determinant $d$ to $|d|^{1/n}D$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$. Then $c\mapsto cD$ for $c\searrow0$ has $\det=-c^n\le0<1$.

**Step 3: conclusion.** The set $\{\det\ne1\}$ is the disjoint union of two non-empty, open, path connected sets; these are its two components.

**Remark.** For $n=1$ the statement is also true (it is just $\mathbb{R}\setminus\{1\}$), but the proof above uses $n\ge2$ only through the connectedness of $SL_n$. Compare with Problem 22's contrast: the complement of $SL_n$ is *disconnected*, whereas the complement of $O(n)$ is connected (Problem 14), because $SL_n$ is a hypersurface (codimension $1$) and $O(n)$ is not.
@@@ mn-conn-idempotents-and-involutions.md
---
title: Components of the sets of idempotents and involutions
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l2
difficulty: 3
tags: idempotent, involution, trace, rank, conjugacy orbits
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\{P\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):P^2=P\}$ has exactly $n+1$ connected components (indexed by the rank) and that $\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):A^2=I\}$ has exactly $n+1$ components (indexed by the trace).

## Hint

Rank and trace are *integer-valued* continuous invariants on these sets. Then show that each fibre is a single orbit connected through $GL_n^+$.

## Solution

**Part 1: idempotents.**

*Step 1: rank = trace.* If $P^2=P$, then $P$ is conjugate to $E_k=\operatorname{diag}(I_k,0)$ with $k=\operatorname{rank}P$ (Problem 15), so $\operatorname{tr}P=k$. On the set $\mathcal{P}$ of idempotents, $\operatorname{tr}$ is continuous and integer-valued with values in $\{0,\dots,n\}$.

*Step 2: the pieces are clopen.* $\mathcal{P}_k=\mathcal{P}\cap\operatorname{tr}^{-1}(\{k\})$ is closed and open in $\mathcal{P}$ (as the preimage of an isolated value under a continuous map to $\mathbb{Z}$), and $\mathcal{P}=\bigsqcup_{k=0}^n\mathcal{P}_k$, all non-empty.

*Step 3: each piece is connected.* $\mathcal{P}_0=\{0\}$, $\mathcal{P}_n=\{I\}$, and for $0<k<n$, $\mathcal{P}_k$ is path connected (Problem 15).

Hence $\mathcal{P}$ has exactly $n+1$ components.

**Part 2: involutions.**

*Step 1: diagonalisation.* If $A^2=I$ the minimal polynomial divides $x^2-1=(x-1)(x+1)$, which has simple real roots, so $A$ is diagonalisable over $\mathbb{R}$ with eigenvalues $\pm1$. If $p$ is the multiplicity of $+1$, $A$ is conjugate to $F_p=\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_{n-p})$ and $\operatorname{tr}A=2p-n$. So $\operatorname{tr}$ takes the $n+1$ values $-n,-n+2,\dots,n$ on $\mathcal{I}=\{A^2=I\}$, and as above the $n+1$ pieces $\mathcal{I}_p=\{A\in\mathcal{I}:\operatorname{tr}A=2p-n\}$ are clopen in $\mathcal{I}$.

*Step 2: each piece is connected.* $\mathcal{I}_0=\{-I\}$ and $\mathcal{I}_n=\{I\}$. For $0<p<n$, $\mathcal{I}_p=\{QF_pQ^{-1}:Q\in GL_n\}$. The matrix $D=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$ is diagonal so it commutes with $F_p$, and $\det D=-1$. As in Problem 15, replacing $Q$ by $QD$ we may take $\det Q>0$. So $\mathcal{I}_p$ is the continuous image of the path connected $GL_n^+$.

Therefore $\mathcal{I}$ has exactly $n+1$ components.

**Remark.** In both cases the invariant (rank, resp. trace) is *discrete*, which is what splits the sets into pieces; the dimension of $\mathcal{P}_k$ is $2k(n-k)$, and of $\mathcal{I}_p$ also $2p(n-p)$.
@@@ mn-conn-invertible-symmetric-matrices.md
---
title: Components of the invertible symmetric matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l3
difficulty: 4
tags: symmetric matrices, signature, eigenvalues, Weyl inequality
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the set of invertible symmetric matrices in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ has exactly $n+1$ path components, indexed by the number $k\in\{0,\dots,n\}$ of positive eigenvalues.

## Hint

Show that $k$ is locally constant, and that each class $\{k\text{ positive eigenvalues}\}$ is the image of a connected set under $(Q,d)\mapsto Q\operatorname{diag}(d)Q^T$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The signature is a continuous integer-valued invariant (the eigenvalues cannot cross $0$ without making the matrix singular), and matrices with the same signature are connected through the spectral theorem.

**Step 1: continuity of eigenvalues.** Let $\mathrm{Sym}_n^*$ be the set of invertible symmetric matrices and $\lambda_1(S)\ge\dots\ge\lambda_n(S)$ the ordered eigenvalues of $S$. By *Weyl's inequality*,
$$|\lambda_i(S)-\lambda_i(S')|\le\|S-S'\|\quad(\text{operator norm}),$$
so each $\lambda_i$ is continuous (even $1$-Lipschitz) on $\mathrm{Sym}_n$.

**Step 2: the classes are clopen.** On $\mathrm{Sym}_n^*$ no $\lambda_i$ vanishes. The number of positive eigenvalues is $k(S)=\#\{i:\lambda_i(S)>0\}$. Since each $\lambda_i$ is continuous and never $0$ on $\mathrm{Sym}_n^*$, the sign of each $\lambda_i$ is locally constant, hence so is $k$. So the classes $\mathcal{C}_k=\{S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n^*:k(S)=k\}$, $k=0,\dots,n$, are open, disjoint, non-empty (they contain $\operatorname{diag}(I_k,-I_{n-k})$) and cover $\mathrm{Sym}_n^*$; each is also closed in $\mathrm{Sym}_n^*$ as a complement of the union of the others.

**Step 3: each class is connected.** By the spectral theorem every $S\in\mathcal{C}_k$ is $S=Q\operatorname{diag}(d)Q^T$ with $Q\in O(n)$ and $d\in\Delta_k=(0,\infty)^k\times(-\infty,0)^{n-k}$.
- *We may take $Q\in SO(n)$.* Let $D'=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$. Since $D'\operatorname{diag}(d)D'=\operatorname{diag}(d)$, $S=(QD')\operatorname{diag}(d)(QD')^T$ and one of $Q,QD'$ has determinant $1$.
- *Connected parameter space.* $SO(n)$ is path connected (Problem 16) and $\Delta_k$ is convex. So $SO(n)\times\Delta_k$ is path connected, and $\mathcal{C}_k$ is its continuous image under $(Q,d)\mapsto Q\operatorname{diag}(d)Q^T$.

**Conclusion.** The path components are exactly $\mathcal{C}_0,\dots,\mathcal{C}_n$: $n+1$ of them.

**Remark.** $\mathcal{C}_n$ is the cone of positive definite matrices and $\mathcal{C}_0$ its negative. $\mathcal{C}_k$ for $0<k<n$ are the sets of *quadratic forms of signature $(k,n-k)$*, in the classification of quadratic forms by Sylvester's law of inertia. For $n=2$ there are three components: the two cones $\{ac>b^2,a>0\}$, $\{ac>b^2,a<0\}$ and the "inside of the light cone" $\{ac<b^2\}$.
@@@ mn-conn-deformation-retraction.md
---
title: SO(n) is a deformation retract of GLâ‚™âº(â„)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l3
difficulty: 4
tags: deformation retraction, homotopy equivalence, polar decomposition
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the inclusion $SO(n)\hookrightarrow GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$ is a homotopy equivalence; more precisely, $SO(n)$ is a strong deformation retract of $GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Use the polar decomposition $A=UP$ and deform $P$ to $I$ linearly.

## Solution

**Idea.** $GL_n^+\cong SO(n)\times\mathcal{P}_n$ where $\mathcal{P}_n$ is the (convex, hence contractible) cone of positive definite matrices.

**Step 1: continuity of the polar decomposition.** For $A\in GL_n^+$ put $P(A)=(A^TA)^{1/2}$ and $U(A)=AP(A)^{-1}$. The map $A\mapsto A^TA$ is continuous (polynomial), the positive square root is continuous on positive definite matrices (e.g. as the inverse of the continuous bijection $P\mapsto P^2$ of $\mathcal{P}_n$ which is a homeomorphism by the spectral theorem and the continuity of the eigen-projections; or by the holomorphic functional calculus $P^{1/2}=\frac1{2\pi i}\oint\sqrt z\,(z-P)^{-1}dz$), and the inverse is continuous. So
$$\Phi:GL_n^+\to SO(n)\times\mathcal{P}_n,\quad A\mapsto(U(A),P(A))$$
is continuous, with continuous inverse $(U,P)\mapsto UP$. (We showed $U(A)\in SO(n)$ when $\det A>0$ in Problem 8.) So $\Phi$ is a homeomorphism.

**Step 2: the deformation.** Define $H:GL_n^+\times[0,1]\to GL_n^+$,
$$H(A,t)=U(A)\big((1-t)P(A)+tI\big).$$
- $H$ is continuous (composition of continuous maps), and $(1-t)P+tI$ is positive definite so $H(A,t)\in GL_n^+$ (its determinant is $\det U(A)\det\big((1-t)P+tI\big)>0$).
- $H(A,0)=U(A)P(A)=A$.
- $H(A,1)=U(A)\in SO(n)$.
- If $A\in SO(n)$ then $P(A)=I$, $U(A)=A$ and $H(A,t)=A$ for all $t$.

**Step 3: conclusion.** $H$ is a deformation retraction of $GL_n^+$ onto $SO(n)$ fixing $SO(n)$ pointwise. So the inclusion $i:SO(n)\to GL_n^+$ and the retraction $r(A)=U(A)$ satisfy $r\circ i=\mathrm{id}$ and $i\circ r\simeq\mathrm{id}$ via $H$: they are inverse homotopy equivalences.

**Remark.** Consequently, all homotopy invariants of $GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$ (fundamental group, homology) are those of $SO(n)$: for instance $\pi_1(GL_2^+)=\pi_1(S^1)=\mathbb{Z}$ (Problem 30), and $\pi_1(GL_n^+)=\mathbb{Z}/2$ for $n\ge3$.
@@@ mn-conn-distinct-real-eigenvalues.md
---
title: Matrices with n distinct real eigenvalues
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l3
difficulty: 4
tags: diagonalisable matrices, Weyl chamber, conjugation, path connected
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the set $\mathcal{D}$ of matrices in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ having $n$ distinct real eigenvalues is path connected.

## Hint

Every such matrix is $PDP^{-1}$ with $D$ diagonal with *strictly increasing* entries. Show that $P$ can be chosen in $GL_n^+$.

## Solution

**Idea.** $\mathcal{D}$ is the image of $GL_n^+\times\mathcal{C}$, with $\mathcal{C}$ the convex cone of strictly increasing diagonals, under a continuous map.

**Step 1: parametrisation.** Let $A\in\mathcal{D}$ with eigenvalues $\lambda_1<\dots<\lambda_n$. The eigenvectors $v_i$ ($Av_i=\lambda_iv_i$) are linearly independent (eigenvectors for distinct eigenvalues), so $P=(v_1|\dots|v_n)\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ and
$$A=P\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n)P^{-1}.$$

**Step 2: $\det P>0$ can be arranged.** Replacing $v_1$ by $-v_1$ changes $\det P$ to $-\det P$ and does not change $A$ (equivalently, $P\mapsto PD'$ with $D'=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$ commutes with the diagonal matrix). So we may take $P\in GL_n^+$.

**Step 3: the parameter space.** $\mathcal{C}=\{(\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n):\lambda_1<\dots<\lambda_n\}\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ is convex (an intersection of open half-spaces), hence path connected. $GL_n^+$ is path connected (Problem 8). So $GL_n^+\times\mathcal{C}$ is path connected.

**Step 4: the map.** $\Theta:GL_n^+\times\mathcal{C}\to M_n(\mathbb{R})$, $\Theta(P,\lambda)=P\operatorname{diag}(\lambda)P^{-1}$ is continuous (the inverse is a rational function with denominator $\det P\ne0$), and by Steps 1 and 2 its image is exactly $\mathcal{D}$. Therefore $\mathcal{D}$ is the continuous image of a path connected set.

**Conclusion.** $\mathcal{D}$ is path connected.

**Remark.** The Weyl chamber $\mathcal{C}$ (ordering the eigenvalues) is what removes the ambiguity of permuting the eigenvectors. The proof shows more: $\mathcal{D}$ is an open dense subset of the set of matrices with real spectrum. By contrast the set of matrices with $n$ distinct (possibly complex) eigenvalues is also connected when $n\ge2$ but requires a different argument.
@@@ mn-conn-two-by-two-matrices-without-real-eigenvalues.md
---
title: 2Ã—2 matrices without real eigenvalues
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l3
difficulty: 4
tags: 2x2 matrices, discriminant, complex eigenvalues, components
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that the set $\mathcal{E}=\{A\in M_2(\mathbb{R}):A\text{ has no real eigenvalue}\}$ has exactly two path components.

## Hint

Write $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$. The characteristic polynomial has no real root iff its discriminant $(a-d)^2+4bc$ is negative. What does this say about $c$?

## Solution

**Idea.** The sign of the lower-left entry $c$ is an invariant, and each sign class is described by explicit inequalities.

**Step 1: description.** The characteristic polynomial of $A$ is $x^2-(a+d)x+(ad-bc)$, with discriminant
$$\Delta=(a+d)^2-4(ad-bc)=(a-d)^2+4bc.$$
$A\in\mathcal{E}$ iff $\Delta<0$, i.e.
$$(a-d)^2<-4bc.$$

**Step 2: sign of $c$.** The left side is $\ge0$, so $-4bc>0$, i.e. $bc<0$. In particular $c\ne0$ (and $b\neq0$, with $b$ of sign opposite to $c$). So $\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_+\sqcup\mathcal{E}_-$ with $\mathcal{E}_\pm=\mathcal{E}\cap\{\pm c>0\}$. Both are open (intersections of the open set $\{\Delta<0\}$ with open half-spaces), disjoint and non-empty (they contain $\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ and its transpose).

**Step 3: $\mathcal{E}_+$ is path connected.** $A\in\mathcal{E}_+$ iff $c>0$, $b<0$ and $|a-d|<2\sqrt{-bc}$. Use the coordinates $(b,c,a,\delta)$ with $\delta=a-d$ (a linear change of coordinates, hence a homeomorphism of $\mathbb{R}^4$):
$$\mathcal{E}_+\cong\{(b,c,a,\delta):b<0<c,\ a\in\mathbb{R},\ |\delta|<2\sqrt{-bc}\}.$$
The base $\{(b,c):b<0<c\}$ is convex (a quadrant). Over each point of the base the fibre $\{(a,\delta)\}$ is $\mathbb{R}\times(-2\sqrt{-bc},2\sqrt{-bc})$, convex, and the bounds depend continuously on $(b,c)$. To join two points $(b_0,c_0,a_0,\delta_0)$ and $(b_1,c_1,a_1,\delta_1)$, first shrink $\delta$ linearly to $0$ (the inequality $|\delta|<2\sqrt{-bc}$ remains true), then move $(b,c)$ along the segment while keeping $\delta=0$ and moving $a$ linearly, then expand $\delta$ linearly to $\delta_1$ (allowed because the constraint at the new $(b_1,c_1)$ is $|\delta_1|<2\sqrt{-b_1c_1}$).

**Step 4: same for $\mathcal{E}_-$**, with $c<0<b$. It is homeomorphic to $\mathcal{E}_+$ via $A\mapsto A^T$ (which swaps $b$ and $c$).

**Conclusion.** $\mathcal{E}$ has exactly two path components, $\mathcal{E}_+$ and $\mathcal{E}_-$.

**Remark.** Geometrically, $A\in\mathcal{E}_\pm$ determines an *orientation*: $A$ acts like a rotation in the plane, counterclockwise for $c>0$ and clockwise for $c<0$ (the sign of $c$ is the sign of $\det\big(v,Av\big)$ for any vector $v\ne0$). This invariant has no analogue for matrices with real eigenvalues.
@@@ mn-conn-complex-structures-on-the-plane.md
---
title: Complex structures on â„Â²
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l3
difficulty: 4
tags: complex structure, A squared = -I, 2x2 matrices, homeomorphism
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $\mathcal{J}=\{A\in M_2(\mathbb{R}):A^2=-I\}$ has exactly two connected components, each homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^2$.

## Hint

Write $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&-a\end{pmatrix}$ and use the equation $a^2+bc=-1$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Describe $\mathcal{J}$ by one equation in three variables and solve for $b$.

**Step 1: $A\in\mathcal{J}$ has trace $0$ and determinant $1$.** If $A^2=-I$, the eigenvalues satisfy $\lambda^2=-1$, so they are $\pm i$ (complex, not real, so the two eigenvalues of the real $2\times2$ matrix are $i$ and $-i$ as it has no real eigenvalue). Hence $\operatorname{tr}A=i+(-i)=0$ and $\det A=i\cdot(-i)=1$. (Alternatively: by Cayleyâ€“Hamilton $A^2-(\operatorname{tr}A)A+(\det A)I=0$, so $(\operatorname{tr}A)A=(\det A+1)I$; if $\operatorname{tr}A\ne0$, $A$ would be a scalar matrix with $A^2=-I$, impossible over $\mathbb{R}$.)

**Step 2: equation.** Hence $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&-a\end{pmatrix}$ and $A^2=(a^2+bc)I$. So
$$\mathcal{J}=\{(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{R}^3:a^2+bc=-1\},$$
(a two-sheeted hyperboloid $bc=-1-a^2$ in the coordinates $(a,b,c)$).

**Step 3: $c\ne0$.** From $bc=-(1+a^2)<0$ we get $c\neq0$ and $b=-(1+a^2)/c$. So $\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}_+\sqcup\mathcal{J}_-$ with $\mathcal{J}_\pm=\mathcal{J}\cap\{\pm c>0\}$ (open in $\mathcal{J}$, disjoint, non-empty: they contain $\pm J$ with $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$).

**Step 4: each part is a plane.** The map
$$\mathcal{J}_+\to\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty),\quad A\mapsto(a,c)$$
is continuous, with continuous inverse $(a,c)\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}a&-(1+a^2)/c\\c&-a\end{pmatrix}$. So $\mathcal{J}_+\cong\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)\cong\mathbb{R}^2$, which is connected. Similarly $\mathcal{J}_-\cong\mathbb{R}\times(-\infty,0)\cong\mathbb{R}^2$.

**Conclusion.** $\mathcal{J}$ has exactly two connected components, each homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^2$.

**Remark.** The sign of $c$ is the *orientation* of the complex structure defined by $A$ (a map $A$ with $A^2=-I$ makes $\mathbb{R}^2$ a complex line with $i$ acting as $A$; the structure is compatible with the standard orientation iff $c>0$). This set is the "two-sheeted hyperboloid" $\simeq GL_2(\mathbb{R})/GL_1(\mathbb{C})$.
@@@ mn-conn-conjugacy-orbits.md
---
title: Connectedness of conjugacy orbits
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: l3
difficulty: 4
tags: conjugacy orbits, centraliser, group action, rotation matrix
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Let $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and let $C(A)=\{Z\in GL_n(\mathbb{R}):ZA=AZ\}$ be its centraliser. The conjugacy orbit of $A$ is $\mathcal{O}(A)=\{PAP^{-1}:P\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})\}$.

1. Show that if $C(A)$ contains a matrix of negative determinant, then $\mathcal{O}(A)$ is path connected.
2. For $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ show that $\mathcal{O}(J)$ has exactly two components.

## Hint

The orbit is the image of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ under $P\mapsto PAP^{-1}$. In part 2 compute the lower-left entry of $PJP^{-1}$.

## Solution

**1. A centraliser element of negative determinant.** Let $Z\in C(A)$ with $\det Z<0$ and $P\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ with $\det P<0$. Then $\det(PZ)>0$ and
$$(PZ)A(PZ)^{-1}=PZAZ^{-1}P^{-1}=PAP^{-1},$$
because $ZAZ^{-1}=A$. So every element of $\mathcal{O}(A)$ can be written $QAQ^{-1}$ with $\det Q>0$, i.e.
$$\mathcal{O}(A)=\{QAQ^{-1}:Q\in GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})\}.$$
The map $Q\mapsto QAQ^{-1}$ is continuous on $GL_n^+$ (the inverse is a rational function of the entries with denominator $\det Q$), and $GL_n^+$ is path connected (Problem 8). Hence $\mathcal{O}(A)$ is path connected.

**2. The orbit of $J$.**

*Centraliser.* A matrix $Z=\begin{pmatrix}p&q\\r&s\end{pmatrix}$ commutes with $J$ iff $ZJ=JZ$, which gives $s=p$ and $r=-q$: $Z=pI+(-q)J$... precisely $Z=\begin{pmatrix}p&q\\-q&p\end{pmatrix}$, with $\det Z=p^2+q^2>0$. So *every* element of $C(J)$ has positive determinant, and part 1 does not apply.

*Lower-left entry.* For $P=\begin{pmatrix}p&q\\r&s\end{pmatrix}\in GL_2$, a direct computation gives
$$PJP^{-1}=\frac1{\det P}\begin{pmatrix}pr+qs&-(p^2+q^2)\\ r^2+s^2&-(pr+qs)\end{pmatrix}.$$
So the lower-left entry of $PJP^{-1}$ is $\dfrac{r^2+s^2}{\det P}$, which has the **sign of $\det P$** (as $r^2+s^2>0$ for an invertible matrix).

*Conclusion.* The continuous function "lower-left entry" takes positive values on $\{PJP^{-1}:\det P>0\}$ and negative values on $\{PJP^{-1}:\det P<0\}$, both non-empty. So $\mathcal{O}(J)$ is the disjoint union of two non-empty open (relatively) sets $\{c>0\}$, $\{c<0\}$. Each is connected (image of $GL_2^+$, resp. $GL_2^-=D\cdot GL_2^+$). Hence $\mathcal{O}(J)$ has exactly two components. (This matches Problem 28, since $\mathcal{O}(J)=\{A:A^2=-I\}$: any real $2\times2$ matrix with $A^2=-I$ is conjugate to $J$.)

**Remark.** The criterion in part 1 is sufficient but not necessary for connectedness of an orbit. It captures the intuition that an orbit of the *connected-component group* $GL_n^+$ is connected, and the orbit of $GL_n^-$ is its mirror image; the orbit is connected exactly when these two images overlap.

**Note.** The source solution calls the orbit "$\{A:A^2=-I\}$ with two components (previous problem)"; the explicit formula for $PJP^{-1}$ above proves both the identification of the components with the orientation $\operatorname{sign}\det P$ and that conjugating by an orientation-reversing $P$ really changes the component.
@@@ mn-conn-the-fundamental-group-of-gl-2.md
---
title: GLâ‚‚âº(â„) is not simply connected
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-connectedness
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: fundamental group, deformation retraction, circle, homotopy
date: 2026-11-07
---

## Statement

Show that $GL_2^+(\mathbb{R})=\{A\in M_2(\mathbb{R}):\det A>0\}$ is path connected but not simply connected. Describe a loop that is not null-homotopic.

## Hint

Retract $GL_2^+(\mathbb{R})$ onto $SO(2)$, which is a circle, and use $\pi_1(S^1)=\mathbb{Z}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** A space and a deformation retract of it have the same fundamental group.

**Step 1: path connected.** Proved in Problem 8 (together with Problem 6 for $SO(2)$).

**Step 2: $SO(2)$ is a circle.** The map $\theta\mapsto R_\theta=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\ \sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$ induces a homeomorphism $\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\to SO(2)$ (continuous bijection from a compact space to a Hausdorff space, Problem 6). So $SO(2)\cong S^1$ and $\pi_1(SO(2),I)\cong\mathbb{Z}$, generated by the loop $\ell(\theta)=R_{2\pi\theta}$, $\theta\in[0,1]$.

**Step 3: deformation retraction.** By Problem 25, $SO(2)$ is a strong deformation retract of $GL_2^+(\mathbb{R})$: there is a continuous $H:GL_2^+\times[0,1]\to GL_2^+$ with $H(\cdot,0)=\mathrm{id}$, $H(\cdot,1)=r$ where $r:GL_2^+\to SO(2)$ is a retraction ($r|_{SO(2)}=\mathrm{id}$). Explicitly $H(A,t)=U(A)((1-t)P(A)+tI)$ with $A=U(A)P(A)$ the polar decomposition. Hence the inclusion $i:SO(2)\to GL_2^+$ induces an isomorphism
$$i_*:\pi_1(SO(2),I)\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\pi_1(GL_2^+,I).$$

**Step 4: conclusion.** $\pi_1(GL_2^+(\mathbb{R}),I)\cong\mathbb{Z}\ne0$. The loop $\ell(\theta)=R_{2\pi\theta}$ is a generator and is not null-homotopic in $GL_2^+$.

**A direct invariant.** The retraction gives an explicit "winding number": to a loop $\gamma$ in $GL_2^+$ associate the loop $r\circ\gamma$ in $SO(2)\cong S^1$; the winding number is the degree. Equivalently, for $\gamma(\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$, it is the winding number around $0$ of the complex number $(a+d)+i(c-b)$, which never vanishes when $\det\gamma>0$ (indeed $|a+d|^2+|c-b|^2=(a^2+b^2+c^2+d^2)+2\det\gamma>0$).

**Remark.** For $n\ge3$, $\pi_1(SO(n))=\mathbb{Z}/2$ (the "plate trick"), so $\pi_1(GL_n^+)=\mathbb{Z}/2$: for $n=2$ the fundamental group is $\mathbb{Z}$, which is exceptional. The universal cover of $GL_2^+(\mathbb{R})$ is a non-matrix group.

@@@ mnspec-trace-and-determinant-bounds-by-the-operator-norm.md
---
title: Trace and determinant bounded by the operator norm
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: operator norm, singular values, trace, determinant
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

On $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ we use the operator norm $\|A\|=\sup_{|x|=1}|Ax|$ (the largest singular value $\sigma_1(A)$). Show that for every $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$
$$|\operatorname{tr}A|\le n\|A\|,\qquad |\det A|\le\|A\|^n.$$

## Hint

For the determinant, write $|\det A|$ as a product of singular values. For the trace, write $\operatorname{tr}A=\sum_i e_i^TAe_i$ and use Cauchyâ€“Schwarz.

## Solution

**Trace.** In the standard basis, $\operatorname{tr}A=\sum_{i=1}^n e_i^TAe_i$. For each $i$, by Cauchyâ€“Schwarz,
$$|e_i^TAe_i|\le|e_i|\,|Ae_i|\le\|A\|\,|e_i|^2=\|A\|.$$
Summing the $n$ terms gives $|\operatorname{tr}A|\le n\|A\|$.

**Determinant.** Let $A=U\Sigma V^T$ be a singular value decomposition, with $U,V$ orthogonal and $\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_1\ge\dots\ge\sigma_n\ge0)$. Orthogonal matrices have determinant $\pm1$, hence
$$|\det A|=|\det U|\,\det\Sigma\,|\det V|=\sigma_1\sigma_2\cdots\sigma_n\le\sigma_1^n=\|A\|^n.$$

**Check.** For $A=\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$: $\|A\|=2$, $\operatorname{tr}A=3\le4$ and $\det A=2\le4$.

**Remark.** Both bounds are attained by $A=I$ (trace) and by any orthogonal matrix times a scalar (determinant). The inequality $|\det A|\le\|A\|^n$ says that $\det$ is a polynomial map of degree $n$ with a controlled growth, which is useful to prove its continuity and to estimate the distance of a matrix to the singular matrices.
@@@ mnspec-all-norm-spheres-are-homeomorphic.md
---
title: Every norm sphere of M_n(â„) is a sphere
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: norms, sphere, homeomorphism, radial projection
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $N$ be any norm on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and $S_N=\{A:N(A)=1\}$ its unit sphere. Show that $S_N$ is homeomorphic to the Euclidean sphere $S^{n^2-1}$.

## Hint

Compare $S_N$ with the Frobenius sphere $S_F=\{\|A\|_F=1\}$ by radial projection. Both $N$ and $\|\cdot\|_F$ are continuous.

## Solution

**Setup.** $M_n(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$, and $\|\cdot\|_F$ is the Euclidean norm of this space, so $S_F=S^{n^2-1}$. All norms on a finite dimensional space are equivalent, hence $N$ is continuous for the usual topology, and $N(A)>0$ for $A\ne0$.

**The two maps.** Define
$$\varphi:S_N\to S_F,\ \ \varphi(A)=\frac{A}{\|A\|_F},\qquad\psi:S_F\to S_N,\ \ \psi(B)=\frac{B}{N(B)}.$$
They are well defined: $A\in S_N$ is nonzero so $\|A\|_F>0$, and $B\in S_F$ is nonzero so $N(B)>0$. They are continuous, being quotients of continuous functions with nonvanishing denominator.

**They are inverse to each other.** Take $A\in S_N$ and set $B=\varphi(A)=A/\|A\|_F$. Then $N(B)=N(A)/\|A\|_F=1/\|A\|_F$, so
$$\psi(B)=\frac{B}{N(B)}=\frac{A/\|A\|_F}{1/\|A\|_F}=A.$$
Symmetrically $\varphi\circ\psi=\mathrm{id}$.

**Conclusion.** $\varphi$ is a homeomorphism $S_N\to S^{n^2-1}$.

**Remark.** The same argument works for every norm on any $\mathbb{R}^m$: all unit spheres are homeomorphic to $S^{m-1}$, and the unit balls are homeomorphic to the closed ball. It also shows that "the" topology of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ does not depend on the chosen norm.
@@@ mnspec-strictly-diagonally-dominant-matrices.md
---
title: Strictly diagonally dominant matrices are invertible
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: diagonal dominance, Gershgorin, open sets, invertibility
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Call $A=(a_{ij})$ *strictly row diagonally dominant* if $|a_{ii}|>\sum_{j\ne i}|a_{ij}|$ for every $i$. Show that such a matrix is invertible, and that the set $\mathcal{D}$ of these matrices is an open subset of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

If $Ax=0$ with $x\ne0$, look at a coordinate $x_i$ of largest absolute value.

## Solution

**Invertibility.** Suppose $Ax=0$ with $x\ne0$. Choose $i$ with $|x_i|=\max_j|x_j|$; then $|x_i|>0$. The $i$-th equation reads $a_{ii}x_i=-\sum_{j\ne i}a_{ij}x_j$, so
$$|a_{ii}|\,|x_i|\le\sum_{j\ne i}|a_{ij}|\,|x_j|\le\Big(\sum_{j\ne i}|a_{ij}|\Big)|x_i|.$$
Dividing by $|x_i|>0$ gives $|a_{ii}|\le\sum_{j\ne i}|a_{ij}|$, contradicting dominance. So $\ker A=\{0\}$ and $A$ is invertible.

**Openness.** For each $i$, the function $f_i(A)=|a_{ii}|-\sum_{j\ne i}|a_{ij}|$ is continuous on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ (a combination of absolute values of coordinates). Therefore
$$\mathcal{D}=\bigcap_{i=1}^n f_i^{-1}\big((0,\infty)\big)$$
is a finite intersection of open sets, hence open. By the first part $\mathcal{D}\subset GL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Example.** $\begin{pmatrix}3&1\\-1&2\end{pmatrix}$ is dominant ($3>1$, $2>1$) with determinant $7\ne0$.

**Remark.** This is the real core of Gershgorin's theorem: every eigenvalue lies in a disc $|z-a_{ii}|\le\sum_{j\ne i}|a_{ij}|$, since $A-zI$ would otherwise be strictly dominant. Dominance is only sufficient: $\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ is invertible but not dominant.
@@@ mnspec-singular-values-form-a-closed-cone.md
---
title: The singular value map is continuous and onto the ordered cone
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: singular values, Weyl inequality, continuity, surjectivity
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

For $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ let $\sigma_1(A)\ge\dots\ge\sigma_n(A)\ge0$ be its singular values. Show that the map $\Sigma:A\mapsto(\sigma_1(A),\dots,\sigma_n(A))$ is continuous from $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ onto the closed cone $K=\{\sigma_1\ge\dots\ge\sigma_n\ge0\}$.

## Hint

Continuity is Weyl's inequality $|\sigma_k(A)-\sigma_k(B)|\le\|A-B\|$. For surjectivity use diagonal matrices.

## Solution

**Continuity.** By the Courantâ€“Fischer minâ€“max formula,
$$\sigma_k(A)=\max_{\dim V=k}\ \min_{x\in V,|x|=1}|Ax|.$$
If $|Ax|\le|Bx|+\|A-B\|$ for all unit $x$, then taking the $\min$ over $x\in V$ and the $\max$ over $V$ gives $\sigma_k(A)\le\sigma_k(B)+\|A-B\|$. Exchanging $A$ and $B$,
$$|\sigma_k(A)-\sigma_k(B)|\le\|A-B\|.$$
So each $\sigma_k$ is $1$-Lipschitz, hence $\Sigma$ is continuous.

**Image in $K$.** By definition $\sigma_1\ge\dots\ge\sigma_n\ge0$.

**Onto.** Given $(s_1\ge\dots\ge s_n\ge0)\in K$, the matrix $D=\operatorname{diag}(s_1,\dots,s_n)$ has $D^TD=\operatorname{diag}(s_i^2)$, so its singular values are exactly $s_1,\dots,s_n$ in this order.

**Remark.** The fibres of $\Sigma$ are the orbits $\{U D V:U,V\in O(n)\}$ of the action of $O(n)\times O(n)$: two matrices have the same singular values iff they are orthogonally equivalent. So $K$ is the quotient $M_n(\mathbb{R})/(O(n)\times O(n))$.
@@@ mnspec-the-pseudo-inverse-is-discontinuous.md
---
title: The Mooreâ€“Penrose pseudo-inverse is discontinuous
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: pseudo-inverse, discontinuity, rank, singular value decomposition
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

For $A=U\Sigma V^T$ (singular value decomposition) the Mooreâ€“Penrose pseudo-inverse is $A^+=V\Sigma^+U^T$, where $\Sigma^+$ inverts the nonzero diagonal entries and keeps the zeros. Show that $A\mapsto A^+$ is discontinuous at every matrix of rank $r<n$.

## Hint

Start with $A=0$ and $A_\varepsilon=\varepsilon e_1e_1^T$. For rank $r>0$, add a small multiple of a rank-one term built from singular vectors.

## Solution

**The case $A=0$.** $0^+=0$. Let $A_\varepsilon=\operatorname{diag}(\varepsilon,0,\dots,0)$ with $\varepsilon>0$. Then $A_\varepsilon\to0$ but $A_\varepsilon^+=\operatorname{diag}(1/\varepsilon,0,\dots,0)$ has norm $1/\varepsilon\to\infty$. So $A\mapsto A^+$ is not continuous at $0$ (it is not even bounded near $0$).

**General rank $r<n$.** Write $A=\sum_{i=1}^r\sigma_iu_iv_i^T$ with $\sigma_i>0$, where $(u_i)$ and $(v_i)$ are orthonormal families. Since $r<n$ we can complete the families and pick unit vectors $u_{r+1},v_{r+1}$ orthogonal to the previous ones. Put
$$A_\varepsilon=A+\varepsilon u_{r+1}v_{r+1}^T.$$
This is a singular value decomposition of $A_\varepsilon$ with $\sigma_{r+1}=\varepsilon>0$, so $\operatorname{rank}A_\varepsilon=r+1$ and
$$A_\varepsilon^+=\sum_{i=1}^r\sigma_i^{-1}v_iu_i^T+\varepsilon^{-1}v_{r+1}u_{r+1}^T,\qquad\|A_\varepsilon^+\|=\max(\sigma_r^{-1},\varepsilon^{-1})\ \xrightarrow[\varepsilon\to0]{}\ \infty.$$
But $\|A_\varepsilon-A\|=\varepsilon\to0$ while $A^+$ is a fixed matrix. So $A^+$ is not the limit of $A_\varepsilon^+$.

**Remark.** The pseudo-inverse is continuous exactly on the set of matrices of *locally constant rank*, i.e. on each stratum $\{\operatorname{rank}=r\}$ and at the invertible matrices (where $A^+=A^{-1}$). This is why numerical rank decisions ("is this singular value zero?") are delicate.
@@@ mnspec-closest-matrix-of-rank-at-most-r-exists.md
---
title: Existence of a closest matrix of rank at most r
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: low rank approximation, compactness, coercivity
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $N$ be any norm on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$, $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and $0\le r\le n$. Show that there exists $B_0$ with $\operatorname{rank}B_0\le r$ such that
$$N(A-B_0)=\min\{N(A-B):\operatorname{rank}B\le r\}.$$

## Hint

Show that $\mathcal{R}_r=\{\operatorname{rank}\le r\}$ is closed and restrict the minimisation to a compact subset.

## Solution

**Closedness of $\mathcal{R}_r$.** $\operatorname{rank}B\le r$ iff all minors of size $r+1$ vanish. Minors are polynomials in the entries, hence continuous, and $\mathcal{R}_r$ is an intersection of zero sets of continuous functions, so it is closed. (For $r=n$, $\mathcal{R}_r=M_n(\mathbb{R})$.)

**Reduction to a compact set.** $0\in\mathcal{R}_r$, so the minimum, if it exists, is at most $N(A)$. Consider
$$K=\mathcal{R}_r\cap\{B:N(B)\le2N(A)\}.$$
This is closed (closed $\cap$ closed ball) and bounded, so it is compact since $M_n(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$. If $B\in\mathcal{R}_r\setminus K$, then $N(B)>2N(A)$ and
$$N(A-B)\ge N(B)-N(A)>N(A)=N(A-0),$$
so such a $B$ is worse than $B=0\in K$.

**Existence.** $B\mapsto N(A-B)$ is continuous, so it attains its minimum on the nonempty compact set $K$, at some $B_0$. By the previous paragraph $B_0$ is a minimiser on all of $\mathcal{R}_r$.

**Remark.** Existence holds for every norm, but uniqueness may fail (for the operator norm there are many best rank-$r$ approximations when $\sigma_{r+1}=\sigma_{r+2}$ or even in general). For the Frobenius and operator norms the minimum is explicit: see the Eckartâ€“Young problem. The statement is false for the *set of rank exactly $r$* matrices, which is not closed.
@@@ mnspec-the-numerical-range-of-a-real-matrix.md
---
title: The numerical range of a real matrix is an interval
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: Rayleigh quotient, connectedness, compactness, symmetric part
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and $S=\frac{A+A^T}2$. Show that
$$\{x^TAx:\ x\in\mathbb{R}^n,\ |x|=1\}=[\lambda_{\min}(S),\lambda_{\max}(S)].$$

## Hint

Show that $x^TAx=x^TSx$. Then use the Rayleigh principle for the endpoints, and connectedness of the unit sphere for the interior.

## Solution

**Step 1: only the symmetric part matters.** $x^TAx$ is a real number, so it equals its transpose $x^TA^Tx$. Hence $x^TAx=\frac12(x^TAx+x^TA^Tx)=x^TSx$.

**Step 2: the endpoints.** $S$ is symmetric; let $\lambda_1\le\dots\le\lambda_n$ be its eigenvalues with an orthonormal eigenbasis $v_1,\dots,v_n$. For a unit vector $x=\sum c_iv_i$ we have $\sum c_i^2=1$ and
$$x^TSx=\sum_i\lambda_ic_i^2\in[\lambda_1,\lambda_n],$$
with equality at $x=v_1$ and $x=v_n$. So $\min$ and $\max$ of $x^TSx$ on the sphere are $\lambda_{\min}$ and $\lambda_{\max}$.

**Step 3: no gaps.** The function $x\mapsto x^TSx$ is continuous. For $n\ge2$ the unit sphere $S^{n-1}$ is connected, so its image is a connected subset of $\mathbb{R}$, i.e. an interval, and it is compact. An interval containing $\lambda_{\min}$ and $\lambda_{\max}$ and contained in $[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]$ is the whole of $[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]$. (For $n=1$ the sphere is $\{\pm1\}$ and both sides equal $\{a\}$.)

**Explicit path.** For a direct proof, $x(t)=\cos t\,v_1+\sin t\,v_n$ gives $x(t)^TSx(t)=\lambda_1\cos^2t+\lambda_n\sin^2t$, which sweeps $[\lambda_1,\lambda_n]$ as $t\in[0,\pi/2]$.

**Remark.** For complex matrices the numerical range is a convex set in $\mathbb{C}$ (Toeplitzâ€“Hausdorff theorem), not an interval. The real quadratic form only sees $S$, which is why a skew-symmetric $A$ has numerical range $\{0\}$.
@@@ mnspec-singular-matrices-are-a-null-set.md
---
title: Singular matrices have measure zero
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: Lebesgue measure, zero set of a polynomial, Fubini, determinant
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that $\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\det A=0\}$ has Lebesgue measure zero in $M_n(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$. Equivalently, $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ has full measure.

## Hint

Prove first that the zero set of any nonzero polynomial in $k$ variables is Lebesgue-null, by induction on $k$ and Fubini's theorem. Then note that $\det$ is a nonzero polynomial.

## Solution

**Lemma.** If $p\in\mathbb{R}[x_1,\dots,x_k]$ is not the zero polynomial, then $Z(p)=\{p=0\}$ has measure zero in $\mathbb{R}^k$.

*Proof by induction on $k$.* For $k=1$, a nonzero polynomial has finitely many roots. Let $k\ge2$ and write
$$p(x_1,\dots,x_k)=\sum_{j=0}^dq_j(x_1,\dots,x_{k-1})\,x_k^j,$$
with $q_d\not\equiv0$. By induction, $N=\{q_d=0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k-1}$ is null. For $x'=(x_1,\dots,x_{k-1})\notin N$, the polynomial $x_k\mapsto p(x',x_k)$ is nonzero, so its zero set in $\mathbb{R}$ is finite, hence null. By Fubiniâ€“Tonelli,
$$\lambda_k(Z(p))=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k-1}}\lambda_1\big(\{x_k:p(x',x_k)=0\}\big)\,dx'=0,$$
because the integrand vanishes for $x'\notin N$ and $N$ is null. $\square$

**Application.** $\det A$ is a polynomial of degree $n$ in the $n^2$ entries, and it is not the zero polynomial since $\det I=1$. By the lemma $\{\det=0\}$ is null.

**Remark.** The same proof shows that $\{\det=0\}$ is also nowhere dense: it is closed with empty interior (a null set cannot contain a ball). So a "random" matrix is almost surely invertible, and generic statements about invertible matrices extend to all matrices by continuity, a technique used throughout this topic.
@@@ mnspec-eigenvalues-are-only-holder-continuous.md
---
title: Eigenvalues are only HÃ¶lder continuous
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: eigenvalues, HÃ¶lder continuity, Jordan block, perturbation
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge2$, $J=J_n(0)$ the nilpotent Jordan block (ones on the superdiagonal), $E_{n1}$ the matrix unit with a single $1$ in position $(n,1)$, and $\varepsilon>0$. Compute the eigenvalues of $J+\varepsilon E_{n1}$ and deduce that the eigenvalues are not Lipschitz functions of the matrix.

## Hint

Compute the characteristic polynomial by expanding $\det(tI-M)$.

## Solution

**The matrix.** $M_\varepsilon=J+\varepsilon E_{n1}$ has $(M_\varepsilon)_{i,i+1}=1$ for $i<n$ and $(M_\varepsilon)_{n,1}=\varepsilon$, all other entries $0$. Equivalently $M_\varepsilon e_1=\varepsilon e_n$ and $M_\varepsilon e_{j}=e_{j-1}$ for $j\ge2$.

**Characteristic polynomial.** Let $x=(x_1,\dots,x_n)$ be an eigenvector for $\mu$: $M_\varepsilon x=\mu x$ reads $x_{i+1}=\mu x_i$ for $i<n$ and $\varepsilon x_1=\mu x_n$. Hence $x_k=\mu^{k-1}x_1$, $x_1\ne0$, and $\varepsilon x_1=\mu\cdot\mu^{n-1}x_1$, so $\mu^n=\varepsilon$. The $n$ numbers $\mu_k=\varepsilon^{1/n}e^{2\pi ik/n}$ are distinct, so
$$\chi_{M_\varepsilon}(t)=t^n-\varepsilon.$$

**Conclusion.** All eigenvalues of $M_\varepsilon$ have modulus $\varepsilon^{1/n}$, while those of $M_0=J$ are $0$. The perturbation has operator norm $\|M_\varepsilon-M_0\|=\varepsilon$, so
$$\max_k|\mu_k-0|=\varepsilon^{1/n}=\|M_\varepsilon-M_0\|^{1/n}\gg\|M_\varepsilon-M_0\|\qquad(\varepsilon\to0,\ n\ge2).$$
No inequality $|\mu-\mu'|\le C\|M-M'\|$ can hold near $J$.

**Example.** $n=2$, $\varepsilon=10^{-8}$: $\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\10^{-8}&0\end{pmatrix}$ has eigenvalues $\pm10^{-4}$, four orders of magnitude larger than the perturbation.

**Remark.** The exponent $1/n$ is optimal in general (Bauerâ€“Fike type bounds for non-diagonalisable matrices give HÃ¶lder $1/n$). For normal matrices the dependence is Lipschitz with constant $1$, as shown in the Bauerâ€“Fike problem below.
@@@ mnspec-power-sums-determine-the-characteristic-polynomial.md
---
title: Power sums determine the characteristic polynomial
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: Newton identities, power sums, characteristic polynomial, continuity
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that $A\mapsto(\operatorname{tr}A,\operatorname{tr}A^2,\dots,\operatorname{tr}A^n)$ is continuous on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and that it determines the characteristic polynomial $\chi_A$.

## Hint

If $\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n$ are the complex eigenvalues of $A$, then $\operatorname{tr}A^k=p_k(\lambda)=\sum_i\lambda_i^k$. Use Newton's identities.

## Solution

**Continuity.** The entries of $A^k$ are polynomials in the entries of $A$, and the trace is linear. So each $\operatorname{tr}A^k$ is a polynomial function of $A$, hence continuous.

**Link with the spectrum.** Over $\mathbb{C}$, triangularise $A=PTP^{-1}$ with diagonal $\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n$ (with algebraic multiplicities). Then $A^k=PT^kP^{-1}$ and $T^k$ is triangular with diagonal $\lambda_i^k$, so
$$\operatorname{tr}A^k=\sum_i\lambda_i^k=:p_k.$$

**Newton's identities.** Write $\chi_A(t)=\prod_i(t-\lambda_i)=t^n-e_1t^{n-1}+e_2t^{n-2}-\dots+(-1)^ne_n$ where $e_k$ are the elementary symmetric polynomials of the $\lambda_i$. Newton's identities state that for $1\le k\le n$
$$ke_k=\sum_{j=1}^k(-1)^{j-1}e_{k-j}\,p_j,\qquad e_0=1.$$
Solving successively gives $e_1=p_1$, $e_2=\frac{p_1^2-p_2}2$, $e_3=\frac{p_1^3-3p_1p_2+2p_3}6$, and so on: each $e_k$ is a polynomial in $p_1,\dots,p_k$ with rational coefficients (dividing by $k$ is allowed in characteristic $0$).

**Conclusion.** The coefficients of $\chi_A$ are polynomials in $\operatorname{tr}A,\dots,\operatorname{tr}A^n$. Hence two matrices with the same first $n$ power traces have the same characteristic polynomial.

**Check.** $n=2$: $\chi_A(t)=t^2-(\operatorname{tr}A)t+\frac{(\operatorname{tr}A)^2-\operatorname{tr}A^2}2$, and indeed $\det A=\frac{(\operatorname{tr}A)^2-\operatorname{tr}(A^2)}2$.

**Remark.** The division by $k$ is essential: over a field of characteristic $p\le n$ the power sums do not determine the characteristic polynomial.
@@@ mnspec-the-spectral-mapping-theorem-for-polynomials.md
---
title: The spectral mapping theorem for polynomials
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: spectrum, triangularisation, polynomial of a matrix
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ with complex spectrum $\operatorname{spec}A$, and let $p$ be a polynomial. Show that $\operatorname{spec}\big(p(A)\big)=p\big(\operatorname{spec}A\big)$ (and in fact the eigenvalues of $p(A)$ with multiplicities are the $p(\lambda_i)$).

## Hint

Work over $\mathbb{C}$ and triangularise $A$.

## Solution

**Triangularisation (Schur).** Over $\mathbb{C}$ there is an invertible $P$ with $A=PTP^{-1}$, $T$ upper triangular with diagonal entries $\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n$, the eigenvalues of $A$ repeated according to multiplicity.

**Powers.** $A^k=PT^kP^{-1}$ for all $k\ge0$. A product of upper triangular matrices is upper triangular, with diagonal entries equal to the products of the diagonal entries; so $T^k$ is upper triangular with diagonal $(\lambda_i^k)$.

**Polynomials.** If $p(x)=\sum_kc_kx^k$, then $p(A)=\sum c_kPT^kP^{-1}=P\,p(T)\,P^{-1}$, and $p(T)=\sum c_kT^k$ is upper triangular with diagonal entries $\sum c_k\lambda_i^k=p(\lambda_i)$.

**Conclusion.** The characteristic polynomial of $p(A)$ is that of $p(T)$, namely $\prod_i(t-p(\lambda_i))$. Therefore the spectrum of $p(A)$ is $\{p(\lambda_1),\dots,p(\lambda_n)\}=p(\operatorname{spec}A)$.

**Example.** If $A$ is nilpotent, $\operatorname{spec}A=\{0\}$, so $A^2+I$ has spectrum $\{1\}$ and is invertible. If $A^2=A$ (projection), $\operatorname{spec}A\subset\{0,1\}$ because $p(x)=x^2-x$ gives $p(\operatorname{spec}A)=\operatorname{spec}(0)=\{0\}$.

**Remark.** The real spectrum is not enough: $A=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ has no real eigenvalue but $p(x)=x^2+1$ gives $p(A)=0$. The theorem must be applied over $\mathbb{C}$.
@@@ mnspec-when-do-the-powers-of-a-matrix-converge-to-zero.md
---
title: A^k â†’ 0 if and only if the spectral radius is less than 1
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: spectral radius, Jordan form, powers of a matrix
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and $\rho(A)=\max\{|\lambda|:\lambda\in\operatorname{spec}A\}$ its spectral radius. Prove that $A^k\to0$ as $k\to\infty$ if and only if $\rho(A)<1$.

## Hint

($\Leftarrow$) Use the Jordan form and the entries $\binom kj\lambda^{k-j}$ of $J_m(\lambda)^k$. ($\Rightarrow$) Apply $A^k$ to a complex eigenvector.

## Solution

**($\Rightarrow$).** Suppose $\rho(A)\ge1$ and take an eigenvalue $\lambda\in\mathbb{C}$ with $|\lambda|\ge1$ and an eigenvector $v\in\mathbb{C}^n$, $v\ne0$. Then $A^kv=\lambda^kv$ and $|A^kv|=|\lambda|^k|v|\ge|v|>0$ for all $k$. Hence $A^kv\not\to0$, so $A^k\not\to0$. Contrapositive: $A^k\to0\Rightarrow\rho(A)<1$.

**($\Leftarrow$).** Assume $\rho(A)<1$. Write $A=PJP^{-1}$ in Jordan form over $\mathbb{C}$; then $A^k=PJ^kP^{-1}$, and it suffices to show $J^k\to0$, i.e. $J_m(\lambda)^k\to0$ for each block with $|\lambda|<1$. Write $J_m(\lambda)=\lambda I+N$ with $N^m=0$ and $\lambda I$ commuting with $N$. By the binomial theorem
$$J_m(\lambda)^k=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\binom kj\lambda^{k-j}N^j.$$
For fixed $j$, $\binom kj\le k^j$, so
$$\left|\binom kj\lambda^{k-j}\right|\le k^j|\lambda|^{k-j}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}0,$$
because geometric decay beats polynomial growth ($|\lambda|<1$; if $\lambda=0$ the term vanishes for $k>j$). A finite sum of terms tending to $0$ tends to $0$.

**Example.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}0.9&1\\0&0.9\end{pmatrix}$: $A^k=\begin{pmatrix}0.9^k&k\,0.9^{k-1}\\0&0.9^k\end{pmatrix}$. The off-diagonal entry first grows (it is $\approx1.7\times10^{...}$, maximal near $k=9$) but eventually tends to $0$.

**Remark.** The transient growth is the reason why $\rho(A)<1$ does not imply $\|A\|<1$ for a given norm. A norm with $\|A\|<1$ exists iff $\rho(A)<1$ (see Gelfand's formula below).
@@@ mnspec-the-set-where-the-powers-converge.md
---
title: The set of matrices whose powers converge is neither open nor closed
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: powers, convergence, open sets, closed sets
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\mathcal{C}=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\lim_{k\to\infty}A^k\text{ exists}\}$. Show that $\mathcal{C}$ is neither open nor closed.

## Hint

Use the diagonal matrices $\operatorname{diag}(x,0,\dots,0)$ and the behaviour of $x^k$ at $x=1$ and $x=-1$.

## Solution

**The family.** Let $D(x)=\operatorname{diag}(x,0,\dots,0)$. Then $D(x)^k=\operatorname{diag}(x^k,0,\dots,0)$, and $x^k$ converges iff $x\in(-1,1]$ (to $0$ for $|x|<1$, to $1$ for $x=1$; for $x=-1$ it oscillates, for $|x|>1$ it diverges). Thus
$$D(x)\in\mathcal{C}\iff x\in(-1,1].$$

**Not open.** $D(1)\in\mathcal{C}$, but $D(1+\varepsilon)\notin\mathcal{C}$ for every $\varepsilon>0$ and $D(1+\varepsilon)\to D(1)$. So $D(1)$ is not an interior point.

**Not closed.** $D(-1+\tfrac1j)\in\mathcal{C}$ for all $j\ge2$, and $D(-1+\tfrac1j)\to D(-1)$, but $D(-1)\notin\mathcal{C}$ since $(-1)^k$ has no limit. So $\mathcal{C}$ does not contain its limit points.

**Remark.** The interior of $\mathcal{C}$ is $\{\rho(A)<1\}$ (where $A^k\to0$) and its closure is the set of power-bounded matrices... more precisely, $\mathcal{C}$ is contained in $\{\rho\le1\}$ and contains $\{\rho<1\}$: the interesting matrices are on the boundary $\rho=1$, where convergence is governed by the Jordan structure of eigenvalue $1$ (semisimple) and the absence of other eigenvalues on the unit circle.
@@@ mnspec-power-bounded-matrices-do-not-form-a-closed-set.md
---
title: Power-bounded matrices do not form a closed set
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: power bounded, Jordan block, closed sets
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

A matrix $A$ is *power bounded* if $\sup_{k\ge0}\|A^k\|<\infty$. Show that the set $\mathcal{P}$ of power-bounded matrices of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ ($n\ge2$) is not closed.

## Hint

Take a limit of matrices with spectral radius less than $1$ which is a Jordan block of size $2$ at the eigenvalue $1$.

## Solution

**The sequence.** For $0<\varepsilon<1$ let
$$A_\varepsilon=\begin{pmatrix}1-\varepsilon&1\\0&1-\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}\oplus0_{n-2}.$$
Its eigenvalues are $1-\varepsilon$ (and $0$), so $\rho(A_\varepsilon)<1$ and $A_\varepsilon^k\to0$ by the previous results. A convergent sequence is bounded, so $A_\varepsilon^k$ is bounded in $k$: $A_\varepsilon\in\mathcal{P}$.

**The limit.** As $\varepsilon\to0$, $A_\varepsilon\to A_0=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\oplus0$. By induction $\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}^k=\begin{pmatrix}1&k\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$, whose norm is at least $k$. Hence $\sup_k\|A_0^k\|=\infty$: $A_0\notin\mathcal{P}$.

**Conclusion.** $A_\varepsilon\in\mathcal{P}$, $A_\varepsilon\to A_0\notin\mathcal{P}$, so $\mathcal{P}$ is not closed.

**Remark.** What is true: $\mathcal{P}$ is contained in $\{\rho\le1\}$ and contains $\{\rho<1\}$; and the set $\{A:\|A^k\|\le1\ \forall k\}$ for a given norm is closed. A matrix is power bounded iff $\rho\le1$ and the eigenvalues of modulus $1$ are semisimple (no nontrivial Jordan block). The growth of $\|A_\varepsilon^k\|$ before decay is of order $\frac1{e\varepsilon}$, which blows up as $\varepsilon\to0$: power-boundedness holds but not *uniformly*.
@@@ mnspec-rank-one-matrices.md
---
title: The cone of rank-one matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: rank one, closed cone, connectedness, minors
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\mathcal{R}_1=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\operatorname{rank}A\le1\}$.

1. Show that $\mathcal{R}_1=\{xy^T:x,y\in\mathbb{R}^n\}$ and that it is closed and stable under multiplication by scalars.
2. Show that $\{\operatorname{rank}A=1\}$ is connected when $n\ge2$.

## Hint

For part 1, a rank-one matrix has one-dimensional column space. For part 2, the set is the image of the connected set $(\mathbb{R}^n\setminus\{0\})^2$ under a continuous map.

## Solution

**1. Description.** If $A=xy^T$ then every column is a multiple of $x$, so $\operatorname{rank}A\le1$. Conversely if $\operatorname{rank}A=1$, pick a nonzero column space generator $x$; each column of $A$ is $y_jx$ for a scalar $y_j$, so $A=xy^T$ (and $A=0=0\cdot0^T$ if the rank is $0$).

**Closedness.** $\operatorname{rank}A\le1$ iff all $2\times2$ minors $a_{ij}a_{kl}-a_{il}a_{kj}$ vanish. These are polynomials, so $\mathcal{R}_1$ is the common zero set of continuous functions and is closed. It is stable under $A\mapsto tA$.

(Alternative: a limit of $x_jy_j^T$ need not obviously be of this form if $x_j,y_j$ blow up; the minor characterisation avoids this issue.)

**2. Connectedness of rank exactly $1$.** The map $\Phi:(\mathbb{R}^n\setminus\{0\})^2\to M_n(\mathbb{R})$, $(x,y)\mapsto xy^T$ is continuous, and its image is exactly the rank-one matrices (by part 1, a nonzero $xy^T$ requires both $x,y\ne0$). For $n\ge2$, $\mathbb{R}^n\setminus\{0\}$ is path connected (it is homotopy equivalent to $S^{n-1}$, which is connected), hence so is the product, hence so is its continuous image.

**Remark.** For $n=1$, "rank exactly $1$" is $\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$, which is disconnected. In general $\{\operatorname{rank}=r\}$ is connected for $1\le r\le n-1$ and splits into two components ($\det>0$ and $\det<0$) for $r=n$.
@@@ mnspec-semicontinuity-of-minimal-polynomial-degree-and-centraliser-dimension.md
---
title: Semicontinuity of the minimal polynomial degree and of the centraliser
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: lower semicontinuity, minimal polynomial, centraliser, rank
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

For $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ let $\mu_A$ be its minimal polynomial and $C(A)=\{X:AX=XA\}$ its centraliser. Show that $A\mapsto\deg\mu_A$ is lower semicontinuous and $A\mapsto\dim C(A)$ is upper semicontinuous.

## Hint

$\deg\mu_A\ge k$ iff $I,A,\dots,A^{k-1}$ are linearly independent. And $C(A)=\ker(\operatorname{ad}_A)$ with $\operatorname{ad}_A(X)=AX-XA$.

## Solution

**Degree of $\mu_A$.** The degree of $\mu_A$ is the smallest $d$ such that $A^d$ is a combination of $I,\dots,A^{d-1}$. So $\deg\mu_A\ge k$ iff $I,A,\dots,A^{k-1}$ are linearly independent, i.e. the $n^2\times k$ matrix $M_k(A)$ whose columns are the vectorised powers has rank $k$. This means that some $k\times k$ minor of $M_k(A)$ is nonzero. Each such minor is a polynomial in the entries of $A$, hence continuous, so
$$\{A:\deg\mu_A\ge k\}=\bigcup_{\text{minors}}\{m(A)\ne0\}$$
is open. A function $f$ with $\{f\ge k\}$ open for each $k$ is lower semicontinuous: $\liminf_{B\to A}f(B)\ge f(A)$.

**Centraliser.** The map $\operatorname{ad}_A:M_n\to M_n$, $X\mapsto AX-XA$, is linear in $X$ and its matrix (in the standard basis of $M_n$) depends linearly on $A$ (it is $I\otimes A-A^T\otimes I$ up to conventions). Rank is lower semicontinuous (same argument with minors: $\operatorname{rank}\ge r$ is open), so near $A$ we have $\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{ad}_B\ge\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{ad}_A$. By rankâ€“nullity,
$$\dim C(B)=n^2-\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{ad}_B\le n^2-\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{ad}_A=\dim C(A).$$
So $\dim C$ is upper semicontinuous.

**Example.** $A=0$: $\deg\mu_A=1$ (minimal possible) and $\dim C(A)=n^2$ (maximal). Near $0$, nearby generic matrices have $\deg\mu=n$ and $\dim C=n$: degrees jump *up* and centraliser dimensions jump *down* off the special matrices.

**Remark.** Always $\dim C(A)\ge n$, with equality iff $A$ is *non-derogatory* ($\deg\mu_A=n$, i.e. $\mu_A=\chi_A$), which is an open dense condition.
@@@ mnspec-inertia-of-symmetric-matrices-is-semicontinuous.md
---
title: Semicontinuity of the inertia of symmetric matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: inertia, symmetric matrices, Weyl, semicontinuity
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

On $\mathrm{Sym}_n(\mathbb{R})$ let $n_+(S)$ be the number of positive eigenvalues (with multiplicity) and $n_0(S)=\dim\ker S$ the nullity. Show that $n_+$ is lower semicontinuous and $n_0$ is upper semicontinuous.

## Hint

Order the eigenvalues $\lambda_1(S)\le\dots\le\lambda_n(S)$ and use that each $\lambda_k$ is a continuous (indeed $1$-Lipschitz) function of $S$.

## Solution

**Eigenvalues are Lipschitz.** For symmetric $S,S'$ Weyl's inequality gives $|\lambda_k(S)-\lambda_k(S')|\le\|S-S'\|$ for each $k$ (minâ€“max formula for the Rayleigh quotient).

**$n_+$ is lower semicontinuous.** Let $m=n_+(S)$, so $\lambda_n(S),\dots,\lambda_{n-m+1}(S)>0$. Let $\delta=\lambda_{n-m+1}(S)>0$ be the smallest of them. If $\|S'-S\|<\delta$ then
$$\lambda_{n-m+1}(S')>\lambda_{n-m+1}(S)-\delta=0,$$
and so at least $m$ eigenvalues of $S'$ are positive: $n_+(S')\ge n_+(S)$ in a neighbourhood.

**$n_0$ is upper semicontinuous.** The nullity is $n-\operatorname{rank}S$, and the rank is lower semicontinuous (a nonzero minor stays nonzero nearby). Hence $n_0(S')=n-\operatorname{rank}S'\le n-\operatorname{rank}S=n_0(S)$ near $S$.

**Example.** $S=0$ has $n_+=0$, $n_0=n$. Nearby $S'=\varepsilon I$ has $n_+=n$ and $n_0=0$: the invariants can only jump up for $n_+$ and down for $n_0$ in a neighbourhood.

**Remark.** By symmetry $n_-$ is also lower semicontinuous, and $n_++n_-+n_0=n$. Near a given $S$ the signature can only change by turning zero eigenvalues into positive or negative ones; $n_+$ and $n_-$ never decrease locally. In particular, $\{n_+=n\}$ (positive definite matrices) is open.
@@@ mnspec-inversion-reverses-the-loewner-order.md
---
title: Inversion reverses the order of positive definite matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: Loewner order, positive definite, inverse, square root
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Write $A\le B$ for symmetric matrices if $B-A$ is positive semidefinite. Show that $S\mapsto S^{-1}$ is an involutive homeomorphism of the cone $\mathrm{PD}$ of positive definite matrices onto itself, and that it reverses the order: $A\le B\Rightarrow B^{-1}\le A^{-1}$.

## Hint

Conjugate by $A^{-1/2}$ to reduce to the case $A=I$.

## Solution

**Homeomorphism.** If $S\in\mathrm{PD}$, its eigenvalues are positive, so $S^{-1}$ is symmetric with eigenvalues $1/\lambda_i>0$: $S^{-1}\in\mathrm{PD}$. The map is continuous on $GL_n$ (entries of $S^{-1}$ are $\operatorname{adj}(S)/\det S$, rational with nonvanishing denominator), it is its own inverse, hence a homeomorphism of $\mathrm{PD}$.

**Order reversal.** Let $0<A\le B$ and let $C=A^{-1/2}$ (symmetric, positive definite). Congruence preserves the order: $X\le Y\Rightarrow CXC\le CYC$, because $v^TC(Y-X)Cv=(Cv)^T(Y-X)(Cv)\ge0$. Thus
$$I=CAC\le CBC=:T.$$
So all eigenvalues of the PD matrix $T$ are $\ge1$, hence the eigenvalues of $T^{-1}$ are $\le1$, i.e. $T^{-1}\le I$. Now $T^{-1}=C^{-1}B^{-1}C^{-1}=A^{1/2}B^{-1}A^{1/2}$, so
$$A^{1/2}B^{-1}A^{1/2}\le I.$$
Conjugating by $A^{-1/2}$ again: $B^{-1}\le A^{-1}$.

**Example.** $A=I$, $B=\operatorname{diag}(1,4)$: $B^{-1}=\operatorname{diag}(1,\frac14)\le I=A^{-1}$.

**Remark.** The map $S\mapsto S^{-1}$ reverses the order but $S\mapsto S^2$ does *not* preserve it (while $S\mapsto\sqrt S$ does, see the operator monotonicity problem). A function $f$ preserving the Loewner order is *operator monotone*; $-1/x$, $\ln x$, $x^{r}$ ($0\le r\le1$) are, and $x^2$ is not.
@@@ mnspec-matrices-with-positive-definite-symmetric-part.md
---
title: Matrices with positive definite symmetric part
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: positive definite, convex cone, eigenvalues, real part
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\mathcal{K}=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):A+A^T\text{ is positive definite}\}$. Show that $\mathcal{K}$ is open and convex, and that every eigenvalue $\lambda\in\mathbb{C}$ of every $A\in\mathcal{K}$ satisfies $\operatorname{Re}\lambda>0$.

## Hint

$\mathcal{K}$ is the preimage of the cone $\mathrm{PD}$ under a linear map. For the eigenvalues, compute $\operatorname{Re}(v^*Av)$ for a complex eigenvector $v$.

## Solution

**Openness and convexity.** The map $L:A\mapsto A+A^T$ is linear, hence continuous, and $\mathrm{PD}$ is open and convex (it is defined by $x^TSx>0$ for all $x\ne0$, a convex condition; openness by Sylvester's criterion or by compactness of the unit sphere). Therefore $\mathcal{K}=L^{-1}(\mathrm{PD})$ is open and convex.

**Eigenvalues.** Let $Av=\lambda v$ with $v\in\mathbb{C}^n\setminus\{0\}$. Write $S=\frac{A+A^T}2$, real symmetric, positive definite on $\mathbb{R}^n$, and also on $\mathbb{C}^n$: $v=x+iy$ gives $v^*Sv=x^TSx+y^TSy>0$ (the cross terms cancel because $S$ is symmetric). Since $A$ is real,
$$\overline{v^*Av}=v^TA^T\bar v=(\bar v)^*\ldots$$
more simply, $v^*A^Tv=\overline{v^*Av}$ because $A^T=A^*$ for real $A$. Hence
$$v^*Sv=\tfrac12\big(v^*Av+v^*A^*v\big)=\operatorname{Re}(v^*Av).$$
But $v^*Av=\lambda\,v^*v=\lambda|v|^2$, so
$$\operatorname{Re}\lambda\,|v|^2=v^*Sv>0\ \Longrightarrow\ \operatorname{Re}\lambda>0.$$

**Example.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&5\\-5&1\end{pmatrix}$: $A+A^T=2I>0$ and the eigenvalues are $1\pm5i$, real part $1>0$.

**Remark.** The converse fails: $\begin{pmatrix}1&10\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ has both eigenvalues $1$ but $A+A^T=\begin{pmatrix}2&10\\10&2\end{pmatrix}$ is indefinite (determinant $4-100<0$). Positivity of the symmetric part is a *uniform* (for all vectors) version of positivity of the spectrum, and it is related to Lyapunov stability with $P=I$.
@@@ mnspec-the-determinantal-hypersurface.md
---
title: Where is {det = 0} a manifold?
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: determinant, implicit function theorem, singular point, cone, local homology
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

1. Show that $\Sigma=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\det A=0\}$ is a smooth hypersurface (a manifold of dimension $n^2-1$) near every $A$ of rank $n-1$.
2. For $n=2$, show that $\Sigma$ is not a topological manifold near $0$.

## Hint

The gradient of $\det$ at $A$ is the transpose of the adjugate matrix. For part 2, diagonalise the quadratic form $ad-bc$ and see $\Sigma$ as a cone over a torus.

## Solution

**1. Gradient.** Expanding $\det$ along row $i$: $\det A=\sum_ja_{ij}C_{ij}$ with the cofactor $C_{ij}$ independent of the entries of row $i$. So $\dfrac{\partial\det}{\partial a_{ij}}=C_{ij}$, i.e. $\nabla\det(A)=\operatorname{adj}(A)^T$ (the cofactor matrix).

If $\operatorname{rank}A=n-1$, then $\operatorname{adj}A\ne0$: indeed $A\operatorname{adj}A=0$ and at least one $(n-1)\times(n-1)$ minor of $A$ is nonzero (rank $n-1$), which is an entry of the cofactor matrix. So $0$ is a regular value of $\det$ near $A$ and by the implicit function theorem $\Sigma$ is a smooth hypersurface near $A$.

**2. The case $n=2$ at $0$.** With $s=a+d$, $u=a-d$, $v=b+c$, $w=b-c$ we have $a=\frac{s+u}2$, $d=\frac{s-u}2$ and
$$ad-bc=\frac{s^2-u^2}4-\frac{v^2-w^2}4=\frac{s^2+w^2-u^2-v^2}4.$$
So $\Sigma\cong\{(s,w,u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^4:s^2+w^2=u^2+v^2\}$, a quadratic cone. Its intersection with the sphere of radius $r$ is $\{s^2+w^2=u^2+v^2=r^2/2\}$, a product of two circles, a torus $T^2$. Hence $\Sigma\setminus\{0\}\cong T^2\times(0,\infty)$ and $\Sigma$ is the open cone over $T^2$.

*$\Sigma$ is not a manifold at $0$.* Suppose $0$ had a neighbourhood in $\Sigma$ homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^3$. Then the local homology $H_2(\Sigma,\Sigma\setminus\{0\})$ would equal $H_2(\mathbb{R}^3,\mathbb{R}^3\setminus0)\cong\tilde H_1(S^2)=0$. For a cone $\Sigma$ over a space $L$, however,
$$H_k(\Sigma,\Sigma\setminus0)\cong\tilde H_{k-1}(L),$$
which for $L=T^2$ and $k=2$ is $H_1(T^2)=\mathbb{Z}^2\ne0$. Contradiction. (Equivalently: the punctured neighbourhoods of $0$ retract onto a torus, which is not a $2$-sphere; a punctured $3$-ball would retract onto $S^2$.)

**Remark.** The singular locus of $\Sigma$ is exactly $\{\operatorname{rank}\le n-2\}$, of codimension $4$ in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$. The hypersurface is smooth elsewhere, so singularities of $\{\det=0\}$ are "rare" and invisible to a transversal curve.
@@@ mnspec-the-discriminant-of-a-2-by-2-matrix.md
---
title: The discriminant of a 2Ã—2 matrix and a cone
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: discriminant, 2x2 matrices, convex cone, complex eigenvalues
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

For $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$ put $u=a-d$, $v=b+c$, $w=b-c$. Show that
$$\operatorname{tr}(A)^2-4\det A=u^2+v^2-w^2,$$
and deduce that $\{A:\operatorname{tr}^2<4\det\}$ (matrices with non-real eigenvalues) has exactly two connected components, each of them convex.

## Hint

Expand $(a+d)^2-4(ad-bc)=(a-d)^2+4bc$ and write $4bc$ with $b+c$ and $b-c$.

## Solution

**The identity.**
$$\operatorname{tr}^2-4\det=(a+d)^2-4ad+4bc=(a-d)^2+4bc.$$
Since $(b+c)^2-(b-c)^2=4bc$,
$$\operatorname{tr}^2-4\det=u^2+(b+c)^2-(b-c)^2=u^2+v^2-w^2.$$

**Non-real spectrum.** The eigenvalues are real iff the discriminant $\operatorname{tr}^2-4\det\ge0$. So the matrices with non-real eigenvalues form
$$\mathcal{E}=\{u^2+v^2<w^2\}\subset\mathbb{R}^4$$
(coordinates $s=a+d,u,v,w$ are a linear change of coordinates, so topology and convexity are preserved). The variable $s$ is free.

**Two components.** The condition $u^2+v^2<w^2$ forces $w\ne0$, so $\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_+\sqcup\mathcal{E}_-$ with $\mathcal{E}_\pm=\{\pm w>\sqrt{u^2+v^2}\}$, both open and disjoint.

**Convexity of each piece.** $\mathcal{E}_+=\mathbb{R}_s\times\{(u,v,w):w>\sqrt{u^2+v^2}\}$. The function $(u,v)\mapsto\sqrt{u^2+v^2}$ is a norm, hence convex, so its epigraph (strict) $\{w>\sqrt{u^2+v^2}\}$ is convex. A product with $\mathbb{R}$ is convex. Same for $\mathcal{E}_-$. Convex sets are connected, so there are exactly two components.

**Example.** The rotation $\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ has $b=-1,c=1$, $w=-2$, $u=v=0$: it lies in $\mathcal{E}_-$; its transpose (rotation by $-90^\circ$) lies in $\mathcal{E}_+$. Orientation of the rotation decides the component.

**Remark.** The sign of $w=b-c$ is the "angular velocity" of the linear flow $\dot x=Ax$ when the eigenvalues are complex: the two components are counter-clockwise and clockwise spiral behaviours. They cannot be connected without passing through matrices with real eigenvalues.
@@@ mnspec-the-bauer-fike-theorem.md
---
title: The Bauerâ€“Fike theorem
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Bauer-Fike, perturbation of eigenvalues, condition number, diagonalisable
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A=VDV^{-1}\in M_n(\mathbb{C})$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(d_1,\dots,d_n)$ and let $\kappa(V)=\|V\|\,\|V^{-1}\|$ (operator $2$-norm). Show that every eigenvalue $\mu$ of $A+E$ satisfies
$$\min_i|\mu-d_i|\le\kappa(V)\,\|E\|.$$

## Hint

If $\mu$ is not an eigenvalue of $A$, write $(A+E-\mu)x=0$ in the basis $V$ and isolate $y=V^{-1}x$.

## Solution

**Trivial case.** If $\mu\in\{d_i\}$ the left side is $0$ and there is nothing to prove. Assume $\mu\notin\{d_i\}$, so $D-\mu I$ is invertible.

**Reformulation.** Let $x\ne0$ with $(A+E)x=\mu x$. Then $(A-\mu I)x=-Ex$. Substituting $A=VDV^{-1}$ and $y=V^{-1}x\ne0$:
$$V(D-\mu I)V^{-1}x=-Ex\ \Longrightarrow\ (D-\mu I)y=-V^{-1}EVy.$$

**Inverting $D-\mu$.**
$$y=-(D-\mu I)^{-1}V^{-1}EV\,y.$$
Take norms and use submultiplicativity:
$$|y|\le\|(D-\mu I)^{-1}\|\,\|V^{-1}\|\,\|E\|\,\|V\|\,|y|.$$

**Conclusion.** Dividing by $|y|>0$ gives $1\le\|(D-\mu I)^{-1}\|\,\kappa(V)\,\|E\|$. For a diagonal matrix, $\|(D-\mu I)^{-1}\|=\max_i\frac1{|d_i-\mu|}=\frac1{\min_i|d_i-\mu|}$. Therefore
$$\min_i|d_i-\mu|\le\kappa(V)\|E\|.$$

**Example.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1+\delta\end{pmatrix}$ has eigenvectors with $\kappa(V)\approx2/\delta$ (nearly parallel eigenvectors), so a perturbation of size $\eta$ can move the eigenvalues by about $\sqrt{\eta}$ when $\delta\approx\sqrt\eta$: the bound is quantitatively sharp as $A$ approaches a Jordan block.

**Remark.** The theorem quantifies how ill-conditioned the eigenvalues of a nearly non-diagonalisable matrix are, and recovers the HÃ¶lder phenomenon of the Jordan block ($\kappa\to\infty$).
@@@ mnspec-eigenvalues-of-normal-matrices-are-well-conditioned.md
---
title: Eigenvalues of normal matrices move by at most the size of the perturbation
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: normal matrices, Bauer-Fike, Weyl, unitary diagonalisation
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A\in M_n(\mathbb{C})$ be normal ($AA^*=A^*A$), for instance real symmetric. Show that for every $E$, every eigenvalue $\mu$ of $A+E$ is within distance $\|E\|$ of some eigenvalue of $A$. For real symmetric $A,E$ show the sharper statement that the sorted eigenvalues satisfy $|\lambda_k(A+E)-\lambda_k(A)|\le\|E\|$.

## Hint

Normal matrices are unitarily diagonalisable, so the condition number of the eigenvector matrix is $1$.

## Solution

**First statement.** By the spectral theorem for normal matrices, $A=UDU^*$ with $U$ unitary and $D$ diagonal. Then $\|U\|=\|U^{-1}\|=1$, so $\kappa(U)=1$. Bauerâ€“Fike gives
$$\min_i|\mu-d_i|\le\kappa(U)\|E\|=\|E\|.$$

**Symmetric case, sorted eigenvalues (Weyl).** For real symmetric $A$ with eigenvalues $\lambda_1\le\dots\le\lambda_n$, the Courantâ€“Fischer formula says
$$\lambda_k(A)=\min_{\dim V=k}\ \max_{x\in V,|x|=1}x^TAx.$$
For a unit $x$, $x^T(A+E)x\le x^TAx+\|E\|$, hence $\lambda_k(A+E)\le\lambda_k(A)+\|E\|$. Applying this to the pair $(A+E,-E)$ gives $\lambda_k(A)\le\lambda_k(A+E)+\|E\|$. So
$$|\lambda_k(A+E)-\lambda_k(A)|\le\|E\|.$$

**Example.** $A=\operatorname{diag}(1,3)$ and $E=\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon\\\varepsilon&0\end{pmatrix}$ with $\|E\|=\varepsilon$: the eigenvalues of $A+E$ are $2\pm\sqrt{1+\varepsilon^2}$, which differ from $1,3$ by $\sqrt{1+\varepsilon^2}-1\le\varepsilon^2/2<\varepsilon$.
