**Remark.** The contrast with the nilpotent Jordan block (change $\varepsilon^{1/n}$ for a perturbation of size $\varepsilon$) shows that non-normality is exactly what makes eigenvalues sensitive. This also explains why symmetric eigenproblems are numerically much easier than general ones.
@@@ mnspec-gelfand-s-spectral-radius-formula.md
---
title: Gelfand's spectral radius formula
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: spectral radius, Gelfand formula, norms, powers
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\|\cdot\|$ be any submultiplicative norm on $M_n(\mathbb{C})$ (or $M_n(\mathbb{R})$). Prove that
$$\rho(A)=\lim_{k\to\infty}\|A^k\|^{1/k}.$$

## Hint

Lower bound: $\rho(A)^k=\rho(A^k)\le\|A^k\|$ by the spectral mapping theorem. Upper bound: apply "$B^k\to0$ if $\rho(B)<1$" to $B=A/(\rho(A)+\varepsilon)$.

## Solution

**Step 1: $\rho(A)\le\|A^k\|^{1/k}$ for every $k$.** If $Av=\lambda v$ with $v\ne0$ ($v$ complex) and $|\lambda|=\rho(A)$, then $A^kv=\lambda^kv$, so $\rho(A)^k=\rho(A^k)$ (spectral mapping with $p(x)=x^k$). For a *submultiplicative matrix norm induced by a vector norm*, $\|A^k\|\ge\rho(A^k)$ since $|A^kv|=|\lambda|^k|v|$ gives $\|A^k\|\ge|\lambda|^k$. For a general submultiplicative norm that is not induced by a vector norm, use the equivalence of norms: $\|\cdot\|\ge c\,\|\cdot\|_{op}$ for some $c>0$, so $\|A^k\|^{1/k}\ge c^{1/k}\rho(A)$, and $c^{1/k}\to1$. Either way,
$$\liminf_{k}\|A^k\|^{1/k}\ge\rho(A).$$

**Step 2: $\limsup\le\rho(A)$.** Fix $\varepsilon>0$ and set $B=A/(\rho(A)+\varepsilon)$. Then $\rho(B)=\frac{\rho(A)}{\rho(A)+\varepsilon}<1$, so $B^k\to0$ (previous problem), hence $\|B^k\|\le1$ for all large $k$. Equivalently
$$\|A^k\|\le(\rho(A)+\varepsilon)^k\quad\text{for large }k,\qquad\text{so}\qquad\|A^k\|^{1/k}\le\rho(A)+\varepsilon.$$
Hence $\limsup\|A^k\|^{1/k}\le\rho(A)+\varepsilon$ for every $\varepsilon>0$.

**Conclusion.** $\liminf\ge\rho(A)\ge\limsup-\varepsilon$ for all $\varepsilon$; the limit exists and equals $\rho(A)$.

**Example.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ has $\rho=0$ and $A^2=0$: $\|A^k\|^{1/k}=0$ for $k\ge2$ âœ“. For $A=\begin{pmatrix}0.9&1\\0&0.9\end{pmatrix}$, $\|A^{50}\|^{1/50}\approx0.9\cdot1.0\!\ldots$ approaches $0.9$ slowly.

**Remark.** As a corollary, $\rho(A)=\inf_{\|\cdot\|}\|A\|$ over all operator norms: for each $\varepsilon$ there is a norm with $\|A\|<\rho(A)+\varepsilon$. This is the topological meaning of the spectral radius.
@@@ mnspec-monotonicity-of-the-spectral-radius-for-nonnegative-matrices.md
---
title: The spectral radius is monotone on nonnegative matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: spectral radius, nonnegative matrices, Gelfand formula, monotonicity
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

If $A,B\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ satisfy $0\le A\le B$ entrywise, show that $\rho(A)\le\rho(B)$.

## Hint

Use Gelfand's formula with the norm $\|M\|_\infty=\max_i\sum_j|m_{ij}|$ (maximum absolute row sum).

## Solution

**Entrywise inequality for powers.** We prove $0\le A^k\le B^k$ entrywise by induction. For $k=1$ it is the hypothesis. If $0\le A^k\le B^k$, then
$$(A^{k+1})_{ij}=\sum_l(A^k)_{il}A_{lj}\le\sum_l(B^k)_{il}B_{lj}=(B^{k+1})_{ij},$$
because all terms are nonnegative and each factor is dominated.

**The norm.** For a matrix with nonnegative entries, $\|M\|_\infty=\max_i\sum_jM_{ij}$ is nondecreasing in the entries. Hence
$$\|A^k\|_\infty\le\|B^k\|_\infty\quad\text{for all }k.$$

**Gelfand.** Taking $k$-th roots and letting $k\to\infty$ (Gelfand's formula for the submultiplicative norm $\|\cdot\|_\infty$):
$$\rho(A)=\lim_k\|A^k\|_\infty^{1/k}\le\lim_k\|B^k\|_\infty^{1/k}=\rho(B).$$

**Example.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}\le B=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$: $\rho(A)=0\le\rho(B)=\frac{1+\sqrt5}2$.

**Remark.** Nonnegativity is essential: for $A=\begin{pmatrix}0&-3\\0&0\end{pmatrix}\le B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ (entrywise) there is no problem, but with $A=\begin{pmatrix}-2&0\\0&-2\end{pmatrix}\le B=I$ we get $\rho(A)=2>\rho(B)=1$. This monotonicity is a first step of the Perronâ€“Frobenius theory.
@@@ mnspec-existence-of-a-stationary-distribution.md
---
title: A stochastic matrix has a stationary distribution
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: stochastic matrices, compactness, CesÃ ro averages, Markov chains
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $P\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ be row-stochastic ($P_{ij}\ge0$, $\sum_jP_{ij}=1$). Show that there is a probability row vector $\pi$ (that is $\pi_i\ge0$, $\sum\pi_i=1$) with $\pi P=\pi$.

## Hint

Apply $P$ repeatedly to a probability vector, average, and use compactness of the simplex.

## Solution

**The simplex is invariant.** Let $\Delta=\{\pi\in\mathbb{R}^n:\pi_i\ge0,\sum\pi_i=1\}$, a compact convex set. If $\pi\in\Delta$ then $\pi P\ge0$ and $\sum_j(\pi P)_j=\sum_i\pi_i\sum_jP_{ij}=1$, so $\pi P\in\Delta$.

**CesÃ ro averages.** Let $\pi_0=(\frac1n,\dots,\frac1n)$ and
$$m_N=\frac1N\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\pi_0P^k\in\Delta$$
(an average of points of the convex set $\Delta$). Then
$$m_NP-m_N=\frac1N\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{N}\pi_0P^k-\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\pi_0P^k\Big)=\frac{\pi_0P^N-\pi_0}N.$$
Since $\pi_0P^N,\pi_0\in\Delta$ have $1$-norm $1$, $\|m_NP-m_N\|_1\le\frac2N\to0$.

**Compactness.** $\Delta$ is compact, so a subsequence $m_{N_j}$ converges to some $\pi\in\Delta$. By continuity of $x\mapsto xP-x$,
$$\pi P-\pi=\lim_j(m_{N_j}P-m_{N_j})=0.$$

**Example.** $P=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$: $P^k$ does not converge, but the averages $m_N\to(\frac12,\frac12)$, which is stationary.

**Remark.** This is the Markovâ€“Kakutani / Brouwer-type argument. The vector $\pi$ is generally not unique (e.g. $P=I$) and $\pi_0P^k$ need not converge, which is why CesÃ ro averaging is needed; under the positivity assumption of the next problem, uniqueness and convergence hold.
@@@ mnspec-dobrushin-s-contraction-argument.md
---
title: Dobrushin's contraction for positive stochastic matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: stochastic matrices, Dobrushin, oscillation, convergence of powers
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $P$ be a row-stochastic $n\times n$ matrix with all entries $P_{ij}\ge\delta>0$. Show that $P^k$ converges, as $k\to\infty$, to a matrix $\mathbf{1}\pi^T$ whose rows are all equal to the same probability vector $\pi$, and that the convergence is geometric with ratio at most $1-n\delta$.

## Hint

For a vector $x$ let $\operatorname{osc}(x)=\max_ix_i-\min_ix_i$. Show $\operatorname{osc}(Px)\le(1-n\delta)\operatorname{osc}(x)$.

## Solution

**Step 1: rows are close.** Let $p,q$ be two rows of $P$, probability vectors with all entries $\ge\delta$. The vector $d=p-q$ has zero sum, so $\sum_id_i^+=\sum_id_i^-=\frac12\|d\|_1$, where $d^\pm$ are the positive/negative parts. Moreover
$$\sum_id_i^+=\sum_i(p_i-q_i)^+=1-\sum_i\min(p_i,q_i)\le1-n\delta.$$

**Step 2: oscillation contracts.** For $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$, $(Px)_r-(Px)_s=d\cdot x$ with $d=p_r-p_s$. Because $\sum d_i=0$,
$$d\cdot x=\sum d_i^+x_i-\sum d_i^-x_i\le\Big(\sum d_i^+\Big)\max x-\Big(\sum d_i^-\Big)\min x=\Big(\sum d_i^+\Big)\operatorname{osc}(x).$$
Hence $\operatorname{osc}(Px)\le(1-n\delta)\operatorname{osc}(x)$, and by induction $\operatorname{osc}(P^kx)\le(1-n\delta)^k\operatorname{osc}(x)$.

**Step 3: $P^kx$ converges to a constant.** Since $P$ is stochastic, each coordinate of $Px$ is an average of the coordinates of $x$: $\max P^kx$ is nonincreasing, $\min P^kx$ is nondecreasing, and both converge; as the oscillation tends to $0$, they converge to the same limit $c(x)$. So $P^kx\to c(x)\mathbf{1}$, with $|P^kx-c(x)\mathbf{1}|_\infty\le(1-n\delta)^k\operatorname{osc}(x)$.

**Step 4: the limit matrix.** $c$ is linear in $x$ (a limit of linear maps), so $c(x)=\pi\cdot x$ for $\pi_i=c(e_i)$. Taking $x=e_i$ with $c(e_i)\ge0$ (the entries of $P^k$ are $\ge0$) and $x=\mathbf{1}$ with $c(\mathbf{1})=1$ shows that $\pi_i\ge0$ and $\sum\pi_i=1$. Therefore $P^kx\to\mathbf{1}\pi^Tx$ for each $x$, i.e. $P^k\to\mathbf{1}\pi^T$.

**Example.** $P=\begin{pmatrix}0.9&0.1\\0.2&0.8\end{pmatrix}$ has $\delta=0.1$ and ratio $\le0.8$ (the actual second eigenvalue is $0.7$); $\pi=(\frac23,\frac13)$.

**Remark.** The constant $1-n\delta$ is the Dobrushin ergodicity coefficient. Also $\pi P=\pi$ follows from $\mathbf{1}\pi^TP=\lim P^{k+1}=\mathbf{1}\pi^T$, and $\pi$ is unique, so the Markov chain forgets its initial state exponentially fast.
@@@ mnspec-a-trace-zero-matrix-has-a-zero-diagonal-in-some-basis.md
---
title: A traceless matrix has a zero diagonal in some orthonormal basis
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: trace, numerical range, orthonormal basis, induction
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ with $\operatorname{tr}A=0$. Prove that there is an orthonormal basis $(f_1,\dots,f_n)$ of $\mathbb{R}^n$ with $f_i^TAf_i=0$ for all $i$; that is, $Q^TAQ$ has zero diagonal for some orthogonal $Q$.

## Hint

Show first that there is a unit vector $x$ with $x^TAx=0$ (the numerical range contains the mean of the diagonal). Then induct on the compression of $A$ to $x^\perp$.

## Solution

**Step 1: a unit vector with $x^TAx=0$.** Let $S=\frac{A+A^T}2$; $\operatorname{tr}S=\operatorname{tr}A=0$. Its eigenvalues $\lambda_1\le\dots\le\lambda_n$ have sum $0$, so $\lambda_1\le0\le\lambda_n$. By the numerical range theorem (previous problem, $n\ge2$) $\{x^TAx:|x|=1\}=[\lambda_1,\lambda_n]\ni0$. So there is a unit $x_1$ with $x_1^TAx_1=0$. (For $n=1$, $A=(0)$ and any unit vector works.)

**Step 2: induction.** Extend $x_1$ to an orthonormal basis and let $W=x_1^\perp$ (dimension $n-1$), with an orthonormal basis forming the columns of an $n\times(n-1)$ matrix $B$. The compression $A'=B^TAB\in M_{n-1}(\mathbb{R})$ has
$$\operatorname{tr}A'=\operatorname{tr}A-x_1^TAx_1=0-0=0,$$
since the trace in the orthonormal basis $(x_1,\text{columns of }B)$ is the sum of the diagonal entries $x_1^TAx_1+\operatorname{tr}A'$.

By the induction hypothesis there is an orthonormal basis $(g_2,\dots,g_n)$ of $W$ in which $A'$ has zero diagonal, i.e. $g_i^TAg_i=0$ (for $g_i\in W$, $g_i^TA g_i$ is exactly the corresponding diagonal entry of the compression).

**Conclusion.** $(x_1,g_2,\dots,g_n)$ is an orthonormal basis of $\mathbb{R}^n$ with all $f_i^TAf_i=0$.

**Example.** $A=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)$: $x=\frac1{\sqrt2}(1,1)$ gives $x^TAx=0$, and $\frac1{\sqrt2}(1,-1)$ also; in this basis $A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$.

**Remark.** Conversely a matrix with zero diagonal in an orthonormal basis has trace zero, so $\operatorname{tr}A=0$ iff $A$ is orthogonally similar to a zero-diagonal matrix. The same statement holds with "similar" in place of "orthogonally similar" (trivially, with a basis that is not orthonormal) and it is the matrix version of: a trace-zero matrix is a sum of commutators (Albertâ€“Muckenhoupt).
@@@ mnspec-sylvester-s-criterion.md
---
title: Sylvester's criterion and the openness of the positive cone
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Sylvester criterion, positive definite, LDL decomposition, leading minors
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n(\mathbb{R})$ and let $\Delta_k=\det(S_{ij})_{1\le i,j\le k}$ be its leading principal minors. Prove that $S$ is positive definite iff $\Delta_1>0,\dots,\Delta_n>0$. Deduce that $\mathrm{PD}$ is open in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$.

## Hint

($\Rightarrow$) Leading blocks are positive definite. ($\Leftarrow$) Gaussian elimination without pivoting gives $S=LDL^T$ with $d_k=\Delta_k/\Delta_{k-1}$.

## Solution

**($\Rightarrow$).** If $S>0$ and $x\in\mathbb{R}^k\setminus0$, extend $x$ by zeros to $\tilde x\in\mathbb{R}^n$: $x^TS_kx=\tilde x^TS\tilde x>0$. So each leading block $S_k$ is positive definite, its eigenvalues are positive and $\Delta_k=\det S_k>0$.

**($\Leftarrow$).** Assume all $\Delta_k>0$. We do Gaussian elimination without row exchanges. The first pivot is $\Delta_1>0$. After eliminating the first column, the lower right block is the Schur complement and is again symmetric; inductively the $k$-th pivot is $d_k=\Delta_k/\Delta_{k-1}$ (with $\Delta_0=1$), since the determinant of the leading $k\times k$ block is the product of the first $k$ pivots and elimination does not change leading minors. All pivots are $>0$, so the elimination never breaks down, and we obtain
$$S=LDL^T,\qquad L\text{ unit lower triangular},\ D=\operatorname{diag}(d_1,\dots,d_n).$$
Therefore for $x\ne0$, with $y=L^Tx\ne0$ ($L$ is invertible),
$$x^TSx=y^TDy=\sum_kd_ky_k^2>0.$$

**Openness.** The functions $\Delta_k:\mathrm{Sym}_n\to\mathbb{R}$ are polynomials, so continuous, and
$$\mathrm{PD}=\bigcap_{k=1}^n\Delta_k^{-1}\big((0,\infty)\big)$$
is open.

**Example.** $S=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\-1&2\end{pmatrix}$: $\Delta_1=2$, $\Delta_2=3$, so $S>0$ and $S=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\-\frac12&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&\frac32\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\frac12\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$.

**Remark.** The criterion needs *leading* minors: $S=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix}$ has $\Delta_1=0$ and $S_{22}=-1<0$. For positive *semi*definiteness one needs *all* principal minors $\ge0$, not only the leading ones: $\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix}$ has leading minors $0,0$ yet is not semidefinite.
@@@ mnspec-lyapunov-s-criterion-for-stability.md
---
title: Lyapunov's criterion for Hurwitz matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Hurwitz, Lyapunov equation, stability, positive definite
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

A real matrix $A$ is *Hurwitz* if all its eigenvalues have negative real part. Show that $A$ is Hurwitz iff there exists a symmetric positive definite $P$ with $A^TP+PA<0$ (negative definite).

## Hint

($\Leftarrow$) Evaluate the quadratic form on a complex eigenvector. ($\Rightarrow$) Take $P=\int_0^\infty e^{A^Tt}e^{At}\,dt$.

## Solution

**($\Leftarrow$).** Let $Av=\lambda v$, $v\in\mathbb{C}^n\setminus\{0\}$. Since $A$ is real, $v^*A^T=(Av)^*=\bar\lambda v^*$. Therefore
$$v^*(A^TP+PA)v=\bar\lambda\,v^*Pv+\lambda\,v^*Pv=2\operatorname{Re}\lambda\ v^*Pv.$$
The left side is $<0$ (a negative definite real symmetric matrix is negative definite on $\mathbb{C}^n$ too), and $v^*Pv>0$. Hence $\operatorname{Re}\lambda<0$.

**($\Rightarrow$).** Suppose $A$ is Hurwitz. Then there are $C,c>0$ with $\|e^{At}\|\le Ce^{-ct}$ for $t\ge0$ (Jordan form: entries of $e^{At}$ are $t^je^{\lambda t}$ with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda<0$). So the integral
$$P=\int_0^\infty e^{A^Tt}e^{At}\,dt$$
converges absolutely (integrand $\le C^2e^{-2ct}$). It is symmetric, and for $x\ne0$,
$$x^TPx=\int_0^\infty|e^{At}x|^2dt>0,$$
because $e^{At}$ is invertible so $e^{At}x\ne0$ for all $t$ and the integrand is continuous and positive. So $P>0$. Moreover
$$A^TP+PA=\int_0^\infty\frac{d}{dt}\Big(e^{A^Tt}e^{At}\Big)dt=\lim_{T\to\infty}e^{A^TT}e^{AT}-I=-I,$$
which is negative definite.

**Example.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}-1&10\\0&-1\end{pmatrix}$ is Hurwitz but $A^T+A$ is indefinite: $P=I$ does not work, yet a suitable $P=\begin{pmatrix}1&5\\5&51\end{pmatrix}$-like matrix does. The Lyapunov function $V(x)=x^TPx$ decreases along the flow $\dot x=Ax$.

**Remark.** In topological terms, the set of Hurwitz matrices is open (eigenvalues are continuous), and it is the union over $P>0$ of the open convex cones $\{A:A^TP+PA<0\}$; each cone is convex but the union is not (the set of Hurwitz matrices is *not* convex).
@@@ mnspec-the-eckart-young-theorem.md
---
title: The Eckartâ€“Young theorem in the operator norm
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Eckart-Young, low rank approximation, singular values, dimension counting
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ have singular values $\sigma_1\ge\dots\ge\sigma_n$ and let $0\le r<n$. Show that
$$\min\{\|A-B\|:\operatorname{rank}B\le r\}=\sigma_{r+1}(A)$$
for the operator norm.

## Hint

Upper bound by truncating the singular value decomposition. Lower bound: $\ker B$ has dimension $\ge n-r$, so it meets $\operatorname{span}(v_1,\dots,v_{r+1})$ nontrivially.

## Solution

**Setup.** Take a singular value decomposition $A=\sum_{i=1}^n\sigma_iu_iv_i^T$ with orthonormal families $(u_i)$, $(v_i)$.

**Upper bound.** Let $B_0=\sum_{i=1}^r\sigma_iu_iv_i^T$, which has rank $\le r$. Then $A-B_0=\sum_{i>r}\sigma_iu_iv_i^T$, whose singular values are $\sigma_{r+1},\dots,\sigma_n$ so $\|A-B_0\|=\sigma_{r+1}$.

**Lower bound.** Let $\operatorname{rank}B\le r$. Then $\dim\ker B\ge n-r$. Let $V=\operatorname{span}(v_1,\dots,v_{r+1})$, of dimension $r+1$. Since $(n-r)+(r+1)=n+1>n$, the subspaces $\ker B$ and $V$ intersect in a nonzero subspace; pick a unit vector $x$ in the intersection. Because $Bx=0$,
$$\|A-B\|\ge|(A-B)x|=|Ax|.$$
Writing $x=\sum_{i\le r+1}c_iv_i$ with $\sum c_i^2=1$, orthonormality of $(u_i)$ gives
$$|Ax|^2=\sum_{i\le r+1}\sigma_i^2c_i^2\ge\sigma_{r+1}^2\sum c_i^2=\sigma_{r+1}^2.$$
Thus $\|A-B\|\ge\sigma_{r+1}$ for every $B$ of rank $\le r$.

**Conclusion.** The minimum is attained at $B_0$ and equals $\sigma_{r+1}$.

**Corollary (distance to singular matrices).** For $r=n-1$: the operator-norm distance from an invertible $A$ to the set $\{\det=0\}$ is $\sigma_n(A)=1/\|A^{-1}\|$.

**Example.** $A=\operatorname{diag}(3,2,1)$, $r=1$: the best rank-one approximation is $\operatorname{diag}(3,0,0)$ at distance $2=\sigma_2$.

**Remark.** The same truncation $B_0$ is optimal for the Frobenius norm, with error $\sqrt{\sigma_{r+1}^2+\dots+\sigma_n^2}$ (Eckartâ€“Youngâ€“Mirsky); the proof is different.
@@@ mnspec-the-square-root-is-operator-monotone.md
---
title: The square root is operator monotone
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: Loewner order, operator monotone, square root, positive semidefinite
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A,B$ be symmetric matrices with $0\le A\le B$ (Loewner order: $B-A$ is positive semidefinite). Show that $\sqrt A\le\sqrt B$, where $\sqrt{\cdot}$ is the positive semidefinite square root.

## Hint

First treat positive definite $A,B$. Put $X=\sqrt B-\sqrt A$ and check the identity $B-A=\sqrt B\,X+X\sqrt A$. Evaluate $v^T(B-A)v$ on an eigenvector $v$ of $X$.

## Solution

**Step 1: positive definite case.** Assume $0<A\le B$ and let $X=\sqrt B-\sqrt A$, a symmetric matrix. We have the algebraic identity
$$\sqrt B\,X+X\sqrt A=B-\sqrt B\sqrt A+\sqrt B\sqrt A-A=B-A.$$
Suppose $X$ had a negative eigenvalue $\lambda<0$ with eigenvector $v\ne0$. Since $X$ is symmetric, $v^TX=\lambda v^T$ and $Xv=\lambda v$. Then
$$v^T(B-A)v=v^T\sqrt B\,Xv+v^TX\sqrt A\,v=\lambda\,v^T\sqrt B\,v+\lambda\,v^T\sqrt A\,v=\lambda\big(v^T\sqrt Bv+v^T\sqrt Av\big)<0,$$
because $\sqrt A$ and $\sqrt B$ are positive definite so the bracket is positive, while $\lambda<0$. This contradicts $B-A\ge0$. So all eigenvalues of $X$ are $\ge0$, i.e. $\sqrt A\le\sqrt B$.

**Step 2: general case.** If $0\le A\le B$, then for each $\varepsilon>0$, $0<A+\varepsilon I\le B+\varepsilon I$, so by Step 1
$$\sqrt{A+\varepsilon I}\le\sqrt{B+\varepsilon I}.$$
The square root is continuous on the cone of positive semidefinite matrices, and the set $\{(X,Y):X\le Y\}$ is closed. Letting $\varepsilon\to0$ gives $\sqrt A\le\sqrt B$.

**Example.** The analogous statement for squares is false: $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}\le B=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}$ but $A^2=\begin{pmatrix}2&2\\2&2\end{pmatrix}\not\le B^2=\begin{pmatrix}5&3\\3&2\end{pmatrix}$, since $B^2-A^2=\begin{pmatrix}3&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ has determinant $-1<0$.

**Remark.** More generally $t\mapsto t^r$ is operator monotone for $0\le r\le1$ (LÃ¶wnerâ€“Heinz inequality) and not for $r>1$. This result is one of the first examples where order on matrices behaves differently from order on numbers.
@@@ mnspec-conjugacy-classes-in-the-plane-of-trace-and-determinant.md
---
title: The fibres of (trace, determinant) on 2Ã—2 matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: conjugacy classes, 2x2 matrices, hyperboloid, trace, determinant
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Consider $F=(\operatorname{tr},\det):M_2(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}^2$. For $s,\delta\in\mathbb{R}$ describe the fibre $F^{-1}(s,\delta)$ geometrically and its decomposition into conjugacy classes (under $GL_2(\mathbb{R})$).

## Hint

Use the coordinates $s=a+d$, $u=a-d$, $v=b+c$, $w=b-c$, in which $\operatorname{tr}^2-4\det=u^2+v^2-w^2$.

## Solution

**The fibre.** Fix $s=\operatorname{tr}$ and $\delta=\det$, and put $\Delta=s^2-4\delta$. In the coordinates $(u,v,w)\in\mathbb{R}^3$ the fibre is the quadric
$$Q_\Delta=\{u^2+v^2-w^2=\Delta\}.$$

**Case $\Delta>0$ (two distinct real eigenvalues).** $Q_\Delta$ is a one-sheeted hyperboloid, connected. All such matrices are diagonalisable with the same eigenvalues $\frac{s\pm\sqrt\Delta}2$, hence all conjugate: the fibre is a single conjugacy class.

**Case $\Delta<0$ (complex conjugate eigenvalues).** $w^2=u^2+v^2+|\Delta|$: a two-sheeted hyperboloid, with two components $w>0$ and $w<0$. Over $\mathbb{R}$, all matrices with the same non-real eigenvalues are similar (rational canonical form), so the fibre is one $GL_2(\mathbb{R})$-orbit. It is disconnected: conjugation by $\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)$ (determinant $-1$) sends $(b,c)\mapsto(-b,-c)$, hence $w\mapsto-w$, swapping the two sheets. Under the connected subgroup $GL_2^+(\mathbb{R})$ each sheet is an orbit.

**Case $\Delta=0$ (double eigenvalue $\frac s2$).** $u^2+v^2=w^2$: a double cone. The apex $u=v=w=0$ is the scalar matrix $\frac s2I$, a one-point conjugacy class. The rest, the cone minus the apex (two nappes, $w>0$ and $w<0$), consists of the matrices $\frac s2I+N$ with $N\ne0$ nilpotent. All nonzero nilpotent $2\times2$ matrices are similar to $\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$, so this is a single $GL_2(\mathbb{R})$-orbit (again with two components, swapped by $\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)$).

**Summary.** Each fibre with $\Delta\ne0$ is one conjugacy class, and the fibre with $\Delta=0$ consists of two: the scalar matrix and the non-diagonalisable matrices.

**Check.** $\Delta=0$, $s=2$, $\delta=1$: $\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ has $u=0$, $v=1$, $w=1$: on the cone. The identity $I$ has $u=v=w=0$: the apex.

**Remark.** The orbit space $M_2(\mathbb{R})/GL_2(\mathbb{R})$ is *not* the plane $\mathbb{R}^2$ of $(s,\delta)$: the quotient is non-Hausdorff along the parabola $\Delta=0$ (the apex and the cone minus apex cannot be separated), while the map $(\operatorname{tr},\det)$ identifies them.
@@@ mnspec-the-space-of-matrices-with-real-distinct-eigenvalues-in-dimension-two.md
---
title: Matrices with two distinct real eigenvalues retract onto a circle
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-rank-spectrum
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: homotopy equivalence, deformation retraction, 2x2 matrices, discriminant
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that $\mathcal{U}=\{A\in M_2(\mathbb{R}):\operatorname{tr}(A)^2>4\det A\}$ (two distinct real eigenvalues) is homotopy equivalent to a circle.

## Hint

In the coordinates $(s,u,v,w)$ with $\operatorname{tr}^2-4\det=u^2+v^2-w^2$, push $w$ to $0$ and forget $s$.

## Solution

**Description.** With $s=a+d$, $u=a-d$, $v=b+c$, $w=b-c$ (an invertible linear change of coordinates),
$$\mathcal{U}=\{(s,u,v,w):u^2+v^2>w^2\}=\mathbb{R}_s\times\mathcal{V},\qquad\mathcal{V}=\{(u,v,w):w^2<u^2+v^2\}.$$

**Deformation retraction of $\mathcal{V}$.** Define $H:\mathcal{V}\times[0,1]\to\mathcal{V}$,
$$H\big((u,v,w),t\big)=(u,v,(1-t)w).$$
It stays in $\mathcal{V}$: $\big((1-t)w\big)^2\le w^2<u^2+v^2$. Also $H_0=\mathrm{id}$, $H_1(u,v,w)=(u,v,0)$, and $H_t$ fixes the points $\mathcal{V}\cap\{w=0\}=\{(u,v,0):(u,v)\ne0\}$. So $\mathcal{V}$ deformation retracts onto
$$\{(u,v,0):(u,v)\ne(0,0)\}\cong\mathbb{R}^2\setminus\{0\}.$$

**Further retraction.** $\mathbb{R}^2\setminus\{0\}$ deformation retracts onto the unit circle by $(u,v)\mapsto\frac{(u,v)}{|(u,v)|^{t}}$-type radial homotopy. Hence $\mathcal{V}\simeq S^1$.

**The factor $\mathbb{R}_s$.** It is contractible, so $\mathcal{U}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{V}\simeq\mathcal{V}\simeq S^1$.

**Interpretation.** A point on the circle is the pair $(u,v)/|(u,v)|$, the direction of the "eigenspace configuration": the matrix $\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\lambda_2)$ conjugated by a rotation of angle $\theta$ gives $(u,v)=(\lambda_1-\lambda_2)(\cos2\theta,\sin2\theta)/2\cdot$-type, and a rotation by $\pi$ returns to the same matrix ($\theta\mapsto\theta+\pi$ gives $2\theta\mapsto2\theta+2\pi$) while $\theta\mapsto\theta+\frac\pi2$ swaps the eigenvalues, going halfway around the circle.

**Remark.** The fundamental group of $\mathcal{U}$ is $\mathbb{Z}$. By contrast the set $\{\operatorname{tr}^2<4\det\}$ has two contractible components (previous discriminant problem), so the "real" region is topologically richer than the "complex" one.

@@@ mnexp-convergence-of-the-matrix-exponential.md
---
title: Convergence of the matrix exponential and its first bounds
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: matrix exponential, absolute convergence, submultiplicative norm
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Throughout, $\|\cdot\|$ is the operator norm on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$, so $\|AB\|\le\|A\|\,\|B\|$ and $\|I\|=1$.

Show that for every $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ the series $e^A=\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{A^k}{k!}$ converges absolutely, and that
$$\|e^A\|\le e^{\|A\|},\qquad\|e^A-I\|\le e^{\|A\|}-1.$$
Deduce that $\exp:M_n(\mathbb{R})\to M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is continuous.

## Hint

By submultiplicativity, $\|A^k\|\le\|A\|^k$. Compare with the scalar exponential series.

## Solution

**Idea.** The series of norms is dominated by the scalar series $\sum\|A\|^k/k!$, which converges.

**Absolute convergence.** Submultiplicativity gives $\|A^k\|\le\|A\|^k$ by induction on $k$ (with $A^0=I$ and $\|I\|=1$ for the operator norm). Hence
$$\sum_{k\ge0}\left\|\frac{A^k}{k!}\right\|\le\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{\|A\|^k}{k!}=e^{\|A\|}<\infty.$$
Since $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is complete (it is $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$), an absolutely convergent series converges, so $e^A$ is well defined.

**The two bounds.** By the triangle inequality for series,
$$\|e^A\|\le\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{\|A\|^k}{k!}=e^{\|A\|},$$
and, removing the term $k=0$ (which is $I$),
$$\|e^A-I\|=\Big\|\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{A^k}{k!}\Big\|\le\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{\|A\|^k}{k!}=e^{\|A\|}-1.$$

**Continuity.** On the ball $\{\|A\|\le R\}$ the $k$-th term is bounded by $R^k/k!$, and $\sum R^k/k!<\infty$. By the Weierstrass M-test the series converges uniformly on this ball. Each partial sum $\sum_{k\le N}A^k/k!$ is a polynomial in the entries, hence continuous, and a uniform limit of continuous functions is continuous. So $\exp$ is continuous on every ball, hence on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Remark.** (For the Frobenius norm $\|I\|=\sqrt n$, and the first bound would need the factor $\sqrt n$; the second bound is unchanged.) The bound $\|e^A-I\|\le e^{\|A\|}-1$ shows that $e^A$ is close to $I$ when $A$ is small: this is the starting point of the local study of $\exp$ and $\log$ near $I$.
@@@ mnexp-exponential-of-a-sum-commuting-and-not.md
---
title: The exponential of a sum: commuting and non-commuting matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: matrix exponential, commutation, counterexample
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

1. Show that if $AB=BA$ then $e^{A+B}=e^Ae^B$.
2. Show that this fails in general, using $A=E_{12}$ and $B=E_{21}$ (the matrix units of $M_2(\mathbb{R})$).

## Hint

For part 1, the binomial theorem holds for commuting matrices; then use the Cauchy product of two absolutely convergent series. For part 2, compute $e^{E_{12}}$ and $e^{E_{21}}$ by hand: both squares are $0$.

## Solution

**1. Commuting case.** Since $AB=BA$, the binomial theorem gives
$$\frac{(A+B)^m}{m!}=\sum_{i+j=m}\frac{A^i}{i!}\cdot\frac{B^j}{j!}.$$
Summing over $m$ gives the Cauchy product of the series for $e^A$ and $e^B$. Both converge absolutely (problem on convergence of $\exp$), so the Cauchy product converges to the product of the sums:
$$e^{A+B}=\sum_m\sum_{i+j=m}\frac{A^i}{i!}\frac{B^j}{j!}=e^Ae^B.$$

**2. A counterexample.** Let $A=E_{12}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ and $B=E_{21}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$. Then $A^2=B^2=0$, so
$$e^A=I+A=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad e^B=I+B=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\1&1\end{pmatrix}.$$
Multiplying,
$$e^Ae^B=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad e^Be^A=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}.$$
These are different. Now $A+B$ is the symmetric matrix $\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$, and $(A+B)^2=I$, so
$$e^{A+B}=\cosh1\,I+\sinh1\,(A+B)=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh1&\sinh1\\\sinh1&\cosh1\end{pmatrix}\approx\begin{pmatrix}1.543&1.175\\1.175&1.543\end{pmatrix},$$
which equals neither $e^Ae^B$ nor $e^Be^A$.

**Remark.** Since $e^Ae^B\ne e^Be^A$, the identity $e^{A+B}=e^Ae^B$ cannot hold when $A$ and $B$ do not commute: the left side is symmetric in $A,B$ but the right sides are not. The correct replacement is the Lie product formula (see below).
@@@ mnexp-the-derivative-of-the-exponential-of-ta.md
---
title: The derivative of t â†¦ e^(tA) and the one-parameter group property
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: matrix exponential, derivative, homomorphism
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$. Show that $t\mapsto e^{tA}$ is differentiable with $\frac d{dt}e^{tA}=Ae^{tA}$, and that it is a continuous group homomorphism $(\mathbb{R},+)\to GL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

A power series in $t$ can be differentiated termwise inside its disc of convergence. For the homomorphism property, use the commuting case $e^{X+Y}=e^Xe^Y$.

## Solution

**Termwise differentiation.** We have $e^{tA}=\sum_k\frac{t^k}{k!}A^k$, a power series in the real variable $t$ with matrix coefficients. Its entries are real power series in $t$ with infinite radius of convergence (each entry is bounded by $\|A\|^k|t|^k/k!$ times a constant). A power series may be differentiated termwise on its interval of convergence, so
$$\frac d{dt}e^{tA}=\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{kt^{k-1}}{k!}A^k=A\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{t^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}A^{k-1}=Ae^{tA}.$$
(The matrix $A$ can be pulled out on the left or on the right: $Ae^{tA}=e^{tA}A$.)

**Homomorphism.** For $s,t\in\mathbb{R}$ the matrices $sA$ and $tA$ commute, so
$$e^{(s+t)A}=e^{sA}e^{tA}.$$
In particular $e^{tA}e^{-tA}=e^{0}=I$, so $e^{tA}$ is invertible with inverse $e^{-tA}$: the map takes values in $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Continuity.** It is differentiable, hence continuous (or: composition of the continuous maps $t\mapsto tA$ and $\exp$).

**Remark.** The matrix function $\Phi(t)=e^{tA}$ is the unique solution of the linear differential equation $\Phi'=A\Phi$, $\Phi(0)=I$: if $\Psi$ is another one, $\frac d{dt}\bigl(e^{-tA}\Psi(t)\bigr)=-Ae^{-tA}\Psi+e^{-tA}A\Psi=0$, so $e^{-tA}\Psi$ is constant $=I$. This uniqueness is used in the classification of one-parameter subgroups below.
@@@ mnexp-the-exponential-is-not-injective.md
---
title: The exponential is not injective: rotations as exponentials
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: matrix exponential, rotation, kernel
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$. Show that $e^{\theta J}=R_\theta=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$ for all real $\theta$. Deduce that $e^{2\pi J}=I$ and that $\exp:M_2(\mathbb{R})\to GL_2(\mathbb{R})$ is not injective.

## Hint

Compute the powers of $J$: $J^2=-I$.

## Solution

**Powers of $J$.** $J^2=-I$, hence $J^3=-J$, $J^4=I$, and in general $J^{2k}=(-1)^kI$, $J^{2k+1}=(-1)^kJ$.

**Splitting the series.** Separating even and odd terms,
$$e^{\theta J}=\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{\theta^{2k}}{(2k)!}J^{2k}+\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{\theta^{2k+1}}{(2k+1)!}J^{2k+1}=\Bigl(\sum_k\frac{(-1)^k\theta^{2k}}{(2k)!}\Bigr)I+\Bigl(\sum_k\frac{(-1)^k\theta^{2k+1}}{(2k+1)!}\Bigr)J.$$
The two scalar series are $\cos\theta$ and $\sin\theta$, so
$$e^{\theta J}=\cos\theta\,I+\sin\theta\,J=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}=R_\theta.$$
(Rearranging the series into even and odd parts is allowed by absolute convergence.)

**Non-injectivity.** $e^{2\pi J}=R_{2\pi}=I=e^{0}$ but $2\pi J\ne0$. More generally $e^{(\theta+2\pi k)J}=e^{\theta J}$ for all integers $k$.

**Remark.** This is the matrix version of $e^{i\theta}=\cos\theta+i\sin\theta$: the subalgebra $\{aI+bJ\}$ of $M_2(\mathbb{R})$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb{C}$, with $J\leftrightarrow i$. Non-injectivity of $\exp$ on the real matrices is the same phenomenon as the multivaluedness of the complex logarithm.
@@@ mnexp-determinant-of-an-exponential.md
---
title: The determinant of an exponential: det e^A = e^(tr A)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: matrix exponential, determinant, trace, triangularisation
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Prove that for every $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$, $\det e^A=e^{\operatorname{tr}A}$. Deduce that $e^A\in GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$ for every real matrix $A$.

## Hint

Over $\mathbb{C}$, every matrix is similar to an upper triangular matrix. Check how $\exp$ behaves under conjugation and on triangular matrices.

## Solution

**Idea.** Reduce to triangular matrices, where both sides can be computed on the diagonal.

**Step 1: conjugation.** For invertible $P$ and any $X$, $(PXP^{-1})^k=PX^kP^{-1}$, so
$$e^{PXP^{-1}}=Pe^XP^{-1}.$$
(Termwise, and $X\mapsto PXP^{-1}$ is linear and continuous.)

**Step 2: triangularisation.** Over $\mathbb{C}$ (Schur or Jordan form) there are an invertible $P\in GL_n(\mathbb{C})$ and an upper triangular $T$ with $A=PTP^{-1}$. The same series definition works in $M_n(\mathbb{C})$, and the identity of step 1 still holds. Hence $e^A=Pe^TP^{-1}$.

**Step 3: triangular matrices.** Let $T$ be upper triangular with diagonal entries $t_{11},\dots,t_{nn}$. Powers of $T$ are upper triangular and the diagonal of $T^k$ is $(t_{11}^k,\dots,t_{nn}^k)$. So $e^T$ is upper triangular with diagonal entries $\sum_kt_{ii}^k/k!=e^{t_{ii}}$.

**Step 4: conclusion.** The determinant of a triangular matrix is the product of its diagonal entries, so
$$\det e^A=\det(Pe^TP^{-1})=\det e^T=\prod_ie^{t_{ii}}=e^{\sum t_{ii}}=e^{\operatorname{tr}T}=e^{\operatorname{tr}A},$$
because the trace is invariant under conjugation.

**Consequence.** For real $A$, $e^A$ is real and $\det e^A=e^{\operatorname{tr}A}>0$. Hence $\exp(M_n(\mathbb{R}))\subset GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$ and in particular $\exp(\mathfrak{sl}_n)\subset SL_n$.

**Check.** For $A=\theta J$ we have $\operatorname{tr}A=0$ and $\det R_\theta=1$ âœ“. For $A=\operatorname{diag}(a,b)$, $\det e^A=e^{a+b}$ âœ“.

**Remark.** An alternative proof: $f(t)=\det e^{tA}$ satisfies $f'(t)=\operatorname{tr}(A)f(t)$ (Jacobi's formula), so $f(t)=e^{t\operatorname{tr}A}$.
@@@ mnexp-the-exponential-of-skew-symmetric-matrices-is-onto-so-n.md
---
title: The exponential maps skew-symmetric matrices onto SO(n)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: orthogonal group, skew-symmetric, normal form, connectedness
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\mathrm{Skew}_n$ be the space of skew-symmetric matrices. Show that $\exp(\mathrm{Skew}_n)=SO(n)$. Deduce that $SO(n)$ is connected.

## Hint

For "$\subset$": $(e^K)^T=e^{K^T}$. For "$\supset$": use the normal form of an orthogonal matrix, a block-diagonal matrix with rotation blocks $R_{\theta_j}$ and a block of $\pm1$.

## Solution

**Inclusion $\exp(\mathrm{Skew}_n)\subset SO(n)$.** Let $K^T=-K$. Transposition commutes with the series (it is linear and continuous), so $(e^K)^T=e^{K^T}=e^{-K}=(e^K)^{-1}$, where the last equality uses $e^Ke^{-K}=e^0=I$. Hence $e^K$ is orthogonal. Moreover $\det e^K=e^{\operatorname{tr}K}=e^0=1$ since a skew-symmetric matrix has zero diagonal.

**Reverse inclusion.** Let $U\in SO(n)$. By the spectral theorem for orthogonal matrices there is $Q\in O(n)$ with
$$U=Q\,\mathrm{diag}\bigl(R_{\theta_1},\dots,R_{\theta_m},\,\varepsilon_1,\dots,\varepsilon_p\bigr)\,Q^T,\qquad\varepsilon_i=\pm1,$$
where the $R_{\theta_j}$ are $2\times2$ rotation blocks (complex eigenvalues $e^{\pm i\theta_j}$ come in conjugate pairs) and the remaining eigenvalues are real, equal to $\pm1$. Since $\det U=1$, the number of $-1$'s among the $\varepsilon_i$ is **even**. Group them in pairs: $\mathrm{diag}(-1,-1)=R_\pi$. So we may assume that all remaining blocks are rotation blocks $R_{\theta_j}$ (with some $\theta_j=\pi$) and a block of $1$'s:
$$U=Q\,\mathrm{diag}\bigl(R_{\theta_1},\dots,R_{\theta_m},I_{p'}\bigr)\,Q^T.$$
By the computation $e^{\theta J}=R_\theta$ and since the exponential of a block-diagonal matrix is the block-diagonal matrix of the exponentials,
$$\mathrm{diag}(R_{\theta_1},\dots,R_{\theta_m},I)=\exp\bigl(\mathrm{diag}(\theta_1J,\dots,\theta_mJ,0)\bigr).$$
Therefore $U=Qe^{D}Q^T=e^{QDQ^T}$ with $QDQ^T$ skew-symmetric ($D$ is skew-symmetric and $Q$ orthogonal).

**Connectedness.** $\mathrm{Skew}_n$ is a vector space, hence path connected, and $\exp$ is continuous, so its image $SO(n)$ is path connected.

**Remark.** The determinant $-1$ component $O(n)\setminus SO(n)$ is not reached by $\exp$ of skew matrices. $O(n)$ has exactly two components, $SO(n)$ and $\det^{-1}(-1)$. In dimension $n=2$, $\exp(\theta J)$ already gives all rotations, and a reflection is not a rotation.
@@@ mnexp-exp-from-symmetric-to-positive-definite-is-a-homeomorphism.md
---
title: exp is a homeomorphism from symmetric to positive definite matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: symmetric matrices, positive definite, homeomorphism, proper map
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ be the space of real symmetric matrices and $\mathrm{PD}$ the open subset of positive definite matrices. Show that $\exp:\mathrm{Sym}_n\to\mathrm{PD}$ is a homeomorphism.

## Hint

Build the inverse $\log$ by diagonalisation. For continuity of the inverse, show that $\exp^{-1}$ of a compact subset of $\mathrm{PD}$ is compact (properness).

## Solution

**Idea.** $\exp$ is a continuous bijection; we show it is also a closed map by checking properness.

**Well defined.** For $S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n$ there is $Q\in O(n)$ with $S=Q\,\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_i)\,Q^T$, so $e^S=Q\,\mathrm{diag}(e^{\lambda_i})\,Q^T$, which is symmetric with eigenvalues $e^{\lambda_i}>0$: positive definite.

**Bijective.** Define $\log P=Q\,\mathrm{diag}(\ln\mu_i)\,Q^T$ for $P=Q\,\mathrm{diag}(\mu_i)\,Q^T\in\mathrm{PD}$. This does not depend on the choice of diagonalisation: if $f$ is any function and $P$ has eigenspaces $E_\mu$, then $f(P)$ is the operator acting as the scalar $f(\mu)$ on each $E_\mu$, and this description is intrinsic. By construction $\exp(\log P)=P$ and $\log(\exp S)=S$. So $\exp$ is a bijection $\mathrm{Sym}_n\to\mathrm{PD}$ with inverse $\log$.

**Continuous.** $\exp$ is continuous on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ carries the induced topology.

**Proper.** Let $K\subset\mathrm{PD}$ be compact. Eigenvalues depend continuously on the matrix (e.g. $\mu_{\max}(P)=\max_{\|x\|=1}x^TPx$ and $\mu_{\min}(P)=\min_{\|x\|=1}x^TPx$ are continuous), so there are $0<a\le b<\infty$ with all eigenvalues of every $P\in K$ in $[a,b]$. Then for $S=\log P$ the eigenvalues $\ln\mu_i$ lie in $[\ln a,\ln b]$, so $\|\log P\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\max(|\ln a|,|\ln b|)$ for $P\in K$: the set $\exp^{-1}(K)\subset\mathrm{Sym}_n$ is bounded. It is closed: $\exp^{-1}(K)=\{S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n:e^S\in K\}$ is the preimage of the compact, hence closed, subset $K\subset M_n(\mathbb{R})$ under the continuous map $\exp$. A closed bounded subset of $\mathrm{Sym}_n\cong\mathbb{R}^{n(n+1)/2}$ is compact.

**Conclusion.** The inverse $\log=\exp^{-1}$ is continuous: if $F\subset\mathrm{Sym}_n$ is closed, then $\log^{-1}(F)=\exp(F)$. To see that $\exp(F)$ is closed in $\mathrm{PD}$, take $P_k=e^{S_k}\to P\in\mathrm{PD}$ with $S_k\in F$. The set $\{P_k\}\cup\{P\}$ is compact in $\mathrm{PD}$, so by properness $(S_k)$ lies in a compact set; a subsequence converges to some $S\in F$ ($F$ closed), and $e^S=\lim e^{S_{k_j}}=P$. So $P\in\exp(F)$.

**Remark.** The same proof shows that $\exp$ is a homeomorphism between the open cone $\mathrm{PD}$ (convex, hence contractible) and the vector space $\mathrm{Sym}_n$. It is even a diffeomorphism (it is smooth with smooth inverse, by the holomorphic functional calculus).
@@@ mnexp-a-matrix-with-positive-determinant-that-is-not-an-exponential.md
---
title: A matrix of positive determinant that is not an exponential
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: matrix exponential, surjectivity, commutation, diagonal matrices
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $B=\mathrm{diag}(-1,-2)\in M_2(\mathbb{R})$. Show that $\det B>0$ but there is no real matrix $A$ with $e^A=B$. Deduce that $\exp:M_2(\mathbb{R})\to GL_2^+(\mathbb{R})$ is not onto.

## Hint

If $e^A=B$, then $A$ commutes with $e^A$. What does it mean for $A$ to commute with a diagonal matrix with distinct entries?

## Solution

**Setup.** $\det B=2>0$, so $B\in GL_2^+(\mathbb{R})$. Suppose $e^A=B$ for some $A\in M_2(\mathbb{R})$.

**$A$ commutes with $B$.** $A$ commutes with each power $A^k$, hence with the sum $\sum A^k/k!=e^A=B$.

**$A$ is diagonal.** Write $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$. Then
$$AB=\begin{pmatrix}-a&-2b\\-c&-2d\end{pmatrix},\qquad BA=\begin{pmatrix}-a&-b\\-2c&-2d\end{pmatrix}.$$
$AB=BA$ forces $-2b=-b$ and $-c=-2c$, i.e. $b=c=0$. So $A=\mathrm{diag}(a,d)$ is diagonal. (This is where the **distinct** eigenvalues of $B$ are used.)

**Contradiction.** For diagonal $A$, $e^A=\mathrm{diag}(e^a,e^d)$, whose entries are positive. But $B$ has negative diagonal entries.

**Conclusion.** $B\in GL_2^+(\mathbb{R})$ is not in the image of $\exp$.

**Remark.** Over $\mathbb{C}$, $B=\exp\mathrm{diag}(i\pi,\ \ln2+i\pi)$, so every invertible complex matrix is an exponential. Over $\mathbb{R}$, a matrix is an exponential iff each Jordan block with a negative eigenvalue occurs an even number of times, which fails for $B$ (two *different* negative eigenvalues). By contrast $-I=e^{\pi J}$ is an exponential.
@@@ mnexp-the-matrix-logarithm-near-the-identity.md
---
title: The logarithm near the identity and local inverse of exp
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: matrix logarithm, local homeomorphism, power series
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Here $\|\cdot\|$ is the operator norm. For $\|X\|<1$ define $\log(I+X)=\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}kX^k$. Show that:

1. the series converges absolutely;
2. $e^{\log(I+X)}=I+X$ for $\|X\|<1$, and $\log(e^A)=A$ for $\|A\|<\ln2$;
3. $\exp$ restricts to a homeomorphism from the ball $B(0,\ln2)$ onto an open set containing $B(I,\tfrac12)$, with inverse $\log$.

## Hint

Use the formal power series identities $\exp(\log(1+x))=1+x$ and $\log(1+(e^x-1))=x$, and justify substitution by absolute convergence.

## Solution

**1.** $\sum_k\frac{\|X\|^k}k=-\ln(1-\|X\|)<\infty$ for $\|X\|<1$.

**2. The identity $e^{\log(I+X)}=I+X$.** Let $x$ be a formal variable. In $\mathbb{Q}[[x]]$ one has $\exp(\log(1+x))=1+x$ (a known identity of formal power series). Substituting $X$ for $x$ is legitimate when the double series of norms converges: the norm-majorant is
$$\sum_{m\ge0}\frac1{m!}\Bigl(\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{\|X\|^k}k\Bigr)^m=\exp\bigl(-\ln(1-\|X\|)\bigr)=\frac1{1-\|X\|}<\infty.$$
Absolute convergence allows rearranging the terms into powers of $X$, and the coefficients are those of the formal identity. Hence $e^{\log(I+X)}=I+X$.

Similarly, for $\|A\|<\ln2$ put $X=e^A-I$. Then $\|X\|\le e^{\|A\|}-1<1$ and the formal identity $\log(\exp x)=x$ (that is, $\log(1+(e^x-1))=x$) gives $\log(e^A)=A$ once the double series converges absolutely: its majorant is
$$\sum_k\frac{(e^{\|A\|}-1)^k}k=-\ln\bigl(2-e^{\|A\|}\bigr)<\infty\quad\text{exactly when }\|A\|<\ln2.$$

**3. Local homeomorphism.** Let $U=\{I+X:\|X\|<1,\ \|\log(I+X)\|<\ln2\}$. It is open, as the preimage of $B(0,\ln2)$ under the continuous map $\log$ on the open ball $B(I,1)$ (intersected with that ball).
- If $\|A\|<\ln2$, then $\|e^A-I\|<1$ and $\log e^A=A$ has norm $<\ln2$, so $e^A\in U$.
- If $I+X\in U$ then $A=\log(I+X)\in B(0,\ln2)$ and $e^A=I+X$.

So $\exp:B(0,\ln2)\to U$ is a bijection with continuous inverse $\log$, hence a homeomorphism. Finally $U\supset B(I,\frac12)$: if $\|X\|<\frac12$ then $\|\log(I+X)\|\le-\ln(1-\|X\|)<\ln2$.

**Remark.** This shows that $\exp$ is a local homeomorphism at $0$: every matrix close to $I$ has a (unique small) logarithm. It is the first step in proving that closed subgroups of $GL_n$ are Lie groups.
@@@ mnexp-unipotent-matrices-and-nilpotent-matrices.md
---
title: Nilpotent matrices and unipotent matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: nilpotent, unipotent, polynomial inverse, exponential
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\mathcal{N}=\{N\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):N^n=0\}$ be the nilpotent cone and $\mathcal{U}=\{I+N:N\in\mathcal{N}\}$ the set of unipotent matrices. Show that $\exp:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{U}$ is a homeomorphism whose inverse is given by a polynomial map.

## Hint

For nilpotent $N$ both series are finite sums. Check that $\exp(N)$ is unipotent and $\log(I+N)$ is nilpotent.

## Solution

**Finite sums.** If $N^n=0$ then $N^k=0$ for all $k\ge n$, so
$$e^N=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{N^k}{k!},\qquad\log(I+N)=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}kN^k.$$
Both are polynomials in $N$ (of degree $<n$), with fixed rational coefficients, so as maps of the entries of $N$ they are polynomial, hence continuous.

**Images.** $e^N-I=N\bigl(I+\frac N2+\cdots\bigr)=N\cdot P(N)$ where $P(N)$ commutes with $N$, so $(e^N-I)^n=N^nP(N)^n=0$: $e^N\in\mathcal{U}$. Similarly $\log(I+N)=N\cdot Q(N)$ is nilpotent ($N$ and $Q(N)$ commute). So $\exp:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{U}$ and $\log:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{N}$ are well defined.

**Mutually inverse.** The formal identities $\exp(\log(1+x))=1+x$ and $\log(\exp x)=x$ hold in $\mathbb{Q}[[x]]$. Substituting a nilpotent $N$ with $N^n=0$ amounts to working modulo $x^n$ (a finite computation), so the identities hold for $N$:
$$e^{\log(I+N)}=I+N,\qquad\log(e^N)=N.$$

**Conclusion.** Both $\exp|_{\mathcal{N}}$ and $\log|_{\mathcal{U}}$ are polynomial in the entries, inverse to each other. So $\exp$ is a homeomorphism (indeed a polynomial bijection with polynomial inverse).

**Example.** For $N=\begin{pmatrix}0&a\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$: $e^N=\begin{pmatrix}1&a\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ and $\log\begin{pmatrix}1&a\\0&1\end{pmatrix}=N$ âœ“.

**Remark.** This fails for non-unipotent matrices, where $\log$ is only defined locally (see the previous problem) and $\exp$ is not even injective.
@@@ mnexp-unitriangular-and-triangular-groups.md
---
title: Topology of the groups of upper triangular matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: triangular matrices, connected components, contractible, unipotent
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $U_n$ be the group of upper unitriangular matrices (ones on the diagonal) and $B_n$ the group of invertible upper triangular matrices in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$. Show that:

1. $U_n$ is homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^{n(n-1)/2}$;
2. $B_n$ has exactly $2^n$ connected components, each contractible.

## Hint

Use $\exp$ on strictly upper triangular matrices for $U_n$, and the factorisation $T=D\cdot(D^{-1}T)$ for $B_n$.

## Solution

**1. $U_n$.** Let $\mathfrak{n}$ be the space of strictly upper triangular matrices, a vector space of dimension $n(n-1)/2$ (the free entries above the diagonal). Each $N\in\mathfrak{n}$ satisfies $N^n=0$ and $\exp(N)$ is upper triangular with ones on the diagonal, so $\exp(\mathfrak{n})\subset U_n$. Conversely $U\in U_n$ is $I+N$ with $N\in\mathfrak{n}$ and $\log(I+N)\in\mathfrak{n}$ (a polynomial in $N$ without constant term, so strictly upper triangular). By the previous problem $\exp:\mathfrak{n}\to U_n$ is a homeomorphism, so $U_n\cong\mathfrak{n}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n(n-1)/2}$.

**2. $B_n$: a product decomposition.** An invertible upper triangular $T$ has nonzero diagonal entries $d_i=t_{ii}$. Let $D=\mathrm{diag}(d_1,\dots,d_n)$. Then $D^{-1}T$ is upper triangular with ones on the diagonal, so $T=D\cdot U$ with $U=D^{-1}T\in U_n$. The map
$$B_n\to\mathcal{D}\times U_n,\qquad T\mapsto\bigl(D,\ D^{-1}T\bigr)$$
(where $\mathcal{D}\cong(\mathbb{R}^\times)^n$ is the group of invertible diagonal matrices) is continuous with continuous inverse $(D,U)\mapsto DU$. So $B_n\cong(\mathbb{R}^\times)^n\times U_n$ as topological spaces.

**Components.** $\mathbb{R}^\times=(-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)$ has two components, each homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}$, hence contractible. So $(\mathbb{R}^\times)^n$ has $2^n$ components (choose a sign for each diagonal entry), each homeomorphic to $(0,\infty)^n\cong\mathbb{R}^n$. Multiplying by $U_n\cong\mathbb{R}^{n(n-1)/2}$ (connected and contractible) does not change the number of components, and each component of $B_n$ is homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^n\times\mathbb{R}^{n(n-1)/2}=\mathbb{R}^{n(n+1)/2}$, which is contractible.

**Check.** $n=1$: $B_1=\mathbb{R}^\times$, $2$ components âœ“. $n=2$: $B_2=\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\0&d\end{pmatrix}:ad\ne0\}$, $4$ components according to the signs of $a$ and $d$ âœ“.

**Remark.** The signs of the diagonal entries are the only topological invariants of $B_n$: there are $2^n$ components, while $GL_n$ has only $2$ (connected by rotations, which are not triangular).
@@@ mnexp-polar-decomposition-via-the-exponential.md
---
title: The polar-exponential decomposition of GL_n
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: polar decomposition, homeomorphism, orthogonal group, GL_n
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that $(U,S)\mapsto Ue^S$ is a homeomorphism $SO(n)\times\mathrm{Sym}_n\to GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$, and that $(U,S)\mapsto Ue^S$ is a homeomorphism $O(n)\times\mathrm{Sym}_n\to GL_n(\mathbb{R})$. Deduce that $GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$ is connected and $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ has exactly two components.

## Hint

Polar decomposition: every $A\in GL_n$ is uniquely $A=UP$ with $U$ orthogonal and $P$ positive definite, and $A\mapsto(U,P)$ is continuous. Then compose with $\exp:\mathrm{Sym}_n\to\mathrm{PD}$.

## Solution

**Polar decomposition.** For $A\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$, $A^TA$ is positive definite. Let $P=(A^TA)^{1/2}$, the unique positive definite square root ($=\exp(\tfrac12\log(A^TA))$), and $U=AP^{-1}$. Then $U^TU=P^{-1}A^TAP^{-1}=I$, so $U\in O(n)$ and $A=UP$. Uniqueness: if $A=UP=U'P'$ then $A^TA=P^2=P'^2$ and uniqueness of the positive square root gives $P=P'$, hence $U=U'$. The map $A\mapsto(U,P)$ is continuous, since $A\mapsto A^TA$ is continuous and $P\mapsto P^{1/2}=\exp(\frac12\log P)$ is continuous (homeomorphism $\exp$ of the previous problem and its inverse). Conversely $(U,P)\mapsto UP$ is continuous. So $GL_n\cong O(n)\times\mathrm{PD}$.

**Composition with $\exp$.** The map $\Phi:(U,S)\mapsto Ue^S$ is the composition of $\mathrm{id}\times\exp:O(n)\times\mathrm{Sym}_n\to O(n)\times\mathrm{PD}$ (a homeomorphism) and the polar homeomorphism $O(n)\times\mathrm{PD}\to GL_n$. So $\Phi:O(n)\times\mathrm{Sym}_n\to GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is a homeomorphism.

**Positive determinant.** $\det(Ue^S)=\det U\cdot e^{\operatorname{tr}S}$ has the sign of $\det U$. So $\Phi$ restricts to a homeomorphism $SO(n)\times\mathrm{Sym}_n\to GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$ (and $O^-(n)\times\mathrm{Sym}_n\to GL_n^-(\mathbb{R})$).

**Connectedness.** $SO(n)$ is connected (it is $\exp(\mathrm{Skew}_n)$) and $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ is a vector space, so $GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})\cong SO(n)\times\mathrm{Sym}_n$ is connected. $GL_n(\mathbb{R})=GL_n^+\sqcup GL_n^-$, two open disjoint nonempty (for $n\ge1$) connected sets, so exactly two components.

**Remark.** Topologically, $GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$ "is" $SO(n)$ times a contractible space: it **deformation retracts** onto $SO(n)$ (shrink $S\to0$). All the interesting topology of $GL_n^+$ is that of $SO(n)$.
@@@ mnexp-homogeneous-spaces-gl-n-over-o-n.md
---
title: The quotients GL_n/O(n) and SL_n/SO(n)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: homogeneous space, quotient topology, positive definite, traceless
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that $GL_n(\mathbb{R})/O(n)$ is homeomorphic to the space $\mathrm{PD}$ of positive definite matrices, and that $SL_n(\mathbb{R})/SO(n)$ is homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^{n(n+1)/2-1}$.

## Hint

The map $A\mapsto AA^T$ is constant on the cosets $A\cdot O(n)$. For $SL_n$, use $\log$ on positive definite matrices of determinant $1$.

## Solution

**The map.** Define $\pi:GL_n\to\mathrm{PD}$, $\pi(A)=AA^T$. It is continuous, and for $U\in O(n)$,
$$\pi(AU)=AUU^TA^T=AA^T=\pi(A),$$
so $\pi$ is constant on left cosets $A\cdot O(n)$ and induces a continuous map $\bar\pi:GL_n/O(n)\to\mathrm{PD}$. (Note: with $A\mapsto A^TA$ one would get the space of right cosets $O(n)\backslash GL_n$.)

**Bijective.** *Surjective:* for $P\in\mathrm{PD}$, $A=P^{1/2}$ satisfies $AA^T=P$. *Injective:* if $AA^T=BB^T$, put $U=B^{-1}A$; then $UU^T=B^{-1}AA^TB^{-T}=B^{-1}BB^TB^{-T}=I$, so $U\in O(n)$ and $A=BU\in B\cdot O(n)$.

**Continuous inverse.** The inverse is $P\mapsto[P^{1/2}]$, the composition of the continuous map $P\mapsto P^{1/2}=\exp(\frac12\log P)$ and the quotient map $GL_n\to GL_n/O(n)$. So $\bar\pi$ is a homeomorphism.

**The case of $SL_n$.** Restricting, $SL_n/SO(n)\to\{P\in\mathrm{PD}:\det P=1\}$ is a continuous bijection: if $A\in SL_n$ then $\det AA^T=1$; conversely, for $\det P=1$ the square root $P^{1/2}$ has determinant $1$ and lies in $SL_n$. Also $AA^T=BB^T$ with $A,B\in SL_n$ gives $U=B^{-1}A\in O(n)$ with $\det U=1$, so $U\in SO(n)$.

**Identification with a vector space.** By $\det e^S=e^{\operatorname{tr}S}$ and the bijection $\exp:\mathrm{Sym}_n\to\mathrm{PD}$, $\det e^S=1$ iff $\operatorname{tr}S=0$. So $\exp$ restricts to a homeomorphism
$$\{S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n:\operatorname{tr}S=0\}\ \to\ \{P\in\mathrm{PD}:\det P=1\},$$
and the space of traceless symmetric matrices is a vector space of dimension $\frac{n(n+1)}2-1$.

**Check.** $n=2$: $SL_2/SO(2)\cong\mathbb{R}^2$, the hyperbolic plane (as a topological space an open disc).

**Remark.** Another consequence: $GL_n$ deformation retracts onto $O(n)$ and $SL_n$ onto $SO(n)$, so topologically $SL_n\simeq SO(n)$.
@@@ mnexp-so-n-over-so-n-1-is-the-sphere.md
---
title: SO(n)/SO(nâˆ’1) is the sphere
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: orbit map, quotient, sphere, compact Hausdorff
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that for $n\ge2$, $SO(n)/SO(n-1)$ is homeomorphic to the sphere $S^{n-1}$, where $SO(n-1)$ is embedded in $SO(n)$ as the matrices fixing $e_1$ (that is, $\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&V\end{pmatrix}$ with $V\in SO(n-1)$).

## Hint

Consider the orbit map $U\mapsto Ue_1$, and use that a continuous bijection from a compact space to a Hausdorff space is a homeomorphism.

## Solution

**Idea.** $SO(n)$ acts transitively on the sphere with stabiliser of $e_1$ equal to $SO(n-1)$.

**The map.** Let $\varphi:SO(n)\to S^{n-1}$, $\varphi(U)=Ue_1$ (the first column). It is continuous and takes values in the unit sphere since $U$ is orthogonal.

**Surjective.** Let $v\in S^{n-1}$. Complete $v$ to an orthonormal basis $(v,w_2,\dots,w_n)$ and let $U_0$ be the matrix with these columns, $U_0\in O(n)$. If $\det U_0=-1$, replace $w_n$ by $-w_n$ (possible for $n\ge2$). Then $U_0\in SO(n)$ and $U_0e_1=v$.

**The fibres are the cosets.** Let $H=\{U\in SO(n):Ue_1=e_1\}$. For $U,V\in SO(n)$:
$$Ue_1=Ve_1\iff V^{-1}Ue_1=e_1\iff V^{-1}U\in H\iff U\in V\cdot H.$$
So $\varphi$ is constant on left cosets and its fibres are exactly the cosets: it induces a continuous bijection $\bar\varphi:SO(n)/H\to S^{n-1}$.

**The stabiliser.** If $Ue_1=e_1$ then (by orthogonality, $U^Te_1=e_1$ too) $U=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&V\end{pmatrix}$ with $V\in O(n-1)$ and $\det V=\det U=1$. So $H\cong SO(n-1)$. For $n=2$, $H=\{I\}$ and the statement reads $SO(2)\cong S^1$.

**Homeomorphism.** $SO(n)$ is compact (closed and bounded), so $SO(n)/H$ is compact, $S^{n-1}$ is Hausdorff, and a continuous bijection from a compact space to a Hausdorff space is a homeomorphism.

**Remark.** $SO(3)/SO(2)\cong S^2$: choosing an axis (a point of the sphere) leaves a rotation about that axis undetermined.
@@@ mnexp-the-projective-linear-group-pgl-n.md
---
title: Connected components of the projective linear group PGL_n(â„)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: projective group, quotient, determinant, parity
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $PGL_n(\mathbb{R})=GL_n(\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{R}^\times I$ be the quotient by the scalar matrices. Show that it is connected when $n$ is odd and has exactly two connected components when $n$ is even.

## Hint

How does $\det$ change when $A$ is replaced by $\lambda A$?

## Solution

**Idea.** $\det(\lambda A)=\lambda^n\det A$: the sign of the determinant is either destroyed or preserved by scaling, according to the parity of $n$.

**Connected images.** Let $q:GL_n\to PGL_n$ be the quotient map. It is continuous and onto, so images of connected sets are connected. We know $GL_n^+$ and $GL_n^-$ are connected (polar decomposition).

**$n$ odd.** Take $A\in GL_n^-$. Then $-A$ has $\det(-A)=(-1)^n\det A=-\det A>0$, so $-A\in GL_n^+$ and $q(A)=q(-A)\in q(GL_n^+)$. Hence $q(GL_n)=q(GL_n^+)$, a continuous image of the connected set $GL_n^+$: $PGL_n$ is connected.

**$n$ even.** Now $\det(\lambda A)=\lambda^n\det A$ has the same sign as $\det A$ for every $\lambda\ne0$. So $\mathrm{sgn}\det$ is constant on each scalar class and induces a well-defined continuous map $\overline{\mathrm{sgn}\det}:PGL_n\to\{\pm1\}$. It is onto (e.g. $I$ and $\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$), so $PGL_n$ is disconnected. Moreover $PGL_n=q(GL_n^+)\cup q(GL_n^-)$ is a union of two connected sets, which are the two (disjoint, since the sign differs) fibres of $\overline{\mathrm{sgn}\det}$. Hence there are exactly two components.

**Check.** $n=1$: $PGL_1$ is a point. $n=2$: $PGL_2(\mathbb{R})\supset PSL_2(\mathbb{R})$ with index 2 and has two components âœ“.

**Remark.** For odd $n$, $PGL_n(\mathbb{R})\cong SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ (every class has a unique representative of determinant $1$, namely the real $n$-th root of $1/\det A$ times $A$).
@@@ mnexp-centres-of-gl-n-and-sl-n.md
---
title: Centres of GL_n and SL_n, and discrete normal subgroups
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: centre, discrete subgroup, connected group
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

1. Compute the centres of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ and $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$.
2. Show that a discrete normal subgroup of a connected topological group is central.

## Hint

For part 1, test commutation with the matrix units $E_{ij}+I$ or with $I+E_{ij}$. For part 2, look at the map $g\mapsto gng^{-1}$ for fixed $n\in N$.

## Solution

**1. Centre of $GL_n$.** Let $Z$ commute with all invertible matrices. Then for $i\ne j$ it commutes with $I+E_{ij}$, hence with $E_{ij}$. Now $ZE_{ij}$ has $Ze_i$ as its $j$-th column and zeros elsewhere, while $E_{ij}Z$ has the $j$-th row of $Z$ as its $i$-th row and zeros elsewhere. Comparing entries: the $(k,j)$ entry for $k\ne i$ gives $z_{ki}=0$, the $(i,l)$ entry for $l\ne j$ gives $z_{jl}=0$, and the $(i,j)$ entry gives $z_{ii}=z_{jj}$. As $i\ne j$ are arbitrary, $Z$ is diagonal with equal diagonal entries. Thus $Z$ is a scalar matrix: $Z(GL_n)=\{\lambda I:\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^\times\}$ (conversely scalar matrices are central).

**Centre of $SL_n$.** The same argument applies since $I+E_{ij}\in SL_n$ for $i\ne j$. So the centre consists of the scalar matrices of determinant $1$: $\lambda^n=1$ with $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$. If $n$ is odd, $\lambda=1$ and $Z(SL_n)=\{I\}$. If $n$ is even, $\lambda=\pm1$ and $Z(SL_n)=\{\pm I\}$.

**2. Discrete normal subgroups.** Let $G$ be connected, $N\trianglelefteq G$ discrete (every point of $N$ is isolated in $N$, with the subspace topology). Fix $m\in N$ and define $c:G\to N$, $c(g)=gmg^{-1}$. It is well defined since $N$ is normal and continuous (into $G$, with values in $N$). Its image is a connected subset of $G$ contained in the discrete space $N$ (in the subspace topology, a connected subset of a discrete space is a point). So $c$ is constant, equal to $c(e)=m$: $gmg^{-1}=m$ for all $g$. Hence $m$ is central.

**Application.** In $SL_2(\mathbb{R})$ (connected) the subgroup $\{\pm I\}$ is discrete, normal and central, as predicted. Connectedness is needed: in the non-connected group $O(2)$ the cyclic group generated by $R_{2\pi/3}$ is finite (so discrete) and normal (conjugating by a reflection gives $R_{-2\pi/3}$ and conjugating by a rotation changes nothing), but it is not central, since a reflection does not commute with $R_{2\pi/3}$.

**Remark.** This is the first step in showing that a covering group's kernel is central: for a connected covering $\tilde G\to G$ of topological groups, the kernel is discrete normal, hence central, hence abelian.
@@@ mnexp-the-closure-of-a-subgroup.md
---
title: The closure of a subgroup of GL_n is a subgroup
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: topological group, closure, subgroup
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $H$ be a subgroup of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ and $\overline H$ its closure in $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$. Show that $\overline H$ is a subgroup.

## Hint

Characterise the closure by sequences and use continuity of multiplication and inversion in $GL_n$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The group operations are continuous, so they pass to limits.

**Closure by sequences.** $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is a metric space (subset of $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$), so $\overline H$ is the set of limits in $GL_n$ of sequences from $H$.

**Subgroup test.** $\overline H$ is nonempty ($I\in H$). Let $a,b\in\overline H$ and choose $a_k,b_k\in H$ with $a_k\to a$, $b_k\to b$ in $GL_n$. Then:
- $b_k^{-1}\to b^{-1}$ (inversion is continuous on $GL_n$: entries of $B^{-1}$ are rational functions of those of $B$, with denominator $\det B\ne0$);
- $a_kb_k^{-1}\to ab^{-1}$ (multiplication is continuous).

Since $a_kb_k^{-1}\in H$, the limit $ab^{-1}$ lies in $\overline H$. So $\overline H$ is closed under $(a,b)\mapsto ab^{-1}$, and $\overline H$ is a subgroup.

**Warning.** The closure must be taken **in $GL_n$**. The closure in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ may contain singular matrices: e.g. $H=\{\lambda I:\lambda>0\}$ has closure $\{\lambda I:\lambda\ge0\}$ in $M_n$, which contains $0$ and is not a group.

**Remark.** If $H$ is normal, so is $\overline H$ (conjugation is continuous), and if $H$ is abelian then $\overline H$ is abelian.
@@@ mnexp-a-dense-subgroup-of-a-torus-in-so-4.md
---
title: A subgroup of SO(4) that is not closed
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: irrational winding, torus, Kronecker, non-closed subgroup
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

For $t\in\mathbb{R}$ let $\varphi(t)=R_t\oplus R_{\sqrt2\,t}\in SO(4)$ (block diagonal with two $2\times2$ rotations). Show that $H=\varphi(\mathbb{R})$ is a subgroup of $SO(4)$ which is not closed.

## Hint

The matrices $R_x\oplus R_y$ form a torus $T\cong(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})^2$. Show that $H$ is dense in $T$ but $H\ne T$.

## Solution

**Subgroup.** $\varphi(s+t)=\varphi(s)\varphi(t)$ and $\varphi(-t)=\varphi(t)^{-1}$, so $H$ is a subgroup of the torus $T=\{R_x\oplus R_y:x,y\in\mathbb{R}\}$, which is a closed subgroup of $SO(4)$ (compact: image of $[0,2\pi]^2$ under a continuous map).

**Density in $T$.** *Step 1: $\{n\sqrt2\bmod1\}$ is dense in $[0,1)$.* Divide $[0,1)$ into $N$ intervals of length $1/N$. Among the $N+1$ numbers $\{k\sqrt2\}$, $k=0,\dots,N$ (fractional parts), two lie in the same interval (pigeonhole): $|\{k\sqrt2\}-\{l\sqrt2\}|<1/N$, so there is $m=k-l\ne0$ with $\|m\sqrt2\|<1/N$ ($\|\cdot\|$: distance to the nearest integer), and $\|m\sqrt2\|\ne0$ since $\sqrt2$ is irrational. The multiples $jm\sqrt2$, $j\in\mathbb{Z}$, move along the circle $\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$ in steps of size $<1/N$, so they come within $1/N$ of every point.

*Step 2.* Let $(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^2$ and $\varepsilon>0$. We have $\varphi(t+2\pi n)=R_t\oplus R_{\sqrt2t+2\pi\sqrt2n}$. Take $t=x$. By step 1 there is an integer $n$ with $2\pi\sqrt2n$ within $\varepsilon$ of $y-\sqrt2x$ modulo $2\pi$ (the multiples $n\sqrt2$ are dense modulo $1$, so the multiples $2\pi n\sqrt2$ are dense modulo $2\pi$). Then $\varphi(x+2\pi n)$ is within distance $O(\varepsilon)$ of $R_x\oplus R_y$. So $H$ is dense in $T$.

**$H\ne T$.** The element $R_0\oplus R_\pi=\mathrm{diag}(1,1,-1,-1)\in T$ is not in $H$: $\varphi(t)=R_0\oplus R_\pi$ requires $t=2\pi m$ and $\sqrt2t=\pi+2\pi l$ for integers $m,l$, i.e. $2\sqrt2m=1+2l$, impossible since the left side is irrational for $m\ne0$ and for $m=0$ it gives $0=1+2l$.

**Not closed.** If $H$ were closed in $SO(4)$, then, being dense in the closed subset $T$, we would get $T\subset\overline H=H$, contradicting $H\ne T$.

**Remark.** $H$ is a one-parameter subgroup that "winds" densely on the torus: it is an injective continuous image of $\mathbb{R}$ (the map $\varphi$ is injective) but not a closed embedded curve. This is the standard example showing that Lie subgroups need not be closed.
@@@ mnexp-the-lie-product-formula.md
---
title: The Lie product formula
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Lie product formula, Trotter, norm estimates, telescoping
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $A,B\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$,
$$\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(e^{A/k}e^{B/k}\right)^k=e^{A+B}.$$

## Hint

Let $X_k=e^{A/k}e^{B/k}$ and $Y_k=e^{(A+B)/k}$, so $Y_k^k=e^{A+B}$. Show $\|X_k-Y_k\|=O(1/k^2)$, then use $X^k-Y^k=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}X^{j}(X-Y)Y^{k-1-j}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Both $X_k$ and $Y_k$ equal $I+\frac{A+B}k$ up to $O(1/k^2)$; raising to the $k$-th power, $k$ errors of size $1/k^2$ add up to $O(1/k)\to0$.

**Step 1: $\|X_k-Y_k\|\le C/k^2$.** Write $e^{M}=I+M+r(M)$ with $r(M)=\sum_{m\ge2}M^m/m!$ and $\|r(M)\|\le\frac{\|M\|^2}2e^{\|M\|}$ (since $\sum_{m\ge2}x^m/m!\le\frac{x^2}2e^x$). Fix $a=\|A\|$, $b=\|B\|$ and let $k\ge1$. Then
$$X_k=\Bigl(I+\tfrac Ak+r_A\Bigr)\Bigl(I+\tfrac Bk+r_B\Bigr)=I+\tfrac{A+B}k+\Bigl(\tfrac{AB}{k^2}+r_A+r_B+\tfrac Akr_B+r_A\tfrac Bk+r_Ar_B\Bigr),$$
$$Y_k=I+\tfrac{A+B}k+r\bigl(\tfrac{A+B}k\bigr),$$
with $\|r_A\|\le\frac{a^2}{2k^2}e^a$, $\|r_B\|\le\frac{b^2}{2k^2}e^b$, $\|r(\tfrac{A+B}k)\|\le\frac{(a+b)^2}{2k^2}e^{a+b}$. All the correction terms are bounded by $C/k^2$ with $C$ depending only on $a,b$. Hence $\|X_k-Y_k\|\le C/k^2$.

**Step 2: norms of $X_k,Y_k$.** $\|X_k\|\le e^{a/k}e^{b/k}=e^{(a+b)/k}$ and $\|Y_k\|\le e^{\|A+B\|/k}\le e^{(a+b)/k}$. So both are bounded by $M_k=e^{(a+b)/k}$ (for the operator norm, or up to the harmless constant $\|I\|$).

**Step 3: telescoping.** The identity $X^k-Y^k=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}X^{j}(X-Y)Y^{k-1-j}$ (check by expanding: consecutive terms cancel) gives
$$\|X_k^k-Y_k^k\|\le\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\|X_k\|^j\|X_k-Y_k\|\|Y_k\|^{k-1-j}\le k\,M_k^{k-1}\cdot\frac C{k^2}\le\frac Ck\,e^{a+b}.$$
(using $M_k^{k-1}\le M_k^k=e^{a+b}$).

**Conclusion.** $\|(e^{A/k}e^{B/k})^k-e^{A+B}\|\le\frac{Ce^{a+b}}k\to0$.

**Remark.** The formula shows that although $e^Ae^B\ne e^{A+B}$ in general, the products of "infinitesimal steps" recover $e^{A+B}$. It is the mathematical core of Trotterâ€“Kato splitting methods in numerical analysis and of the fact that the Lie algebra of a closed subgroup is a vector space (next problem).
@@@ mnexp-the-lie-algebra-of-a-closed-subgroup-is-a-vector-space.md
---
title: The Lie algebra of a closed subgroup is a linear subspace
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Lie algebra, closed subgroup, Lie product formula
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $H\le GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ be a closed subgroup and
$$\mathfrak h=\{X\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):e^{tX}\in H\ \text{for all }t\in\mathbb{R}\}.$$
Show that $\mathfrak h$ is a linear subspace of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Closure under scalar multiples is easy. For sums, use the Lie product formula applied to $tX$ and $tY$, together with the closedness of $H$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The sum $X+Y$ is approximated through products of elements of $H$, and $H$ is closed.

**Contains $0$.** $e^{t\cdot0}=I\in H$, so $0\in\mathfrak h$.

**Scalar multiples.** If $X\in\mathfrak h$ and $c\in\mathbb{R}$, then for every $t$: $e^{t(cX)}=e^{(tc)X}\in H$ because $tc$ is just another real number. So $cX\in\mathfrak h$.

**Sums.** Let $X,Y\in\mathfrak h$ and fix $t\in\mathbb{R}$. For every integer $k\ge1$, $e^{tX/k}\in H$ and $e^{tY/k}\in H$ ($H$ contains $e^{sX}$ for every real $s$), hence the product
$$g_k=\bigl(e^{tX/k}e^{tY/k}\bigr)^k\in H.$$
By the Lie product formula applied to $A=tX$, $B=tY$, $g_k\to e^{t(X+Y)}$ as $k\to\infty$. The limit is invertible (it is an exponential), so it lies in $GL_n$, and $H$ is closed in $GL_n$: $e^{t(X+Y)}\in H$. This holds for every $t$, so $X+Y\in\mathfrak h$.

**Remark.** The hypothesis "closed" is used in the last step. For the non-closed subgroup $\varphi(\mathbb{R})$ of the torus in $SO(4)$ the limit $e^{t(X+Y)}$ need not stay in the subgroup: its closure is the whole torus, whose Lie algebra is two-dimensional, larger than the line generated by the winding direction.
@@@ mnexp-continuous-one-parameter-subgroups-are-exponentials.md
---
title: Every continuous one-parameter subgroup is e^(tA)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: one-parameter subgroup, regularity, integration trick
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ be a continuous group homomorphism. Show that there is $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ with $\varphi(t)=e^{tA}$ for all $t$.

## Hint

Integrate $\varphi$ over a short interval: the integral is invertible, and it makes $\varphi$ differentiable.

## Solution

**Idea.** Continuity of $\varphi$ upgrades to smoothness by an averaging trick, after which $\varphi$ solves $\varphi'=A\varphi$.

**Step 1: an invertible average.** $\varphi$ is continuous and $\varphi(0)=I$. For $c>0$ put $M_c=\int_0^c\varphi(s)\,ds$ (entrywise integral). Then $\frac1cM_c\to\varphi(0)=I$ as $c\to0^+$ (continuity of $\varphi$ at $0$). Since $GL_n$ is open, $\frac1cM_c$ is invertible for $c$ small enough. Fix such a $c$ and write $M=M_c$.

**Step 2: differentiability.** For every $t$, using the homomorphism property $\varphi(t)\varphi(s)=\varphi(t+s)$,
$$\varphi(t)M=\int_0^c\varphi(t+s)\,ds=\int_t^{t+c}\varphi(u)\,du.$$
The right side is differentiable in $t$ (fundamental theorem of calculus, $\varphi$ continuous), with derivative $\varphi(t+c)-\varphi(t)$. Hence $\varphi(t)=\bigl(\int_t^{t+c}\varphi\bigr)M^{-1}$ is differentiable (even $C^1$).

**Step 3: the differential equation.** From $\varphi(t+h)=\varphi(t)\varphi(h)$,
$$\frac{\varphi(t+h)-\varphi(t)}h=\varphi(t)\,\frac{\varphi(h)-I}h\ \xrightarrow[h\to0]{}\ \varphi(t)A,\qquad A:=\varphi'(0).$$
So $\varphi'(t)=\varphi(t)A=A\varphi(t)$ (they commute since $\varphi(t)\varphi(h)=\varphi(h)\varphi(t)$).

**Step 4: uniqueness for the ODE.** Let $\Psi(t)=e^{-tA}\varphi(t)$. Then
$$\Psi'(t)=-Ae^{-tA}\varphi(t)+e^{-tA}A\varphi(t)=0,$$
so $\Psi$ is constant, equal to $\Psi(0)=I$. Therefore $\varphi(t)=e^{tA}$.

**Remark.** Without continuity there exist wild homomorphisms $\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^\times$, $t\mapsto e^{f(t)}$, with $f$ an additive non-measurable function (Hamel basis). (It is a classical theorem, proved by a Steinhaus-type argument, that measurable homomorphisms are automatically continuous.)
@@@ mnexp-subgroups-generated-by-a-neighbourhood-of-the-identity.md
---
title: A neighbourhood of the identity generates the connected group
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: generating set, open subgroup, connected group, GL_n^+
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

1. Show that if $G$ is a connected topological group and $V$ a neighbourhood of the identity, then $V$ generates $G$.
2. Deduce that $GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$ is generated by $\exp(M_n(\mathbb{R}))$, and by any neighbourhood of $I$ in $GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

A subgroup that contains a neighbourhood of $e$ is open. An open subgroup is also closed.

## Solution

**1. Open subgroup.** Let $H=\langle V\rangle$ be the subgroup generated by $V$. Shrink $V$ to an open neighbourhood $V'\subset V$ of the identity. For $h\in H$, the left translate $hV'$ is open (left multiplication by $h$ is a homeomorphism), contains $h$ and is contained in $H$. So $H$ is open.

**Closed.** The complement of $H$ is the union of the cosets $gH$ with $g\notin H$, each of which is open (translate of the open set $H$). So $H$ is closed.

**Conclusion.** $H$ is open, closed and nonempty in the connected space $G$, hence $H=G$.

**2. Application.** $GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$ is a topological group; it is connected (polar decomposition: $GL_n^+\cong SO(n)\times\mathrm{Sym}_n$). By part 1 it is generated by any neighbourhood of $I$ in $GL_n^+$. The set $\exp(M_n)$ is contained in $GL_n^+$ (since $\det e^A=e^{\operatorname{tr}A}>0$), and by the logarithm near the identity it contains a ball $B(I,\frac12)$. So $\exp(M_n)$ contains a neighbourhood of $I$ in $GL_n^+$ and therefore generates $GL_n^+$.

**Concretely:** every matrix of positive determinant is a **finite product of exponentials**: $A=e^{X_1}e^{X_2}\cdots e^{X_m}$ (one needs only $m=2$: polar decomposition gives $A=e^{K}e^{S}$ with $K$ skew, $S$ symmetric).

**Remark.** The statement is false without connectedness: in $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ the exponentials generate only $GL_n^+$, never a matrix of negative determinant.
@@@ mnexp-which-matrices-of-sl-2-are-exponentials.md
---
title: Which matrices of SLâ‚‚(â„) are exponentials?
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: SL_2, trace, Cayleyâ€“Hamilton, exponential image
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\mathfrak{sl}_2(\mathbb{R})$ be the space of real traceless $2\times2$ matrices. Show that
$$\exp\bigl(\mathfrak{sl}_2(\mathbb{R})\bigr)=\{g\in SL_2(\mathbb{R}):\operatorname{tr}g>-2\}\ \cup\ \{-I\}.$$
In particular $\exp:\mathfrak{sl}_2(\mathbb{R})\to SL_2(\mathbb{R})$ is not surjective.

## Hint

For traceless $X$, Cayleyâ€“Hamilton gives $X^2=-\det X\cdot I$. Separate the cases $\det X>0$, $=0$, $<0$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The trace of $e^X$ is $2\cos\omega$ or $2$ or $2\cosh\omega$, according to the sign of $\det X$.

**Cayleyâ€“Hamilton.** For $X$ with $\operatorname{tr}X=0$: $X^2-(\operatorname{tr}X)X+(\det X)I=0$, so $X^2=-(\det X)I$.

**Inclusion $\subset$.** Let $X\in\mathfrak{sl}_2$.
- *$\det X=\omega^2>0$.* Then $X^2=-\omega^2I$, so $X^{2k}=(-1)^k\omega^{2k}I$ and $X^{2k+1}=(-1)^k\omega^{2k}X$. Summing the series,
$$e^X=\cos\omega\,I+\frac{\sin\omega}\omega\,X,\qquad\operatorname{tr}e^X=2\cos\omega\in[-2,2].$$
The value $-2$ occurs iff $\cos\omega=-1$, which forces $\sin\omega=0$ and then $e^X=-I$. Otherwise $\operatorname{tr}e^X>-2$.
- *$\det X=0$.* Then $X^2=0$ and $e^X=I+X$, with trace $2>-2$.
- *$\det X=-\omega^2<0$.* Then $X^2=\omega^2I$ and $e^X=\cosh\omega\,I+\frac{\sinh\omega}\omega X$, with trace $2\cosh\omega>2$.

Also $\det e^X=e^{\operatorname{tr}X}=1$, so $e^X\in SL_2$. Thus every exponential is in the right-hand set.

**Inclusion $\supset$.** Let $g\in SL_2(\mathbb{R})$ with $\operatorname{tr}g>-2$; its characteristic polynomial is $\lambda^2-(\operatorname{tr}g)\lambda+1$.
- *$|\operatorname{tr}g|<2$:* eigenvalues $e^{\pm i\theta}$ with $\theta\in(0,\pi)$ (non-real, conjugate). Then $g=PR_{\pm\theta}P^{-1}$ for some $P\in GL_2(\mathbb{R})$ (real canonical form), and $g=\exp(\pm\theta PJP^{-1})$ with $\theta PJP^{-1}$ traceless.
- *$\operatorname{tr}g>2$:* distinct positive eigenvalues $\lambda,\lambda^{-1}$. Then $g=P\,\mathrm{diag}(\lambda,\lambda^{-1})P^{-1}=\exp\bigl(P\,\mathrm{diag}(\ln\lambda,-\ln\lambda)P^{-1}\bigr)$.
- *$\operatorname{tr}g=2$:* the only eigenvalue is $1$ (double). Either $g=I=e^0$, or $g=P(I+N)P^{-1}$ with $N=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ (Jordan form, with the scaling absorbed into $P$), and $g=\exp(PNP^{-1})$ with $PNP^{-1}$ traceless.
- *$g=-I$:* $-I=e^{\pi J}$ with $\operatorname{tr}J=0$.

**Not onto.** The matrix $\begin{pmatrix}-1&1\\0&-1\end{pmatrix}\in SL_2(\mathbb{R})$ has trace $-2$ and is not $-I$, so it is not an exponential. Likewise $\mathrm{diag}(-2,-\frac12)$ (trace $-\frac52<-2$).

**Remark.** The failure of surjectivity reflects the topology: $SL_2(\mathbb{R})\simeq SO(2)\simeq S^1$ is not simply connected, and the image of $\exp$ misses exactly the elements with negative eigenvalues other than $-I$ (the hyperbolic ones with trace $<-2$ and the non-trivial parabolic ones with trace $-2$). Compare with the problem on a matrix of positive determinant that is not an exponential.
@@@ mnexp-compact-subgroups-of-sl-n-are-conjugate-into-so-n.md
---
title: Compact subgroups of SL_n are conjugate into SO(n)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: compact group, Haar measure, invariant inner product, conjugation
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that every compact subgroup $K$ of $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is conjugate (in $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$) to a subgroup of $SO(n)$.

## Hint

Average the standard inner product over $K$ with respect to Haar measure to obtain a $K$-invariant inner product, then change basis.

## Solution

**Idea.** A compact group preserves some inner product. In an orthonormal basis for that inner product, $K$ acts by orthogonal matrices.

**Step 1: an invariant inner product.** $K$ is a compact topological group, so it carries a left-invariant probability measure $\mu$ (Haar measure). For $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^n$ define
$$\langle x,y\rangle'=\int_K\langle gx,gy\rangle\,d\mu(g),$$
where $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle$ is the standard inner product. The integrand is continuous in $g$ (compact domain, so integrable). $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle'$ is bilinear, symmetric, and positive definite: $\langle x,x\rangle'=\int\|gx\|^2d\mu>0$ for $x\ne0$ since $\|gx\|>0$.

**Step 2: it is $K$-invariant.** For $h\in K$,
