$$\langle hx,hy\rangle'=\int_K\langle ghx,ghy\rangle\,d\mu(g)=\int_K\langle g'x,g'y\rangle\,d\mu(g')=\langle x,y\rangle'$$
by right invariance of the Haar measure on a compact group (compact groups are unimodular, so left and right invariant measures coincide).

**Step 3: change of basis.** There is a symmetric positive definite matrix $S$ with $\langle x,y\rangle'=\langle Sx,y\rangle$. Let $P=S^{1/2}\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$, so $\langle x,y\rangle'=\langle Px,Py\rangle$. For $h\in K$ the invariance reads $\langle Phx,Phy\rangle=\langle Px,Py\rangle$ for all $x,y$. Putting $x'=Px$, $y'=Py$ gives
$$\langle PhP^{-1}x',PhP^{-1}y'\rangle=\langle x',y'\rangle\quad\text{for all }x',y',$$
so $PhP^{-1}\in O(n)$.

**Step 4: determinant.** $\det(PhP^{-1})=\det h=1$ since $K\subset SL_n$. So $PKP^{-1}\subset SO(n)$.

**Remark.** The same proof shows that any compact subgroup of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is conjugate into $O(n)$. It gives a "maximal compact subgroup" theorem: $SO(n)$ is a maximal compact subgroup of $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$, unique up to conjugation (Cartanâ€“Iwasawaâ€“Malcev).
@@@ mnexp-so-3-is-the-real-projective-3-space.md
---
title: SO(3) is homeomorphic to â„PÂ³
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: SO(3), quaternions, real projective space, double cover
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Identify $\mathbb{R}^3$ with the purely imaginary quaternions $\mathrm{Im}\,\mathbb{H}$. For a unit quaternion $q\in S^3$ define $\Phi(q):\mathrm{Im}\,\mathbb{H}\to\mathrm{Im}\,\mathbb{H}$ by $\Phi(q)(x)=qx\bar q$. Show that $\Phi:S^3\to SO(3)$ is a continuous, surjective group homomorphism with kernel $\{\pm1\}$, and deduce that $SO(3)\cong S^3/\{\pm1\}=\mathbb{RP}^3$.

## Hint

Show that conjugation preserves the norm and imaginary quaternions. For surjectivity, compute $\Phi$ on $q=\cos\frac\theta2+u\sin\frac\theta2$ with $u$ a unit imaginary quaternion: it is the rotation by $\theta$ about $u$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Unit quaternions double-cover the rotation group.

**Well defined.** For $x\in\mathrm{Im}\,\mathbb{H}$ we have $\bar x=-x$. Then $\overline{qx\bar q}=q\bar x\bar q=-qx\bar q$, so $qx\bar q$ is again purely imaginary. The map $x\mapsto qx\bar q$ is $\mathbb{R}$-linear. The norm is multiplicative, $|qx\bar q|=|q|\,|x|\,|\bar q|=|x|$ for $|q|=1$, so $\Phi(q)\in O(3)$.

**Homomorphism.** $\Phi(q_1q_2)(x)=q_1q_2x\bar q_2\bar q_1=\Phi(q_1)\bigl(\Phi(q_2)(x)\bigr)$ since $\overline{q_1q_2}=\bar q_2\bar q_1$.

**Determinant $1$.** $q\mapsto\det\Phi(q)\in\{\pm1\}$ is continuous on the connected space $S^3$ and equals $1$ at $q=1$ ($\Phi(1)=\mathrm{id}$). So $\Phi(q)\in SO(3)$. Also $\Phi$ is continuous (polynomial in the coordinates of $q$).

**Kernel.** $\Phi(q)=\mathrm{id}$ iff $qx=xq$ for all imaginary $x$, iff $q$ is real (it commutes with $i,j,k$). Unit real quaternions: $q=\pm1$.

**Surjectivity.** Let $u$ be a unit imaginary quaternion, $\theta\in\mathbb{R}$ and $q=\cos\frac\theta2+u\sin\frac\theta2$ (so $\bar q=\cos\frac\theta2-u\sin\frac\theta2$ and $|q|=1$). Then:
- $q$ commutes with $u$, so $\Phi(q)u=qu\bar q=u$: $\Phi(q)$ fixes the axis $u$.
- Let $v$ be a unit imaginary quaternion orthogonal to $u$. Then $uv=-vu$ (orthogonal imaginary quaternions anticommute), hence $v\bar q=v\cos\frac\theta2-vu\sin\frac\theta2=qv$. Therefore $qv\bar q=q\cdot qv=q^2v=(\cos\theta+u\sin\theta)v=\cos\theta\,v+\sin\theta\,uv$.
Since $uv$ is a unit imaginary quaternion orthogonal to both $u$ and $v$, $\Phi(q)$ acts on the plane $u^\perp$ (basis $v,uv$) as the rotation by angle $\theta$. So $\Phi(q)$ is the rotation by $\theta$ about the axis $u$.
Every element of $SO(3)$ is a rotation about some axis: its eigenvalues are $e^{\pm i\alpha}$ and a real $\varepsilon$ of modulus $1$, with product $\varepsilon=1$ since $\det=1$, so $1$ is an eigenvalue, and in an orthonormal basis starting with a unit eigenvector it is $1\oplus R_\alpha$. So $\Phi$ is onto.

**Homeomorphism.** $\Phi$ induces a continuous bijection $\bar\Phi:S^3/\{\pm1\}\to SO(3)$. The source is compact, the target is Hausdorff, so $\bar\Phi$ is a homeomorphism. And $S^3/\{\pm1\}$ is $\mathbb{RP}^3$ (antipodal identification).

**Check.** $\Phi(i)$ is the rotation by $\pi$ about the $i$-axis: $i\,i\,(-i)=i$, $i\,j\,(-i)=-j$, $i\,k\,(-i)=-k$ âœ“.

**Remark.** This is the origin of the use of quaternions for 3D rotations in computer graphics: two unit quaternions $q$ and $-q$ give the same rotation.
@@@ mnexp-the-fundamental-group-of-so-3.md
---
title: The fundamental group of SO(3)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: fundamental group, covering space, SO(3), deck transformations
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that $\pi_1(SO(3))\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}$.

## Hint

The map $\Phi:S^3\to SO(3)$ of the previous problem is a $2$-fold covering, and $S^3$ is simply connected.

## Solution

**Idea.** A connected, simply connected cover of $X$ with deck group $G$ has $\pi_1(X)\cong G$.

**$\Phi$ is a covering map.** $\Phi$ identifies $SO(3)$ with the quotient $S^3/\{\pm1\}$, where $\{\pm1\}$ acts by $q\mapsto-q$. This action is free (no fixed points: $-q\ne q$) and properly discontinuous, so the quotient map $S^3\to S^3/\{\pm1\}$ is a covering map of degree $2$. Via the homeomorphism $\bar\Phi$ of the previous problem, $\Phi:S^3\to SO(3)$ is a $2$-sheeted covering.

**$S^3$ is simply connected.** The sphere $S^m$ is simply connected for $m\ge2$ (e.g. by the Seifertâ€“van Kampen theorem, covering $S^3$ by two open sets homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^3$ with connected intersection $\simeq S^2$). So $\Phi$ is the **universal cover** of $SO(3)$.

**Deck group.** The deck transformations of the covering $S^3\to S^3/\{\pm1\}$ are $\mathrm{id}$ and $q\mapsto-q$, a group of order $2$. For the universal cover of a path-connected, locally path-connected space $X$, the deck group is isomorphic to $\pi_1(X)$. Hence
$$\pi_1(SO(3))\cong\{\pm1\}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}.$$

**Alternative viewpoint (lifting).** Take a loop $\gamma$ in $SO(3)$ based at $I$ and lift it to $\tilde\gamma$ in $S^3$ starting at $1$. The endpoint is $1$ or $-1$; the class of $\gamma$ is determined by this endpoint. Loops with endpoint $-1$ are the non-trivial class.

**Remark.** Consequence: the group $SU(2)\cong S^3$, the *spin group* $\mathrm{Spin}(3)$, is the universal cover of $SO(3)$. This is why electrons (spinors) change sign under a $2\pi$ rotation but not under a $4\pi$ rotation (next problem).
@@@ mnexp-the-dirac-belt-trick.md
---
title: The Dirac belt trick: a 2Ï€ rotation is non-trivial, a 4Ï€ rotation is trivial
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-exp-log
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: homotopy, lifting, SO(3), quaternions, Dirac belt
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\gamma(\theta)=R_\theta\oplus1\in SO(3)$ for $\theta\in[0,2\pi]$ (rotation of angle $\theta$ about the third axis), a loop based at $I$. Show that $\gamma$ is not null-homotopic but that the loop $\gamma^2$ (going around twice, $\theta\in[0,4\pi]$) is null-homotopic.

## Hint

Lift the loops to $S^3$ through the double cover $\Phi:S^3\to SO(3)$: the lift of $\gamma$ is $q(\theta)=\cos\frac\theta2+k\sin\frac\theta2$. Use that homotopic loops have lifts with the same endpoint.

## Solution

**Idea.** Whether a loop is trivial is decided by whether its lift to the universal cover $S^3$ is closed.

**Lifting $\gamma$.** By the previous computation, $\Phi\bigl(\cos\frac\theta2+k\sin\frac\theta2\bigr)$ is the rotation by $\theta$ about the third axis ($u=k$), that is $R_\theta\oplus1=\gamma(\theta)$. So
$$\tilde\gamma(\theta)=\cos\frac\theta2+k\sin\frac\theta2,\qquad\theta\in[0,2\pi],$$
is the lift of $\gamma$ starting at $\tilde\gamma(0)=1$. It ends at $\tilde\gamma(2\pi)=\cos\pi+k\sin\pi=-1\ne1$: the lift is not closed.

**$\gamma$ is not null-homotopic.** Suppose $H:[0,1]\times[0,2\pi]\to SO(3)$ were a homotopy of loops based at $I$ from $\gamma$ to the constant loop. By the homotopy lifting property of coverings, $H$ lifts to $\tilde H$ with $\tilde H(0,\cdot)=\tilde\gamma$ and $\tilde H(s,0)=1$ for all $s$ (the path $s\mapsto H(s,0)=I$ lifts to the constant path $1$), and the endpoint $\tilde H(s,2\pi)$ lies in the discrete fibre $\{\pm1\}$ above $I$ and varies continuously in $s$, so it is constant. But for the constant loop the lift is constant $1$ with endpoint $1$, whereas the endpoint of $\tilde\gamma$ is $-1$. Contradiction. So $\gamma$ is not null-homotopic.

**$\gamma^2$ is null-homotopic.** The loop $\gamma^2(\theta)=R_\theta\oplus1$, $\theta\in[0,4\pi]$, lifts to $\tilde\gamma^{(2)}(\theta)=\cos\frac\theta2+k\sin\frac\theta2$, $\theta\in[0,4\pi]$, which ends at $\cos2\pi+k\sin2\pi=1$: a **closed** loop in $S^3$. Since $S^3$ is simply connected, it contracts to the constant loop within $S^3$ via a homotopy $\tilde H$; composing with $\Phi$ gives a homotopy of $\gamma^2$ to the constant loop in $SO(3)$ (the base point stays fixed).

**Physical interpretation (Dirac's belt).** Rotating a body (with a belt attached to a fixed wall) by $2\pi$ twists the belt in a way that cannot be undone without rotating the body back, whereas after a rotation by $4\pi$ the belt can be untangled by moving it around the body, without rotating the body. This models spin-$\frac12$ particles: their wave function changes sign under a $2\pi$ rotation.

**Remark.** Equivalently, $[\gamma]$ is the generator of $\pi_1(SO(3))\cong\mathbb{Z}/2$, and $[\gamma]^2=[\gamma^2]=1$.

@@@ mnden-the-complement-of-a-polynomial-zero-set-is-open-and-dense.md
---
title: The complement of a polynomial zero set is open and dense
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: polynomials, density, Taylor expansion
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $p\ne0$ be a polynomial on $\mathbb{R}^k$. Show that $\{p\ne0\}$ is open and dense in $\mathbb{R}^k$.

## Hint

If $p$ vanishes on a whole ball, what can you say about all its partial derivatives at the centre of that ball?

## Solution

**Idea.** Openness is continuity. Density means that the zero set $Z=\{p=0\}$ contains no ball, and a polynomial that vanishes on a ball is the zero polynomial.

**Open.** $p$ is continuous, so $\{p\ne0\}=p^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})$ is open.

**Dense.** A set is dense iff its complement has empty interior. Suppose, for contradiction, that $p=0$ on a ball $B(a,r)$.
- On this ball $p$ is the constant function $0$, so every partial derivative $\partial^\alpha p$ vanishes on $B(a,r)$, in particular at $a$.
- A polynomial equals its Taylor expansion at $a$, which is a *finite* sum $p(x)=\sum_\alpha\frac{\partial^\alpha p(a)}{\alpha!}(x-a)^\alpha$. All coefficients are $0$, so $p\equiv0$, contradicting $p\ne0$.

Hence $Z$ has empty interior, and $\{p\ne0\}=\mathbb{R}^k\setminus Z$ is dense.

**Remark.** This is the basic tool of the whole section. In $M_n(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$, "polynomial in the entries" covers $\det$, the coefficients of the characteristic polynomial, minors, discriminants and so on. **Lesson.** To prove a property holds on an open dense set, find a nonzero polynomial whose non-vanishing implies it.
@@@ mnden-three-open-dense-subsets-of-the-matrices.md
---
title: Three open dense subsets of M_n(â„)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: density, discriminant, principal minors, polynomials
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that each of the following subsets of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is open and dense:
1. the invertible matrices,
2. the matrices with $n$ distinct complex eigenvalues,
3. the matrices all of whose principal minors are nonzero.

## Hint

Each set is of the form $\{p\ne0\}$ for a polynomial $p$ in the entries. By the previous result you only need to check that $p$ is not the zero polynomial: find one matrix where $p\ne0$.

## Solution

**Method.** If $p(A)$ is a polynomial in the $n^2$ entries of $A$ and $p(A_0)\ne0$ for one $A_0$, then $p\not\equiv0$ and $\{p\ne0\}$ is open and dense.

**1. Invertible matrices.** $p=\det$ is a polynomial in the entries and $\det I=1\ne0$. The set is $\{\det\ne0\}$.

**2. $n$ distinct eigenvalues.** The matrix has $n$ distinct complex eigenvalues iff the characteristic polynomial $\chi_A$ has $n$ distinct roots iff its discriminant $\operatorname{Disc}(\chi_A)\ne0$. The discriminant is a polynomial in the coefficients of $\chi_A$ (it is $\pm$ the resultant of $\chi_A$ and $\chi_A'$ divided by the leading coefficient $1$), and these coefficients are polynomials in the entries of $A$. So $p(A)=\operatorname{Disc}(\chi_A)$ is a polynomial in the entries. At $A_0=\operatorname{diag}(1,2,\dots,n)$ the eigenvalues are distinct, so $p(A_0)\ne0$.

**3. All principal minors nonzero.** A principal minor is the determinant of the submatrix obtained by keeping rows and columns indexed by a nonempty subset $S\subset\{1,\dots,n\}$. The product $p(A)$ of the $2^n-1$ principal minors is a polynomial in the entries, and $\{p\ne0\}$ is exactly the set where all of them are nonzero. For $A_0=I$, every principal minor equals $1$, so $p(I)=1\ne0$.

**Conclusion.** In each case the set is $\{p\ne0\}$ for a nonzero polynomial, hence open and dense.

**Remark.** "Generic" matrices are therefore invertible, diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$, and have nonzero principal minors: any claim that is closed and true on such matrices extends to all matrices by continuity. **Lesson.** Memorise the three standard dense open sets.
@@@ mnden-matrices-with-an-lu-factorisation.md
---
title: Matrices with an LU factorisation
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: LU decomposition, leading principal minors, density
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that the set of matrices $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ admitting a factorisation $A=LU$ with $L$ unit lower triangular and $U$ upper triangular contains an open dense subset of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Show that if all the leading principal minors of $A$ are nonzero, then $A$ has an LU factorisation (induction on $n$, adding a last row and column).

## Solution

**The open dense set.** Let $\Delta_k(A)$ be the $k\times k$ leading principal minor (top-left $k\times k$ block). The product $p=\Delta_1\cdots\Delta_n$ is a polynomial with $p(I)=1\ne0$, so $\Omega=\{p\ne0\}$ is open and dense. We show every $A\in\Omega$ has an LU factorisation.

**Induction on $n$.** For $n=1$, $A=(a)=(1)\cdot(a)$. Assume the claim for $n-1$ and write
$$A=\begin{pmatrix}A'&b\\c^T&d\end{pmatrix},$$
with $A'$ of size $n-1$ and all leading minors of $A'$ nonzero (they are $\Delta_1,\dots,\Delta_{n-1}$ of $A$). By induction $A'=L'U'$ with $L'$ unit lower triangular. Since $\det A'=\det U'\ne0$, $U'$ is invertible, and $L'$ is invertible too.

**Constructing the factors.** Look for
$$L=\begin{pmatrix}L'&0\\v^T&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad U=\begin{pmatrix}U'&u\\0&\delta\end{pmatrix}.$$
Then $LU=\begin{pmatrix}L'U'&L'u\\v^TU'&v^Tu+\delta\end{pmatrix}$. To match $A$ we need
$$L'u=b\ (\Rightarrow u=L'^{-1}b),\qquad v^TU'=c^T\ (\Rightarrow v^T=c^TU'^{-1}),\qquad\delta=d-v^Tu.$$
All three are solvable, so $A=LU$ (and $\delta=\det A/\det A'\ne0$).

**Remark.** The condition is not necessary: $\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ has an LU factorisation with $L=I$. It fails for some invertible matrices, like $\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ (if it were $LU$, $L=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\\ell&1\end{pmatrix}$ and $U=\begin{pmatrix}u_{11}&u_{12}\\0&u_{22}\end{pmatrix}$ would give $u_{11}=0$ and then $\ell u_{11}=1$, impossible). This is why pivoting is needed in practice. **Lesson.** Genericity statements often come from "no pivot vanishes".
@@@ mnden-cayley-hamilton-by-density.md
---
title: The Cayleyâ€“Hamilton theorem by density
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: Cayleyâ€“Hamilton, density, diagonalisation
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Prove that $\chi_A(A)=0$ for every $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ by a density argument.

## Hint

First check the identity for matrices with $n$ distinct eigenvalues, which are diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$. Then use continuity.

## Solution

**Step 1: matrices with distinct eigenvalues.** Suppose $A$ has $n$ distinct eigenvalues $\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n\in\mathbb{C}$. Then $A=PDP^{-1}$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_i)$ and $P\in GL_n(\mathbb{C})$. For every polynomial $q$, $q(A)=Pq(D)P^{-1}$. Taking $q=\chi_A$,
$$\chi_A(A)=P\operatorname{diag}\bigl(\chi_A(\lambda_1),\dots,\chi_A(\lambda_n)\bigr)P^{-1}=0,$$
since each $\lambda_i$ is a root of $\chi_A$.

**Step 2: continuity.** The map $\Phi:A\mapsto\chi_A(A)$ from $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ to $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is continuous: if $\chi_A(X)=X^n+c_{n-1}(A)X^{n-1}+\dots+c_0(A)$, each coefficient $c_j(A)$ is a polynomial in the entries of $A$, and $A\mapsto A^j$ is polynomial. So $\Phi$ is polynomial in the entries.

**Step 3: density.** The set $\Omega$ of matrices with $n$ distinct complex eigenvalues is dense (it is $\{\operatorname{Disc}\chi_A\ne0\}$, a nonempty polynomial open set). The continuous map $\Phi$ vanishes on the dense set $\Omega$, so $\Phi\equiv0$: $\chi_A(A)=0$ for all $A$.

**Remark.** The same argument proves the theorem over $\mathbb{C}$. Note that Step 1 genuinely needs complex diagonalisability, even for real $A$. **Lesson.** For polynomial identities in matrix entries, it suffices to check them on diagonalisable matrices.
@@@ mnden-ab-and-ba-have-the-same-characteristic-polynomial.md
---
title: AB and BA have the same characteristic polynomial
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: characteristic polynomial, density, similarity
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that $\chi_{AB}=\chi_{BA}$ for all $A,B\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

If $A$ is invertible, $AB$ and $BA$ are similar.

## Solution

**Invertible $A$.** If $A\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$, then
$$A(BA)A^{-1}=ABAA^{-1}=AB,$$
so $AB$ and $BA$ are similar, and similar matrices have the same characteristic polynomial: $\chi_{AB}=\chi_{BA}$.

**General $A$.** Fix $B$ and consider $F(A)=\chi_{AB}-\chi_{BA}$, whose coefficients are polynomials in the entries of $A$ (the coefficients of $\chi_M$ are polynomials in the entries of $M$, and $AB$, $BA$ are linear in $A$). So $F$ is continuous. It vanishes on $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$, which is dense in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ ($\det$ is a nonzero polynomial). Hence $F\equiv0$.

**Check.** For $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ and $B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$: $AB=B$ and $BA=0$, which are *not* similar (different ranks), yet $\chi_{AB}=\chi_{BA}=X^2$.

**Remark.** Consequently $\operatorname{tr}(AB)=\operatorname{tr}(BA)$ and $AB$, $BA$ have the same nonzero eigenvalues with the same algebraic multiplicities. For non-square $A\in M_{n,p}$, $B\in M_{p,n}$ one gets $X^p\chi_{AB}=X^n\chi_{BA}$ in the same way (pad with zeros). **Lesson.** When invertibility makes a problem trivial, density gives the rest.
@@@ mnden-adjugate-identities-by-density.md
---
title: Adjugate identities by density
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: adjugate, determinant, density
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

For $A,B\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$, show that
$$\operatorname{adj}(AB)=\operatorname{adj}(B)\operatorname{adj}(A),\qquad\det\operatorname{adj}(A)=(\det A)^{n-1}.$$

## Hint

For invertible $A$, $\operatorname{adj}A=\det(A)\,A^{-1}$.

## Solution

**Reminder.** $\operatorname{adj}A$ is the transpose of the cofactor matrix; its entries are polynomials (of degree $n-1$) in the entries of $A$, and $A\operatorname{adj}A=\operatorname{adj}A\cdot A=(\det A)I$. On $GL_n$ this gives $\operatorname{adj}A=\det(A)A^{-1}$.

**First identity on $GL_n\times GL_n$.** For invertible $A,B$,
$$\operatorname{adj}(AB)=\det(AB)(AB)^{-1}=\det A\det B\,B^{-1}A^{-1}=\bigl(\det B\,B^{-1}\bigr)\bigl(\det A\,A^{-1}\bigr)=\operatorname{adj}B\operatorname{adj}A.$$

**Second identity on $GL_n$.** The matrix $\operatorname{adj}A=\det A\cdot A^{-1}$ is a scalar multiple $c=\det A$ of $A^{-1}$, and $\det(cM)=c^n\det M$, so
$$\det\operatorname{adj}A=(\det A)^n\det(A^{-1})=(\det A)^n(\det A)^{-1}=(\det A)^{n-1}.$$

**Extension.** Each side of each identity is a polynomial in the entries of $A$ (and $B$). A polynomial identity valid on the dense set $GL_n\times GL_n$ (dense in $M_n\times M_n$) holds everywhere, by continuity.

**Check.** $n=2$: $\operatorname{adj}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}d&-b\\-c&a\end{pmatrix}$ and $\det\operatorname{adj}A=ad-bc=(\det A)^1$ âœ“.

**Remark.** For $A$ of rank $n-1$, $\operatorname{adj}A$ has rank $1$, and for rank $\le n-2$ it is $0$; the identity $\det\operatorname{adj}A=(\det A)^{n-1}=0$ is consistent when $n\ge2$. **Lesson.** Identities with $A^{-1}$ become identities with $\operatorname{adj}A$ after multiplying by $\det A$, which makes them polynomial.
@@@ mnden-nonderogatory-matrices-form-an-open-dense-set.md
---
title: Nonderogatory matrices form an open dense set
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: minimal polynomial, nonderogatory, density
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that the set $\mathcal{C}$ of matrices $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ for which $I,A,\dots,A^{n-1}$ are linearly independent (equivalently, the minimal polynomial equals the characteristic polynomial) is open and dense.

## Hint

Vectorise the powers: put $I,A,\dots,A^{n-1}$ as columns of an $n^2\times n$ matrix. Linear independence means it has rank $n$, i.e. some $n\times n$ minor is nonzero.

## Solution

**Open.** Let $V(A)=\bigl[\operatorname{vec}I\,|\,\operatorname{vec}A\,|\dots|\operatorname{vec}A^{n-1}\bigr]\in M_{n^2,n}(\mathbb{R})$. The columns are independent iff $\operatorname{rank}V(A)=n$ iff at least one of the $n\times n$ minors $m_S(A)$ of $V(A)$ (one for each choice $S$ of $n$ rows) is nonzero. Each $m_S$ is a polynomial in the entries of $A$, hence continuous. So
$$\mathcal{C}=\bigcup_S\{m_S\ne0\}$$
is a union of open sets.

**Dense.** If $A$ has $n$ distinct eigenvalues $\lambda_i$ then $I,A,\dots,A^{n-1}$ are independent: a relation $\sum_jc_jA^j=0$ means the polynomial $q=\sum c_jX^j$, of degree $<n$, satisfies $q(A)=0$; in an eigenbasis this gives $q(\lambda_i)=0$ for all $i$, i.e. $n$ roots for a polynomial of degree $<n$, so $q=0$. The set of matrices with distinct eigenvalues is dense, hence so is $\mathcal{C}$.

**Equivalence with the minimal polynomial.** Independence of $I,\dots,A^{n-1}$ means no nonzero polynomial of degree $<n$ annihilates $A$, i.e. $\deg\mu_A\ge n$; since $\mu_A\mid\chi_A$ and $\deg\chi_A=n$, this means $\mu_A=\chi_A$.

**Remark.** Matrices in $\mathcal{C}$ are called nonderogatory (or cyclic): they have a cyclic vector. The complement, e.g. the scalar matrices, is a closed subset with empty interior. **Lesson.** "Some minor $\ne0$" is a typical way to see openness of a rank condition.
@@@ mnden-the-hypersurface-det-of-a-minus-i-is-zero.md
---
title: The closed set det(A âˆ’ I) = 0 has empty interior
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: hypersurface, determinant, closed set
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that $Z=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\det(A-I)=0\}$ (matrices having $1$ as an eigenvalue) is closed with empty interior.

## Hint

It is the zero set of a polynomial. Evaluate this polynomial at one matrix.

## Solution

**Closed.** $p(A)=\det(A-I)$ is a polynomial in the entries, hence continuous, and $Z=p^{-1}(\{0\})$ is closed.

**Empty interior.** $p$ is not the zero polynomial: $p(0)=\det(-I)=(-1)^n\ne0$. By the first problem of this section, $\{p=0\}$ has empty interior.

**Direct argument.** If $A\in Z$ then $A+\varepsilon I$ for small $\varepsilon\neq0$ is not in $Z$: $1$ is an eigenvalue of $A$, so the eigenvalues of $A+\varepsilon I$ are those of $A$ shifted by $\varepsilon$, and $1$ is an eigenvalue of $A+\varepsilon I$ iff $1-\varepsilon$ is an eigenvalue of $A$. Since $A$ has finitely many eigenvalues, this fails for all small $\varepsilon\ne0$.

**Remark.** Same argument for any $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$: $\{\det(A-\lambda I)=0\}$ is a closed hypersurface with empty interior. **Lesson.** Zero sets of nonzero polynomials are "thin".
@@@ mnden-matrices-with-an-eigenvalue-of-modulus-one.md
---
title: Matrices with an eigenvalue of modulus one
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: spectrum, closed sets, empty interior, scaling
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $Z=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):A\text{ has a complex eigenvalue of modulus }1\}$. Show that $Z$ is closed and has empty interior.

## Hint

For closedness, pass to the limit in $\det(A_k-\lambda_kI)=0$ using compactness of the unit circle. For the interior, scale: look at $tA$ for $t$ near $1$.

## Solution

**Closed.** Let $A_k\in Z$, $A_k\to A$. Choose eigenvalues $\lambda_k\in\mathbb{C}$ with $|\lambda_k|=1$ and $\det(A_k-\lambda_kI)=0$. The unit circle is compact, so a subsequence $\lambda_{k_j}\to\lambda$ with $|\lambda|=1$. The map $(M,\mu)\mapsto\det(M-\mu I)$ is continuous (polynomial in the entries of $M$ and in $\mu$), so
$$\det(A-\lambda I)=\lim_j\det(A_{k_j}-\lambda_{k_j}I)=0.$$
So $\lambda$ is an eigenvalue of $A$ of modulus $1$: $A\in Z$.

**Empty interior.** Take any $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$; we show every neighbourhood of $A$ contains matrices outside $Z$.
- The eigenvalues of $tA$ are $t\lambda$, where $\lambda$ ranges over the eigenvalues of $A$. So $tA\in Z$ iff $|t||\lambda|=1$ for some eigenvalue $\lambda$ of $A$, i.e. $|t|=1/|\lambda|$ with $\lambda\ne0$.
- $A$ has at most $n$ eigenvalues, so only finitely many $t>0$ satisfy this.
- Hence there are $t>0$ arbitrarily close to $1$ with $tA\notin Z$, and $\|tA-A\|=|t-1|\|A\|\to0$.

So $A$ is not an interior point of $Z$.

**Remark.** The complement of $Z$ splits into the open sets of matrices with all eigenvalues in the open unit disc ("Schur stable") and with some eigenvalue outside. That is relevant for dynamical systems $x_{k+1}=Ax_k$. **Lesson.** Scaling $A\mapsto tA$ is a one-parameter family that quickly shows a set is thin.
@@@ mnden-the-diagonalisable-matrices-are-not-dense.md
---
title: Real-diagonalisable matrices are not dense
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 2
tags: diagonalisation, non-real eigenvalues, continuity of roots
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that the set of $\mathbb{R}$-diagonalisable matrices is not dense in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ for $n\ge2$.

## Hint

An $\mathbb{R}$-diagonalisable matrix has only real eigenvalues. Show that having a non-real eigenvalue is an *open* property.

## Solution

**Idea.** The set $\mathcal{N}$ of matrices having at least one non-real eigenvalue is open and nonempty, and it is disjoint from the $\mathbb{R}$-diagonalisable matrices (their eigenvalues are real). A set that misses a nonempty open set is not dense.

**Nonempty.** For $n=2$, the rotation $R=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ has eigenvalues $\pm i$. For $n\ge2$, the block matrix $R\oplus0_{n-2}$ has a non-real eigenvalue.

**Openness (continuity of eigenvalues).** Let $A\in\mathcal{N}$ with eigenvalue $\mu$, $\operatorname{Im}\mu=\delta\ne0$. For a matrix $A'$ with eigenvalues $\lambda_1',\dots,\lambda_n'$,
$$\prod_{i=1}^n|\mu-\lambda_i'|=|\chi_{A'}(\mu)|,\qquad\text{so}\qquad\min_i|\mu-\lambda_i'|\le|\chi_{A'}(\mu)|^{1/n}.$$
As $A'\to A$, $\chi_{A'}(\mu)\to\chi_A(\mu)=0$ (the coefficients of $\chi_{A'}$ are continuous in $A'$). So for $A'$ close enough to $A$ there is an eigenvalue $\lambda'$ of $A'$ with $|\lambda'-\mu|<\delta/2$, hence $\operatorname{Im}\lambda'\ne0$: $A'\in\mathcal{N}$.

**Conclusion.** $\mathcal{N}$ is a nonempty open set containing no diagonalisable matrix, so the real-diagonalisable matrices are not dense.

**Remark.** In contrast, the matrices diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$ are dense (distinct eigenvalues). The discrepancy is that $\mathbb{R}$ is not algebraically closed. **Lesson.** The inequality $\min_i|\mu-\lambda_i'|\le|\chi_{A'}(\mu)|^{1/n}$ is a cheap way to prove continuity of the spectrum.
@@@ mnden-the-orbit-of-a-jordan-block-accumulates-on-the-identity.md
---
title: The orbit of a Jordan block accumulates on the identity
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: conjugacy orbit, closure, Jordan block
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $J=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ and let $\mathcal{O}(J)=\{PJP^{-1}:P\in GL_2(\mathbb{R})\}$. Show that $I$ lies in the closure of $\mathcal{O}(J)$ but $I\notin\mathcal{O}(J)$.

## Hint

Conjugate $J$ by $\operatorname{diag}(t,1)$.

## Solution

**$I\in\overline{\mathcal{O}(J)}$.** For $t>0$, let $D_t=\operatorname{diag}(t,1)$. Then
$$D_tJD_t^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t^{-1}&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}t&t\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t^{-1}&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&t\\0&1\end{pmatrix}.$$
As $t\to0$ this tends to $I$, and each $D_tJD_t^{-1}$ belongs to $\mathcal{O}(J)$.

**$I\notin\mathcal{O}(J)$.** For every $P$, $PIP^{-1}=I$, so the orbit of $I$ is $\{I\}$. If $I=PJP^{-1}$ then $J=P^{-1}IP=I$, which is false.

**Consequence.** The orbit $\mathcal{O}(J)$ is not closed. In terms of group actions: the quotient $M_n/\!\!\sim$ by conjugation is not Hausdorff. Any continuous function constant on conjugacy classes (such as the trace or the determinant) takes the same values on $J$ and $I$ â€” and indeed $\operatorname{tr}J=\operatorname{tr}I=2$, $\det J=\det I=1$ â€” so it cannot distinguish them.

**Remark.** $J$ and $I$ have the same characteristic polynomial $(X-1)^2$ but different minimal polynomials. **Lesson.** Rescaling the basis shrinks off-diagonal parts: this is the mechanism behind all orbit closure arguments.
@@@ mnden-orbits-of-diagonalisable-matrices-are-closed.md
---
title: Orbits of real-diagonalisable matrices are closed
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: conjugacy orbit, minimal polynomial, closed sets
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ be $\mathbb{R}$-diagonalisable. Show that its conjugacy orbit $\mathcal{O}(A)=\{PAP^{-1}:P\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})\}$ is closed in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Describe the orbit by polynomial equations: one saying $B$ is diagonalisable with the right eigenvalues, one saying the multiplicities are right.

## Solution

**Setup.** Let $\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_r$ be the distinct eigenvalues of $A$ (real), and $p(X)=\prod_{i=1}^r(X-\lambda_i)$. Since $A$ is diagonalisable, $p$ is its minimal polynomial and $p(A)=0$.

**Claim.** $\mathcal{O}(A)=\mathcal{F}$ where $\mathcal{F}=\{B\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):p(B)=0\text{ and }\chi_B=\chi_A\}$.

*($\subseteq$)* If $B=PAP^{-1}$, then $p(B)=Pp(A)P^{-1}=0$ and $\chi_B=\chi_A$.

*($\supseteq$)* Let $B\in\mathcal{F}$. The minimal polynomial of $B$ divides $p$, which has simple real roots, so $B$ is diagonalisable over $\mathbb{R}$. Its eigenvalues with multiplicities are read from $\chi_B=\chi_A$, so $B$ is similar over $\mathbb{R}$ to the same diagonal matrix as $A$, i.e. $B=PAP^{-1}$ with $P$ real.

**Closedness.** $\{B:p(B)=0\}$ is the zero set of the polynomial map $B\mapsto p(B)$, hence closed; $\{B:\chi_B=\chi_A\}$ is the preimage of a point under the continuous map $B\mapsto\chi_B$ (coefficients are polynomial in the entries). The intersection $\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{O}(A)$ is closed.

**Remark.** Together with the next problem this shows: *the orbit of a real matrix is closed iff the matrix is diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$.* Here the "if" for complex eigenvalues needs a similar argument with the real polynomial $p=\prod(X-\lambda_i)$ over the distinct complex eigenvalues. **Lesson.** To prove an orbit closed, describe it by closed algebraic conditions (minimal polynomial + characteristic polynomial).
@@@ mnden-orbits-of-non-diagonalisable-matrices-are-not-closed.md
---
title: Orbits of non-diagonalisable matrices are not closed
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: conjugacy orbit, Jordan form, scaling, closure
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that if $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is not diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$, then its conjugacy orbit $\mathcal{O}(A)$ is not closed in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Put $A$ in real Jordan form $S+N$ ($S$ semisimple, $N\ne0$ nilpotent commuting with $S$) and conjugate by a diagonal matrix that multiplies $N$ by $t$ without changing $S$.

## Solution

**Real Jordan form.** Over $\mathbb{R}$, $A$ is similar to a block-diagonal matrix whose blocks are:
- for a real eigenvalue $\lambda$: the Jordan block $J_k(\lambda)=\lambda I_k+N_k$, with $N_k$ having ones on the superdiagonal;
- for a pair of complex eigenvalues $a\pm ib$: the block built from $C=\begin{pmatrix}a&-b\\b&a\end{pmatrix}$ on the diagonal and $I_2$ on the block superdiagonal.

So $A\sim S+N$ with $S$ block diagonal (the diagonal parts), $N$ the "superdiagonal-of-blocks" part, and $SN=NS$. Since $A$ is not diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$, some Jordan block has size $\ge2$, so $N\ne0$.

**Rescaling.** Index the diagonal positions of each Jordan block by $i=1,2,\dots$ (for a complex pair, each index stands for a $2\times2$ block) and let $D_t$ be the block diagonal matrix with scalar block $t^{-i}$ in position $i$ of each Jordan block. Then
- $D_tSD_t^{-1}=S$, because inside each position $S$ is a scalar $\lambda I$ or a $2\times2$ block $C$, and $D_t$ acts there as a scalar;
- an entry of $N$ linking position $i$ to position $i+1$ is multiplied by $t^{-i}/t^{-(i+1)}=t$: $D_tND_t^{-1}=tN$.

Hence $D_t(S+N)D_t^{-1}=S+tN\to S$ as $t\to0$.

**Conclusion.** $S\in\overline{\mathcal{O}(A)}$. But $S$ is diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$ while $A$ is not, so $S$ is not similar to $A$: $S\notin\mathcal{O}(A)$. Thus $\mathcal{O}(A)$ is not closed.

**Check.** For $J_2(1)=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$: $S=I$, $N=E_{12}$, and the limit is $I$, matching the previous problem.

**Remark.** The converse of this result is true: orbits of matrices diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$ are closed (the characterisation $\{B:p(B)=0,\chi_B=\chi_A\}$ with $p$ the squarefree polynomial vanishing at the distinct eigenvalues, now over $\mathbb{R}$). **Lesson.** Jordan blocks are "unstable": the nilpotent part can be shrunk to zero by conjugation.
@@@ mnden-the-regular-nilpotent-orbit-is-dense-in-the-nilpotent-cone.md
---
title: The regular nilpotent orbit is dense in the nilpotent cone
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: nilpotent matrices, Jordan form, orbit closure
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\mathcal{N}_n=\{N\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):N^n=0\}$ be the nilpotent cone and $J_n(0)$ the regular nilpotent Jordan block (ones on the superdiagonal). Show that the orbit of $J_n(0)$ is dense in $\mathcal{N}_n$.

## Hint

Take a nilpotent matrix in Jordan form with several blocks, and put small nonzero entries on the superdiagonal positions that link consecutive blocks.

## Solution

**Step 1: reduction to Jordan forms.** The closure $\overline{\mathcal{O}}$ of the orbit $\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}(J_n(0))$ is invariant under conjugation: if $M_k\to M$ with $M_k\in\mathcal{O}$ then $PM_kP^{-1}\to PMP^{-1}$ and $PM_kP^{-1}\in\mathcal{O}$. Every $N\in\mathcal{N}_n$ is conjugate to a Jordan form $J_\lambda=\operatorname{diag}\bigl(J_{\lambda_1}(0),\dots,J_{\lambda_r}(0)\bigr)$ with $\lambda_1+\dots+\lambda_r=n$. So it suffices to show $J_\lambda\in\overline{\mathcal{O}}$ for every such $\lambda$.

**Step 2: perturbing a Jordan form.** $J_\lambda$ has ones in the superdiagonal positions $(i,i+1)$ except at the $r-1$ positions between consecutive blocks, where it has zeros. For $\varepsilon>0$ let $J_\lambda(\varepsilon)$ be obtained by putting $\varepsilon$ at those $r-1$ positions. Then $J_\lambda(\varepsilon)\to J_\lambda$ as $\varepsilon\to0$.

**Step 3: $J_\lambda(\varepsilon)\in\mathcal{O}$.** $M=J_\lambda(\varepsilon)$ is strictly upper triangular, so nilpotent, and all $n-1$ entries $m_{i,i+1}$ are nonzero. The $(1,n)$ entry of $M^{n-1}$ is $\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}m_{i,i+1}\ne0$ (only the path $1\to2\to\dots\to n$ contributes in a strictly upper triangular product), so $M^{n-1}\ne0$. A nilpotent matrix with $M^{n-1}\ne0$ has a single Jordan block of size $n$, so $M$ is conjugate to $J_n(0)$.

**Conclusion.** $J_\lambda$ is a limit of elements of $\mathcal{O}$, hence in $\overline{\mathcal{O}}$; by Step 1, $\mathcal{N}_n\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{O}}$. Since $\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_n$ and $\mathcal{N}_n$ is closed, $\overline{\mathcal{O}}=\mathcal{N}_n$.

**Remark.** Thus the nilpotent cone is the closure of a single orbit (the "regular" one), and all other nilpotent orbits lie in the boundary. **Lesson.** Perturb a Jordan form by linking blocks to increase the size of the largest block.
@@@ mnden-idempotents-of-a-given-rank-form-a-closed-set.md
---
title: Idempotents of a given rank form a closed set
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: idempotent, projections, trace, rank
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that for each $k$ the set $\mathcal{P}_k=\{P\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):P^2=P,\ \operatorname{rank}P=k\}$ is closed.

## Hint

For an idempotent, the trace equals the rank.

## Solution

**Trace equals rank.** Let $P^2=P$. Then $X^2-X=X(X-1)$ annihilates $P$, which has simple roots, so $P$ is diagonalisable with eigenvalues in $\{0,1\}$. If the eigenvalue $1$ has multiplicity $m$, then $\operatorname{rank}P=m$ (the image is the $1$-eigenspace) and $\operatorname{tr}P=m\cdot1+(n-m)\cdot0=m$. So $\operatorname{tr}P=\operatorname{rank}P$.

**Description of $\mathcal{P}_k$.** Therefore
$$\mathcal{P}_k=\{P:P^2=P\}\cap\{P:\operatorname{tr}P=k\}.$$
Indeed, "$\subseteq$" holds by the trace-equals-rank fact, and "$\supseteq$" because for an idempotent, $\operatorname{tr}P=k$ means $\operatorname{rank}P=k$.

**Closed.** The first set is the zero set of the polynomial map $P\mapsto P^2-P$, the second the preimage of $\{k\}$ under the continuous map $\operatorname{tr}$. Their intersection is closed.

**Remark.** The set $\{P^2=P\}$ has $n+1$ connected components $\mathcal{P}_0,\dots,\mathcal{P}_n$: since the trace is continuous and integer-valued on it, $\mathcal{P}_k$ are open in $\{P^2=P\}$ as well, and each is closed. **Lesson.** Integer-valued continuous functions (like the rank of an idempotent) separate components.
@@@ mnden-closure-of-the-positive-semidefinite-matrices-of-rank-k.md
---
title: Closure of the PSD matrices of rank k
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: positive semidefinite, rank, closure
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ be the space of real symmetric $n\times n$ matrices and $0\le k\le n$. Show that the closure of $\{S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n:S\ge0,\operatorname{rank}S=k\}$ is $\{S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n:S\ge0,\operatorname{rank}S\le k\}$.

## Hint

For "$\supseteq$": if $\operatorname{rank}S<k$, increase the rank by adding a small multiple of a projection onto a subspace of $\ker S$.

## Solution

**"$\subseteq$": the right-hand side is closed and contains the set.**
- $\{S\ge0\}=\{x^TSx\ge0\ \forall x\}$ is an intersection of closed half-spaces, hence closed.
- $\{\operatorname{rank}S\le k\}$ is the common zero set of all $(k+1)\times(k+1)$ minors (polynomials), hence closed.

So the closure of the set of rank $k$ PSD matrices lies in $\{S\ge0,\operatorname{rank}S\le k\}$.

**"$\supseteq$".** Let $S\ge0$ with $s=\operatorname{rank}S\le k$. If $s=k$ there is nothing to prove. If $s<k$, the kernel $\ker S$ has dimension $n-s\ge n-k+1\ge k-s$ (we need $k-s\le n-s$, true as $k\le n$). Choose a subspace $W\subseteq\ker S$ of dimension $k-s$ and let $P$ be the orthogonal projection onto $W$ (a symmetric idempotent of rank $k-s$). Put
$$S_\varepsilon=S+\varepsilon P,\qquad\varepsilon>0.$$
- $S_\varepsilon\ge0$ since $S\ge0$ and $P\ge0$.
- $\ker S=(\operatorname{im}S)^\perp$ because $S$ is symmetric, so $W\perp\operatorname{im}S$ and $\operatorname{im}S_\varepsilon=\operatorname{im}S\oplus W$ (on $\operatorname{im}S$, $S_\varepsilon=S$ since $P$ kills $\operatorname{im}S\subseteq W^\perp$; on $W$, $S_\varepsilon=\varepsilon\operatorname{id}$). So $\operatorname{rank}S_\varepsilon=s+(k-s)=k$.
- $S_\varepsilon\to S$ as $\varepsilon\to0$.

**Remark.** For $k=n$ this says the positive definite matrices are dense in the PSD cone. **Lesson.** The PSD cone of rank $\le k$ is exactly the closure of the rank-$k$ stratum.
@@@ mnden-finite-order-rotations-are-dense-in-so2.md
---
title: Finite-order rotations are dense in SO(2)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: rotations, roots of unity, density
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that the set of elements of finite order in $SO(2)$ is dense in $SO(2)$.

## Hint

The rotation by $\theta$ has finite order iff $\theta/2\pi$ is rational.

## Solution

**Parametrisation.** $SO(2)=\{R_\theta:\theta\in\mathbb{R}\}$ with $R_\theta=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$, and $R_\theta R_\varphi=R_{\theta+\varphi}$, $R_\theta=R_\varphi$ iff $\theta-\varphi\in2\pi\mathbb{Z}$. The map $\theta\mapsto R_\theta$ is a continuous surjection $\mathbb{R}\to SO(2)$.

**Finite order.** $R_\theta^m=R_{m\theta}=I$ iff $m\theta\in2\pi\mathbb{Z}$. So $R_\theta$ has finite order iff $\theta/2\pi\in\mathbb{Q}$.

**Density.** Let $\theta\in\mathbb{R}$ and $\varepsilon>0$. Rationals are dense, so there is $q\in\mathbb{Q}$ with $|2\pi q-\theta|<\varepsilon$. Then $R_{2\pi q}$ has finite order and, since $\theta\mapsto R_\theta$ is continuous, $R_{2\pi q}$ is arbitrarily close to $R_\theta$ when $\varepsilon$ is small.

**Remark.** These are the roots of unity $e^{2\pi iq}$ in the unit circle, to which $SO(2)$ is isomorphic. Finite-order elements are dense but of course not all of $SO(2)$ (it also contains rotations of infinite order). **Lesson.** Density in a compact group often comes from torsion elements.
@@@ mnden-kronecker-s-theorem-for-rotations-of-the-circle.md
---
title: Powers of a rotation: when are they dense?
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: Kronecker, pigeonhole, rotation group, dense subgroup
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that $\{R_{k\alpha}:k\in\mathbb{Z}\}$ is dense in $SO(2)$ if and only if $\alpha/\pi$ is irrational.

## Hint

Rational case: the subgroup is finite. Irrational case: use the pigeonhole principle to find a rotation by a very small nonzero angle among the powers.

## Solution

Write $\theta$ for the angle modulo $2\pi$. Note that $\alpha/\pi\in\mathbb{Q}$ iff $\alpha/2\pi\in\mathbb{Q}$.

**Rational case.** If $\alpha/2\pi=p/q$, then $R_{q\alpha}=R_{2\pi p}=I$, so $\{R_{k\alpha}\}$ has at most $q$ elements: finite, hence closed and not dense (as $SO(2)$ is infinite).

**Irrational case.** Suppose $\alpha/2\pi\notin\mathbb{Q}$. Then the $R_{k\alpha}$, $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, are pairwise distinct: $R_{j\alpha}=R_{k\alpha}$ would give $(j-k)\alpha\in2\pi\mathbb{Z}$.

Fix $N\ge1$ and cut the circle into $N$ arcs of length $2\pi/N$. Among the $N+1$ distinct points $R_{0},R_\alpha,\dots,R_{N\alpha}$ two lie in the same arc, say $R_{i\alpha}$ and $R_{j\alpha}$ with $i<j$. Then $R_{(j-i)\alpha}=R_\delta$ for some angle $\delta$ with $0<|\delta|<2\pi/N$ (nonzero by distinctness, and we may represent $\delta$ in $(-\pi,\pi]$; its absolute value is the angular distance between two points of the same arc).

The powers $R_{m\delta}=R_{m(j-i)\alpha}$ belong to our set, and the angles $m\delta$, $m\in\mathbb{Z}$, form an arithmetic progression of step $|\delta|<2\pi/N$ going around the circle. So every point of the circle is within $2\pi/N$ (even $|\delta|/2$) of some $R_{m\delta}$.

Since $N$ was arbitrary, the set is dense.

**Remark.** This is Kronecker's theorem in dimension one: the orbit $\{k\alpha\bmod2\pi\}$ of an irrational rotation is dense (and in fact equidistributed). **Lesson.** Pigeonhole produces arbitrarily small elements in an infinite subgroup of a compact group.
@@@ mnden-a-discrete-cyclic-subgroup-of-gl2.md
---
title: A discrete cyclic subgroup of GLâ‚‚(â„)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: discrete subgroup, closed set, powers of a matrix
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A=\operatorname{diag}(2,\tfrac12)$. Show that $\Gamma=\{A^k:k\in\mathbb{Z}\}$ is closed and discrete in $GL_2(\mathbb{R})$ (indeed in $M_2(\mathbb{R})$).

## Hint

Show that $\|A^k\|\to\infty$ and that distinct powers are at distance at least $1$ from each other.

## Solution

**Computation.** $A^k=\operatorname{diag}(2^k,2^{-k})$ for $k\in\mathbb{Z}$.

**Uniformly discrete.** Use the max-entry distance $d(X,Y)=\max_{i,j}|x_{ij}-y_{ij}|$ (any norm on $M_2$ is at least a constant times it; for the operator or Frobenius norm this distance is a lower bound). Let $j<k$.
- If $j\ge0$: $2^k-2^j\ge2^j(2-1)\ge1$.
- If $k\le0$: $2^{-j}-2^{-k}=2^{-k}(2^{k-j}-1)\ge2^{-k}\ge1$.
- If $j<0<k$: $2^k-2^j\ge2-\tfrac12=\tfrac32>1$.

So $d(A^j,A^k)\ge1$ for all $j\ne k$. In particular every point of $\Gamma$ is isolated: $\Gamma$ is discrete.

**Closed.** Suppose $A^{k_m}\to X$ in $M_2(\mathbb{R})$. A convergent sequence is Cauchy, so for large $m,m'$ we would have $d(A^{k_m},A^{k_{m'}})<1$, which forces $k_m=k_{m'}$. So the sequence is eventually constant and $X\in\Gamma$. Hence $\Gamma$ is closed in $M_2(\mathbb{R})$, and therefore in $GL_2(\mathbb{R})$.

**Remark.** Contrast with $\{R_{k\alpha}\}$ in the previous problem, which can be dense: powers of a matrix with eigenvalues of modulus $\ne1$ escape to infinity, while eigenvalues of modulus $1$ can make the powers accumulate. **Lesson.** Uniform discreteness gives closedness for free.
@@@ mnden-invertible-rational-matrices-are-dense-in-gl-n.md
---
title: Invertible rational matrices are dense in GL_n(â„)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: rational matrices, density, open set
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that $GL_n(\mathbb{Q})=GL_n(\mathbb{R})\cap M_n(\mathbb{Q})$ is dense in $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

If $U$ is open and $D$ is dense in a space $X$, then $U\cap D$ is dense in $U$.

## Solution

**General fact.** Let $D$ be dense in $X$ and $U\subseteq X$ open. Take $x\in U$ and a neighbourhood $V$ of $x$. Then $V\cap U$ is a nonempty open set, so it meets $D$; hence $x\in\overline{U\cap D}$.

**Application.** $X=M_n(\mathbb{R})$, $D=M_n(\mathbb{Q})$ is dense (each entry is approximated by rationals), and $U=GL_n(\mathbb{R})=\{\det\ne0\}$ is open. So $M_n(\mathbb{Q})\cap GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is dense in $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Subtlety.** The elements of this set are the rational matrices that are invertible over $\mathbb{R}$; their determinant is a nonzero rational number, so they are also invertible over $\mathbb{Q}$, with inverse in $M_n(\mathbb{Q})$ (by the adjugate formula). So the set is exactly the group $GL_n(\mathbb{Q})$.

**Remark.** The same argument shows $\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{Q})\}$ is dense in any open subset of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$. It fails for closed subsets such as $SL_n$ (see the next problems), where rationality interacts with the constraint $\det=1$. **Lesson.** Open $\cap$ dense is dense in the open set.
@@@ mnden-rational-matrices-of-determinant-one-are-dense-in-sl-n.md
---
title: SL_n(â„š) is dense in SL_n(â„)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: special linear group, transvections, density
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that $SL_n(\mathbb{Q})$ is dense in $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

$SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is generated by the transvections $I+tE_{ij}$ ($i\ne j$, $t\in\mathbb{R}$).

## Solution

**Fact used.** Over a field, $SL_n$ is generated by elementary matrices $T_{ij}(t)=I+tE_{ij}$, $i\ne j$ (Gauss elimination: any matrix of determinant $1$ is reduced to $I$ by elementary row operations of this type, with the last diagonal entry fixed by the determinant being $1$).

**Approximation.** Let $g\in SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ and write $g=T_1T_2\cdots T_m$ with $T_l=I+t_lE_{i_lj_l}$. Choose rationals $t_l^{(k)}\to t_l$ ($k\to\infty$) and set
$$g^{(k)}=\prod_{l=1}^m\bigl(I+t_l^{(k)}E_{i_lj_l}\bigr).$$
- Each factor has determinant $1$ and rational entries, so $g^{(k)}\in SL_n(\mathbb{Q})$.
- Matrix multiplication is continuous, so $g^{(k)}\to g$.

Therefore $SL_n(\mathbb{Q})$ is dense in $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Remark.** The naive attempt (approximate $g$ entrywise by a rational matrix) gives a rational matrix with determinant close to, but not exactly, $1$; fixing the determinant by dividing by an $n$-th root is not possible over $\mathbb{Q}$. The generation argument bypasses this. **Lesson.** To approximate within a group, use generators.
@@@ mnden-rational-points-of-so2-are-dense.md
---
title: Rational rotation matrices are dense in SO(2)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: rational points, circle, stereographic projection, Pythagorean triples
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that $SO(2)\cap M_2(\mathbb{Q})$ is dense in $SO(2)$.

## Hint

A matrix of $SO(2)$ is $\begin{pmatrix}c&-s\\s&c\end{pmatrix}$ with $c^2+s^2=1$: it is determined by a point of the unit circle. Parametrise the rational points of the circle.

## Solution

**Reduction.** $SO(2)\cap M_2(\mathbb{Q})$ corresponds to the points $(c,s)\in\mathbb{Q}^2$ of the unit circle, and the correspondence $(c,s)\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}c&-s\\s&c\end{pmatrix}$ is a homeomorphism from the circle to $SO(2)$. So we must show that the rational points of the unit circle are dense.

**Stereographic parametrisation.** For $u\in\mathbb{R}$ put
$$\varphi(u)=\left(\frac{1-u^2}{1+u^2},\ \frac{2u}{1+u^2}\right).$$
- $\left(\frac{1-u^2}{1+u^2}\right)^2+\left(\frac{2u}{1+u^2}\right)^2=\frac{(1-u^2)^2+4u^2}{(1+u^2)^2}=1$, so $\varphi(u)$ lies on the circle.
- $\varphi$ is a continuous bijection from $\mathbb{R}$ onto the circle minus the point $(-1,0)$; it is the inverse of the stereographic projection from $(-1,0)$ (the line through $(-1,0)$ with slope $u$ meets the circle again at $\varphi(u)$).
- If $u\in\mathbb{Q}$, then $\varphi(u)\in\mathbb{Q}^2$.

**Density.** Let $P=\varphi(u_0)$ be a point of the circle other than $(-1,0)$. Rationals $u_k\to u_0$ give rational points $\varphi(u_k)\to P$ by continuity. For the point $(-1,0)$: $\varphi(u)\to(-1,0)$ as $u\to\pm\infty$, and $(-1,0)$ is also approached by $\varphi(k)$, $k\in\mathbb{N}$, integers being rational. So every point of the circle is a limit of rational points.

**Example.** $u=\frac12$ gives $\left(\frac35,\frac45\right)$, the $3$-$4$-$5$ triple.

**Remark.** This is the classical parametrisation of Pythagorean triples. In particular $SO(2)$ contains elements of infinite order with rational entries, since $\frac35+\frac45i$ is not a root of unity. **Lesson.** Rational points on conics with one rational point are dense.
@@@ mnden-orthogonally-invariant-functions-of-symmetric-matrices.md
---
title: Orthogonally invariant functions of symmetric matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: spectral theorem, invariant functions, eigenvalues
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathrm{Sym}_n\to\mathbb{R}$ be continuous with $f(QSQ^T)=f(S)$ for all $Q\in O(n)$ and $S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n$. Show that there is a continuous function $g$ on $\{\lambda_1\ge\dots\ge\lambda_n\}\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ with $f(S)=g(\lambda_1(S),\dots,\lambda_n(S))$, where $\lambda_1(S)\ge\dots\ge\lambda_n(S)$ are the eigenvalues of $S$.

## Hint

Define $g$ by evaluating $f$ on diagonal matrices with ordered entries.

## Solution

**Definition of $g$.** For $\lambda=(\lambda_1\ge\dots\ge\lambda_n)$ put
$$g(\lambda)=f\bigl(\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_n)\bigr).$$

**Continuity.** $g$ is the composition of the continuous map $\lambda\mapsto\operatorname{diag}(\lambda)$ with $f$, so it is continuous.

**$f=g\circ\lambda$.** Let $S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n$. By the spectral theorem there is $Q\in O(n)$ with $S=Q\operatorname{diag}(\lambda(S))Q^T$, where we order the eigenvalues decreasingly. By invariance,
$$f(S)=f\bigl(\operatorname{diag}(\lambda(S))\bigr)=g(\lambda(S)).$$

**Remark (converse and continuity of the spectrum).** Conversely, every continuous $g$ gives a continuous invariant $f=g\circ\lambda$, because the ordered eigenvalues depend continuously on $S$: by Weyl's inequality $|\lambda_i(S)-\lambda_i(S')|\le\|S-S'\|$ (operator norm). Examples: $\operatorname{tr}S=\sum\lambda_i$, $\det S=\prod\lambda_i$, $\|S\|_F^2=\sum\lambda_i^2$ and $\lambda_1(S)$ are all invariant functions. **Lesson.** An invariant function is determined by its restriction to a "slice" (here the diagonal matrices).
@@@ mnden-the-set-of-normal-matrices.md
---
title: The set of real normal matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: normal matrices, closed sets, subspaces
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\mathcal{N}=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):AA^T=A^TA\}$ be the set of real normal matrices.
1. Show that $\mathcal{N}$ is closed and contains $\mathrm{Sym}_n$, $\mathrm{Skew}_n$ and $O(n)$.
2. Show that $\mathcal{N}$ is not a vector subspace, and that it is not open (for $n\ge2$).

## Hint

For (2), a sum of a symmetric and a skew-symmetric matrix can be any matrix.

## Solution

**1. Closed.** $\mathcal{N}$ is the zero set of the continuous (polynomial) map $A\mapsto AA^T-A^TA$.

**Contains the families.**
- $A$ symmetric: $A^T=A$, so $AA^T=A^2=A^TA$.
- $A$ skew: $A^T=-A$, so $AA^T=-A^2=A^TA$.
- $A$ orthogonal: $AA^T=I=A^TA$.

**2. Not a subspace.** Take $S=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac12\\\frac12&0\end{pmatrix}$ (symmetric) and $K=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac12\\-\frac12&0\end{pmatrix}$ (skew). Both are in $\mathcal{N}$, but
$$S+K=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}=E_{12},\quad E_{12}E_{12}^T=E_{11},\ \ E_{12}^TE_{12}=E_{22},$$
which differ: $E_{12}\notin\mathcal{N}$. (For $n>2$, add a zero block.)

**Not open.** $I\in\mathcal{N}$, but $A_\varepsilon=I+\varepsilon E_{12}\to I$ is not normal for $\varepsilon\ne0$:
$$A_\varepsilon A_\varepsilon^T=I+\varepsilon(E_{12}+E_{21})+\varepsilon^2E_{11},\qquad A_\varepsilon^TA_\varepsilon=I+\varepsilon(E_{12}+E_{21})+\varepsilon^2E_{22},$$
and $E_{11}\ne E_{22}$. So no neighbourhood of $I$ lies in $\mathcal{N}$.

**Remark.** $\mathcal{N}$ is the set of matrices that commute with their transpose; by the spectral theorem, normal real matrices are exactly those orthogonally similar to a block diagonal matrix with $1\times1$ real blocks and $2\times2$ blocks $\begin{pmatrix}a&-b\\b&a\end{pmatrix}$. It is a closed cone (stable under real scaling) but not convex. **Lesson.** Sums of normal matrices are generally not normal.
@@@ mnden-the-closure-of-the-hurwitz-matrices.md
---
title: The closure of the Hurwitz matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: Hurwitz, stability, spectrum, closure
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

A matrix is *Hurwitz* if all its eigenvalues have strictly negative real part. Show that the closure of the set $\mathcal{H}$ of Hurwitz matrices is $\{A:\operatorname{Re}\lambda\le0\text{ for all }\lambda\in\operatorname{spec}A\}$.

## Hint

For "$\subseteq$", eigenvalues of $A$ are limits of eigenvalues of $A_k$. For "$\supseteq$", shift: $A-\varepsilon I$.

## Solution

Let $\mathcal{K}=\{A:\operatorname{Re}\lambda\le0\ \forall\lambda\in\operatorname{spec}A\}$.

**"$\overline{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq\mathcal{K}$".** Let $A_k\in\mathcal{H}$, $A_k\to A$, and let $\mu$ be an eigenvalue of $A$. We show that $\mu$ is a limit of eigenvalues of $A_k$. As in the density section: $\prod_i|\mu-\lambda_i(A_k)|=|\chi_{A_k}(\mu)|\to|\chi_A(\mu)|=0$, so $\min_i|\mu-\lambda_i(A_k)|\to0$. Pick $\lambda^{(k)}$ an eigenvalue of $A_k$ with $\lambda^{(k)}\to\mu$. Since $\operatorname{Re}\lambda^{(k)}<0$, passing to the limit gives $\operatorname{Re}\mu\le0$. So $A\in\mathcal{K}$.

**"$\mathcal{K}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{H}}$".** Let $A\in\mathcal{K}$ and $\varepsilon>0$. The eigenvalues of $A-\varepsilon I$ are $\lambda-\varepsilon$ with $\lambda\in\operatorname{spec}A$, and $\operatorname{Re}(\lambda-\varepsilon)\le-\varepsilon<0$. So $A-\varepsilon I\in\mathcal{H}$, and $A-\varepsilon I\to A$ as $\varepsilon\to0$.

**Remark.** $\mathcal{H}$ is open (continuity of eigenvalues), and $\mathcal{K}$ is closed; the boundary consists of matrices in $\mathcal{K}$ with an eigenvalue on the imaginary axis. $e^{tA}\to0$ for $A\in\mathcal{H}$: this is the stability of $x'=Ax$. **Lesson.** Shifting by $\varepsilon I$ moves the whole spectrum; it is the simplest perturbation.
@@@ mnden-closure-of-the-matrices-with-a-non-real-eigenvalue.md
---
title: Closure of the matrices with a non-real eigenvalue
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: discriminant, non-real eigenvalues, closure, odd dimension
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\mathcal{N}_n\subset M_n(\mathbb{R})$ be the set of matrices with at least one non-real eigenvalue.
1. For $n=2$, describe $\overline{\mathcal{N}_2}$.
2. Show that $\overline{\mathcal{N}_3}\ne M_3(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

For $n=2$ use the discriminant $\Delta=\operatorname{tr}^2-4\det$. For $n=3$ find an open set of matrices with three distinct real eigenvalues.

## Solution

**1. $n=2$.** For $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$, $\chi_A=X^2-(a+d)X+(ad-bc)$ has a non-real root iff its discriminant $\Delta(A)=(a+d)^2-4(ad-bc)=(a-d)^2+4bc$ is negative. So $\mathcal{N}_2=\{\Delta<0\}$.

*Claim:* $\overline{\mathcal{N}_2}=\{\Delta\le0\}$.
- "$\subseteq$": $\{\Delta\le0\}$ is closed and contains $\mathcal{N}_2$.
- "$\supseteq$": let $\Delta(A)=0$. Consider $\Delta$ as a function of $(x,b,c)$ where $x=a-d$: $\Delta=x^2+4bc$, with gradient $(2x,4c,4b)$. If $(x,b,c)\ne0$, the gradient is nonzero and $\Delta$ decreases strictly along $-\nabla\Delta$ near this point, so there are points with $\Delta<0$ arbitrarily close. If $x=b=c=0$ (so $A=aI$), the matrices $A_\varepsilon=aI+\begin{pmatrix}0&-\varepsilon\\\varepsilon&0\end{pmatrix}$ have $\Delta=-4\varepsilon^2<0$ and tend to $A$.

**2. $n=3$.** Let $A_0=\operatorname{diag}(1,2,3)$ and $\rho=\frac13$. Take $A$ with $\|A-A_0\|$ small enough that each of the intervals around $1,2,3$ of radius $\rho$ contains an eigenvalue of $A$ (by the inequality $\min_i|\mu-\lambda_i(A)|\le|\chi_A(\mu)|^{1/3}\to0$ for $\mu=1,2,3$). The three eigenvalues are in disjoint discs, hence distinct, and they are all the eigenvalues of $A$ (there are only $3$). A non-real eigenvalue $\lambda$ of the real matrix $A$ would have the conjugate $\bar\lambda\ne\lambda$ as another eigenvalue in the *same* disc (the discs are centred on the real axis and stable under conjugation), contradicting the fact that each disc contains exactly one eigenvalue. So all eigenvalues of $A$ are real.

Thus a neighbourhood of $\operatorname{diag}(1,2,3)$ misses $\mathcal{N}_3$, so $\operatorname{diag}(1,2,3)\notin\overline{\mathcal{N}_3}$.

**Remark.** For $n=2$, scalar matrices $aI$ are on the boundary. In odd dimension the characteristic polynomial always has a real root, but that is not what we use. **Lesson.** Open conditions (distinct real eigenvalues) show a set is not dense.
@@@ mnden-an-algebraic-subset-has-measure-zero.md
---
title: Proper algebraic subsets of M_n(â„) have measure zero
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-density
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: Lebesgue measure, polynomials, Fubini, induction
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that a proper algebraic subset of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ (a proper subset defined as the common zero set of polynomials in the entries) has Lebesgue measure zero.

## Hint

Show first that the zero set of a nonzero polynomial on $\mathbb{R}^k$ is null, by induction on $k$ and Fubini's theorem.

## Solution

**Reduction.** A proper algebraic subset $Z=\{p_1=\dots=p_m=0\}\subsetneq M_n$ must have some $p_i\not\equiv0$ (if all were $0$, $Z$ would be everything). Then $Z\subseteq\{p_i=0\}$, so it suffices to treat one nonzero polynomial $p$ on $\mathbb{R}^k$, $k=n^2$.

**Claim.** If $p\not\equiv0$ is a polynomial on $\mathbb{R}^k$, then $\{p=0\}$ has Lebesgue measure $0$.

*$k=1$:* a nonzero polynomial has finitely many roots.

*Induction step.* Assume the claim in dimension $k-1$. Write
$$p(x',x_k)=\sum_{j=0}^dq_j(x')x_k^j,\qquad x'=(x_1,\dots,x_{k-1}),$$
with $q_d\not\equiv0$ (the top coefficient is a nonzero polynomial in $x'$). Let $Z'=\{q_d=0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k-1}$, null by the induction hypothesis.
- For $x'\notin Z'$, $x_k\mapsto p(x',x_k)$ is a polynomial of degree exactly $d$, so it has at most $d$ roots: the slice $\{x_k:p(x',x_k)=0\}$ is finite, hence null.
- The set $\{p=0\}$ is closed, hence measurable, so by Tonelli
$$\lambda_k(\{p=0\})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k-1}}\lambda_1\bigl(\{x_k:p(x',x_k)=0\}\bigr)dx'=\int_{Z'}(\cdots)dx'+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\setminus Z'}0\,dx'=0,$$
because $Z'$ is null.

**Remark.** So "generic" in the sense of Zariski (complement of an algebraic set) implies "almost every" in the sense of Lebesgue. For example, almost every matrix is invertible, has distinct eigenvalues and is diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$. **Lesson.** Induction on the number of variables plus Fubini is the standard way to handle null sets of polynomials.

@@@ mnappb2-the-idempotents-of-rank-one-and-the-involutions-of-m2-are-cylinders.md
---
title: Rank-one idempotents and involutions of Mâ‚‚(â„) are cylinders
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: M2(R), complex coordinates, idempotents, involutions, trace and determinant
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Identify $\mathbb{R}^2$ with $\mathbb{C}$ (the vector $(x_1,x_2)$ is the number $x_1+ix_2$). A matrix $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}\in M_2(\mathbb{R})$ then acts by
$$Ax=zx+w\bar x,\qquad z=\frac{(a+d)+i(c-b)}2,\quad w=\frac{(a-d)+i(b+c)}2,$$
and $A\mapsto(z,w)$ is an $\mathbb{R}$-linear bijection $M_2(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{C}^2\cong\mathbb{R}^4$. Show that
$$\mathcal I=\{A\in M_2(\mathbb{R}):A^2=A,\ \operatorname{rank}A=1\},\qquad \mathcal J=\{A\in M_2(\mathbb{R}):A^2=I,\ A\ne\pm I\}$$
are both homeomorphic to the cylinder $\mathbb{R}\times S^1$.

## Hint

Characterise both sets by the trace and the determinant (Cayleyâ€“Hamilton), then use $\operatorname{tr}A=2\operatorname{Re}z$ and $\det A=|z|^2-|w|^2$. After that, the coordinates are $\operatorname{Im}z$ and the argument of $w$.

## Solution

**Idea.** In $M_2$ a matrix is "almost determined" by its trace and determinant. Fixing them cuts $\mathbb{R}^4$ down to a surface, which the coordinates $(z,w)$ describe explicitly.

**Step 0: the dictionary.** Writing $x=x_1+ix_2$, one checks that the real part of $zx+w\bar x$ is $(z_1+w_1)x_1+(w_2-z_2)x_2$ and the imaginary part is $(z_2+w_2)x_1+(z_1-w_1)x_2$, where $z=z_1+iz_2$, $w=w_1+iw_2$. With the given $z,w$ these coefficients are $a,b,c,d$. Moreover, directly from the formulas,
$$\operatorname{tr}A=a+d=2\operatorname{Re}z,\qquad |z|^2-|w|^2=\tfrac14\bigl[(a+d)^2+(c-b)^2-(a-d)^2-(b+c)^2\bigr]=ad-bc=\det A.$$
Conversely, $a=\operatorname{Re}z+\operatorname{Re}w$, $d=\operatorname{Re}z-\operatorname{Re}w$, $c=\operatorname{Im}z+\operatorname{Im}w$, $b=\operatorname{Im}w-\operatorname{Im}z$, so $(z,w)\mapsto A$ is the inverse linear map.

**Step 1: $\mathcal I$ in terms of trace and determinant.** We claim $A\in\mathcal I\iff\operatorname{tr}A=1$ and $\det A=0$.
- ($\Rightarrow$) $A$ has rank $1$, so $\det A=0$ and $A\ne0$. Cayleyâ€“Hamilton gives $A^2-(\operatorname{tr}A)A+(\det A)I=0$, i.e. $A^2=(\operatorname{tr}A)A$. Since $A^2=A$ and $A\ne0$ we get $\operatorname{tr}A=1$.
- ($\Leftarrow$) Cayleyâ€“Hamilton with $\operatorname{tr}A=1$, $\det A=0$ gives $A^2=A$. Also $A\neq0$ (its trace is $1$) and $\det A=0$, so the rank is exactly $1$.

**Step 2: $\mathcal J$ in terms of trace and determinant.** We claim $A\in\mathcal J\iff\operatorname{tr}A=0$ and $\det A=-1$.
- ($\Rightarrow$) The minimal polynomial of $A$ divides $t^2-1=(t-1)(t+1)$. It cannot be $t-1$ or $t+1$ because $A\ne\pm I$. So it equals $t^2-1$, which has degree $2$, hence it is the characteristic polynomial: $\operatorname{tr}A=0$, $\det A=-1$.
- ($\Leftarrow$) Cayleyâ€“Hamilton gives $A^2-0\cdot A+(-1)I=0$, i.e. $A^2=I$, and $A\ne\pm I$ since $\operatorname{tr}(\pm I)=\pm2\ne0$.

**Step 3: coordinates on $\mathcal I$.** By Steps 0 and 1, $A\in\mathcal I$ iff $\operatorname{Re}z=\frac12$ and $|z|^2=|w|^2$. Write $z=\frac12+iq$ with $q\in\mathbb{R}$. Then $|w|=|z|=\sqrt{\frac14+q^2}>0$, so $w\ne0$ and $u=w/|w|\in S^1$ is defined. Define
$$\Phi:\mathcal I\to\mathbb{R}\times S^1,\qquad A\mapsto\Bigl(\operatorname{Im}z,\ \frac w{|w|}\Bigr).$$
$\Phi$ is continuous (coordinates are linear in $A$ and $w\neq0$). Its inverse sends $(q,u)$ to the matrix with $z=\frac12+iq$, $w=\sqrt{\frac14+q^2}\,u$; this is continuous, and the matrix obtained satisfies $\operatorname{Re}z=\frac12$, $|z|=|w|$, hence lies in $\mathcal I$. So $\Phi$ is a homeomorphism: $\mathcal I\cong\mathbb{R}\times S^1$.

**Step 4: coordinates on $\mathcal J$.** Now $\operatorname{Re}z=0$ and $|z|^2-|w|^2=-1$. With $z=iq$ this reads $|w|^2=q^2+1$, so $|w|\ge1$ and again $w\ne0$. The same argument with $|w|=\sqrt{1+q^2}$ gives a homeomorphism $\mathcal J\to\mathbb{R}\times S^1$, $A\mapsto(\operatorname{Im}z,w/|w|)$.

**Check.** The matrix $\operatorname{diag}(1,0)$ has $a=1$, $b=c=d=0$, hence $z=w=\frac12$: it corresponds to $q=0$ and $u=1$ in $\mathcal I$. The matrix $\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)$ has $z=0$, $w=1$: it is in $\mathcal J$ with $q=0$, $u=1$ (here $|w|=\sqrt{1+0}=1$ âœ“).

**Remark.** A rank-one idempotent is determined by its image $\ell_1$ and its kernel $\ell_2$, which are two distinct lines of $\mathbb{R}^2$. The set of pairs of distinct lines is $(\mathbb{RP}^1\times\mathbb{RP}^1)\setminus\text{diagonal}\cong S^1\times(S^1\setminus\{pt\})\cong S^1\times\mathbb{R}$, confirming the cylinder. An involution $\ne\pm I$ is determined by its $(+1)$-eigenline and $(-1)$-eigenline, again a pair of distinct lines.

**Lesson.** In $M_2$, conjugacy classes with prescribed trace and determinant are surfaces, and the coordinates $(z,w)$ turn the two equations into explicit formulas.
@@@ mnappb2-the-square-roots-of-minus-the-identity-in-m2-form-two-planes.md
---
title: The square roots of âˆ’I in Mâ‚‚(â„) form two disjoint planes
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: M2(R), complex coordinates, complex structures, connected components
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Identify $\mathbb{R}^2$ with $\mathbb{C}$ and write $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}\in M_2(\mathbb{R})$ as $Ax=zx+w\bar x$ with $z=\frac{(a+d)+i(c-b)}2$ and $w=\frac{(a-d)+i(b+c)}2$ (the map $A\mapsto(z,w)$ is a linear bijection onto $\mathbb{C}^2$, with $\operatorname{tr}A=2\operatorname{Re}z$ and $\det A=|z|^2-|w|^2$). Show that
$$\{A\in M_2(\mathbb{R}):A^2=-I\}$$
is homeomorphic to the disjoint union $\mathbb{R}^2\sqcup\mathbb{R}^2$ of two planes.

## Hint

$A^2=-I$ forces $\operatorname{tr}A=0$ and $\det A=1$. In coordinates this gives $z=iq$ and $q^2=1+|w|^2$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Fixing the trace and the determinant, the only freedom left is $w\in\mathbb{C}$, together with a sign for $\operatorname{Im}z$.

**Step 1: trace and determinant.** Let $A^2=-I$. The minimal polynomial of $A$ divides $t^2+1$, which is irreducible over $\mathbb{R}$, so it equals $t^2+1$. Since it has degree $2=n$, it is the characteristic polynomial: $\operatorname{tr}A=0$ and $\det A=1$. Conversely, if $\operatorname{tr}A=0$ and $\det A=1$, Cayleyâ€“Hamilton gives $A^2-0\cdot A+I=0$, i.e. $A^2=-I$. Hence
$$\{A^2=-I\}=\{\operatorname{tr}A=0,\ \det A=1\}.$$

**Step 2: coordinates.** The conditions read $\operatorname{Re}z=0$ and $|z|^2-|w|^2=1$. Writing $z=iq$ with $q\in\mathbb{R}$ we get $q^2=1+|w|^2$, that is
$$q=+\sqrt{1+|w|^2}\quad\text{or}\quad q=-\sqrt{1+|w|^2}.$$
Note $q\ne0$ since $q^2\ge1$. So the set is the union of two graphs over $w\in\mathbb{C}$:
$$S_\pm=\{(z,w):z=\pm i\sqrt{1+|w|^2}\}.$$

**Step 3: each sheet is a plane.** The projection $A\mapsto w$ is continuous on $S_+$, and it has the continuous inverse $w\mapsto(i\sqrt{1+|w|^2},w)$ (then back to the matrix by the inverse linear map). So $S_+\cong\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{R}^2$, and likewise $S_-$.

**Step 4: the sheets are separated.** $S_+$ and $S_-$ are disjoint (their $\operatorname{Im}z$ have opposite signs and $|\operatorname{Im}z|\ge1$). Each is closed (the zero set of a continuous equation inside the set), and the set is their union, so each is also open in the union (its complement there is the other, closed, sheet). Therefore the topological space is $S_+\sqcup S_-\cong\mathbb{R}^2\sqcup\mathbb{R}^2$.

**Check.** $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ has $z=i$, $w=0$: it sits at the bottom of the sheet $S_+$ (rotation by $+90^\circ$). The matrix $-J$ has $z=-i$, $w=0$ and lies on $S_-$.

**Remark.** The two sheets are exchanged by $A\mapsto-A$, and $\operatorname{sign}(\operatorname{Im}z)=\operatorname{sign}(c-b)$ records the orientation in which $A$ "rotates" the plane. This is the case $n=2$ of the theorem that $\{A\in M_{2m}(\mathbb{R}):A^2=-I\}$ has exactly two components (see the problem on complex structures).

**Lesson.** Slice the coordinate space by the equations given by trace and determinant, solve for one coordinate, and read the topology from the resulting graphs.
@@@ mnappb2-the-nonzero-nilpotents-of-m2-form-two-components.md
---
title: The nonzero nilpotents of Mâ‚‚(â„) form two components
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: M2(R), nilpotent matrices, connected components, complex coordinates
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Identify $\mathbb{R}^2$ with $\mathbb{C}$ and write $A\in M_2(\mathbb{R})$ as $Ax=zx+w\bar x$, with $z=\frac{(a+d)+i(c-b)}2$, $w=\frac{(a-d)+i(b+c)}2$ for $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$ (so $\operatorname{tr}A=2\operatorname{Re}z$, $\det A=|z|^2-|w|^2$). Show that the set $\mathcal N^*$ of nonzero nilpotent matrices of $M_2(\mathbb{R})$ has exactly two connected components, each homeomorphic to $S^1\times(0,\infty)$.

## Hint

A $2\times2$ matrix is nilpotent iff $\operatorname{tr}A=\det A=0$. Translate this into $z=iq$ and $|w|=|q|$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The two equations $\operatorname{tr}=\det=0$ leave only a "cone" in $\mathbb{R}^4$; removing the vertex $0$ separates the cone into two nappes, distinguished by the sign of $\operatorname{Im}z$.

**Step 1: nilpotent iff $\operatorname{tr}=\det=0$.** If $A$ is nilpotent, all its eigenvalues vanish, so $\chi_A(t)=t^2$, i.e. $\operatorname{tr}A=\det A=0$. Conversely, if $\operatorname{tr}A=\det A=0$, Cayleyâ€“Hamilton gives $A^2=0$.

**Step 2: coordinates.** The two equations become $\operatorname{Re}z=0$ and $|w|^2=|z|^2$. Write $z=iq$ with $q\in\mathbb{R}$; then $|w|=|q|$. The matrix is nonzero iff $(z,w)\ne(0,0)$ iff $q\ne0$ (if $q=0$ then $z=0$ and $|w|=0$).

**Step 3: structure.** So
$$\mathcal N^*\cong\{(q,w):q\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},\ |w|=|q|\}.$$
The map $(q,w)\mapsto\bigl(q,\ w/|w|\bigr)\in(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})\times S^1$ is continuous with continuous inverse $(q,u)\mapsto(q,|q|u)$. Thus
$$\mathcal N^*\cong(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})\times S^1=\bigl((-\infty,0)\times S^1\bigr)\sqcup\bigl((0,\infty)\times S^1\bigr).$$

**Step 4: components.** Each piece is a product of connected spaces, hence connected. The sign of $q=\operatorname{Im}z$ is a continuous function on $\mathcal N^*$ taking the two values $\pm$, so the two pieces are open and closed in $\mathcal N^*$. Hence they are exactly the connected components.

**Check.** $E=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ has $a=d=c=0$, $b=1$, so $z=-\frac i2$, $w=\frac i2$: $q=-\frac12<0$ and $|w|=\frac12=|q|$ âœ“. The matrix $E^T=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ has $z=\frac i2$, $w=\frac i2$: $q=+\frac12$, on the other component.

**Remark.** Including $0$, the set $\{A^2=0\}=\{|w|=|\operatorname{Im}z|,\ \operatorname{Re}z=0\}$ is a double cone over a circle in a 3-dimensional subspace; it is connected (through its vertex $0$), and removing the vertex disconnects it. For $n\ge3$ the situation is different: the nonzero nilpotent cone of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is connected (it is star-shaped about $0$ and $0$ can be approached through other orbits).

**Lesson.** A cone minus its vertex can be disconnected; a continuous sign function (here $\operatorname{sign}\operatorname{Im}z$) is the tool to prove disconnection.
@@@ mnappb2-the-convex-hull-of-so2-is-a-flat-disc.md
---
title: The convex hull of SO(2) is a flat disc
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: M2(R), convex hull, rotations, complex coordinates
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Identify $\mathbb{R}^2$ with $\mathbb{C}$ and write $A\in M_2(\mathbb{R})$ as $Ax=zx+w\bar x$, where $z=\frac{(a+d)+i(c-b)}2$ and $w=\frac{(a-d)+i(b+c)}2$ for $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$ ($A\mapsto(z,w)$ is a linear bijection onto $\mathbb{C}^2$). Let $R_\alpha=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\alpha&-\sin\alpha\\\sin\alpha&\cos\alpha\end{pmatrix}$ and $SO(2)=\{R_\alpha\}$. Show that
$$\operatorname{conv}SO(2)=\{A:\ w=0,\ |z|\le1\},$$
a closed disc (of dimension $2$) in the $4$-dimensional space $M_2(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

The rotation $R_\alpha$ is multiplication by $e^{i\alpha}$ on $\mathbb{C}$, so it has $(z,w)=(e^{i\alpha},0)$. A linear bijection preserves convex hulls. Then write any $z$ with $|z|\le1$ as a convex combination of two opposite points of the unit circle.

## Solution

**Idea.** Under the linear isomorphism $A\mapsto(z,w)$, $SO(2)$ becomes the unit circle $\{(e^{i\alpha},0)\}$ in the plane $\{w=0\}$, and the convex hull of a circle is the closed disc.

**Step 1: $SO(2)$ in coordinates.** $R_\alpha x=e^{i\alpha}x$ for $x\in\mathbb{C}$. From the entries: $a=d=\cos\alpha$, $c=\sin\alpha$, $b=-\sin\alpha$, so $z=\frac{2\cos\alpha+2i\sin\alpha}2=e^{i\alpha}$ and $w=0$. Hence
$$SO(2)\longleftrightarrow\{(e^{i\alpha},0):\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\}.$$

**Step 2: linear bijections preserve convex hulls.** Let $T:M_2(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{C}^2$ be $A\mapsto(z,w)$. $T$ is linear and bijective, so it maps convex combinations to convex combinations, and $T(\operatorname{conv}X)=\operatorname{conv}T(X)$. So we must show $\operatorname{conv}\{(e^{i\alpha},0)\}=\{(z,0):|z|\le1\}$.

**Step 3: $\subseteq$.** The set $D=\{(z,0):|z|\le1\}$ is convex (the closed unit disc is convex, by the triangle inequality $|\lambda z_1+(1-\lambda)z_2|\le\lambda|z_1|+(1-\lambda)|z_2|\le1$) and contains the circle. So $\operatorname{conv}\{(e^{i\alpha},0)\}\subseteq D$.

**Step 4: $\supseteq$.** Let $|z|\le1$ and write $z=re^{i\varphi}$ with $r=|z|\in[0,1]$. Then
$$z=\frac{1+r}2\,e^{i\varphi}+\frac{1-r}2\,\bigl(-e^{i\varphi}\bigr).$$
Indeed $\frac{1+r}2-\frac{1-r}2=r$. The two weights are $\ge0$ and sum to $1$, and $e^{i\varphi}$, $-e^{i\varphi}=e^{i(\varphi+\pi)}$ lie on the unit circle. So $(z,0)$ is a convex combination of two points of the circle.

**Back in matrices.** In real terms: $\operatorname{conv}SO(2)=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}p&-q\\q&p\end{pmatrix}:p^2+q^2\le1\right\}$, and the matrix with $(p,q)=r(\cos\varphi,\sin\varphi)$ equals $\frac{1+r}2R_\varphi+\frac{1-r}2R_{\varphi+\pi}$.

**Remark.** This hull is only $2$-dimensional. In contrast, $\operatorname{conv}O(2)$ is the whole operator-norm ball $\{\|A\|\le1\}=\{|z|+|w|\le1\}$, which is $4$-dimensional: the improper rotations $\{z=0,|w|=1\}$ are needed to fill it. In particular the $2\times2$ matrices of operator norm $\le1$ cannot be written as convex combinations of rotations alone.

**Lesson.** Convex hulls are invariant under linear isomorphisms, so choose coordinates in which the set is a familiar one (here a circle).
@@@ mnappb2-the-operator-norm-distance-between-two-rotations.md
---
title: The operator-norm distance between two rotations is a chord length
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: M2(R), rotations, operator norm, chord length
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Let $R_\alpha=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\alpha&-\sin\alpha\\\sin\alpha&\cos\alpha\end{pmatrix}$ and let $\|\cdot\|$ be the operator norm on $M_2(\mathbb{R})$ (induced by the Euclidean norm). Show that
$$\|R_\alpha-R_\beta\|=2\left|\sin\frac{\alpha-\beta}2\right|\qquad(\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}).$$
Deduce the diameter of $SO(2)$ in this norm, and the corresponding distance in the Frobenius norm.

## Hint

Factor $R_\alpha-R_\beta=R_\beta\,(R_{\alpha-\beta}-I)$ and recall that multiplying by an orthogonal matrix does not change the operator norm. Then compute $R_\gamma-I$ on a unit vector, thinking of $\mathbb{R}^2$ as $\mathbb{C}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Rotations are multiplications by $e^{i\alpha}$ in $\mathbb{C}$, so the distance between two rotations is the distance between two points of the unit circle.

**Step 1: reduce to $R_\gamma-I$.** Since $R_\alpha=R_\beta R_{\alpha-\beta}$ (composition of rotations adds angles),
$$R_\alpha-R_\beta=R_\beta R_{\alpha-\beta}-R_\beta=R_\beta\,(R_{\alpha-\beta}-I).$$
For an orthogonal $U$ and any $B$ we have $\|UB\|=\|B\|$ because $|UBx|=|Bx|$ for all $x$. Hence $\|R_\alpha-R_\beta\|=\|R_\gamma-I\|$ with $\gamma=\alpha-\beta$.

**Step 2: compute $\|R_\gamma-I\|$.** Identify $\mathbb{R}^2$ with $\mathbb{C}$, so that $R_\gamma x=e^{i\gamma}x$ and $(R_\gamma-I)x=(e^{i\gamma}-1)x$. Then $|(R_\gamma-I)x|=|e^{i\gamma}-1|\,|x|$ for every $x$: the matrix scales all vectors by the same factor, so its operator norm equals this factor.

**Step 3: the modulus.** Using $e^{i\gamma}-1=e^{i\gamma/2}\bigl(e^{i\gamma/2}-e^{-i\gamma/2}\bigr)=e^{i\gamma/2}\cdot2i\sin\frac\gamma2$,
$$|e^{i\gamma}-1|=2\left|\sin\frac\gamma2\right|.$$
(Alternatively, $|e^{i\gamma}-1|^2=(\cos\gamma-1)^2+\sin^2\gamma=2-2\cos\gamma=4\sin^2\frac\gamma2$.) This proves the formula.

**Consequences.** The maximum of $2|\sin\frac\gamma2|$ is $2$ (for $\gamma=\pi$): the diameter of $SO(2)$ in the operator norm is $2$, attained by $I$ and $-I=R_\pi$. In the Frobenius norm, $R_\gamma-I=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\gamma-1&-\sin\gamma\\\sin\gamma&\cos\gamma-1\end{pmatrix}$ has $\|\cdot\|_F^2=2\bigl((\cos\gamma-1)^2+\sin^2\gamma\bigr)=2\cdot4\sin^2\frac\gamma2$, so $\|R_\alpha-R_\beta\|_F=2\sqrt2\left|\sin\frac{\alpha-\beta}2\right|$, i.e. $\sqrt2$ times the operator-norm distance.
