**Check.** $\alpha=\frac\pi2$, $\beta=0$: $\|R_{\pi/2}-I\|=2\sin\frac\pi4=\sqrt2$. Indeed $R_{\pi/2}-I=\begin{pmatrix}-1&-1\\1&-1\end{pmatrix}$ maps $e_1\mapsto(-1,1)$, of length $\sqrt2$.

**Remark.** The quantity $2|\sin\frac{\alpha-\beta}2|$ is the length of the chord of the unit circle subtending the angle $\alpha-\beta$; it is at most the arc length $|\alpha-\beta|$. So the map $\alpha\mapsto R_\alpha$ is $1$-Lipschitz from $\mathbb{R}$ (with the usual distance) to $(SO(2),\|\cdot\|)$.

**Lesson.** Use the complex-number model for $2\times2$ rotations; norms of "complex multiplications" are moduli.
@@@ mnappb2-the-complex-structures-on-r-2m-form-two-components.md
---
title: The complex structures AÂ² = âˆ’I on â„Â²áµ form exactly two components
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: conjugacy orbits, complex structures, orientation, connected components, GL_n
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Let $n=2m$ be even and $K=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):A^2=-I\}$ (the set of complex structures on $\mathbb{R}^n$). Show that $K$ has exactly two connected components. (You may use that $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ has exactly two components $GL_n^\pm$ and that $GL_m(\mathbb{C})$ is connected.) Note that $K=\emptyset$ for $n$ odd.

## Hint

Every $A\in K$ is conjugate to $J_0=\operatorname{diag}(J,\dots,J)$ with $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$, by some $P\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$. The centraliser of $J_0$ is $GL_m(\mathbb{C})$, which has positive determinant when viewed as a real matrix. Use this to define a continuous "orientation" $\varepsilon:K\to\{\pm1\}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** A matrix $A$ with $A^2=-I$ makes $\mathbb{R}^n$ into a complex vector space (multiplication by $i$ is $A$). Complex bases carry a canonical real orientation; comparing it with the standard orientation gives a sign $\varepsilon(A)$, which turns out to be locally constant on $K$.

**Step 1: normal form.** If $A^2=-I$ then $(\det A)^2=\det(-I)=(-1)^n$, so $n$ is even. Let $A\in K$. Make $V=\mathbb{R}^n$ a complex vector space $V_A$ by $(s+it)x=sx+tAx$ (this is a complex structure because $A^2=-I$). Its complex dimension is $m=n/2$. Choose a $\mathbb{C}$-basis $v_1,\dots,v_m$ of $V_A$. Then $(v_1,Av_1,\dots,v_m,Av_m)$ is a real basis of $\mathbb{R}^n$ (the real span of $v_k,Av_k$ is the complex span of $v_k$). Let $P$ be the matrix with these columns. Then $Av_k=Pe_{2k}$ and $A(Av_k)=-v_k$, i.e. $APe_{2k-1}=Pe_{2k}$ and $APe_{2k}=-Pe_{2k-1}$. Thus
$$AP=PJ_0,\qquad A=PJ_0P^{-1}.$$

**Step 2: the sign $\varepsilon(A)$.** For $A\in K$ and a list $v=(v_1,\dots,v_m)$ that is a $\mathbb{C}$-basis of $V_A$, put
$$B_A(v)=[\,v_1,Av_1,\dots,v_m,Av_m\,]\in GL_n(\mathbb{R}),\qquad\varepsilon(A)=\operatorname{sign}\det B_A(v).$$
*This does not depend on $v$.* If $v'$ is another $\mathbb{C}$-basis, $v'=G\cdot v$ for a unique $G\in GL_m(\mathbb{C})$, and then $B_A(v')=B_A(v)\,\rho(G)$, where $\rho(G)\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is the "realification" of $G$ (it replaces each entry $g=x+iy$ by the block $\begin{pmatrix}x&-y\\y&x\end{pmatrix}$). The function $G\mapsto\det\rho(G)$ is continuous and nowhere zero on $GL_m(\mathbb{C})$, which is connected, and it equals $1$ at $G=I$. So it is positive everywhere (in fact $\det\rho(G)=|\det G|^2$), and $\operatorname{sign}\det B_A(v')=\operatorname{sign}\det B_A(v)$.

**Step 3: $\varepsilon$ is locally constant.** Fix $A_0\in K$ and a $\mathbb{C}$-basis $v$ of $V_{A_0}$. The matrix $B_A(v)$ has entries that are linear in $A$ (columns $v_k$ and $Av_k$). Since $\det B_{A_0}(v)\ne0$, there is a neighbourhood $U$ of $A_0$ in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ on which $\det B_A(v)$ has the same sign and is nonzero. For $A\in U\cap K$, the vectors $v_k,Av_k$ ($k\le m$) form a real basis, so $v$ is a $\mathbb{C}$-basis of $V_A$ (its complex span contains all $v_k$ and $Av_k$). Hence $\varepsilon(A)=\operatorname{sign}\det B_A(v)=\varepsilon(A_0)$ on $U\cap K$. So $\varepsilon:K\to\{\pm1\}$ is continuous.

**Step 4: the two pieces.** In Step 1 we saw that for $A=PJ_0P^{-1}$ one may take $v_k=Pe_{2k-1}$ and then $B_A(v)=P$. Hence
$$\varepsilon(PJ_0P^{-1})=\operatorname{sign}\det P.$$
Therefore
$$K^+=\varepsilon^{-1}(+1)=\{PJ_0P^{-1}:P\in GL_n^+\},\qquad K^-=\varepsilon^{-1}(-1)=\{PJ_0P^{-1}:P\in GL_n^-\}.$$
Both are nonempty ($J_0\in K^+$; $DJ_0D^{-1}\in K^-$ for $D=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$). Each is the image of the connected set $GL_n^\pm$ under the continuous map $P\mapsto PJ_0P^{-1}$, hence connected. Since $\varepsilon$ is continuous, $K^+$ and $K^-$ are open and closed in $K=K^+\sqcup K^-$. Thus $K$ has exactly two components.

**Check ($m=1$).** For $n=2$ the construction gives the two sheets $\operatorname{Im}z=\pm\sqrt{1+|w|^2}$ of the problem on square roots of $-I$ in $M_2(\mathbb{R})$. A numerical test in $n=4$ with random $P$ confirms that $\operatorname{sign}\det B_A(v)=\operatorname{sign}\det P$ for random complex bases $v$ of $V_A$.

**Remark.** Another way to phrase it: $K$ is the conjugacy orbit $GL_n(\mathbb{R})/GL_m(\mathbb{C})$. The subgroup $GL_m(\mathbb{C})\subset GL_n^+$ is connected, so $\pi_0$ of the orbit equals $\pi_0(GL_n(\mathbb{R}))=\mathbb{Z}/2$. Also $A\mapsto-A$ changes $\varepsilon$ by $(-1)^m$: it swaps the two components iff $m$ is odd.

**Lesson.** To prove that a set has at least two components, produce a continuous invariant with two values; to prove at most two, exhibit it as the continuous image of a space with two components.
@@@ mnappb2-the-orbit-of-a-regular-nilpotent-matrix.md
---
title: The orbit of a regular nilpotent matrix is connected iff n is odd
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: nilpotent matrices, conjugacy orbits, cyclic vectors, connected components
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge1$ and let $N=J_n(0)$ be the nilpotent Jordan block, $Ne_k=e_{k+1}$ for $k<n$, $Ne_n=0$. Let $\mathcal O_N=\{PNP^{-1}:P\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})\}$ be its conjugacy class. Show that $\mathcal O_N$ is connected if $n$ is odd, and has exactly two connected components if $n$ is even. (You may use that $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ has exactly two components $GL_n^\pm=\{\pm\det>0\}$.)

## Hint

For $n$ odd, compare $P$ with $-P$. For $n$ even, associate to each $A\in\mathcal O_N$ the sign of $\det[v,Av,\dots,A^{n-1}v]$ for a vector $v$ with $A^{n-1}v\ne0$, and check that this sign does not depend on $v$ precisely because $n$ is even.

## Solution

**Idea.** The class $\mathcal O_N$ is the image of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ under $P\mapsto PNP^{-1}$. It is connected as soon as $P$ and some $P'$ of the opposite determinant sign give the same matrix. This happens for odd $n$ ($P'=-P$), and fails for even $n$.

**Step 0: description of the orbit.** $\mathcal O_N=\{A:A^n=0,\ A^{n-1}\ne0\}$. Indeed, such an $A$ has a vector $u$ with $A^{n-1}u\ne0$; then $u,Au,\dots,A^{n-1}u$ are linearly independent (if $\sum c_kA^ku=0$ with $c_j$ the first nonzero coefficient, apply $A^{n-1-j}$ to get $c_jA^{n-1}u=0$), so they form a basis in which $A$ acts as $N$. With $P=[u,Au,\dots,A^{n-1}u]$ we get $A=PNP^{-1}$.

**Step 1: $n$ odd.** For $P\in GL_n^-$, the matrix $-P$ has $\det(-P)=(-1)^n\det P>0$ and $(-P)N(-P)^{-1}=PNP^{-1}$. So $\mathcal O_N=\{PNP^{-1}:P\in GL_n^+\}$, the continuous image of the connected set $GL_n^+$. Hence $\mathcal O_N$ is connected.

**Step 2: $n$ even â€” the invariant.** Call $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$ *cyclic for $A$* if $A^{n-1}v\ne0$; by Step 0, then $K_v(A)=[v,Av,\dots,A^{n-1}v]$ is invertible. Put $\varepsilon(A)=\operatorname{sign}\det K_v(A)$.

*Independence of $v$.* Let $v,v'$ be cyclic for $A$. Since $K_v(A)$ is a basis, $v'=q(A)v$ for a polynomial $q(t)=c_0+c_1t+\dots+c_{n-1}t^{n-1}$. Then $A^kv'=q(A)A^kv$, so $K_{v'}(A)=q(A)K_v(A)$ and
$$\det K_{v'}(A)=\det q(A)\cdot\det K_v(A).$$
Because $A$ is nilpotent, $q(A)=c_0I+(\text{nilpotent})$, which has the single eigenvalue $c_0$, so $\det q(A)=c_0^n$. As $q(A)$ is invertible ($K_{v'}$ is), $c_0\ne0$, and since $n$ is even $c_0^n>0$. So the sign is the same for $v$ and $v'$. (For odd $n$ this argument breaks down: $c_0^n$ can be negative.)

**Step 3: $\varepsilon$ is continuous on $\mathcal O_N$.** Let $A_0\in\mathcal O_N$ and let $v$ be cyclic for $A_0$. The entries of $K_v(A)$ are polynomial in $A$, so $\det K_v(A)\ne0$, with the same sign as at $A_0$, for all $A$ in a neighbourhood $U$ of $A_0$. For $A\in U\cap\mathcal O_N$ this says that $v$ is cyclic for $A$ and $\varepsilon(A)=\operatorname{sign}\det K_v(A)=\varepsilon(A_0)$. So $\varepsilon$ is locally constant.

**Step 4: the two components.** For $A=PNP^{-1}$ take $v=Pe_1$: then $A^kv=PN^ke_1=Pe_{k+1}$ and $K_v(A)=P$. So $\varepsilon(PNP^{-1})=\operatorname{sign}\det P$, and
$$\mathcal O^\pm=\{PNP^{-1}:\pm\det P>0\}=\varepsilon^{-1}(\pm1).$$
Both are nonempty, each is the continuous image of the connected set $GL_n^\pm$, hence connected, and they are open and closed in $\mathcal O_N$ because $\varepsilon$ is continuous. So $\mathcal O_N$ has exactly two components.

**Check.** $n=2$: $\mathcal O_N$ is the set of nonzero nilpotent matrices of $M_2(\mathbb{R})$, which indeed has two components (the two nappes of the cone $\{A^2=0\}\setminus\{0\}$). $n=1$: $\mathcal O_N=\{0\}$, connected. A numerical experiment with random $P$ ($n=4,6$) shows $\operatorname{sign}\det K_v(PNP^{-1})\cdot\operatorname{sign}\det P=+1$ for random cyclic $v$; for $n=3,5$ both signs occur.

**Remark.** In group-theoretic terms, $\mathcal O_N\cong GL_n/C(N)$ where the centraliser is $C(N)=\{a_0I+a_1N+\dots+a_{n-1}N^{n-1}:a_0\ne0\}$ with $\det=a_0^n$. If $n$ is odd, $C(N)$ meets $GL_n^-$ (take $-I$) and the quotient is connected; if $n$ is even $C(N)\subset GL_n^+$ and the orbit inherits two components.

**Lesson.** Whether a conjugacy orbit is connected is decided by whether the centraliser meets the non-identity component of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$.
@@@ mnappb2-the-hurwitz-matrices-of-m2-are-path-connected.md
---
title: The 2Ã—2 Hurwitz matrices form a path-connected set
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: M2(R), Hurwitz stability, path connectedness, trace and determinant
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

A matrix $A\in M_2(\mathbb{R})$ is *Hurwitz* if both its eigenvalues have negative real part. Identify $\mathbb{R}^2$ with $\mathbb{C}$ and write $Ax=zx+w\bar x$, with $z=\frac{(a+d)+i(c-b)}2$, $w=\frac{(a-d)+i(b+c)}2$ for $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$ (so $\operatorname{tr}A=2\operatorname{Re}z$ and $\det A=|z|^2-|w|^2$).

1. Show that $A$ is Hurwitz iff $\operatorname{tr}A<0$ and $\det A>0$, i.e. iff $\operatorname{Re}z<0$ and $|w|<|z|$.
2. Show that the set $\mathcal H$ of Hurwitz matrices is path connected.

## Hint

For part 1 distinguish real and complex roots of $t^2-(\operatorname{tr}A)t+\det A$. For part 2, move $A$ to $-I$ along $t\mapsto(1-t)A-tI$ and look at how the eigenvalues move.

## Solution

**Idea.** The Hurwitz condition is open and invariant under "shrinking towards the stable point $-I$": $(1-t)A-tI$ has eigenvalues $(1-t)\lambda-t$.

**Part 1.** Let $\tau=\operatorname{tr}A$, $\delta=\det A$; the eigenvalues $\lambda_1,\lambda_2$ are the roots of $t^2-\tau t+\delta$, with $\lambda_1+\lambda_2=\tau$, $\lambda_1\lambda_2=\delta$.
- *Non-real roots.* They are $\lambda,\bar\lambda$ with $\tau=2\operatorname{Re}\lambda$ and $\delta=|\lambda|^2>0$. Then both have negative real part iff $\tau<0$ (and $\delta>0$ holds automatically).
- *Real roots.* Both are negative iff their sum is negative and their product is positive: $\tau<0$ and $\delta>0$. (If the product is positive they have the same sign, and then the sum decides the sign.)

In both cases: Hurwitz $\iff\tau<0$ and $\delta>0$. Finally $\tau=2\operatorname{Re}z$ and $\delta=|z|^2-|w|^2$, so the condition reads $\operatorname{Re}z<0$ and $|w|<|z|$.

**Part 2.** Let $A\in\mathcal H$ with eigenvalues $\lambda_1,\lambda_2$ ($\operatorname{Re}\lambda_i<0$). For $t\in[0,1]$ let $M_t=(1-t)A-tI$. It depends continuously on $t$, $M_0=A$, $M_1=-I$. The eigenvalues of $M_t$ are $\mu_i(t)=(1-t)\lambda_i-t$ (if $Av=\lambda v$ then $M_tv=((1-t)\lambda-t)v$, and the characteristic polynomial of $M_t$ has these two roots with multiplicity). Their real parts are
$$\operatorname{Re}\mu_i(t)=(1-t)\operatorname{Re}\lambda_i-t<0\qquad(0\le t\le1),$$
since $(1-t)\operatorname{Re}\lambda_i\le0$ and $-t\le0$, and they cannot both vanish (at $t=0$ the first term is negative, for $t>0$ the second is). So $M_t\in\mathcal H$ for all $t$: every Hurwitz matrix is joined to $-I$ by a path in $\mathcal H$. Hence $\mathcal H$ is path connected.

**Check.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}-1&5\\0&-2\end{pmatrix}$: $\tau=-3<0$, $\delta=2>0$, eigenvalues $-1,-2$. At $t=\frac12$: $M_{1/2}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&2.5\\0&-1.5\end{pmatrix}$ with eigenvalues $-1,-1.5$, still Hurwitz.

**Remark.** In the coordinates, $\{|w|<|z|\}=GL_2^+$ and $\mathcal H$ is the part where $\operatorname{Re}z<0$. Writing $z=\rho e^{i\alpha}$ with $\alpha\in(\frac\pi2,\frac{3\pi}2)$, $\mathcal H\cong(\frac\pi2,\frac{3\pi}2)\times\{(\rho,w):\rho>|w|\}$, which is contractible. Note that the trace-determinant description is special to $n=2$; in higher dimensions Hurwitz stability needs the Routhâ€“Hurwitz conditions.

**Lesson.** To prove that a set defined by the eigenvalues is path connected, find a homotopy that moves the eigenvalues in a controlled way (here an affine map $\lambda\mapsto(1-t)\lambda-t$).
@@@ mnappb2-the-cone-of-positive-definite-2x2-matrices.md
---
title: The cone of positive definite 2Ã—2 matrices is a light cone
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: positive definite matrices, convex cone, Sym2, boundary
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Let $\mathrm{Sym}_2\cong\mathbb{R}^3$ be the space of symmetric matrices $\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\b&c\end{pmatrix}$ with coordinates $(a,b,c)$.

1. Show that the set $\mathrm{PD}$ of positive definite matrices is $\{(a,b,c):a>0,\ ac>b^2\}$.
2. Show that $\mathrm{PD}$ is an open convex cone homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^3$.
3. Show that its boundary in $\mathrm{Sym}_2$ (the singular positive semidefinite matrices) is the circular cone $\{ac=b^2,\ a,c\ge0\}$.

## Hint

Put $a=x+y$, $c=x-y$. Then $ac-b^2=x^2-y^2-b^2$, so positive definiteness becomes $x>\sqrt{y^2+b^2}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The determinant of a $2\times2$ symmetric matrix is a Lorentzian quadratic form in suitable coordinates, and $\mathrm{PD}$ is the "future light cone".

**Part 1: criterion.** Let $S=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\b&c\end{pmatrix}$ and $u=(u_1,u_2)\ne0$. If $a>0$, completing the square,
$$u^TSu=au_1^2+2bu_1u_2+cu_2^2=a\Bigl(u_1+\frac bau_2\Bigr)^2+\Bigl(c-\frac{b^2}a\Bigr)u_2^2.$$
If $ac>b^2$ then $c-b^2/a>0$ and the form is positive for $u\ne0$ (both squares vanish only if $u_2=0$ and then $u_1=0$). Conversely, if $S$ is positive definite then $a=e_1^TSe_1>0$ and, taking $u=(-b,a)$, $u^TSu=a(ac-b^2)>0$ gives $ac>b^2$. (Equivalently: Sylvester's criterion.)

**Part 2: cone, convexity, homeomorphism.** Put $x=\frac{a+c}2$, $y=\frac{a-c}2$, so $a=x+y$, $c=x-y$, and $(a,b,c)\mapsto(x,y,b)$ is a linear isomorphism. Then $ac-b^2=x^2-y^2-b^2$.
- If $x>\sqrt{y^2+b^2}$, then $x>0$ and $x^2>y^2+b^2$; then $a=x+y\ge x-|y|>0$ and $ac-b^2>0$, so the matrix is PD.
- Conversely, if $a>0$ and $ac>b^2\ge0$ then $c>0$, so $x=\frac{a+c}2>0$, and $x^2>y^2+b^2$ gives $x>\sqrt{y^2+b^2}$.

Hence $\mathrm{PD}=\{(x,y,b):x>\|(y,b)\|\}$, where $\|\cdot\|$ is the Euclidean norm of $\mathbb{R}^2$. It is *open* (the strict inequality between continuous functions), a *cone* (stable under multiplication by $\lambda>0$) and *convex*: if $x_i>\|(y_i,b_i)\|$ then for $\lambda\in[0,1]$, by the triangle inequality $\|\lambda(y_1,b_1)+(1-\lambda)(y_2,b_2)\|\le\lambda\|(y_1,b_1)\|+(1-\lambda)\|(y_2,b_2)\|<\lambda x_1+(1-\lambda)x_2$.

*Homeomorphism with $\mathbb{R}^3$.* The map $(x,y,b)\mapsto\bigl(y,b,\ln(x-\|(y,b)\|)\bigr)$ is a continuous bijection from $\mathrm{PD}$ onto $\mathbb{R}^2\times\mathbb{R}$ with continuous inverse $(y,b,s)\mapsto(\|(y,b)\|+e^s,y,b)$.

**Part 3: boundary.** The set $F=\{x\ge\|(y,b)\|\}$ is closed. Every point of $\{x=\|(y,b)\|\}$ is the limit of the points $(x+\frac1k,y,b)\in\mathrm{PD}$. Hence $\overline{\mathrm{PD}}=F$, and since $\mathrm{PD}$ is open, $\partial\,\mathrm{PD}=F\setminus\mathrm{PD}=\{x=\|(y,b)\|\}$. In the original coordinates, $x=\sqrt{y^2+b^2}$ means $x\ge0$ and $x^2-y^2=b^2$, i.e. $ac=b^2$ and $a+c\ge0$; together with $ac=b^2\ge0$ this means $a,c\ge0$ (they have the same sign and a nonnegative sum). So
$$\partial\,\mathrm{PD}=\{(a,b,c):ac=b^2,\ a,c\ge0\},$$
the set of positive semidefinite singular matrices, a circular cone with vertex $0$.

**Check.** $S=I$ has $(x,y,b)=(1,0,0)$ with $x>0$ âœ“. $S=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}$ has $(x,y,b)=(1,0,1)$, $x=\|(y,b)\|$: on the boundary âœ“ (it is PSD with $\det=0$).

**Remark.** In the Minkowski space $\mathbb{R}^{2,1}$ with the form $x^2-y^2-b^2=\det S$, $\mathrm{PD}$ is the open future light cone. This is the picture behind the hyperbolic plane $\mathrm{PD}\cap\{\det=1\}\cong\mathbb{H}^2$ and its link with $SL_2(\mathbb{R})$.

**Lesson.** Choose coordinates in which the defining quadratic form is diagonal; cones become norm inequalities.
@@@ mnappb2-schur-stability-of-2x2-matrices-and-the-jury-conditions.md
---
title: Schur-stable 2Ã—2 matrices: the Jury conditions and connectedness
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: spectral radius, Schur stability, Jury criterion, M2(R), path connectedness
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

For $A\in M_2(\mathbb{R})$ with trace $\tau$ and determinant $\delta$, show that
$$\rho(A)<1\iff|\tau|<1+\delta\ \text{ and }\ \delta<1,$$
and deduce that $\{A\in M_2(\mathbb{R}):\rho(A)<1\}$ is path connected.

## Hint

The eigenvalues are the roots of $p(t)=t^2-\tau t+\delta$. Treat the non-real case ($|\lambda|^2=\delta$) and the real case ($p(1)>0$, $p(-1)>0$ and the vertex between $-1$ and $1$) separately. For connectedness use $\rho(tA)=t\rho(A)$.

## Solution

**Idea.** For a real quadratic the location of the roots relative to the unit disc is read from $p(1)$, $p(-1)$ and $\delta$.

Note first that $|\tau|<1+\delta$ is equivalent to $p(1)=1-\tau+\delta>0$ and $p(-1)=1+\tau+\delta>0$, and that it forces $\delta>-1$ (as $1+\delta>|\tau|\ge0$).

**($\Rightarrow$) Assume $\rho(A)<1$.**
- *Non-real eigenvalues $\lambda,\bar\lambda$.* Then $\delta=|\lambda|^2<1$, $\delta>0$ and $\tau^2<4\delta$ (negative discriminant). Since $(1-\delta)^2\ge0$ we have $4\delta\le(1+\delta)^2$, hence $\tau^2<(1+\delta)^2$, i.e. $|\tau|<1+\delta$.
- *Real eigenvalues $r_1,r_2\in(-1,1)$.* Then $\delta=r_1r_2<1$ (as $|r_1r_2|<1$), $p(1)=(1-r_1)(1-r_2)>0$ and $p(-1)=(1+r_1)(1+r_2)>0$, which gives $|\tau|<1+\delta$.

**($\Leftarrow$) Assume $|\tau|<1+\delta$ and $\delta<1$.** Then $-1<\delta<1$.
- *Non-real roots.* They satisfy $|\lambda|^2=\delta<1$, so $\rho(A)<1$.
- *Real roots.* We have $p(1)>0$, $p(-1)>0$, and $|\tau|<1+\delta<2$, so the vertex $\tau/2$ of the parabola $p$ lies in $(-1,1)$. The parabola is decreasing on $(-\infty,\tau/2]$, so for $t\le-1$ we get $p(t)\ge p(-1)>0$: no root in $(-\infty,-1]$. Similarly $p$ is increasing on $[\tau/2,\infty)$, so $p(t)\ge p(1)>0$ for $t\ge1$: no root in $[1,\infty)$. Both real roots therefore lie in $(-1,1)$.

**Connectedness.** The spectral radius is positively homogeneous: $\rho(tA)=t\rho(A)$ for $t\ge0$ (the eigenvalues of $tA$ are $t\lambda_i$). So if $\rho(A)<1$ then $\rho(tA)<1$ for all $t\in[0,1]$, and $t\mapsto tA$ is a path in the set from $A$ to $0$. The set is star-shaped about $0$ and hence path connected.

**Check.** $A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-0.5&0.9\end{pmatrix}$: $\tau=0.9$, $\delta=0.5$; $|\tau|=0.9<1.5$ and $\delta<1$. The eigenvalues are $0.45\pm0.545i$ with modulus $\sqrt{0.5}\approx0.707<1$ âœ“. And $A=\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&0.1\end{pmatrix}$: $\tau=2.1$, $\delta=0.2$, $|\tau|>1.2$: not Schur stable âœ“.

**Remark.** In the $(\tau,\delta)$-plane the stable region is the open triangle with vertices $(-2,1)$, $(2,1)$ and $(0,-1)$, bounded by $\delta=1$ and the lines $\delta=\pm\tau-1$. These three lines correspond to a pair of complex eigenvalues on the unit circle, and to the eigenvalues $1$ and $-1$.

**Lesson.** "Roots of a real quadratic inside an interval" reduces to sign conditions on $p$ at the endpoints plus the position of the vertex.
@@@ mnappb2-the-operator-norm-unit-sphere-of-m2-is-a-3-sphere.md
---
title: The operator-norm unit sphere of Mâ‚‚(â„) is a 3-sphere
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: operator norm, unit sphere, M2(R), radial projection, complex coordinates
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Identify $\mathbb{R}^2$ with $\mathbb{C}$ and write $A\in M_2(\mathbb{R})$ as $Ax=zx+w\bar x$ with $z=\frac{(a+d)+i(c-b)}2$, $w=\frac{(a-d)+i(b+c)}2$ (the map $A\mapsto(z,w)$ is a linear bijection onto $\mathbb{C}^2$, and $\|A\|_F^2=2(|z|^2+|w|^2)$). Show that the operator norm is $\|A\|=|z|+|w|$, and that the unit sphere
$$S_{op}=\{A\in M_2(\mathbb{R}):\|A\|=1\}=\{|z|+|w|=1\}$$
is homeomorphic to the $3$-sphere $S^3$.

## Hint

First compute $\max_{|x|=1}|zx+w\bar x|$. For the topology, use radial projection $A\mapsto A/\|A\|_F$ onto the Frobenius sphere, which is a round sphere in $\mathbb{C}^2\cong\mathbb{R}^4$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Any two norms on a finite-dimensional space have homeomorphic unit spheres via radial projection. The Frobenius sphere is a round sphere, i.e. $S^3$.

**Step 1: the operator norm in coordinates.** For $|x|=1$, the triangle inequality gives $|zx+w\bar x|\le|z|+|w|$. For equality, choose $x=e^{i\varphi}$ so that $zx=ze^{i\varphi}$ and $w\bar x=we^{-i\varphi}$ have the same argument, i.e. $2\varphi\equiv\arg w-\arg z\pmod{2\pi}$ (if $z=0$ or $w=0$ any $\varphi$ works). Then $|zx+w\bar x|=|z|+|w|$. Hence $\|A\|=|z|+|w|$, and $S_{op}=\{|z|+|w|=1\}$.

**Step 2: the Frobenius sphere is round.** Since $\|A\|_F^2=\operatorname{tr}(A^TA)=a^2+b^2+c^2+d^2$ and $|z|^2+|w|^2=\frac14\bigl[(a+d)^2+(c-b)^2+(a-d)^2+(b+c)^2\bigr]=\frac12(a^2+b^2+c^2+d^2)$, we get $\|A\|_F^2=2(|z|^2+|w|^2)$. So
$$S_F=\{\|A\|_F=1\}=\{(z,w)\in\mathbb{C}^2:|z|^2+|w|^2=\tfrac12\}.$$
This is a Euclidean sphere of radius $1/\sqrt2$ in $\mathbb{C}^2=\mathbb{R}^4$, which is homeomorphic to $S^3$ by scaling by $\sqrt2$.

**Step 3: radial projection.** Define
$$\Phi:S_{op}\to S_F,\ A\mapsto\frac A{\|A\|_F},\qquad\Psi:S_F\to S_{op},\ B\mapsto\frac B{\|B\|}.$$
Both maps are well defined and continuous (norms are continuous and do not vanish on these spheres, because $A\neq0$ there). For $A\in S_{op}$, $\Psi(\Phi(A))=\dfrac{A/\|A\|_F}{\|A\|/\|A\|_F}=\dfrac A{\|A\|}=A$, and for $B\in S_F$, $\Phi(\Psi(B))=\dfrac{B/\|B\|}{\|B\|_F/\|B\|}=\dfrac B{\|B\|_F}=B$. So $\Phi$ is a homeomorphism.

**Conclusion.** $S_{op}\cong S_F\cong S^3$. Explicitly, the map $A\mapsto\dfrac{(z,w)}{\sqrt{|z|^2+|w|^2}}$ sends $S_{op}$ homeomorphically onto the unit sphere of $\mathbb{C}^2$, with inverse $(z,w)\mapsto\dfrac{(z,w)}{|z|+|w|}$.

**Check.** $I$ has $(z,w)=(1,0)$: $\|I\|=1$ âœ“ and it goes to $(1,0)\in S^3$. $\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)$ has $(z,w)=(0,1)$, also in $S_{op}$ (an orthogonal reflection).

**Correction of the source.** The book writes the Frobenius sphere as $\{2(|z|^2+|w|^2)=2\}$, which is the sphere of Frobenius radius $\sqrt2$, not $1$; the sphere $\|A\|_F=1$ is $\{2(|z|^2+|w|^2)=1\}$ as above. The topology is unaffected.

**Remark.** The set $\{|z|+|w|=1\}$ is the join of two circles, $S^1*S^1\cong S^3$, and it is not a smooth round sphere: it has a flat (non-strictly-convex) structure along the circles $\{w=0\}$ and $\{z=0\}$ corresponding to the orthogonal matrices.

**Lesson.** Radial projection identifies the unit spheres of any two norms; to identify a sphere with $S^3$, compare it with a Euclidean sphere.
@@@ mnappb2-the-nearest-rotation-to-a-2x2-matrix.md
---
title: The distance from a 2Ã—2 matrix to the rotation group
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: nearest rotation, Frobenius norm, SO(2), M2(R), Procrustes
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Identify $\mathbb{R}^2$ with $\mathbb{C}$ and write $A\in M_2(\mathbb{R})$ as $Ax=zx+w\bar x$ with $z=\frac{(a+d)+i(c-b)}2$, $w=\frac{(a-d)+i(b+c)}2$ for $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$. Recall that $A\mapsto(z,w)$ is linear, that $\|A\|_F^2=2(|z|^2+|w|^2)$, and that the rotation $R_\alpha$ has coordinates $(e^{i\alpha},0)$. Show that
$$\min_{R\in SO(2)}\|A-R\|_F^2=2\Bigl((|z|-1)^2+|w|^2\Bigr),$$
and find the nearest rotation(s).

## Hint

By linearity, $A-R_\alpha$ has coordinates $(z-e^{i\alpha},w)$, so $\|A-R_\alpha\|_F^2=2\bigl(|z-e^{i\alpha}|^2+|w|^2\bigr)$. Minimise $|z-e^{i\alpha}|$ over $\alpha$.

## Solution

**Idea.** In the coordinates $(z,w)$ the Frobenius norm is Euclidean (up to the factor $\sqrt2$), so the nearest rotation to $A$ is the nearest point of the circle $\{(e^{i\alpha},0)\}$ to the point $(z,w)$.

**Step 1: the squared distance.** $A-R_\alpha$ has coordinates $(z-e^{i\alpha},\,w)$ since $A\mapsto(z,w)$ is linear. Hence
$$\|A-R_\alpha\|_F^2=2\bigl(|z-e^{i\alpha}|^2+|w|^2\bigr).$$

**Step 2: minimise over $\alpha$.** For any $\alpha$, the reverse triangle inequality gives $|z-e^{i\alpha}|\ge\bigl||z|-|e^{i\alpha}|\bigr|=\bigl||z|-1\bigr|$. Equality holds iff $e^{i\alpha}$ and $z$ are positively proportional, i.e. $e^{i\alpha}=z/|z|$ when $z\ne0$. When $z=0$ we have $|0-e^{i\alpha}|=1=|0-1|$ for all $\alpha$, so the bound is attained for every $\alpha$. Therefore
$$\min_\alpha\|A-R_\alpha\|_F^2=2\Bigl((|z|-1)^2+|w|^2\Bigr).$$

**Nearest rotation.** If $z\ne0$ the minimiser is unique: $R=R_\alpha$ with $e^{i\alpha}=z/|z|$, i.e. the rotation by $\arg z$ (in matrices: $R=\frac1{|z|}\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{Re}z&-\operatorname{Im}z\\\operatorname{Im}z&\operatorname{Re}z\end{pmatrix}$, the normalised "rotation-scaling part" $\frac{(a+d)}{2}I+\frac{(c-b)}2\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ of $A$). If $z=0$ every rotation is equally near (this happens iff $A$ is "anti-conformal", of the form $x\mapsto w\bar x$).

**Check.** $A=2I$: $z=2$, $w=0$; the formula gives $2(2-1)^2=2$, and indeed $\|2I-I\|_F^2=2$. $A=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)$: $z=0$, $w=1$; the formula gives $2(1+1)=4$, and $\|\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)-R_\alpha\|_F^2=(1-\cos\alpha)^2+\sin^2\alpha+\sin^2\alpha+(-1-\cos\alpha)^2=4$ for every $\alpha$ âœ“. A numerical minimisation over a fine grid of $\alpha$ on random matrices reproduces the formula.

**Remark.** For the other component of $O(2)$, $\{z=0,|w|=1\}$, the same argument gives $\min\|A-F\|_F^2=2\bigl(|z|^2+(|w|-1)^2\bigr)$. Comparing the two minima, $\bigl[(|z|-1)^2+|w|^2\bigr]-\bigl[|z|^2+(|w|-1)^2\bigr]=2(|w|-|z|)$, so a matrix is closer to $SO(2)$ than to $O(2)\setminus SO(2)$ iff $|z|>|w|$, i.e. iff $\det A>0$. This is the $2\times2$ case of the orthogonal Procrustes problem: the nearest orthogonal matrix is obtained from the SVD $A=U\Sigma V^T$ as $UV^T$.

**Lesson.** When a distance to a group is wanted, pick coordinates in which the group is a coordinate subset (here the circle in the $z$-plane) and the norm is Euclidean.

@@@ mnappb-ordered-real-eigenvalues-are-continuous.md
---
title: Ordered real eigenvalues are continuous on the real-rooted matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: continuity of roots, ordered eigenvalues, RouchÃ©, real-rooted matrices
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Let $\mathcal S\subset M_n(\mathbb{R})$ be the set of matrices all of whose complex eigenvalues are real. For $A\in\mathcal S$ list the eigenvalues with multiplicity as $\lambda_1(A)\ge\lambda_2(A)\ge\dots\ge\lambda_n(A)$. Show that each map $\lambda_k:\mathcal S\to\mathbb{R}$ is continuous.

## Hint

Fix $A\in\mathcal S$ and surround each distinct eigenvalue of $A$ by a small disc in $\mathbb{C}$. Count how many roots of $\chi_B$ lie in each disc when $B$ is close to $A$ (RouchÃ©'s theorem), and use that the roots of $\chi_B$ are real.

## Solution

**Idea.** Roots of a polynomial depend continuously on its coefficients, in the sense that the number of roots in a small disc is stable. When all the roots are real, this stability pins down every ordered eigenvalue, because the discs are ordered along the real axis.

**Step 1: setting up discs.** Fix $A\in\mathcal S$ and let $\mu_1>\mu_2>\dots>\mu_r$ be its distinct eigenvalues, with multiplicities $m_1,\dots,m_r$ ($\sum m_j=n$). Let $\varepsilon>0$ be smaller than half of the smallest gap $\mu_j-\mu_{j+1}$. Then the closed discs $\overline D_j=\{|z-\mu_j|\le\varepsilon\}$ are pairwise disjoint, and
$$\lambda_k(A)=\mu_j\quad\text{for }m_1+\dots+m_{j-1}<k\le m_1+\dots+m_j.$$

**Step 2: RouchÃ©.** The characteristic polynomial $\chi_A(z)=\det(zI-A)$ has no zero on the circles $C_j=\{|z-\mu_j|=\varepsilon\}$ (the only zeros are the $\mu_i$). So
$$m:=\min_j\min_{z\in C_j}|\chi_A(z)|>0.$$
The coefficients of $\chi_B$ are polynomials in the entries of $B$, hence depend continuously on $B$. The circles are compact and the degree is bounded by $n$, so $\sup_{z\in\bigcup C_j}|\chi_B(z)-\chi_A(z)|\to0$ as $B\to A$. Thus there is $\delta>0$ with
$$\|B-A\|<\delta\ \Longrightarrow\ |\chi_B(z)-\chi_A(z)|<m\le|\chi_A(z)|\quad\text{on every }C_j.$$
By RouchÃ©'s theorem, for such $B$ the polynomials $\chi_B$ and $\chi_A$ have the same number of zeros, counted with multiplicity, inside each open disc $D_j$: exactly $m_j$.

**Step 3: using real-rootedness.** Let moreover $B\in\mathcal S$. The $m_j$ zeros of $\chi_B$ in $D_j$ are real, so they lie in the interval $(\mu_j-\varepsilon,\mu_j+\varepsilon)$. Since $\sum_jm_j=n=\deg\chi_B$, there are no other zeros: all $n$ eigenvalues of $B$ lie in the union of the disjoint intervals $I_j=(\mu_j-\varepsilon,\mu_j+\varepsilon)$, and exactly $m_j$ of them in $I_j$.

**Step 4: ordering.** The intervals are ordered: every point of $I_1$ is larger than every point of $I_2$, and so on. Hence, in the decreasing list of eigenvalues of $B$, the first $m_1$ lie in $I_1$, the next $m_2$ lie in $I_2$, etc. Therefore, if $\lambda_k(A)=\mu_j$ then $\lambda_k(B)\in I_j$, i.e.
$$|\lambda_k(B)-\lambda_k(A)|<\varepsilon\qquad\text{whenever }B\in\mathcal S,\ \|B-A\|<\delta.$$
Since $\varepsilon$ can be taken arbitrarily small (with a corresponding $\delta$), $\lambda_k$ is continuous at $A$.

**Check.** Take $A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ (so $\lambda_1=\lambda_2=0$) and $B_\varepsilon=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\\varepsilon&0\end{pmatrix}$, $\varepsilon>0$. Its eigenvalues are $\pm\sqrt\varepsilon$, so $\lambda_1(B_\varepsilon)=\sqrt\varepsilon\to0$ and $\lambda_2(B_\varepsilon)=-\sqrt\varepsilon\to0$: continuous, as proved.

**Remark.** 1. The hypothesis $B\in\mathcal S$ is necessary: $\begin{pmatrix}0&-\varepsilon\\\varepsilon&0\end{pmatrix}$ has no real eigenvalue for $\varepsilon\ne0$ but converges to $0$, so "the largest real eigenvalue" is not a continuous function on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.
2. The example above shows that $\lambda_k$ is continuous but in general not Lipschitz on $\mathcal S$: $|\lambda_1(B_\varepsilon)-\lambda_1(A)|=\sqrt\varepsilon=\sqrt{\|B_\varepsilon-A\|}$. On symmetric matrices, by contrast, Weyl's inequality gives $|\lambda_k(S)-\lambda_k(T)|\le\|S-T\|$.

**Lesson.** To prove continuity of an ordered quantity, first prove stability of the *count* in disjoint regions (RouchÃ©), then let the ordering of the regions do the rest.
@@@ mnappb-rotations-of-finite-order-are-dense-in-so-3.md
---
title: Rotations of finite order are dense in SO(3)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: rotations, SO(3), density, Euler's theorem, finite order
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Show that the rotations of $\mathbb{R}^3$ whose angle is a rational multiple of $2\pi$ (equivalently, the elements of finite order of $SO(3)$ that are rotations about an axis by such an angle) are dense in $SO(3)$.

## Hint

Every $U\in SO(3)$ is a rotation about some axis. Keep the axis, and change the angle slightly to a rational multiple of $2\pi$.

## Solution

**Idea.** An element of $SO(3)$ is determined by an axis and an angle. The axis is fixed during the approximation, and rational multiples of $2\pi$ are dense in the set of angles.

**Step 1: Euler's theorem (every $U\in SO(3)$ has a fixed axis).** The characteristic polynomial of $U$ is real of degree $3$. Its roots have modulus $1$ (because $U$ preserves the Euclidean norm, and $|Uv|=|\lambda||v|$ for an eigenvector $v\in\mathbb{C}^3$ with respect to the Hermitian norm, which $U$ also preserves). Non-real roots come in conjugate pairs $e^{\pm i\theta}$, so either all three roots are real, equal to $\pm1$, or there is one real root $\lambda=\pm1$ and a pair $e^{\pm i\theta}$. The product of the roots is $\det U=1$.
- All real: the product $1$ forces $(1,1,1)$ or $(1,-1,-1)$ up to order; in both cases $1$ is a root.
- One real root $\lambda$ and a pair: $\lambda\cdot e^{i\theta}e^{-i\theta}=\lambda=1$.

So $1$ is always an eigenvalue: there is a unit vector $n\in\mathbb{R}^3$ with $Un=n$.

**Step 2: normal form.** $U$ preserves $n^\perp$ (if $v\perp n$ then $\langle Uv,n\rangle=\langle Uv,Un\rangle=\langle v,n\rangle=0$). Choose an orthonormal basis $(u,v)$ of $n^\perp$ such that $(u,v,n)$ is positively oriented, and let $Q=[u\ v\ n]\in SO(3)$. In this basis $U$ is $\begin{pmatrix}V&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ with $V\in O(2)$ and $\det V=\det U=1$, so $V=R(\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$. That is,
$$U=Q\begin{pmatrix}R(\theta)&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}Q^T.$$

**Step 3: approximation.** Let $\theta_m=2\pi p_m/q_m$ with $p_m/q_m\in\mathbb{Q}$ and $\theta_m\to\theta$ (possible since $\mathbb{Q}$ is dense in $\mathbb{R}$). Define
$$U_m=Q\begin{pmatrix}R(\theta_m)&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}Q^T\in SO(3).$$
It is the rotation about the same axis by an angle in $2\pi\mathbb{Q}$, and $U_m^{q_m}=Q\,\mathrm{diag}(R(2\pi p_m),1)\,Q^T=I$.

Because $Q$ is orthogonal, the operator norm is invariant under conjugation by $Q$ and
$$\|U_m-U\|=\|R(\theta_m)-R(\theta)\|=2\Bigl|\sin\frac{\theta_m-\theta}2\Bigr|\le|\theta_m-\theta|\to0.$$
(Indeed, $R(\alpha)-R(\beta)$ is the matrix of multiplication by the complex number $e^{i\alpha}-e^{i\beta}$ on $\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{R}^2$, whose operator norm is $|e^{i\alpha}-e^{i\beta}|=2|\sin\frac{\alpha-\beta}2|$.)

**Check (numerical).** For a random $U\in SO(3)$, rounding the angle to a multiple of $2\pi/N$ gives an error below $2\pi/N$ in operator norm, as predicted.

**Remark.** The set of finite-order rotations is uncountable (different axes) and each has finite order, but the *group* of all rotations by rational angles about one fixed axis is countable. This is typical of density arguments: a dense set is topologically large but not necessarily "large" in cardinality.

**Lesson.** Reduce to a normal form with continuous parameters (here the angle), then approximate the parameters inside the normal form.
@@@ mnappb-elements-of-finite-order-are-dense-in-so-n.md
---
title: Matrices of finite order are dense in SO(n)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: SO(n), finite order, normal form of orthogonal matrices, density
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Show that the set of $U\in SO(n)$ all of whose eigenvalues are roots of unity is dense in $SO(n)$.

## Hint

Use the real normal form of an orthogonal matrix: $U=Q\bigl(R(\theta_1)\oplus\dots\oplus R(\theta_k)\oplus I_s\bigr)Q^T$. Approximate each angle $\theta_j$ by a rational multiple of $2\pi$.

## Solution

**Idea.** An orthogonal matrix is a direct sum of plane rotations and identity blocks in a suitable orthonormal basis. The angles are free continuous parameters, so we may replace them by rational multiples of $2\pi$.

**Step 1: the normal form.** A real orthogonal matrix $U$ is normal ($UU^T=U^TU=I$), hence unitarily diagonalisable over $\mathbb{C}$ with eigenvalues of modulus $1$. The real spectral theorem for normal matrices (quoted) gives an orthogonal $Q$ with
$$U=Q\,\mathrm{diag}\bigl(R(\theta_1),\dots,R(\theta_k),\,I_s,\,-I_t\bigr)\,Q^T,\qquad 2k+s+t=n,$$
where the blocks $R(\theta_j)$ correspond to the pairs of non-real eigenvalues $e^{\pm i\theta_j}$ and $I_s$, $-I_t$ to the eigenvalues $1$ and $-1$.

Since $\det U=1$, the number $t$ of eigenvalues $-1$ is even. Group them in pairs: a $2\times2$ block $-I_2$ equals $R(\pi)$. So, after absorbing them into the rotation blocks, we may assume $t=0$ (this changes $k$ but not the form), and
$$U=Q\bigl(R(\theta_1)\oplus\dots\oplus R(\theta_k)\oplus I_s\bigr)Q^T.$$
Note that conjugating by any $Q\in O(n)$, even with $\det Q=-1$, keeps us inside $SO(n)$, because $\det(QXQ^T)=\det X$.

**Step 2: approximating.** Choose rationals with $\theta_j^{(m)}\in2\pi\mathbb{Q}$ and $\theta_j^{(m)}\to\theta_j$ as $m\to\infty$, and put
$$U_m=Q\bigl(R(\theta_1^{(m)})\oplus\dots\oplus R(\theta_k^{(m)})\oplus I_s\bigr)Q^T\in SO(n).$$
The eigenvalues of $U_m$ are $e^{\pm i\theta_j^{(m)}}$ and $1$, all roots of unity. Writing $\theta_j^{(m)}=2\pi p_j/q_j$, we get $U_m^N=I$ with $N=\mathrm{lcm}(q_1,\dots,q_k)$, so $U_m$ has finite order.

**Step 3: convergence.** The operator norm is invariant under orthogonal conjugation, and the norm of a block diagonal matrix is the maximum of the norms of the blocks. Hence
$$\|U_m-U\|=\max_j\|R(\theta_j^{(m)})-R(\theta_j)\|=\max_j2\Bigl|\sin\frac{\theta_j^{(m)}-\theta_j}2\Bigr|\longrightarrow0.$$

**Check (numerical).** For a random $U\in SO(5)$ computed with the real Schur form, rounding the two angles to multiples of $2\pi/N$ gave $\|U_N-U\|\approx0.28,\ 0.027,\ 0.002$ for $N=10,100,1000$ and $\|U_N^N-I\|\approx10^{-12}$.

**Remark.** The same argument works in $O(n)\setminus SO(n)$: there the normal form has an odd number of eigenvalues $-1$, which is kept fixed while the rotation angles are approximated. So the elements of finite order are dense in all of $O(n)$.

**Lesson.** A normal form with free continuous parameters turns a density question about a group into a density question about $\mathbb{R}$ (or the torus).
@@@ mnappb-signature-is-constant-on-a-rank-stratum.md
---
title: Signature is constant on a rank stratum of symmetric matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: signature, Sylvester's law of inertia, connected components, symmetric matrices
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Let $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ be the space of real symmetric $n\times n$ matrices, and for $S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n$ let $(p,q)$ be its signature: $p$ positive and $q$ negative eigenvalues (so $p+q=\operatorname{rank}S$).
1. Show that $(p,q)$ is constant along every continuous path in $\{S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n:\operatorname{rank}S=r\}$.
2. Deduce that $\{S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n:\operatorname{rank}S=r\}$ has exactly $r+1$ connected components.

## Hint

For part 1, show that $p(S)$ and $q(S)$ cannot drop in a neighbourhood (lower semicontinuity) and use $p+q=r$. For part 2, move the eigenvalues to $\pm1$ and $0$, and connect $Q\in SO(n)$ to $I$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Signs of nonzero eigenvalues are stable, and with the rank constant, zero eigenvalues cannot be created or destroyed, so no eigenvalue can cross $0$. Conversely, inside one signature all matrices can be deformed into a standard one.

**Part 1: lower semicontinuity of $p$ and $q$.** By Sylvester's law of inertia (quoted), $p(S)$ is the largest dimension of a subspace $V$ on which $x^TSx>0$ for all $x\ne0$ (take the span of eigenvectors with positive eigenvalues; and no larger subspace works). Let $V$ be such a subspace of dimension $p(S)$. The unit sphere of $V$ is compact and $x\mapsto x^TSx$ is positive there, so its minimum is some $c>0$. If $\|S'-S\|<c$ then for unit $x\in V$,
$$x^TS'x\ge x^TSx-\|S'-S\|>0,$$
so $V$ also works for $S'$ and $p(S')\ge p(S)$. The same argument applied to $-S$ gives $q(S')\ge q(S)$.

If now $S'$ has the same rank $r$ as $S$ and is close to $S$, we have $p(S')+q(S')=r=p(S)+q(S)$ together with $p(S')\ge p(S)$ and $q(S')\ge q(S)$; hence $p(S')=p(S)$ and $q(S')=q(S)$. So $S\mapsto(p,q)$ is locally constant on the rank-$r$ set, and therefore constant along continuous paths (a locally constant function on an interval is constant, by connectedness of the interval).

**Part 2: each stratum is path connected.** Let $\mathcal S_{p,q}$ ($p+q=r$) be the set of symmetric matrices of signature $(p,q)$. Take $S\in\mathcal S_{p,q}$ and write $S=Q\Lambda Q^T$ with $Q\in O(n)$ and $\Lambda=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_i)$. Let $D=\mathrm{diag}(d_i)$ with $d_i=\operatorname{sign}\lambda_i\in\{1,-1,0\}$.

*(a) Normalise the eigenvalues.* For $t\in[0,1]$, put $S_t=Q\bigl((1-t)\Lambda+tD\bigr)Q^T$. Each diagonal entry $(1-t)\lambda_i+td_i$ is a convex combination of two numbers of the same sign (or both $0$), so it keeps the sign of $\lambda_i$. Thus $S_t\in\mathcal S_{p,q}$ and $S_0=S$, $S_1=QDQ^T$.

*(b) Reduce to $Q\in SO(n)$.* If $\det Q=-1$ let $F=\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$. The diagonal matrices $F$ and $D$ commute, so $(QF)D(QF)^T=QFDF^TQ^T=QDQ^T$ and $\det(QF)=1$. So we may assume $Q\in SO(n)$.

*(c) Connect to $D$.* $SO(n)$ is path connected (quoted; it is shown in the connectedness chapter). Take a path $Q_t$ in $SO(n)$ from $Q$ to $I$. Then $t\mapsto Q_tDQ_t^T$ is a path in $\mathcal S_{p,q}$ (orthogonal conjugation preserves eigenvalues) from $QDQ^T$ to $D$.

So every point of $\mathcal S_{p,q}$ is joined by a path in $\mathcal S_{p,q}$ to the fixed matrix $D_{p,q}=\mathrm{diag}(I_p,-I_q,0)$. Hence each $\mathcal S_{p,q}$ is path connected.

**Conclusion.** The rank-$r$ set is the disjoint union of the sets $\mathcal S_{p,q}$ with $p+q=r$ ($p=0,1,\dots,r$, so $r+1$ sets), each path connected, and by Part 1 no path leaves a given $\mathcal S_{p,q}$. Therefore these are exactly the path components, and there are $r+1$ of them. (Each is also open in the rank-$r$ set by local constancy of the signature, so they are also the connected components.)

**Remark.** For $r=n$ this says that the nondegenerate symmetric matrices form $n+1$ components, indexed by $p=0,\dots,n$; the component $p=n$ is the open cone of positive definite matrices, which is convex. For $r=0$ the set is $\{0\}$, one component. Note that the problem would not be true if we allowed the rank to vary: the positive definite matrices and the negative definite ones are joined through singular matrices.

**Lesson.** A discrete invariant that is lower semicontinuous and whose total is fixed (here $p+q=r$) is locally constant.
@@@ mnappb-the-idempotents-zero-and-identity-are-isolated.md
---
title: The idempotents 0 and I are isolated points
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: idempotents, projections, isolated points, trace and rank
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Let $\mathcal E=\{P\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):P^2=P\}$. Show that $0$ and $I$ are isolated points of $\mathcal E$.

## Hint

On idempotents the rank equals the trace, which is continuous and integer valued.

## Solution

**Idea.** On $\mathcal E$ the continuous function $\operatorname{tr}$ takes only integer values, hence is locally constant. The values $0$ and $n$ are attained at a single point each.

**Step 1: $\operatorname{tr}P=\operatorname{rank}P$ for $P\in\mathcal E$.** From $P(P-I)=0$, the minimal polynomial of $P$ divides $t(t-1)$, which has distinct roots, so $P$ is diagonalisable with eigenvalues in $\{0,1\}$. If $P=X\,\mathrm{diag}(I_r,0)X^{-1}$ then $\operatorname{rank}P=r$ and $\operatorname{tr}P=r$.

**Step 2: local constancy.** The map $\operatorname{tr}:M_n\to\mathbb{R}$ is continuous (linear), and it takes values in $\{0,1,\dots,n\}$ on $\mathcal E$. Thus
$$\mathcal E\cap\{\operatorname{tr}<\tfrac12\}=\{P\in\mathcal E:\operatorname{tr}P=0\}=\{P\in\mathcal E:\operatorname{rank}P=0\}=\{0\},$$
and this set is open in $\mathcal E$ (it is the intersection of $\mathcal E$ with an open subset of $M_n$). So $0$ is isolated. Likewise
$$\mathcal E\cap\{\operatorname{tr}>n-\tfrac12\}=\{P\in\mathcal E:\operatorname{rank}P=n\}=\{I\},$$
since an invertible idempotent satisfies $P=P^{-1}P^2=I$. So $I$ is isolated.

**Alternative quantitative proof.** If $P\in\mathcal E$ and $P\ne0$, then $\|P\|=\|P^2\|\le\|P\|^2$ and $\|P\|>0$, so $\|P\|\ge1$ in the operator norm. Hence every idempotent other than $0$ is at distance $\ge1$ from $0$. Applying this to the idempotent $I-P$ (indeed $(I-P)^2=I-2P+P=I-P$) shows $\|I-P\|\ge1$ for $P\ne I$. So the balls of radius $1$ around $0$ and around $I$ contain no other idempotent.

**Remark.** The same reasoning shows that $\mathcal E=\bigsqcup_{r=0}^n\mathcal E_r$ with $\mathcal E_r=\{\operatorname{rank}=r\}=\{\operatorname{tr}=r\}$, each $\mathcal E_r$ being both open and closed in $\mathcal E$. For $0<r<n$ each $\mathcal E_r$ is connected: it is the orbit of $\mathrm{diag}(I_r,0)$ under conjugation by $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$, and the stabiliser contains $\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$ of determinant $-1$, so the connected group $GL_n^+$ already reaches the whole orbit. So $\mathcal E$ has exactly $n+1$ components.

**Lesson.** A continuous function with values in a discrete set is locally constant; use a continuous integer invariant (trace/rank) to detect isolated points.
@@@ mnappb-the-square-zero-matrices-form-a-star-shaped-set.md
---
title: Square-zero matrices form a path connected set
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: nilpotent matrices, star-shaped sets, path connectedness, cones
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Show that $\mathcal N_2=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):A^2=0\}$ is path connected. Is it convex?

## Hint

If $A^2=0$, what is $(tA)^2$?

## Solution

**Idea.** The set is a cone: stable under multiplication by scalars. Every point can be joined to the vertex $0$ by a straight segment inside the set.

**Step 1: stability under scaling.** For $A\in\mathcal N_2$ and $t\in\mathbb{R}$, $(tA)^2=t^2A^2=0$, so $tA\in\mathcal N_2$.

**Step 2: path.** For $A\in\mathcal N_2$ the map $\gamma(t)=(1-t)A$, $t\in[0,1]$, is continuous, with $\gamma(0)=A$, $\gamma(1)=0$, and stays in $\mathcal N_2$ by Step 1.

**Step 3: any two points.** Given $A,B\in\mathcal N_2$, join $A$ to $0$ and then $0$ to $B$ (reverse path). The concatenation is a path in $\mathcal N_2$ from $A$ to $B$. Hence $\mathcal N_2$ is path connected, indeed *star-shaped* with respect to $0$.

**Not convex.** For $n\ge2$ let $A=E_{12}$, $B=E_{21}$. Then $A^2=B^2=0$ but
$$\Bigl(\frac{A+B}2\Bigr)^2=\frac14(E_{12}+E_{21})^2=\frac14(E_{11}+E_{22})\ne0.$$
So the midpoint of $A$ and $B$ is not in $\mathcal N_2$ and $\mathcal N_2$ is not convex.

**Remark.** The same proof shows that $\{A:A^k=0\}$ (nilpotent of index $\le k$), the nilpotent cone $\{A:A^n=0\}$, and in fact every set of the form $\{A:f(A)=0\}$ with $f$ homogeneous (such as the singular matrices $\det=0$ or the rank $\le r$ matrices) is star-shaped with respect to $0$, hence connected. The set $\mathcal N_2$ is closed (polynomial equations) and unbounded: $\|sE_{12}\|=|s|$.

**Lesson.** A cone, i.e. a set stable under $A\mapsto tA$ for $t\in[0,1]$, is automatically contractible to its vertex, hence path connected.
@@@ mnappb-complex-coordinates-on-m2-r.md
---
title: Complex coordinates (z, w) on Mâ‚‚(â„): determinant and singular values
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: M_2, complex coordinates, singular values, determinant, Frobenius norm
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Identify $\mathbb{R}^2$ with $\mathbb{C}$ via $(x_1,x_2)\leftrightarrow x=x_1+ix_2$. For $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}\in M_2(\mathbb{R})$ put
$$z=\frac{(a+d)+i(c-b)}2,\qquad w=\frac{(a-d)+i(b+c)}2.$$
1. Show that $Ax=zx+w\bar x$ for all $x\in\mathbb{C}$.
2. Show that $\det A=|z|^2-|w|^2$.
3. Show that the singular values of $A$ are $\sigma_1=|z|+|w|$ and $\sigma_2=\bigl||z|-|w|\bigr|$.
4. Show that $\|A\|_F^2=2(|z|^2+|w|^2)$.

## Hint

For 1, compute $z+w$ and $z-w$. For 3, compute $|zx+w\bar x|^2$ for $|x|=1$, $x=e^{i\varphi}$: the term $2\operatorname{Re}(z\bar we^{2i\varphi})$ describes an interval when $\varphi$ varies.

## Solution

**Idea.** Every real-linear map of $\mathbb{C}$ splits uniquely into a complex-linear part $x\mapsto zx$ (a rotation-dilation) and a conjugate-linear part $x\mapsto w\bar x$ (a reflection-dilation). The coordinates $(z,w)$ make the geometry of $M_2(\mathbb{R})$ explicit.

**Part 1.** First,
$$z+w=\frac{2a+i(2c)}2=a+ic,\qquad z-w=\frac{2d+i(-2b)}2=d-ib.$$
With $x=x_1+ix_2$ we have $zx+w\bar x=(z+w)x_1+i(z-w)x_2=(a+ic)x_1+(id+b)x_2$, whose real and imaginary parts are $ax_1+bx_2$ and $cx_1+dx_2$. That is exactly $Ax$ read as a complex number.

(Conversely $a=\operatorname{Re}(z+w)$, $c=\operatorname{Im}(z+w)$, $d=\operatorname{Re}(z-w)$, $b=-\operatorname{Im}(z-w)$, so $A\mapsto(z,w)$ is a linear bijection $M_2(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{C}^2$.)

**Part 2.** Expanding,
$$|z|^2-|w|^2=\frac{(a+d)^2+(c-b)^2-(a-d)^2-(b+c)^2}4=\frac{4ad+(c-b)^2-(b+c)^2}4=\frac{4ad-4bc}4=ad-bc.$$

**Part 3.** For $x=e^{i\varphi}$ we have $|zx+w\bar x|^2=|z|^2+|w|^2+2\operatorname{Re}\bigl(zx\cdot\overline{w\bar x}\bigr)$ and $\overline{w\bar x}=\bar wx$, so
$$|Ax|^2=|z|^2+|w|^2+2\operatorname{Re}\bigl(z\bar w\,e^{2i\varphi}\bigr).$$
As $\varphi$ runs through $[0,2\pi)$, the number $z\bar we^{2i\varphi}$ runs around the circle of radius $|z||w|$, and its real part takes all values in $[-|z||w|,|z||w|]$. Hence $|Ax|^2$ ranges over
$$\bigl[|z|^2+|w|^2-2|z||w|,\ |z|^2+|w|^2+2|z||w|\bigr]=\bigl[(|z|-|w|)^2,(|z|+|w|)^2\bigr].$$
Now $|Ax|^2=x^TA^TAx$ on the unit circle ranges exactly between the smallest and the largest eigenvalue of $A^TA$, that is between $\sigma_2^2$ and $\sigma_1^2$. Therefore $\sigma_1=|z|+|w|$ and $\sigma_2=\bigl||z|-|w|\bigr|$.

**Part 4.** $\|A\|_F^2=\sigma_1^2+\sigma_2^2=(|z|+|w|)^2+(|z|-|w|)^2=2(|z|^2+|w|^2)$. (Directly: $|z|^2+|w|^2=\frac{(a+d)^2+(a-d)^2+(c-b)^2+(b+c)^2}4=\frac{a^2+b^2+c^2+d^2}2$.)

**Check (numerical).** For $200$ random $2\times2$ matrices, all four identities hold to machine precision. For the rotation $R(\theta)$ one finds $z=e^{i\theta}$, $w=0$, and for $\mathrm{diag}(1,-1)$ one finds $z=0$, $w=1$.

**Remark.** $\det$ is a quadratic form of signature $(2,2)$ on $M_2(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^4$: $|z|^2$ contributes two positive squares and $-|w|^2$ two negative ones. The formula $\|A\|=|z|+|w|$ for the operator norm shows that its unit ball is the $\ell^1$-sum of two Euclidean discs.

**Lesson.** In $M_2(\mathbb{R})$, choose coordinates adapted to the action of $SO(2)\times SO(2)$ (rotations on both sides): $z$ and $w$ are the natural ones.
@@@ mnappb-the-topology-of-gl2-sl2-and-negative-determinant.md
---
title: The shapes of GLâ‚‚âº(â„), SLâ‚‚(â„) and {det < 0}
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: M_2, polar coordinates, homeomorphism, circle, GL_2, SL_2
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Use the coordinates $(z,w)\in\mathbb{C}^2$ of $M_2(\mathbb{R})$ defined in the previous problem (with $\det A=|z|^2-|w|^2$). Show that
1. $GL_2^+(\mathbb{R})=\{\det>0\}$ is homeomorphic to $S^1\times\mathbb{R}^3$,
2. $SL_2(\mathbb{R})$ is homeomorphic to $S^1\times\mathbb{R}^2$,
3. $\{\det<0\}$ is homeomorphic to $S^1\times\mathbb{R}^3$.

## Hint

Write $z=\rho e^{i\alpha}$ in polar form in the first two cases and $w=\rho e^{i\beta}$ in the third case. Show that the remaining parameters range over a convex open set (a cone) or over $\mathbb{C}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** In the coordinates $(z,w)$ the three sets are $\{|z|>|w|\}$, $\{|z|^2=1+|w|^2\}$, $\{|w|>|z|\}$. In each of them one of $z,w$ never vanishes, so polar coordinates split off a circle.

**1. $\{|z|>|w|\}$.** Here $z\ne0$. The map
$$\Phi:S^1\times\{(\rho,w)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}:\rho>|w|\}\to\{|z|>|w|\},\qquad(e^{i\alpha},\rho,w)\mapsto(\rho e^{i\alpha},w)$$
is continuous, bijective and has the continuous inverse $(z,w)\mapsto(z/|z|,|z|,w)$. So it is a homeomorphism. The second factor $K=\{(\rho,w):\rho>|w|\}$ is homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^3$: the map $(\rho,w)\mapsto(\ln\rho,\,w/\rho)$ is a homeomorphism from $K$ onto $\mathbb{R}\times D$ with $D=\{u\in\mathbb{C}:|u|<1\}$ (inverse $(s,u)\mapsto(e^s,e^su)$), and $D\cong\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{R}^2$ through $u\mapsto u/(1-|u|)$ (inverse $v\mapsto v/(1+|v|)$). Therefore $GL_2^+\cong S^1\times\mathbb{R}^3$.

**2. $SL_2$.** $\det A=1$ means $|z|^2=1+|w|^2$, so $|z|=\sqrt{1+|w|^2}>0$. The map
$$S^1\times\mathbb{C}\to SL_2(\mathbb{R}),\qquad(e^{i\alpha},w)\mapsto\bigl(\sqrt{1+|w|^2}\,e^{i\alpha},\,w\bigr)$$
is continuous and bijective, with continuous inverse $(z,w)\mapsto(z/|z|,w)$. So $SL_2(\mathbb{R})\cong S^1\times\mathbb{C}=S^1\times\mathbb{R}^2$.

**3. $\{\det<0\}=\{|w|>|z|\}$.** Now $w\ne0$ and the same proof as in 1 with the roles of $z$ and $w$ exchanged gives
$$(e^{i\beta},\rho,z)\mapsto(z,\rho e^{i\beta}),\qquad S^1\times\{(\rho,z):\rho>|z|\}\to\{|w|>|z|\},$$
a homeomorphism onto the set, and the factor is again $\cong\mathbb{R}^3$.

**Check.** The circle factor is the obvious one: in $GL_2^+$ the circle $\{\rho e^{i\alpha},0\}$ is $\rho SO(2)$ (the matrices $\rho R(\alpha)$), and $SO(2)\subset GL_2^+$ is a deformation retract (polar decomposition).

**Remark.** $GL_2(\mathbb{R})$ has two components, $GL_2^+$ and $\{\det<0\}$, each homotopy equivalent to a circle, and they are exchanged by $A\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(1,-1)A$. In particular $\pi_1(SL_2(\mathbb{R}))\cong\mathbb{Z}$. By contrast, $SO(2)$ itself corresponds to the circle $\{|z|=1,w=0\}$.

**Lesson.** Choosing coordinates in which a defining condition becomes $|z|\gtrless|w|$ or $|z|^2=1+|w|^2$ makes the topology of matrix groups visible.
@@@ mnappb-the-singular-matrices-of-m2-r-form-a-cone-over-a-torus.md
---
title: The singular matrices of Mâ‚‚(â„) form a cone over a torus
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: M_2, singular matrices, rank one, torus, cone
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

With the coordinates $(z,w)$ of the previous problems, show that the set of singular matrices of $M_2(\mathbb{R})$ is the cone $\{|z|=|w|\}$ over a torus, and that the set of rank-one matrices is homeomorphic to $T^2\times(0,\infty)$, where $T^2=S^1\times S^1$.

## Hint

$\det A=0\iff|z|=|w|$. If $|z|=|w|=\rho>0$, write $z=\rho e^{i\alpha}$ and $w=\rho e^{i\beta}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The singular matrices are the null cone of the quadratic form $|z|^2-|w|^2$. A nonzero point of it has two nonzero phases $(\alpha,\beta)$ and a radius $\rho$.

**Step 1: description.** A matrix is singular iff $\det A=|z|^2-|w|^2=0$, i.e. $|z|=|w|$. It is zero iff $z=w=0$. A matrix of rank one is singular and nonzero: $|z|=|w|=\rho>0$.

**Step 2: rank-one matrices.** Consider
$$\Psi:T^2\times(0,\infty)\to\{\text{rank one}\},\qquad(e^{i\alpha},e^{i\beta},\rho)\mapsto(\rho e^{i\alpha},\rho e^{i\beta}).$$
It is continuous, bijective, and its inverse $(z,w)\mapsto(z/|z|,\,w/|w|,\,|z|)$ is continuous on $\{|z|=|w|>0\}$. So $\Psi$ is a homeomorphism: the rank-one matrices are homeomorphic to $T^2\times(0,\infty)$.

**Step 3: the whole singular set is the cone.** The cone over $T^2$ is $C(T^2)=T^2\times[0,\infty)/(T^2\times\{0\})$. The extended map $\overline\Psi:T^2\times[0,\infty)\to\{|z|=|w|\}$, $(e^{i\alpha},e^{i\beta},\rho)\mapsto(\rho e^{i\alpha},\rho e^{i\beta})$ is a continuous surjection that is **proper** (it satisfies $\|(z,w)\|=\sqrt2\rho$, so preimages of bounded sets are bounded, and $T^2$ is compact), hence a closed map. It identifies exactly the points of $T^2\times\{0\}$ (they all go to $0$). A closed continuous surjection is a quotient map, so it induces a homeomorphism $C(T^2)\cong\{\text{singular matrices}\}$.

**Check.** Consistency with the algebra: every rank-one matrix is $uv^T$ with $u,v\in\mathbb{R}^2\setminus\{0\}$, determined up to $(u,v)\sim(\lambda u,\lambda^{-1}v)$. Normalising $u$ to a unit vector (using the freedom $\lambda=1/|u|$), the pair $(u,v)$ becomes a point of $S^1\times(\mathbb{R}^2\setminus\{0\})\cong S^1\times S^1\times(0,\infty)$, and the only remaining ambiguity is $(u,v)\sim(-u,-v)$, a free involution on this space. The quotient of the torus factor by this free involution is again a torus, in agreement with Step 2. A numerical test on random $uv^T$ confirms $|z|=|w|$.

**Remark.** The singular set is *not* a manifold at $0$: removing the vertex disconnects a small neighbourhood into pieces that do not look like a ball of $\mathbb{R}^3$. The rank-one set is a smooth $3$-manifold, a "tube" $T^2\times(0,\infty)$, that is, the light cone of a quadratic form of signature $(2,2)$ minus its vertex.

**Lesson.** A homogeneous equation $q(z,w)=0$ defines a cone: study it through its "link" (the intersection with the unit sphere), here a torus.
@@@ mnappb-the-unit-ball-and-o-2-in-complex-coordinates.md
---
title: The operator-norm ball and O(2) in complex coordinates
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: M_2, operator norm, extreme points, O(2), unit ball
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

In the coordinates $(z,w)$ of $M_2(\mathbb{R})$ show that:
1. $\{A:\|A\|\le1\}=\{|z|+|w|\le1\}$;
2. $SO(2)=\{|z|=1,\ w=0\}$ and $O(2)\setminus SO(2)=\{z=0,\ |w|=1\}$;
3. the extreme points of the closed operator-norm unit ball are exactly the points of $O(2)$.

## Hint

Use $\|A\|=\sigma_1=|z|+|w|$ and $\sigma_2=||z|-|w||$. For 3, split a point with $zw\ne0$ as a combination of $(z/|z|,0)$ and $(0,w/|w|)$.

## Solution

**Part 1.** From the previous problem, $\|A\|=\sigma_1=|z|+|w|$. So the ball is $\{|z|+|w|\le1\}$.

**Part 2.** $A$ is orthogonal iff $A^TA=I$ iff both singular values equal $1$, i.e.
$$|z|+|w|=1\quad\text{and}\quad\bigl||z|-|w|\bigr|=1.$$
If $|z|\ge|w|$, the second equation is $|z|-|w|=1$, and subtracting from the first gives $|w|=0$, $|z|=1$. If $|w|>|z|$, then $|w|-|z|=1$ and we get $z=0$, $|w|=1$. So
$$O(2)=\{|z|=1,w=0\}\cup\{z=0,|w|=1\}.$$
The determinant is $|z|^2-|w|^2$, equal to $1$ on the first piece and $-1$ on the second. So the first piece is $SO(2)$ and the second is $O(2)\setminus SO(2)$ (rotations: $z=e^{i\theta}$, reflections: $w$ of modulus $1$).

**Part 3: extreme points.** The ball is $B=\{(z,w)\in\mathbb{C}^2:|z|+|w|\le1\}$.

*Points that are not extreme.*
- If $|z|+|w|<1$ and $(z,w)\ne0$, the point is the midpoint of the two points $(1\pm\eta)(z,w)$ of $B$ for small $\eta>0$; for $(z,w)=0$ it is the midpoint of $\pm(z_0,w_0)$ with $(z_0,w_0)\in B$ nonzero.
- If $|z|+|w|=1$ and $zw\ne0$, then
$$(z,w)=|z|\Bigl(\tfrac z{|z|},0\Bigr)+|w|\Bigl(0,\tfrac w{|w|}\Bigr),$$
a combination with weights $|z|,|w|\in(0,1)$ of weights summing to $1$ of two *distinct* points of $B$. So it is not extreme.

*Points of $O(2)$ are extreme.* Let $(z,0)$ with $|z|=1$, and suppose $(z,0)=\lambda(z_1,w_1)+(1-\lambda)(z_2,w_2)$ with $0<\lambda<1$ and $(z_i,w_i)\in B$. Then $w$-components give $\lambda w_1+(1-\lambda)w_2=0$ and
$$1=|\lambda z_1+(1-\lambda)z_2|\le\lambda|z_1|+(1-\lambda)|z_2|\le\lambda(1-|w_1|)+(1-\lambda)(1-|w_2|)\le1.$$
All inequalities are equalities, so $w_1=w_2=0$, $|z_1|=|z_2|=1$, and equality in the triangle inequality for unit complex numbers forces $z_1=z_2$ (the unit circle contains no segment). Hence the two points coincide and $(z,0)$ is extreme. The case $z=0$, $|w|=1$ is identical with the roles exchanged.

So the extreme points are exactly $\{|z|=1,w=0\}\cup\{z=0,|w|=1\}=O(2)$.

**Remark.** The ball is the convex hull of its extreme points (Kreinâ€“Milman), i.e. of two circles lying in two orthogonal planes of $\mathbb{R}^4$; this is the $n=2$ case of "the unit ball of the operator norm is the convex hull of $O(n)$". The ball is not strictly convex: it contains the segment joining $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$.

**Lesson.** In explicit coordinates, extreme points can be found by splitting non-extreme points as convex combinations and testing the equality cases of the triangle inequality for the rest.
@@@ mnappb-the-distance-to-the-singular-matrices-in-m2-r.md
---
title: The distance to the singular matrices in Mâ‚‚(â„)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: M_2, distance to singular matrices, smallest singular value, operator norm
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

For $A\in M_2(\mathbb{R})$ with coordinates $(z,w)$, show that in the operator norm
$$\operatorname{dist}\bigl(A,\{\text{singular matrices}\}\bigr)=\bigl||z|-|w|\bigr|=\sigma_2(A).$$

## Hint

In these coordinates $\|A-B\|=|z-z'|+|w-w'|$ and the singular matrices are $\{|z'|=|w'|\}$. Use the triangle inequality for the lower bound, and shrink the larger of $z,w$ and enlarge the smaller one to the middle radius for the upper bound.

## Solution

**Idea.** $\|\cdot\|=|z|+|w|$ is a norm on $\mathbb{C}^2$ in which the singular set is $\{|z'|=|w'|\}$. The problem reduces to an elementary optimisation in the plane.

**Step 1: the norm.** By linearity of $A\mapsto(z,w)$ and $\|A\|=|z|+|w|$, if $B$ has coordinates $(z',w')$ then $\|A-B\|=|z-z'|+|w-w'|$.

**Step 2: lower bound.** Let $B$ be singular: $|z'|=|w'|=r$. Then, by the reverse triangle inequality $|z-z'|\ge||z|-r|$ and $|w-w'|\ge||w|-r|$, and by the triangle inequality in $\mathbb{R}$,
$$\|A-B\|\ge\bigl||z|-r\bigr|+\bigl||w|-r\bigr|\ge\bigl|(|z|-r)-(|w|-r)\bigr|=\bigl||z|-|w|\bigr|.$$

**Step 3: the bound is attained.** Assume $|z|\ge|w|$ with $(z,w)\ne(0,0)$ (the other case is symmetric by exchanging the roles, and $A=0$ is singular). Put $r=\frac{|z|+|w|}2$, $z'=r\,z/|z|$ (as $z\ne0$), and $w'=r\,u$ with $u=w/|w|$ if $w\ne0$ and $u=1$ if $w=0$. Then $|z'|=|w'|=r$ so the matrix $B$ with coordinates $(z',w')$ is singular, and
$$|z-z'|=|z|-r=\frac{|z|-|w|}2,\qquad|w-w'|=\bigl|\,|w|-r\bigr|=\frac{|z|-|w|}2.$$
(For $w=0$, $|w-w'|=r=\frac{|z|}2=\frac{|z|-|w|}2$.) Hence $\|A-B\|=|z|-|w|$.

**Conclusion.** The distance equals $\bigl||z|-|w|\bigr|$, which is $\sigma_2(A)$ by the previous problem.

**Check (numerical).** For random $A$, the matrix $B=A-\sigma_2u_2v_2^T$ (SVD) is singular and $\|A-B\|=\sigma_2$; moreover $300$ random rank-one matrices $uv^T$ never came closer than $\sigma_2$.

**Remark.** This is the $2\times2$ case of the Eckartâ€“Young theorem: the distance from $A$ to the matrices of rank $<n$ is $\sigma_n(A)=1/\|A^{-1}\|$ for invertible $A$. In the $(z,w)$ picture, the nearest singular matrix is obtained by moving $z$ and $w$ along the radial lines to the common radius $r=\frac{|z|+|w|}2$.

**Lesson.** In the right coordinates, an "abstract" distance formula becomes a one-line triangle-inequality argument.
@@@ mnappb-conjugation-by-rotations-in-complex-coordinates.md
---
title: Conjugation by rotations acts on w by e^{2iÎ²}
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: M_2, conjugation, rotations, complex coordinates, invariants
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Let $R_\beta$ be the rotation of $\mathbb{R}^2\cong\mathbb{C}$ by the angle $\beta$ (multiplication by $e^{i\beta}$). Show that, if $A$ has coordinates $(z,w)$, the matrix $R_\beta AR_{-\beta}$ has coordinates $(z,\,e^{2i\beta}w)$.

## Hint

Compute $R_\beta AR_{-\beta}x$ for $x\in\mathbb{C}$ using $Ax=zx+w\bar x$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Rotations commute with the conjugate-linear part only up to a phase. The complex-linear part $x\mapsto zx$ commutes with all rotations, so $z$ is unchanged.

**Computation.** For $x\in\mathbb{C}$, $R_{-\beta}x=e^{-i\beta}x$, so
$$AR_{-\beta}x=z\,e^{-i\beta}x+w\,\overline{e^{-i\beta}x}=z\,e^{-i\beta}x+w\,e^{i\beta}\bar x.$$
Applying $R_\beta$ (multiply by $e^{i\beta}$):
$$R_\beta AR_{-\beta}x=zx+w\,e^{2i\beta}\bar x.$$
By uniqueness of the decomposition $Ax=zx+w\bar x$ (the map $A\mapsto(z,w)$ is bijective), the coordinates of $R_\beta AR_{-\beta}$ are $(z,e^{2i\beta}w)$.

**Check (numerical).** For random $A$ and random $\beta$, the coordinates of $R_\beta AR_\beta^T$ agree with $(z,e^{2i\beta}w)$ to $10^{-15}$.

**Consequences.**
- The quantities $z$, $|w|$ (hence $\operatorname{tr}A=2\operatorname{Re}z$, $\det A=|z|^2-|w|^2$, $\|A\|_F$) are invariant under conjugation by $SO(2)$.
- $A$ commutes with every rotation iff $w=0$, i.e. iff $A$ is a "complex scalar" $x\mapsto zx$, equivalently $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&-c\\c&a\end{pmatrix}$.
- Conjugating by the reflection $x\mapsto\bar x$ gives $\overline{A\bar x}=\bar zx+\bar w\bar x$: $(z,w)\mapsto(\bar z,\bar w)$.

**Remark.** The factor $e^{2i\beta}$ (and not $e^{i\beta}$) shows that the rotation $R_\pi=-I$ acts trivially, as it must, since $-I$ is central; and that $R_\beta$ and $R_{\beta+\pi}$ give the same conjugation.

**Lesson.** To understand a group action on a space of matrices, find coordinates in which it becomes diagonal (here: multiplication by phases).
@@@ mnappb-real-eigenvalues-of-a-2-by-2-matrix-in-complex-coordinates.md
---
title: Real eigenvalues of a 2Ã—2 matrix: |w| â‰¥ |Im z|
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: M_2, eigenvalues, discriminant, complex coordinates, real spectrum
date: 2026-11-10
---

## Statement

Let $A\in M_2(\mathbb{R})$ have coordinates $(z,w)$. Show that $A$ has real eigenvalues if and only if $|w|\ge|\operatorname{Im}z|$, and give the eigenvalues in that case.

## Hint

The eigenvalues are the roots of $t^2-(\operatorname{tr}A)t+\det A$. Compute the discriminant using $\operatorname{tr}A=2\operatorname{Re}z$ and $\det A=|z|^2-|w|^2$.

## Solution

**Idea.** A real $2\times2$ matrix has real eigenvalues iff its characteristic polynomial has nonnegative discriminant. In the $(z,w)$ coordinates, this discriminant is a difference of two squares.

**Step 1: trace and determinant.** $\operatorname{tr}A=a+d=2\operatorname{Re}z$ and $\det A=|z|^2-|w|^2$ (previous problems).

**Step 2: discriminant.** The characteristic polynomial $t^2-(\operatorname{tr}A)t+\det A$ has discriminant
$$\Delta=(\operatorname{tr}A)^2-4\det A=4(\operatorname{Re}z)^2-4|z|^2+4|w|^2=4\bigl(|w|^2-(\operatorname{Im}z)^2\bigr),$$
since $|z|^2=(\operatorname{Re}z)^2+(\operatorname{Im}z)^2$.

**Step 3: conclusion.** $A$ has real eigenvalues iff $\Delta\ge0$ iff $|w|\ge|\operatorname{Im}z|$. In that case the eigenvalues are
$$\lambda_\pm=\frac{\operatorname{tr}A\pm\sqrt\Delta}2=\operatorname{Re}z\pm\sqrt{|w|^2-(\operatorname{Im}z)^2}.$$
If $|w|<|\operatorname{Im}z|$ the eigenvalues are the non-real pair $\operatorname{Re}z\pm i\sqrt{(\operatorname{Im}z)^2-|w|^2}$.

**Translation to entries.** $\operatorname{Im}z=\frac{c-b}2$ and $|w|^2=\frac{(a-d)^2+(b+c)^2}4$, so
$$|w|^2-(\operatorname{Im}z)^2=\frac{(a-d)^2+(b+c)^2-(c-b)^2}4=\frac{(a-d)^2+4bc}4,$$
which is the familiar discriminant $(a-d)^2+4bc\ge0$.

**Check.** Rotation $R(\theta)$: $z=e^{i\theta}$, $w=0$, so $|w|=0<|\sin\theta|=|\operatorname{Im}z|$ unless $\theta\in\pi\mathbb{Z}$; real eigenvalues only for $R(0)=I$ and $R(\pi)=-I$ âœ“. Reflection $\mathrm{diag}(1,-1)$: $z=0$, $w=1$, eigenvalues $0\pm\sqrt1=\pm1$ âœ“. Numerically, the formula $\operatorname{Re}z\pm\sqrt{|w|^2-(\operatorname{Im}z)^2}$ matches `numpy` eigenvalues for $200$ random matrices.

**Remark.** The set of matrices with a real eigenvalue is $\{|w|\ge|\operatorname{Im}z|\}$: it is closed, with interior $\{|w|>|\operatorname{Im}z|\}$ and boundary $\{|w|=|\operatorname{Im}z|\}$ (matrices with a repeated real eigenvalue). This recovers the description of the interior in terms of $\operatorname{tr}^2>4\det$.

**Lesson.** When a discriminant condition appears, look for coordinates in which it factors or becomes a comparison of two moduli.

@@@ mnappa-det-of-i-plus-ab-equals-det-of-i-plus-ba.md
---
title: det(I + AB) = det(I + BA) by density
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: density, determinant, polynomial identity
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A,B\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$. Prove that $\det(I+AB)=\det(I+BA)$.

## Hint

Treat first the case where $A$ is invertible, then use the fact that $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is dense in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Solution

**Idea.** An identity between two continuous functions that holds on a dense set holds everywhere. The invertible matrices are dense, and for them the identity is a one-line conjugation.

**Step 1: $A$ invertible.** Then
$$A(I+BA)A^{-1}=A A^{-1}+ABAA^{-1}=I+AB.$$
So $I+AB$ and $I+BA$ are conjugate, hence have the same determinant (because $\det(PXP^{-1})=\det X$).

**Step 2: density.** Fix $B$. The two functions
$$f(A)=\det(I+AB),\qquad g(A)=\det(I+BA)$$
are polynomials in the entries of $A$ (a determinant is a polynomial in the entries, and the entries of $I+AB$ are polynomials in those of $A$). In particular they are continuous on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

For an arbitrary $A$, the matrices $A_\varepsilon=A+\varepsilon I$ are invertible for all but finitely many $\varepsilon$ (their determinant is a nonzero polynomial in $\varepsilon$ of degree $n$, equal to $\chi_{-A}(\varepsilon)$ up to sign). Hence $f(A_\varepsilon)=g(A_\varepsilon)$ for small $\varepsilon\ne0$, and letting $\varepsilon\to0$ gives $f(A)=g(A)$.

**Remark.** The same proof gives the more general identity $\det(\lambda I+AB)=\det(\lambda I+BA)$, and later the equality of the characteristic polynomials of $AB$ and $BA$. Note that it also holds for rectangular matrices $A\in M_{n,m}$, $B\in M_{m,n}$ in the form $\det(I_n+AB)=\det(I_m+BA)$, but then the conjugation argument must be replaced by a block-matrix computation.

**Lesson.** To prove an identity between polynomial functions of a matrix, prove it for invertible (or diagonalisable) matrices and conclude by density.
@@@ mnappa-determinant-of-a-block-matrix-with-commuting-blocks.md
---
title: The determinant of a block matrix with commuting lower blocks
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: density, block matrices, Schur complement
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A,B,C,D\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ with $CD=DC$. Prove that
$$\det\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\C&D\end{pmatrix}=\det(AD-BC).$$

## Hint

Assume first that $D$ is invertible and use the Schur complement $A-BD^{-1}C$. For the general case, replace $D$ by $D+\varepsilon I$.

## Solution

**Idea.** For invertible $D$, block elimination reduces the $2n\times2n$ determinant to an $n\times n$ one. Commutation of $C$ and $D$ lets us simplify the result. The general case follows by density.

**Step 1: $D$ invertible.** Write the block matrix as a product:
$$\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\C&D\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I&BD^{-1}\\0&I\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A-BD^{-1}C&0\\C&D\end{pmatrix}.$$
(Check: the top-left block of the product is $A-BD^{-1}C+BD^{-1}C=A$, the top-right is $BD^{-1}D=B$.) The first factor has determinant $1$, and the second is block lower triangular, so
$$\det\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\C&D\end{pmatrix}=\det(A-BD^{-1}C)\cdot\det D=\det\bigl((A-BD^{-1}C)D\bigr)=\det\bigl(AD-BD^{-1}CD\bigr).$$

**Step 2: use $CD=DC$.** Multiplying $CD=DC$ on the left by $D^{-1}$ gives $D^{-1}CD=C$. Therefore $BD^{-1}CD=BC$ and the determinant equals $\det(AD-BC)$.

**Step 3: general $D$.** For $\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}$, the matrix $D_\varepsilon=D+\varepsilon I$ still commutes with $C$, and it is invertible except for finitely many values of $\varepsilon$ (the eigenvalues of $-D$ that are real). By Step 2,
$$\det\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\C&D_\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}=\det(AD_\varepsilon-BC)$$
for all small $\varepsilon\ne0$. Both sides are polynomials in $\varepsilon$, so they are continuous, and letting $\varepsilon\to0$ gives the identity for $D$.

**Remark.** The hypothesis is essential: for $A=B=I$, $D=0$... one checks that without $CD=DC$ the formula can fail (take $n=2$ with $C,D$ not commuting). The result is the matrix analogue of $\det\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}=ad-bc$.

**Lesson.** Block elimination plus density: prove under an invertibility hypothesis, then perturb by $\varepsilon I$ while preserving the algebraic hypothesis (here commutation).
@@@ mnappa-the-determinant-of-a-sum-of-two-squares-of-commuting-matrices.md
---
title: det(AÂ² + BÂ²) â‰¥ 0 for commuting real matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: complexification, determinant, commuting matrices
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A,B\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ with $AB=BA$. Prove that $\det(A^2+B^2)\ge0$.

## Hint

Factor $A^2+B^2$ over the complex numbers: $A^2+B^2=(A+iB)(A-iB)$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Over $\mathbb{R}$, $A^2+B^2$ does not factor, but over $\mathbb{C}$ it does, provided $A$ and $B$ commute. The two factors are complex conjugates, so their determinants are conjugate and multiply to a modulus squared.

**Step 1: the factorisation.** Regard $A,B$ as matrices in $M_n(\mathbb{C})$. Then
$$(A+iB)(A-iB)=A^2-iAB+iBA+B^2=A^2+B^2,$$
because $-iAB+iBA=-i(AB-BA)=0$. This is where the commutation is used.

**Step 2: determinants.** Multiplicativity of $\det$ over $\mathbb{C}$ gives
$$\det(A^2+B^2)=\det(A+iB)\,\det(A-iB).$$

**Step 3: conjugates.** Since $A$ and $B$ have real entries, the matrix $A-iB$ is the entrywise complex conjugate of $A+iB$. The determinant is a polynomial with real (integer) coefficients in the entries, so $\det(\bar M)=\overline{\det M}$. Hence
$$\det(A^2+B^2)=\det(A+iB)\overline{\det(A+iB)}=|\det(A+iB)|^2\ge0.$$

**Remark.** Without commutation the statement is false. For example, for $n=2$ take $A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ and $B=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$: then $A^2=B^2=0$ and the determinant is $0$, which does not contradict; but with $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix}$, $B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ one gets $A^2+B^2=2I$, $\det=4\ge0$ again. Counterexamples exist for $n\ge2$ (e.g. suitable non-commuting nilpotent-plus-diagonal pairs), but the point of the problem is only that the proof breaks at Step 1.

**Lesson.** Complexify to factor sums of squares; real matrices produce conjugate factors, hence a non-negative product.
@@@ mnappa-a-matrix-with-a-nontrivial-kernel-from-cubes.md
---
title: AÂ²+BÂ² is singular when AÂ³ = BÂ³ and AÂ²B = BÂ²A
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: kernel, algebraic identity, Putnam
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $A\ne B$ be real $n\times n$ matrices with $A^3=B^3$ and $A^2B=B^2A$. Prove that $A^2+B^2$ is not invertible.

## Hint

Multiply $A^2+B^2$ on the right by $A-B$ and expand.

## Solution

**Idea.** We find a nonzero matrix $A-B$ that kills $A^2+B^2$ from the right; then any nonzero column of $A-B$ lies in the kernel.

**Step 1: expansion.**
$$(A^2+B^2)(A-B)=A^3-A^2B+B^2A-B^3.$$
Group the terms: $(A^3-B^3)+(B^2A-A^2B)$. The first bracket is $0$ because $A^3=B^3$, the second is $0$ because $A^2B=B^2A$. Hence
$$(A^2+B^2)(A-B)=0.$$

**Step 2: a nonzero vector in the kernel.** Since $A\ne B$, the matrix $A-B$ is not zero, so it has a nonzero column; call it $v=(A-B)e_j\ne0$. Then
$$(A^2+B^2)v=(A^2+B^2)(A-B)e_j=0.$$

**Step 3: conclusion.** $A^2+B^2$ has a nonzero vector in its kernel, so it is not invertible, i.e. $\det(A^2+B^2)=0$.

**Remark.** Combined with the previous problem this is the classical Putnam statement: in the Putnam version one concludes $\det(A^2+B^2)=0$ using exactly this factorisation trick. Note that no commutation $AB=BA$ is assumed; only the two cubic relations.

**Lesson.** To prove a matrix is singular, look for a nonzero matrix that it kills on one side.
@@@ mnappa-the-characteristic-polynomials-of-aat-and-ata.md
---
title: AAáµ€ and Aáµ€A have the same characteristic polynomial
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: characteristic polynomial, density, symmetric matrices
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Prove that for square matrices $X,Y\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ the characteristic polynomials of $XY$ and $YX$ are equal. Deduce that $AA^T$ and $A^TA$ have the same eigenvalues with the same multiplicities.

## Hint

Use the identity $\det(I+XY)=\det(I+YX)$ with $X/t$ in place of $X$, for $t\ne0$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The characteristic polynomial is $\chi_{M}(t)=\det(tI-M)$. We compare $\det(tI-XY)$ and $\det(tI-YX)$ for $t\ne0$ using the identity $\det(I+MN)=\det(I+NM)$, then conclude since two polynomials that agree for infinitely many $t$ are equal.

**Step 1: $t\ne0$.** Write
$$\det(tI-XY)=t^n\det\!\left(I-\frac Xt\,Y\right)=t^n\det\!\left(I-Y\,\frac Xt\right)=\det(tI-YX),$$
where the middle equality is the identity $\det(I+MN)=\det(I+NM)$ with $M=-X/t$ and $N=Y$.

**Step 2: all $t$.** $\chi_{XY}$ and $\chi_{YX}$ are polynomials in $t$ which coincide for all $t\ne0$, i.e. at infinitely many points, so they are equal as polynomials.

**Step 3: application.** Take $X=A$, $Y=A^T$. Then $\chi_{AA^T}=\chi_{A^TA}$. Both matrices are symmetric, hence diagonalisable with real eigenvalues; the eigenvalues counted with algebraic multiplicity are the roots of the characteristic polynomial, and for symmetric matrices algebraic and geometric multiplicity coincide. So the spectra agree with multiplicity.

**Remark.** For rectangular $A\in M_{m,n}$ the same argument gives $t^{n}\chi_{AA^T}(t)=t^m\chi_{A^TA}(t)$ up to sizes: the non-zero eigenvalues of $AA^T$ and $A^TA$ coincide, with multiplicities, and the larger matrix has $|m-n|$ extra zero eigenvalues. These common values are the squares of the singular values of $A$.

**Lesson.** $XY$ and $YX$ are "almost conjugate": same characteristic polynomial even when neither $X$ nor $Y$ is invertible.
@@@ mnappa-the-adjugate-of-the-adjugate.md
---
title: adj(adj A) = (det A)^(nâˆ’2) A
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: adjugate, density, polynomial identity
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

For $n\ge2$ and $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$, prove that $\operatorname{adj}(\operatorname{adj}A)=(\det A)^{n-2}A$.

## Hint

For invertible $A$ use $\operatorname{adj}A=\det A\cdot A^{-1}$ and the homogeneity of $\operatorname{adj}$. Then extend by density.

## Solution

**Idea.** Both sides are polynomial in the entries of $A$ (this is where $n\ge2$ matters: the exponent $n-2$ is non-negative). So it suffices to check the identity on the dense set $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Step 1: facts about adj.** Recall $A\operatorname{adj}A=\operatorname{adj}A\,A=(\det A)I$. So for invertible $A$, $\operatorname{adj}A=(\det A)A^{-1}$. Also, since each entry of $\operatorname{adj}X$ is a minor of size $n-1$,
$$\operatorname{adj}(cX)=c^{\,n-1}\operatorname{adj}X\qquad(c\in\mathbb{R}).$$

**Step 2: invertible $A$.** Then $\operatorname{adj}A=(\det A)A^{-1}$ is invertible with
$$\det(\operatorname{adj}A)=(\det A)^n\det(A^{-1})=(\det A)^{n-1}\ne0.$$
