Apply the formula $\operatorname{adj}X=(\det X)X^{-1}$ to $X=\operatorname{adj}A$:
$$\operatorname{adj}(\operatorname{adj}A)=(\det A)^{n-1}\bigl((\det A)A^{-1}\bigr)^{-1}=(\det A)^{n-1}\cdot\frac{A}{\det A}=(\det A)^{n-2}A.$$
(Alternatively use homogeneity: $\operatorname{adj}\bigl((\det A)A^{-1}\bigr)=(\det A)^{n-1}\operatorname{adj}(A^{-1})$ and $\operatorname{adj}(A^{-1})=\det(A^{-1})A$.)

**Step 3: density.** The map $A\mapsto\operatorname{adj}(\operatorname{adj}A)-(\det A)^{n-2}A$ is polynomial in the entries (entries of $\operatorname{adj}$ are polynomials; the power $n-2\ge0$ is fine), hence continuous, and vanishes on $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$. As $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is dense in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$, it vanishes everywhere.

**Check.** For $n=2$, $\operatorname{adj}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}d&-b\\-c&a\end{pmatrix}$ and applying adj again returns the original matrix: this is $(\det A)^0A$ âœ“.

**Remark.** For a singular matrix of rank $\le n-2$ both sides vanish when $n\ge3$: $\operatorname{adj}A=0$, so $\operatorname{adj}\operatorname{adj}A=0$ and $\det A=0$, so $(\det A)^{n-2}A=0$ (for $n\ge3$).

**Lesson.** Polynomial identities with a non-negative exponent extend from $GL_n$ to all of $M_n$ for free.
@@@ mnappa-convexity-of-the-largest-eigenvalue-and-the-spectral-norm.md
---
title: Convexity of Î»_max and the spectral norm on symmetric matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: convexity, Rayleigh quotient, norm, symmetric matrices
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ be the space of real symmetric $n\times n$ matrices.

1. Show that $S\mapsto\lambda_{\max}(S)$ is convex on $\mathrm{Sym}_n$.
2. Show that $\|S\|=\max_i|\lambda_i(S)|$ is a norm on $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ (the operator norm).

## Hint

By the spectral theorem, $\lambda_{\max}(S)=\max_{|x|=1}x^TSx$, a supremum of linear functions of $S$.

## Solution

**Idea.** A pointwise supremum of convex functions is convex, and linear functions are convex. The Rayleigh representation turns the largest eigenvalue into such a supremum.

**Step 1: Rayleigh formula.** Let $S=Q\Lambda Q^T$ with $\Lambda=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1\ge\dots\ge\lambda_n)$. For $|x|=1$ put $y=Q^Tx$ (also a unit vector). Then
$$x^TSx=\sum_i\lambda_iy_i^2\le\lambda_1\sum_iy_i^2=\lambda_1,$$
with equality for $x=Qe_1$. Hence $\lambda_{\max}(S)=\max_{|x|=1}x^TSx$.

**Step 2: convexity.** For each fixed unit vector $x$, the function $\ell_x:S\mapsto x^TSx$ is linear in $S$. For $S,T\in\mathrm{Sym}_n$ and $t\in[0,1]$,
$$x^T\bigl(tS+(1-t)T\bigr)x=t\,x^TSx+(1-t)\,x^TTx\le t\lambda_{\max}(S)+(1-t)\lambda_{\max}(T).$$
Taking the maximum over $x$ on the left gives $\lambda_{\max}(tS+(1-t)T)\le t\lambda_{\max}(S)+(1-t)\lambda_{\max}(T)$.

**Step 3: the norm.** Note $-\lambda_{\min}(S)=\lambda_{\max}(-S)$, so
$$\|S\|=\max_i|\lambda_i(S)|=\max\bigl(\lambda_{\max}(S),\lambda_{\max}(-S)\bigr)=\max_{|x|=1}|x^TSx|.$$
- *Definiteness:* $\|S\|=0$ forces all eigenvalues to vanish, hence $S=0$.
- *Homogeneity:* $\|cS\|=|c|\,\|S\|$ since the eigenvalues are multiplied by $c$.
- *Triangle inequality:* $|x^T(S+T)x|\le|x^TSx|+|x^TTx|\le\|S\|+\|T\|$ for every unit $x$; take the maximum over $x$.

**Remark.** $\lambda_{\max}$ itself is convex and positively homogeneous but *not* a norm: it is not symmetric ($\lambda_{\max}(-S)\ne\lambda_{\max}(S)$ in general) and it can be negative. The norm above equals the operator norm $\sup_{|x|=1}|Sx|$ for symmetric $S$.

**Lesson.** A supremum of linear functionals is convex; this is the standard way to prove convexity of spectral quantities.
@@@ mnappa-the-ky-fan-maximum-principle.md
---
title: Ky Fan's maximum principle
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Ky Fan, eigenvalues, orthogonal projections, convexity
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n$ have eigenvalues $\lambda_1\ge\dots\ge\lambda_n$. Show that
$$\lambda_1+\dots+\lambda_k=\max\{\operatorname{tr}(SP):\ P\text{ an orthogonal projection of rank }k\},$$
and deduce that $S\mapsto\lambda_1(S)+\dots+\lambda_k(S)$ is convex.

## Hint

Diagonalise $S=Q\Lambda Q^T$ and conjugate $P$ by $Q$: the diagonal entries of the new projection lie in $[0,1]$ and sum to $k$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Reduce to a diagonal $S$. Then $\operatorname{tr}(\Lambda P')=\sum\lambda_ip'_{ii}$ is a weighted sum with weights in $[0,1]$ adding up to $k$, which is maximal when the weights sit on the $k$ largest $\lambda_i$.

**Step 1: reduction.** Let $S=Q\Lambda Q^T$ and $P$ an orthogonal projection of rank $k$ (i.e. $P=P^T=P^2$, $\operatorname{tr}P=k$). Put $P'=Q^TPQ$; it is again an orthogonal projection of rank $k$ and
$$\operatorname{tr}(SP)=\operatorname{tr}(Q\Lambda Q^TP)=\operatorname{tr}(\Lambda Q^TPQ)=\operatorname{tr}(\Lambda P')=\sum_i\lambda_ip'_{ii}.$$

**Step 2: the weights.** Let $p_i=p'_{ii}$. Then $p_i=e_i^TP'e_i=e_i^TP'^TP'e_i=|P'e_i|^2\in[0,1]$ (because $P'$ is a projection, $|P'v|\le|v|$), and $\sum_ip_i=\operatorname{tr}P'=k$.

**Step 3: the bound.** Write
$$\sum_i\lambda_ip_i-k\lambda_k=\sum_i(\lambda_i-\lambda_k)p_i.$$
For $i\le k$, $\lambda_i-\lambda_k\ge0$ and $p_i\le1$, so those terms are at most $\lambda_i-\lambda_k$. For $i>k$, $\lambda_i-\lambda_k\le0$ and $p_i\ge0$, so those terms are $\le0$. Therefore
$$\sum_i\lambda_ip_i-k\lambda_k\le\sum_{i\le k}(\lambda_i-\lambda_k)=\sum_{i\le k}\lambda_i-k\lambda_k,$$
that is $\operatorname{tr}(SP)\le\lambda_1+\dots+\lambda_k$.

**Step 4: attained.** Let $P_0$ be the orthogonal projection onto the span of the first $k$ columns of $Q$ (the top $k$ eigenvectors). Then $\operatorname{tr}(SP_0)=\lambda_1+\dots+\lambda_k$. So the maximum exists (also because the set of such projections is compact) and equals the sum.

**Step 5: convexity.** For each fixed $P$, $S\mapsto\operatorname{tr}(SP)$ is linear. The function $S\mapsto\lambda_1+\dots+\lambda_k$ is the maximum of these linear functions, hence convex.

**Remark.** For $k=1$ this is the Rayleigh formula for $\lambda_{\max}$ (previous problem). For $k=n$ it is the trace, which is linear.

**Lesson.** Variational formulas as maxima of linear functions give convexity for free.
@@@ mnappa-every-odd-sized-real-matrix-has-a-real-eigenvalue.md
---
title: Odd-sized real matrices have a real eigenvalue
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: intermediate value theorem, characteristic polynomial, eigenvalue
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that every real $n\times n$ matrix with $n$ odd has at least one real eigenvalue.

## Hint

The characteristic polynomial is a real monic polynomial of odd degree. Look at its sign at $\pm\infty$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Eigenvalues are roots of the characteristic polynomial $\chi_A(t)=\det(tI-A)$. A real polynomial of odd degree changes sign, so the intermediate value theorem gives a real root.

**Step 1.** Expanding the determinant, $\chi_A(t)=t^n+c_{n-1}t^{n-1}+\dots+c_0$ with real $c_j$: it is monic of degree $n$.

**Step 2: signs at infinity.** Write $\chi_A(t)=t^n\bigl(1+c_{n-1}/t+\dots+c_0/t^n\bigr)$. The bracket tends to $1$ as $|t|\to\infty$, and $t^n\to+\infty$ as $t\to+\infty$, $t^n\to-\infty$ as $t\to-\infty$ because $n$ is odd. Hence there are $a<b$ with $\chi_A(a)<0<\chi_A(b)$.

**Step 3: IVT.** $\chi_A$ is continuous, so it vanishes at some $t_0\in(a,b)$. Then $\det(t_0I-A)=0$, i.e. $t_0$ is a real eigenvalue.

**Remark.** For $n$ even the statement is false: the rotation matrix $\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ has eigenvalues $\pm i$ only. Since non-real roots of a real polynomial come in conjugate pairs, an equivalent argument is that an odd number of roots cannot all be paired.

**Lesson.** Odd degree plus continuity yields a real root.
@@@ mnappa-a-positive-matrix-has-a-positive-eigenvector.md
---
title: A 2Ã—2 matrix with positive entries has a positive eigenvector
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: intermediate value theorem, Perronâ€“Frobenius, eigenvector
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that every real $2\times2$ matrix $A$ with all entries positive has an eigenvector with positive coordinates, and that the corresponding eigenvalue is positive.

## Hint

Parametrise candidate vectors by $x_t=(t,1-t)^T$, $t\in[0,1]$, and apply the intermediate value theorem to $g(t)=\dfrac{(Ax_t)_1}{(Ax_t)_1+(Ax_t)_2}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** A vector $x_t$ on the segment $\{x\ge0,\ x_1+x_2=1\}$ is an eigenvector iff $Ax_t$ has the same direction, i.e. iff the normalised first coordinate of $Ax_t$ equals $t$. We find such a $t$ by the IVT.

**Step 1: $Ax_t>0$.** For $t\in[0,1]$, $x_t\ge0$ and $x_t\ne0$; since all entries of $A$ are positive, both coordinates of $y=Ax_t$ are positive. So $g(t)=y_1/(y_1+y_2)$ is well defined, continuous in $t$, and $g(t)\in(0,1)$.

**Step 2: sign change.** Put $h(t)=g(t)-t$. Then $h(0)=g(0)>0$ and $h(1)=g(1)-1<0$. By the IVT there exists $t_0\in(0,1)$ with $h(t_0)=0$, i.e. $g(t_0)=t_0$.

**Step 3: eigenvector.** With $y=Ax_{t_0}$ and $s=y_1+y_2>0$ we get $y_1=t_0s$, and then $y_2=s-y_1=(1-t_0)s$. So
$$Ax_{t_0}=s\,x_{t_0},$$
where $x_{t_0}=(t_0,1-t_0)^T$ has both coordinates in $(0,1)$ and $s>0$.

**Remark.** This is the 2-dimensional case of the Perronâ€“Frobenius theorem. The same idea (a continuous self-map of the simplex $\{x\ge0,\sum x_i=1\}$ has a fixed point) proves it in dimension $n$ via Brouwer's theorem: the map $x\mapsto Ax/\sum(Ax)_i$ is such a self-map.

**Lesson.** Eigenvector problems can be turned into fixed point problems on a compact convex set.
@@@ mnappa-rotations-of-odd-dimension-have-an-axis.md
---
title: Odd-dimensional rotations have an axis
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: orthogonal group, determinant, eigenvalue 1, Euler's theorem
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that every $U\in SO(n)$ with $n$ odd has the eigenvalue $1$. In particular (Euler's theorem), every rotation of $\mathbb{R}^3$ has an axis.

## Hint

Show $\det(U-I)=(-1)^n\det(U-I)$ using $U^TU=I$ and $\det U=1$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The matrix $U-I$ is related to its transpose by a factor $-U$ up to conjugation; taking determinants gives an equation that forces $\det(U-I)=0$ when $n$ is odd.

**Step 1: a factorisation.** Since $UU^T=I$,
$$U-I=U-UU^T=U(I-U^T).$$

**Step 2: determinants.** Using $\det U=1$ and $\det M^T=\det M$,
$$\det(U-I)=\det U\cdot\det(I-U^T)=\det\bigl((I-U)^T\bigr)=\det(I-U).$$
But $I-U=-(U-I)$, so $\det(I-U)=(-1)^n\det(U-I)$. Therefore
$$\det(U-I)=(-1)^n\det(U-I).$$

**Step 3: $n$ odd.** For $n$ odd this reads $\det(U-I)=-\det(U-I)$, so $\det(U-I)=0$. Hence $1$ is an eigenvalue: there is a unit vector $e$ with $Ue=e$.

**Euler's theorem.** For $n=3$ this unit vector spans the axis, and $U$ restricts to an orientation preserving isometry of the plane $e^\perp$ (preserved because $U$ is orthogonal and fixes $e$), i.e. to a plane rotation. So $U$ is a rotation about $e$.

**Remark.** For $n$ even the statement fails: $\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}\in SO(2)$ has no eigenvalue $1$. The same computation shows that for $n$ even and $\det U=-1$ one has $\det(U-I)=0$: every reflection-type orthogonal matrix has eigenvalue $1$ (and also $-1$).

**Lesson.** Parity of the dimension decides the existence of fixed vectors for rotations.
@@@ mnappa-the-rotation-angle-is-a-continuous-function-of-the-trace.md
---
title: The rotation angle of a 3D rotation is continuous
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: SO(3), rotation angle, trace, normal form
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

For $U\in SO(3)$ define $\theta(U)=\arccos\dfrac{\operatorname{tr}U-1}2\in[0,\pi]$.

1. Show that this is well defined and continuous on $SO(3)$.
2. Show that $\theta(U)$ is the rotation angle of $U$ (the angle of the rotation about its axis, up to sign).

## Hint

Every $U\in SO(3)$ is conjugate by an orthogonal matrix to $1\oplus R_\varphi$, where $R_\varphi$ is the plane rotation of angle $\varphi$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Choose an orthonormal basis adapted to the axis. In that basis $U$ is block diagonal and its trace is $1+2\cos\varphi$.

**Step 1: normal form.** By the previous problem there is a unit vector $e$ with $Ue=e$. Since $U$ is orthogonal, it maps $e^\perp$ to itself and acts there as an orthogonal map of determinant $\det U/1=1$, i.e. as a rotation $R_\varphi$ of the plane $e^\perp$ (identified with $\mathbb{R}^2$ via an orthonormal basis $(f,g)$). In the orthonormal basis $(e,f,g)$,
$$U\sim\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&R_\varphi\end{pmatrix},\qquad R_\varphi=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\varphi&-\sin\varphi\\\sin\varphi&\cos\varphi\end{pmatrix}.$$

**Step 2: trace.** The trace is invariant under conjugation, so $\operatorname{tr}U=1+2\cos\varphi$, i.e. $\dfrac{\operatorname{tr}U-1}2=\cos\varphi\in[-1,1]$. This shows both that $\arccos$ is defined at this number and that $\arccos(\cos\varphi)=|\varphi|$ (mod $2\pi$, reduced to $[0,\pi]$) is the rotation angle of $U$, up to the sign ambiguity coming from the orientation of the axis.

**Step 3: continuity.** $U\mapsto\operatorname{tr}U$ is linear, hence continuous; $x\mapsto\arccos x$ is continuous on $[-1,1]$. So $\theta$ is continuous on $SO(3)$ as a composition.

**Check.** For $U=I$: $\theta=\arccos1=0$. For $U=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,-1)$ (rotation by $\pi$ about the first axis): $\operatorname{tr}U=-1$, $\theta=\arccos(-1)=\pi$.

**Remark.** The angle alone does not determine the rotation (there is also the axis), and the axis direction is not a continuous function on $SO(3)$ (it is undefined at $I$); but the angle is, as shown.

**Lesson.** Conjugation invariants such as the trace give continuous invariants of the conjugacy class.
@@@ mnappa-rotations-by-pi-form-a-projective-plane.md
---
title: Rotations by Ï€ form a projective plane; the Cayley transform covers the rest
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: SO(3), Cayley transform, projective plane, cut locus
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $C=\{U\in SO(3):\theta(U)=\pi\}$ be the set of rotations by angle $\pi$.

1. Show that $C=\{2nn^T-I:\ n\in S^2\}$ and that $C$ is homeomorphic to $\mathbb{RP}^2$.
2. Show that the Cayley transform $K\mapsto(I+K)(I-K)^{-1}$ maps the space $\mathrm{Skew}_3$ of skew-symmetric $3\times3$ matrices bijectively onto $SO(3)\setminus C$.

## Hint

For part 1, a rotation by $\pi$ about $n$ is the map $x\mapsto2(n\cdot x)n-x$. For part 2, show that the image consists of rotations with no eigenvalue $-1$.

## Solution

**Part 1: description of $C$.** By the normal form of the previous problem, $\theta(U)=\pi$ means $\operatorname{tr}U=-1$, i.e. $U\sim1\oplus R_\pi=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,-1)$. So $U$ fixes a unit vector $n$ and acts as $-1$ on $n^\perp$: $Ux=2(n\cdot x)n-x$, i.e. $U=2nn^T-I$. Conversely such matrices are orthogonal with determinant $(+1)(-1)(-1)=1$ and trace $2-3=-1$.

**Part 1: topology.** The map $\phi:S^2\to C$, $n\mapsto2nn^T-I$, is continuous and onto. It satisfies $\phi(n)=\phi(m)$ iff $nn^T=mm^T$ iff $m=\pm n$. So $\phi$ induces a continuous bijection $S^2/\{\pm1\}=\mathbb{RP}^2\to C$. As $\mathbb{RP}^2$ is compact and $C$ is Hausdorff, this is a homeomorphism.

**Part 2: well defined.** If $K$ is skew, then $I-K$ is invertible: if $(I-K)x=0$ then $x^TKx=x^Tx$, but $x^TKx=0$ for skew $K$, so $x=0$. Put $U=(I+K)(I-K)^{-1}$. Since $I+K$ and $(I-K)^{-1}$ commute (both are functions of $K$),
$$U^TU=(I+K^T)^{-1}\ldots$$
more directly: $U^T=(I-K^T)^{-1}(I+K^T)=(I+K)^{-1}(I-K)$, and so $U^TU=(I+K)^{-1}(I-K)(I+K)(I-K)^{-1}=I$ (again using commutation of functions of $K$). Moreover $\det U=\det(I+K)/\det(I-K)=1$ because $\det(I+K)=\det\bigl((I+K)^T\bigr)=\det(I-K)$. So $U\in SO(3)$.

**Part 2: avoids $C$.** If $Ux=-x$ with $x\ne0$, put $y=(I-K)^{-1}x\ne0$; then $(I+K)y=-(I-K)y$, so $2y=0$, a contradiction. So $-1$ is not an eigenvalue of $U$. But every element of $C$ has the eigenvalue $-1$ (it is $-1$ on $n^\perp$). Hence the image lies in $SO(3)\setminus C$.

**Part 2: onto.** Let $U\in SO(3)$ without eigenvalue $-1$, so $I+U$ is invertible. Define $K=(U-I)(U+I)^{-1}$. Then, using $U^T=U^{-1}$,
$$K^T=(U^T+I)^{-1}(U^T-I)=(U^{-1}+I)^{-1}(U^{-1}-I)=(I+U)^{-1}(I-U)=-K,$$
where in the last step we multiplied numerator and denominator by $U$ and used that $(U-I)$ and $(U+I)^{-1}$ commute. So $K$ is skew, and solving $K(U+I)=U-I$ for $U$ gives $U=(I+K)(I-K)^{-1}$: the Cayley transform of $K$ is $U$.

**Part 2: injective.** From $U=(I+K)(I-K)^{-1}$ we get $U(I-K)=I+K$, hence $K(U+I)=U-I$, which determines $K=(U-I)(U+I)^{-1}$ uniquely.

**Remark.** Since $SO(3)\setminus C$ is the image of the *whole vector space* $\mathrm{Skew}_3\cong\mathbb{R}^3$ under a homeomorphism, $SO(3)=C\cup(\text{open ball})$: this is the cell decomposition of $SO(3)\cong\mathbb{RP}^3$ as the one-point-more... namely $\mathbb{RP}^3=\mathbb{RP}^2\cup\mathbb{R}^3$.

**Lesson.** The "cut locus" of a Lie group chart is the set where the chart fails to cover; here it is exactly the rotations by $\pi$.
@@@ mnappa-exponential-coordinates-on-so-3.md
---
title: Exponential coordinates on SO(3)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: exponential map, SO(3), Rodrigues formula, homeomorphism
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

For $v\in\mathbb{R}^3$ let $\hat v\in\mathrm{Skew}_3$ be the matrix of $x\mapsto v\times x$. Show that, with $\|\cdot\|$ the operator norm,
$$\exp:\{K\in\mathrm{Skew}_3:\ \|K\|<\pi\}\longrightarrow SO(3)\setminus\{U:\ \theta(U)=\pi\}$$
is a homeomorphism.

## Hint

Use Rodrigues' formula $e^{\hat v}=I+\dfrac{\sin|v|}{|v|}\hat v+\dfrac{1-\cos|v|}{|v|^2}\hat v^2$, and note $\hat v^3=-|v|^2\hat v$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The skew matrix $\hat v$ generates the rotation of angle $|v|$ about the axis $v$. The restriction $|v|<\pi$ removes the ambiguity ($e^{\hat v}=e^{\hat w}$ for $|v|=|w|=\pi$, $w=-v$, and for the periodicity).

**Step 1: norms.** Every skew $3\times3$ matrix is $\hat v$ for a unique $v$. Its eigenvalues are $0,\pm i|v|$ and it is normal, so its operator norm is $\|\hat v\|=|v|$. So the domain is the open ball $|v|<\pi$ in $\mathbb{R}^3$.

**Step 2: Rodrigues.** From $\hat v^3=-|v|^2\hat v$ the exponential series splits into odd and even powers:
$$e^{\hat v}=I+\Bigl(\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{(-1)^k|v|^{2k}}{(2k+1)!}\Bigr)\hat v+\Bigl(\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{(-1)^k|v|^{2k}}{(2k+2)!}\Bigr)\hat v^2=I+\frac{\sin|v|}{|v|}\hat v+\frac{1-\cos|v|}{|v|^2}\hat v^2.$$
This is the rotation by angle $|v|$ about the axis $v/|v|$. Its trace is $1+2\cos|v|$, so $\theta(e^{\hat v})=|v|$ for $|v|\le\pi$. In particular $\exp$ maps the ball $|v|<\pi$ into $\{\theta<\pi\}=SO(3)\setminus C$.

**Step 3: injective.** Suppose $e^{\hat v}=e^{\hat w}=U$ with $|v|,|w|<\pi$. Taking traces gives $\cos|v|=\cos|w|$, so $|v|=|w|=:r\in[0,\pi)$ (cosine is injective on $[0,\pi]$). If $r=0$, both are $0$. If $r>0$, subtract from Rodrigues its transpose ($\hat v^T=-\hat v$, $\hat v^2$ symmetric):
$$U-U^T=\frac{2\sin r}{r}\hat v,$$
and similarly with $w$. Since $\sin r>0$ on $(0,\pi)$, $\hat v=\hat w$, i.e. $v=w$.

**Step 4: surjective.** Let $U\in SO(3)$ with $\theta=\theta(U)<\pi$. If $\theta=0$, $U=I=e^{0}$. If $0<\theta<\pi$: by the normal form $U$ is a rotation by some angle $\pm\theta$ about a unit vector $n$; changing $n$ to $-n$ if necessary we may suppose the angle is $+\theta$. Then $U=e^{\hat v}$ with $v=\theta n$, $|v|=\theta<\pi$.

**Step 5: continuity of the inverse.** For $U\in SO(3)\setminus C$, Step 3 gives $\hat v=\dfrac{\theta(U)}{2\sin\theta(U)}(U-U^T)$ for $\theta>0$, and $\hat v=0$ for $U=I$. Since $\theta(U)$ is continuous (previous problem) and $\theta/\sin\theta\to1$ as $\theta\to0$, the right-hand side (with the value $1$ at $\theta=0$) is a continuous function of $U$ on $\{\theta<\pi\}$. So the inverse is continuous, and $\exp$ is a homeomorphism.

**Remark.** The boundary sphere $|v|=\pi$ is mapped onto $C\cong\mathbb{RP}^2$ by identifying antipodal points $v\sim-v$. This realises $SO(3)$ as the closed ball of radius $\pi$ with antipodal boundary points identified, i.e. $\mathbb{RP}^3$.

**Lesson.** Exponential coordinates are a chart; the injectivity radius is $\pi$, exactly the distance to the cut locus.
@@@ mnappa-no-continuous-square-root-on-gl2-plus.md
---
title: No continuous square root on GLâ‚‚âº(â„)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: square root, rotations, commutant, non-existence
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $GL_2^+(\mathbb{R})=\{A:\det A>0\}$. Show that there is no continuous map $S:GL_2^+(\mathbb{R})\to GL_2^+(\mathbb{R})$ with $S(A)^2=A$ for all $A$.

## Hint

Restrict $S$ to the rotations $R_\theta$, $\theta\in(0,2\pi)$, and use that a matrix commuting with $R_\theta$ ($\theta\ne0,\pi$) has the form $aI+bJ$, with $J=R_{\pi/2}$.

## Solution

**Idea.** On the circle of rotations $R_\theta=\cos\theta\,I+\sin\theta\,J$, the square root must be $\pm e^{i\theta/2}$, and there is no way to choose the sign continuously all the way around the circle: this is the usual non-existence of a continuous square root of $z\in S^1$.

**Step 1: the commutant.** Let $\theta\notin\pi\mathbb{Z}$. If $M$ commutes with $R_\theta=\cos\theta\,I+\sin\theta\,J$ then, as $\sin\theta\ne0$, $M$ commutes with $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$. Writing $M=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$, the relation $MJ=JM$ gives $MJ=\begin{pmatrix}b&-a\\d&-c\end{pmatrix}$ and $JM=\begin{pmatrix}-c&-d\\a&b\end{pmatrix}$, hence $d=a$, $c=-b$. So $M=aI-bJ$ is of the form $xI+yJ$, which we identify with the complex number $x+iy$ (the map $xI+yJ\mapsto x+iy$ is a ring isomorphism since $J^2=-I$). Under this identification $R_\theta=e^{i\theta}$.

**Step 2: sign choices.** Suppose $S$ exists. For $\theta\in(0,\pi)\cup(\pi,2\pi)$ the matrix $S(R_\theta)$ commutes with $S(R_\theta)^2=R_\theta$, so it corresponds to a complex number $z$ with $z^2=e^{i\theta}$: $z=\varepsilon(\theta)e^{i\theta/2}$, $\varepsilon(\theta)\in\{\pm1\}$. Since $S$ is continuous and $\theta\mapsto e^{i\theta/2}$ is continuous, $\varepsilon$ is continuous with values in $\{\pm1\}$, hence constant on each of the two intervals: $\varepsilon=\varepsilon_1$ on $(0,\pi)$ and $\varepsilon=\varepsilon_2$ on $(\pi,2\pi)$.

**Step 3: across $\theta=\pi$.** By continuity of $S$ at $R_\pi$, the limits of $S(R_\theta)$ as $\theta\to\pi^-$ and $\theta\to\pi^+$ coincide. They are $\varepsilon_1e^{i\pi/2}=\varepsilon_1i$ and $\varepsilon_2e^{i\pi/2}=\varepsilon_2i$. So $\varepsilon_1=\varepsilon_2=:\varepsilon$.

**Step 4: contradiction at $\theta=0\equiv2\pi$.** As $\theta\to0^+$, $S(R_\theta)\to\varepsilon e^{0}=\varepsilon I$. As $\theta\to2\pi^-$, $S(R_\theta)\to\varepsilon e^{i\pi}=-\varepsilon I$. But $R_\theta\to I$ in both cases, so by continuity of $S$ both limits must equal $S(I)$: $\varepsilon I=-\varepsilon I$, which is absurd.

**Remark.** The obstruction is topological: $GL_2^+(\mathbb{R})$ deformation retracts onto $SO(2)\cong S^1$, which is not simply connected. On the simply connected set $\{A:\ A\text{ has no real negative eigenvalue}\}$ a continuous principal square root does exist (given by a convergent series).

**Lesson.** Non-simply connected domains obstruct continuous choices of roots and logarithms.
@@@ mnappa-the-space-of-symmetric-matrices-with-distinct-eigenvalues-is-connected.md
---
title: Symmetric matrices with distinct eigenvalues form a connected set
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: connectedness, symmetric matrices, spectral theorem, SO(n)
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that the set $\Omega=\{S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n:\ \text{the eigenvalues of }S\text{ are pairwise distinct}\}$ is connected.

## Hint

Show that $\Omega$ is the continuous image of $SO(n)\times\{\lambda_1>\dots>\lambda_n\}$, a product of connected sets.

## Solution

**Idea.** The spectral theorem gives a parametrisation of $\Omega$ by an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors and an ordered list of eigenvalues. Both parameter spaces are connected (provided we use $SO(n)$ and not $O(n)$).

**Step 1: the parametrisation.** Let $\Delta=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^n:\lambda_1>\dots>\lambda_n\}$, an open convex set, hence connected. Define
$$\Phi:SO(n)\times\Delta\to\mathrm{Sym}_n,\qquad\Phi(Q,\lambda)=Q\operatorname{diag}(\lambda)Q^T.$$
$\Phi$ is continuous, and $\Phi(Q,\lambda)\in\Omega$ since its eigenvalues are the $\lambda_i$.

**Step 2: surjectivity onto $\Omega$.** Let $S\in\Omega$ with eigenvalues $\lambda_1>\dots>\lambda_n$. By the spectral theorem $S=Q\Lambda Q^T$ with $Q\in O(n)$ (columns = unit eigenvectors in the order of the eigenvalues). If $\det Q=-1$, replace $Q$ by $Q'=QD$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)$, i.e. flip the sign of the first eigenvector. Then $\det Q'=1$ and $Q'\Lambda Q'^T=QD\Lambda DQ^T=Q\Lambda Q^T=S$ because $D$ commutes with the diagonal matrix $\Lambda$. So $S=\Phi(Q',\lambda)$.

**Step 3: connectedness of the source.** $SO(n)$ is connected (standard: every $U\in SO(n)$ is orthogonally conjugate to a block diagonal matrix with rotation blocks $R_{\theta_j}$ and $1$'s, and $t\mapsto$ the same matrix with $\theta_j$ replaced by $t\theta_j$ is a path to the identity; the conjugating matrix can be taken in $SO(n)$ by flipping the sign of a $+1$ eigenvector or of one vector in a plane, which only changes $\theta_j$ to $-\theta_j$). A product of connected spaces is connected.

**Step 4: conclusion.** $\Omega=\Phi(SO(n)\times\Delta)$ is a continuous image of a connected space, hence connected.

**Remark.** $\Omega$ is open and dense in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$. Its complement (matrices with a repeated eigenvalue) has codimension $2$ in $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ (a double eigenvalue costs $3$ conditions but frees... one finds $\dim=\frac{n(n+1)}2-2$), which is why removing it does not disconnect $\mathrm{Sym}_n$. For $n=2$, $\mathrm{Sym}_2\cong\mathbb{R}^3$ and the complement is the line of scalar matrices.

**Lesson.** Parametrise a set by connected pieces and use that continuous images of connected sets are connected; use $SO(n)$ rather than $O(n)$ when connectedness is needed.
@@@ mnappa-invertible-skew-symmetric-matrices-and-the-pfaffian.md
---
title: Invertible skew-symmetric matrices have two components
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Pfaffian, connected components, skew-symmetric matrices
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $n$ be even and let $\mathcal S=\{K\in\mathrm{Skew}_n:\det K\ne0\}$. Show that $\mathcal S$ has exactly two connected components, distinguished by the sign of the Pfaffian $\operatorname{Pf}(K)$.

## Hint

Every nondegenerate skew matrix has the form $K=PJP^T$ with $P\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ and $J$ the standard block matrix, and $\operatorname{Pf}(PJP^T)=\det P\cdot\operatorname{Pf}(J)$.

## Solution

**Facts used.** The Pfaffian is a polynomial in the entries of $K$ with $\operatorname{Pf}(K)^2=\det K$ and $\operatorname{Pf}(PKP^T)=\det(P)\operatorname{Pf}(K)$. For $J=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n/2}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}$ one has $\operatorname{Pf}(J)=1$. We also use that $GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$ and $GL_n^-(\mathbb{R})$ are connected (standard).

**Step 1: the Pfaffian separates.** Since $\det K=\operatorname{Pf}(K)^2$, $K\in\mathcal S$ iff $\operatorname{Pf}(K)\ne0$. So $\mathcal S=\mathcal S_+\sqcup\mathcal S_-$ with $\mathcal S_\pm=\{\pm\operatorname{Pf}>0\}$. Each is open (Pfaffian is continuous) and they are disjoint, so there are at least two components if both are nonempty. They are nonempty: $J\in\mathcal S_+$ and $PJP^T\in\mathcal S_-$ for any $P$ with $\det P<0$.

**Step 2: normal form.** A real skew-symmetric matrix is orthogonally block diagonalisable: $K=Q\bigl(\bigoplus_js_j\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}\bigr)Q^T$ with $Q\in O(n)$ and, if $K$ is invertible, $s_j>0$. Put $D=\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{s_1},\sqrt{s_1},\sqrt{s_2},\sqrt{s_2},\dots)$. Then $\bigoplus s_j\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}=DJD^T$, so $K=PJP^T$ with $P=QD\in GL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Step 3: each sign set is connected.** If $K\in\mathcal S_+$ then $K=PJP^T$ and $\operatorname{Pf}(K)=\det P>0$, so $P\in GL_n^+$. Hence $\mathcal S_+=\{PJP^T:P\in GL_n^+\}$ is the continuous image of the connected group $GL_n^+(\mathbb{R})$, so is connected. Similarly $\mathcal S_-=\{PJP^T:P\in GL_n^-\}$ is the image of the connected set $GL_n^-$.

**Conclusion.** $\mathcal S$ has exactly the two components $\mathcal S_+$ and $\mathcal S_-$.

**Remark.** For $n$ odd every skew matrix is singular ($\det K=\det K^T=(-1)^n\det K$), so $\mathcal S$ is empty. For $n=2$, $\mathrm{Skew}_2\cong\mathbb{R}$ via $K=\begin{pmatrix}0&a\\-a&0\end{pmatrix}$ and $\mathcal S=\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$, with Pfaffian $a$: the two components are $a>0$ and $a<0$.

**Lesson.** The Pfaffian is the "square root of the determinant" that remembers an orientation; it detects the extra component.
@@@ mnappa-continuous-multiplicative-functions-on-gl-n.md
---
title: Continuous homomorphisms GLâ‚™(â„) â†’ â„*
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: homomorphism, commutator subgroup, determinant, continuous characters
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge2$ and let $f:GL_n(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}^\times$ be a continuous group homomorphism. Show that there is a real number $c$ such that either $f(A)=|\det A|^c$ for all $A$, or $f(A)=\operatorname{sgn}(\det A)\,|\det A|^c$ for all $A$.

## Hint

Show $f$ is trivial on $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ (which equals its own commutator subgroup, while $\mathbb{R}^\times$ is abelian). Then $f$ depends only on $\det A$.

## Solution

**Idea.** A homomorphism to an abelian group kills commutators. Since $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is generated by commutators, $f$ factors through the determinant. The problem then reduces to continuous homomorphisms $\mathbb{R}^\times\to\mathbb{R}^\times$.

**Step 1: $f$ is trivial on commutators.** For $A,B$ in $GL_n$, $f(ABA^{-1}B^{-1})=f(A)f(B)f(A)^{-1}f(B)^{-1}=1$, because $\mathbb{R}^\times$ is abelian.

**Step 2: $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is generated by commutators.** $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is generated by elementary matrices $E_{ij}(t)=I+tE_{ij}$ ($i\ne j$) (row reduction). For $n\ge3$ and distinct $i,j,k$, $[E_{ik}(a),E_{kj}(b)]=E_{ij}(ab)$, so every $E_{ij}(t)$ is a commutator (take $a=t$, $b=1$). For $n=2$, let $D=\operatorname{diag}(2,\tfrac12)\in GL_2$; then $DE_{12}(s)D^{-1}=E_{12}(4s)$, so $E_{12}(3s)=DE_{12}(s)D^{-1}E_{12}(-s)=[D,E_{12}(s)]$ is a commutator in $GL_2$, and so is every $E_{12}(t)$ (take $s=t/3$); similarly $E_{21}(t)$. In all cases the generators of $SL_n$ lie in the commutator subgroup of $GL_n$. By Step 1, $f\equiv1$ on $SL_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Step 3: factorisation through $\det$.** Let $\Delta(d)=\operatorname{diag}(d,1,\dots,1)$. For $A\in GL_n$ with $\det A=d$, $A\Delta(d)^{-1}\in SL_n$, so
$$f(A)=f\bigl(A\Delta(d)^{-1}\bigr)f(\Delta(d))=g(d),\qquad g(d):=f(\Delta(d)).$$
Since $\Delta(d)\Delta(d')=\Delta(dd')$, $g:\mathbb{R}^\times\to\mathbb{R}^\times$ is a continuous homomorphism.

**Step 4: continuous homomorphisms $\mathbb{R}^\times\to\mathbb{R}^\times$.** For $x>0$, $g(x)=g(\sqrt x)^2>0$. So $h(s)=\ln g(e^s)$ is a continuous additive function $\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}$, hence $h(s)=cs$ for some $c$ (Cauchy's equation with continuity). Thus $g(x)=x^c$ for $x>0$. Finally $g(-1)^2=g(1)=1$ gives $g(-1)=\pm1$, and $g(-x)=g(-1)g(x)$. So $g(d)=|d|^c$ (if $g(-1)=1$) or $g(d)=\operatorname{sgn}(d)|d|^c$ (if $g(-1)=-1$).

**Conclusion.** $f(A)=g(\det A)$ is of the stated form.

**Remark.** Continuity is needed only to exclude pathological homomorphisms (as for Cauchy's equation). For $n=1$ the same classification holds for continuous homomorphisms of $\mathbb{R}^\times$ to itself, but the factorisation argument is not needed.

**Lesson.** Homomorphisms into abelian groups factor through the abelianisation; $GL_n(\mathbb{R})^{ab}\cong\mathbb{R}^\times$ via $\det$.
@@@ mnappa-the-quotient-of-matrices-by-orthogonal-left-multiplication.md
---
title: M_n(â„)/O(n) is the cone of positive semidefinite matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: quotient space, proper maps, positive semidefinite, orbit space
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $O(n)$ act on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ by left multiplication, $U\cdot A=UA$. Show that the map $p(A)=A^TA$ induces a homeomorphism from the quotient space $M_n(\mathbb{R})/O(n)$ onto the cone $\mathrm{Sym}_n^+$ of positive semidefinite matrices.

## Hint

Show that $p$ is continuous, onto, closed, and that its fibres are exactly the $O(n)$-orbits.

## Solution

**Idea.** A continuous closed surjection is a quotient map. So it suffices to show that $p$ is a closed continuous surjection whose fibres are the orbits.

**Step 1: values and surjectivity.** $A^TA$ is symmetric positive semidefinite ($x^TA^TAx=|Ax|^2\ge0$). Conversely if $S\in\mathrm{Sym}_n^+$, then $S=(S^{1/2})^TS^{1/2}=p(S^{1/2})$ with $S^{1/2}$ the positive square root. So $p:M_n\to\mathrm{Sym}_n^+$ is onto, and continuous (polynomial).

**Step 2: constant on orbits.** $(UA)^T(UA)=A^TU^TUA=A^TA$.

**Step 3: fibres are orbits.** Suppose $A^TA=B^TB$. Then $|Ax|^2=x^TA^TAx=|Bx|^2$ for all $x$. Define $V:\operatorname{im}A\to\operatorname{im}B$ by $V(Ax)=Bx$. It is well defined (if $Ax=Ax'$ then $|B(x-x')|=|A(x-x')|=0$) and norm-preserving, so it is an isometry between subspaces of equal dimension ($\operatorname{rank}A=\operatorname{rank}B$ since $\ker A=\ker B$). Extend $V$ by any isometry $(\operatorname{im}A)^\perp\to(\operatorname{im}B)^\perp$ to an orthogonal matrix $U$. Then $UAx=Bx$ for all $x$, i.e. $B=UA$.

**Step 4: $p$ is closed.** Let $F\subset M_n$ be closed and $p(A_k)\to S$ with $A_k\in F$. Then $\|A_k\|_F^2=\operatorname{tr}(A_k^TA_k)=\operatorname{tr}p(A_k)$ is bounded, so a subsequence $A_{k_j}\to A$; $A\in F$ and $p(A)=S$ by continuity. So $S\in p(F)$, and $p(F)$ is closed.

**Step 5: conclusion.** $p$ is a closed continuous surjection, hence a quotient map; since its fibres are the $O(n)$-orbits, $\mathrm{Sym}_n^+\cong M_n(\mathbb{R})/O(n)$.

**Remark.** The inverse can be seen as polar decomposition: the class of $A$ corresponds to the positive part $|A|=(A^TA)^{1/2}$, and $A=U|A|$ with $U$ orthogonal.

**Lesson.** To identify an orbit space, find a continuous closed (or proper) map that separates orbits.
@@@ mnappa-the-orbit-space-of-symmetric-matrices-under-conjugation.md
---
title: Sym_n/O(n) is a closed Weyl chamber
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: orbit space, eigenvalues, Weyl chamber, quotient
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $O(n)$ act on $\mathrm{Sym}_n$ by conjugation, $U\cdot S=USU^T$. Show that the quotient $\mathrm{Sym}_n/O(n)$ is homeomorphic to the closed chamber $\mathcal C=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^n:\lambda_1\ge\dots\ge\lambda_n\}$ via the ordered eigenvalue map.

## Hint

Show that $\lambda:S\mapsto(\lambda_1(S)\ge\dots\ge\lambda_n(S))$ is continuous, closed and has the conjugation orbits as fibres. Continuity comes from the Courantâ€“Fischer min-max formula.

## Solution

**Idea.** The spectral theorem says two symmetric matrices are orthogonally conjugate iff they have the same eigenvalues, so the orbits are the fibres of $\lambda$.

**Step 1: fibres.** If $S=Q\Lambda Q^T$ and $T=Q'\Lambda Q'^T$ with the same ordered diagonal $\Lambda$, then $T=(Q'Q^T)S(Q'Q^T)^T$ is a conjugate of $S$ by an orthogonal matrix. Conversely conjugation preserves eigenvalues.

**Step 2: surjectivity.** For $\mu\in\mathcal C$, $\lambda(\operatorname{diag}\mu)=\mu$.

**Step 3: continuity.** By the Courantâ€“Fischer formula
$$\lambda_k(S)=\max_{\dim V=k}\ \min_{x\in V,\ |x|=1}x^TSx.$$
If $\|S-T\|\le\delta$ (operator norm), then $|x^TSx-x^TTx|\le\delta$ for unit $x$, so each min changes by at most $\delta$, and so does the max: $|\lambda_k(S)-\lambda_k(T)|\le\|S-T\|$ (Weyl's inequality). So $\lambda$ is $1$-Lipschitz in each coordinate.

**Step 4: closedness.** Let $F\subset\mathrm{Sym}_n$ be closed with $\lambda(S_m)\to\mu$, $S_m\in F$. Then $\|S_m\|=\max_i|\lambda_i(S_m)|$ is bounded, so $S_{m_j}\to S\in F$ along a subsequence and $\lambda(S)=\mu$ by continuity. So $\lambda(F)$ is closed.

**Step 5: conclusion.** $\lambda$ is a continuous closed surjection with fibres equal to the orbits, so it induces a homeomorphism $\mathrm{Sym}_n/O(n)\to\mathcal C$.

**Remark.** The same pattern works for $\mathrm{Herm}_n/U(n)$ and, with singular values in place of eigenvalues, for the next problem. The chamber is a cone with non-empty interior; the interior corresponds to matrices with simple spectrum.

**Lesson.** "Eigenvalues are a complete conjugacy invariant" is a topological statement once properness is checked.
@@@ mnappa-the-orbit-space-of-matrices-under-orthogonal-bi-multiplication.md
---
title: M_n(â„)/(O(n)Ã—O(n)) is the space of singular values
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: singular values, SVD, orbit space, Weyl inequality
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $O(n)\times O(n)$ act on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ by $(U,V)\cdot A=UAV^T$. Show that the quotient is homeomorphic to $\{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^n:\sigma_1\ge\dots\ge\sigma_n\ge0\}$ via the singular values.

## Hint

Use the singular value decomposition. Continuity follows from $\sigma_i(A)=\sqrt{\lambda_i(A^TA)}$ and the continuity of ordered eigenvalues.

## Solution

**Idea.** Same strategy as for conjugation on $\mathrm{Sym}_n$: the SVD says the orbits are the fibres of the singular value map, and boundedness of $\|A\|=\sigma_1$ gives closedness.

**Step 1: the map.** Let $\sigma(A)=(\sigma_1(A)\ge\dots\ge\sigma_n(A)\ge0)$, with $\sigma_i(A)=\sqrt{\lambda_i(A^TA)}$. It is constant on orbits: $(UAV^T)^T(UAV^T)=VA^TAV^T$ has the same eigenvalues as $A^TA$.

**Step 2: fibres are orbits.** By the SVD, $A=U_1\Sigma V_1^T$ with $\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma(A))$ and $U_1,V_1\in O(n)$. If $\sigma(B)=\sigma(A)$ then $B=U_2\Sigma V_2^T$, so $B=(U_2U_1^T)A(V_2V_1^T)^T$ lies in the orbit of $A$.

**Step 3: surjective, continuous.** $\sigma(\operatorname{diag}\mu)=\mu$ for $\mu$ in the chamber. Continuity: $A\mapsto A^TA$ is continuous, the ordered eigenvalues are continuous (Weyl), and $\sqrt\cdot$ is continuous on $[0,\infty)$.

**Step 4: closed.** If $\sigma(A_m)\to\mu$ then $\|A_m\|=\sigma_1(A_m)$ (operator norm) is bounded, so a subsequence of $A_m$ converges to some $A$ in $F$, with $\sigma(A)=\mu$.

**Step 5: conclusion.** $\sigma$ is a continuous closed surjection with orbits as fibres, hence descends to a homeomorphism $M_n/(O(n)\times O(n))\to\{\sigma_1\ge\dots\ge\sigma_n\ge0\}$.

**Remark.** The singular values are exactly the Euclidean distances needed to describe an $n$-dimensional ellipsoid $A(B^n)$: the quotient forgets the orientation of the ellipsoid in the domain and codomain and keeps only its semi-axes.

**Lesson.** Orbit spaces of compact group actions on Euclidean spaces are often Weyl chambers; SVD is the case $O(n)\times O(n)$.
@@@ mnappa-rank-one-projections-and-projective-space.md
---
title: Rank-one orthogonal projections form â„Pâ¿â»Â¹
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: projective space, projections, compact Hausdorff, homeomorphism
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that the space $\mathcal P_1=\{P\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\ P=P^T=P^2,\ \operatorname{rank}P=1\}$ of rank-one orthogonal projections is homeomorphic to the real projective space $\mathbb{RP}^{n-1}$.

## Hint

Map a unit vector $x$ to $xx^T$.

## Solution

**Idea.** A line through the origin is determined by a unit vector up to sign, and the orthogonal projection onto it is $xx^T$, which does not see the sign.

**Step 1: description of $\mathcal P_1$.** If $P\in\mathcal P_1$, its image is a line $\mathbb{R}x$ with $|x|=1$, and $P$ is the orthogonal projection onto it: $Py=(x\cdot y)x=xx^Ty$. So $P=xx^T$. Conversely, for a unit vector $x$, $xx^T$ is symmetric, satisfies $(xx^T)^2=x(x^Tx)x^T=xx^T$, and has rank $1$.

**Step 2: the map.** $\phi:S^{n-1}\to\mathcal P_1$, $\phi(x)=xx^T$, is continuous and onto by Step 1. Moreover $xx^T=yy^T$ iff $x=\pm y$: apply both sides to $x$ to get $x=(y\cdot x)y$, so $x$ is a multiple of $y$, and both are unit vectors.

**Step 3: homeomorphism.** $\phi$ induces a continuous bijection $\bar\phi:\mathbb{RP}^{n-1}=S^{n-1}/\{\pm1\}\to\mathcal P_1$. The source is compact (a quotient of the sphere) and $\mathcal P_1\subset M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is Hausdorff. A continuous bijection from a compact space to a Hausdorff space is a homeomorphism.

**Remark.** The same construction with $x\in\mathbb{C}^n$ and $xx^*$ gives $\mathbb{CP}^{n-1}$ as the space of rank-one Hermitian projections. For $n=2$, $\mathcal P_1$ is a circle: $\mathbb{RP}^1\cong S^1$.

**Lesson.** Compact-to-Hausdorff continuous bijections are homeomorphisms: use them to identify quotient spaces.
@@@ mnappa-the-grassmannian-is-compact-and-connected.md
---
title: The Grassmannian of orthogonal projections is compact and connected
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: Grassmannian, compactness, connectedness, SO(n) orbit
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $\mathrm{Gr}(k,n)=\{P\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\ P=P^T=P^2,\ \operatorname{rank}P=k\}$, $0\le k\le n$. Show that $\mathrm{Gr}(k,n)$ is compact and connected.

## Hint

Show that it is the orbit of $E_k=\operatorname{diag}(I_k,0)$ under conjugation by $SO(n)$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Closed and bounded gives compactness. Being the orbit of a point under the connected group $SO(n)$ gives connectedness.

**Step 1: compact.** A symmetric idempotent has eigenvalues $0$ or $1$, so $\operatorname{rank}P=\operatorname{tr}P$. Hence $\mathrm{Gr}(k,n)=\{P:\ P^T=P,\ P^2=P,\ \operatorname{tr}P=k\}$ is defined by polynomial equations, so it is closed. It is bounded: $\|P\|_F^2=\operatorname{tr}(P^TP)=\operatorname{tr}P=k$. By Heineâ€“Borel it is compact.

**Step 2: it is one orbit.** Let $P\in\mathrm{Gr}(k,n)$. By the spectral theorem, $P=UE_kU^T$ for some $U\in O(n)$ (an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors, the first $k$ spanning the image). If $\det U=-1$ and $k<n$, replace $U$ by $U'=UD$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(1,\dots,1,-1)$: $D$ commutes with $E_k$ (the last diagonal entry of $E_k$ is $0$), so $U'E_kU'^T=UDE_kDU^T=UE_kU^T=P$, and $\det U'=1$. So every $P$ is of the form $UE_kU^T$ with $U\in SO(n)$. (If $k=n$, $P=I$ and the set is a single point.)

**Step 3: connected.** The map $SO(n)\to\mathrm{Gr}(k,n)$, $U\mapsto UE_kU^T$, is continuous and onto, and $SO(n)$ is connected. Hence $\mathrm{Gr}(k,n)$ is connected.

**Remark.** $\mathrm{Gr}(k,n)$ is the set of $k$-dimensional subspaces of $\mathbb{R}^n$ (a projection is determined by its image). It is a manifold of dimension $k(n-k)$, and $\mathrm{Gr}(1,n)=\mathbb{RP}^{n-1}$ (previous problem). Note: using $O(n)$ instead of $SO(n)$ in Step 3 would not suffice since $O(n)$ is disconnected.

**Lesson.** An orbit of a connected group is connected; always check whether you need $SO(n)$ or $O(n)$.
@@@ mnappa-the-adjoint-quotient-is-not-hausdorff.md
---
title: The quotient of M_n(â„) by conjugation is not Hausdorff
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l3
difficulty: 3
tags: quotient topology, conjugation, Jordan block, separation axioms
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Let $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ act on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ by conjugation and give the orbit space $M_n(\mathbb{R})/GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ the quotient topology. Show that for $n\ge2$ it is not Hausdorff, and not even $T_1$.

## Hint

Compare the orbit of the identity with the orbit of the Jordan block $J=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\oplus I_{n-2}$. The matrices $J_t=\begin{pmatrix}1&t\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\oplus I_{n-2}$ tend to $I$ as $t\to0$ but are all conjugate to $J$ for $t\ne0$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The orbit of the Jordan block is not closed: its closure contains the orbit $\{I\}$ of the identity. In the quotient, any neighbourhood of $[I]$ then contains $[J]$.

**Step 1: $J_t$ is conjugate to $J$.** For $t\ne0$, let $D_t=\operatorname{diag}(t,1,\dots,1)$. Then
$$D_tJD_t^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&t\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\oplus I_{n-2}=J_t.$$
(Check in the $2\times2$ block: $\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1/t&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}t&t\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1/t&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&t\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$.) Also $J_t\to I$ as $t\to0$, and $J\ne I$ (and $J$ is not conjugate to $I$, since $PIP^{-1}=I$).

**Step 2: neighbourhoods of $[I]$ contain $[J]$.** Let $\pi:M_n\to M_n/GL_n$ be the quotient map and $W$ an open set of the quotient containing $[I]$. Then $\pi^{-1}(W)$ is open, contains $I$, and is a union of orbits. As $J_t\to I$, $J_t\in\pi^{-1}(W)$ for small $t\ne0$; since $J_t$ is conjugate to $J$ and $\pi^{-1}(W)$ is a union of orbits, $J\in\pi^{-1}(W)$, i.e. $[J]\in W$.

**Step 3: conclusion.** Every open neighbourhood of $[I]$ contains the different point $[J]$. So no open sets separate $[I]$ and $[J]$ (not Hausdorff), and there is no neighbourhood of $[I]$ missing $[J]$ (not $T_1$: the point $[J]$ is not closed, because every neighbourhood of $[I]$ meets $\{[J]\}$).

**Remark.** The quotient is well behaved on the open set of matrices with $n$ distinct real eigenvalues, where conjugacy classes are classified by the eigenvalues (and are closed). The "bad" behaviour comes from non-diagonalisable matrices: the closure of a conjugacy class contains matrices with the same characteristic polynomial but "more diagonal" Jordan form.

**Lesson.** Closures of orbits that are not closed destroy separation axioms in the quotient.
@@@ mnappa-the-inverse-does-not-extend-continuously-to-singular-matrices.md
---
title: The inverse does not extend continuously across singular matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr/mn-applications
level: l2
difficulty: 1
tags: inversion, unboundedness, continuity, extension
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Show that there is no continuous map $F:M_n(\mathbb{R})\to M_n(\mathbb{R})$ with $F(A)=A^{-1}$ for every invertible $A$.

## Hint

Look at the matrices $\varepsilon I$ as $\varepsilon\to0^+$.

## Solution

**Idea.** A continuous map is bounded on a neighbourhood of any point, but the inverse blows up as we approach a singular matrix.

**Step 1: suppose $F$ exists.** Then $F$ is continuous at $0$, so there are $\delta>0$ and $M<\infty$ with $\|F(A)\|\le M$ whenever $\|A\|<\delta$ (norm of your choice).

**Step 2: test matrices.** For $0<\varepsilon<\delta/\sqrt n$ the matrix $\varepsilon I$ is invertible with $\|\varepsilon I\|_F=\varepsilon\sqrt n<\delta$, and
$$\|F(\varepsilon I)\|_F=\|\varepsilon^{-1}I\|_F=\frac{\sqrt n}{\varepsilon}.$$
This tends to $+\infty$ as $\varepsilon\to0^+$, contradicting $\|F(\varepsilon I)\|\le M$.

**Remark.** The same argument works at any singular matrix $A_0$. Choose $x\ne0$ with $A_0x=0$. Then $(A_0+\varepsilon I)x=\varepsilon x$, so for small $\varepsilon\ne0$ (where $A_0+\varepsilon I$ is invertible) $(A_0+\varepsilon I)^{-1}x=x/\varepsilon$, hence $\|(A_0+\varepsilon I)^{-1}\|_{op}\ge1/\varepsilon\to\infty$. So the inverse cannot be extended continuously to any point of the singular set: $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is the maximal domain on which inversion is continuous (in the sense of extension to a larger set of limit points).

**Lesson.** Unboundedness near a point kills any continuous extension.

@@@ topology-MnR-convergence-entries.md
---
title: Convergence in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ via entries
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: matrix convergence, entrywise convergence, sequence of matrices
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

A sequence $(A^{(k)})_{k\ge1}$ of matrices in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is said to *converge entrywise* to $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ if for every $i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$, the real sequence $(a^{(k)}_{ij})_{k\ge1}$ converges to $a_{ij}$. Show that entrywise convergence is equivalent to convergence for the metric
$$d(A,B)=\max_{i,j}|a_{ij}-b_{ij}|.$$

## Hint

Compare $d(A^{(k)},A)$ with the largest entrywise difference; use the fact that a maximum of finitely many quantities vanishes iff each one vanishes.

## Solution

**$(\Leftarrow)$.** Suppose $d(A^{(k)},A)\to0$. For each $(i,j)$, $|a^{(k)}_{ij}-a_{ij}|\le d(A^{(k)},A)\to0$, so entrywise convergence holds.

**$(\Rightarrow)$.** Suppose entrywise convergence. For each $(i,j)$, $|a^{(k)}_{ij}-a_{ij}|\to0$. There are finitely many pairs $(i,j)$, so
$$d(A^{(k)},A)=\max_{i,j}|a^{(k)}_{ij}-a_{ij}|\to0.$$

**Conclusion.** Entrywise convergence and convergence for the sup metric coincide on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

@@@ topology-MnR-metrics-equivalent.md
---
title: Equivalent metrics on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: equivalent metrics, norms on matrices, finite dimension
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Consider the following three norms on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$:
$$\|A\|_\infty=\max_{i,j}|a_{ij}|,\qquad \|A\|_1=\sum_{i,j}|a_{ij}|,\qquad \|A\|_F=\left(\sum_{i,j}|a_{ij}|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$
Show that they are equivalent.

## Hint

Bound each by the others using the number of entries $n^2$.

## Solution

**Bounds.** For every $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$:
$$\|A\|_\infty\le\|A\|_F\le\|A\|_1\le n^2\|A\|_\infty.$$
- $\|A\|_\infty\le\|A\|_F$: each entry squared is $\le\sum|a_{ij}|^2$.
- $\|A\|_F\le\|A\|_1$: from $\|A\|_F^2=\sum|a_{ij}|^2\le(\sum|a_{ij}|)^2$.
- $\|A\|_1\le n^2\|A\|_\infty$: sum of $n^2$ terms each $\le\|A\|_\infty$.

**Conclusion.** The three norms define the same topology on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

@@@ topology-MnR-complete.md
---
title: Completeness of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: completeness, Cauchy sequence, identification with R^{n^2}
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Show that $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ equipped with the sup metric is complete.

## Hint

Identify $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ with $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$; use completeness of $\mathbb{R}$.

## Solution

**Identification.** The map $\Phi:M_n(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$ sending $A$ to its entries is an isometry for the sup metric.

**Cauchy sequences.** A Cauchy sequence in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ corresponds to a Cauchy sequence in $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$. Since $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$ is complete, it converges; identifying back gives convergence in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Conclusion.** $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is complete.

@@@ topology-MnR-closed-subgroups.md
---
title: Classical closed subgroups of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: closed subgroups, GL_n, SL_n, O_n, determinant
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Show that the following subsets of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ are closed:
$$SL_n(\mathbb{R})=\{A:\det A=1\},\qquad O_n(\mathbb{R})=\{A:A^TA=I_n\},\qquad S_n(\mathbb{R})=\{A:A^T=A\}.$$

## Hint

Each is the preimage of a closed set under a continuous map.

## Solution

**$SL_n$.** The determinant $\det:M_n(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}$ is continuous (polynomial in the entries). $SL_n=\det^{-1}(\{1\})$ is closed as preimage of the closed set $\{1\}$.

**$O_n$.** The map $\Phi(A)=A^TA-I_n$ is continuous into the symmetric matrices. $O_n=\Phi^{-1}(\{0\})$ is closed.

**$S_n$.** The map $A\mapsto A^T-A$ is linear (continuous). $S_n$ is its kernel, hence closed.

**Conclusion.** $SL_n$, $O_n$, $S_n$ are closed subgroups (or subspace) of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

@@@ topology-MnR-open-GLn.md
---
title: $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is open in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: GL_n, open set, determinant continuity
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Show that $GL_n(\mathbb{R})=\{A:\det A\neq0\}$ is open in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

$\det$ is continuous and $GL_n=\det^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})$.

## Solution

**Argument.** $\det:M_n(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}$ is continuous, and $\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$ is open. Therefore $GL_n=\det^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})$ is open.

**Conclusion.** $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ is open, and its complement $SL_n$ (as well as $\{\det=0\}$) is closed.

@@@ topology-MnR-compact-groups.md
---
title: Compactness of orthogonal and orthogonal groups
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: compactness, O_n, SO_n, boundedness
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Show that $O_n(\mathbb{R})$ and $SO_n(\mathbb{R})=\{A\in O_n:\det A=1\}$ are compact subsets of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

$O_n$ is closed (previous exercise) and bounded: every entry of an orthogonal matrix satisfies $|a_{ij}|\le1$.

## Solution

**Closed.** $O_n=\Phi^{-1}(\{0\})$ with $\Phi(A)=A^TA-I_n$, continuous: closed.

**Bounded.** For $A\in O_n$ and any row $i$: $\|(\text{row }i)\|_2=1$, so $|a_{ij}|\le1$. Hence $\|A\|_\infty\le1$: bounded.

**Conclusion.** $O_n$ is closed and bounded, hence compact (Heine–Borel in $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$). $SO_n$ is closed (as $O_n\cap SL_n$) and bounded, hence compact.

@@@ topology-MnR-compact-trace-zero.md
---
title: A non-compact closed subspace: trace-zero matrices
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: closed unbounded, trace, non-compactness
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $H=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb{R}):\operatorname{tr}(A)=0\}$. Show that $H$ is closed but not compact.

## Hint

$H$ is the kernel of the trace functional; show it is unbounded.

## Solution

**Closed.** The trace is linear, hence continuous; $H=\operatorname{tr}^{-1}(\{0\})$ is closed.

**Not compact.** The matrices $A_k=k\,E_{12}$ have trace $0$ for every $k$, but $\|A_k\|_\infty=k\to+\infty$: unbounded.

**Conclusion.** $H$ is closed and unbounded, hence not compact.

@@@ topology-MnR-interior.md
---
title: Interior and boundary of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: interior, boundary, GL_n
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Determine the interior and the boundary of $GL_n(\mathbb{R})$ in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

$GL_n$ is open; its boundary is the set of singular matrices.

## Solution

**Interior.** Since $GL_n$ is open, $\mathring{GL_n}=GL_n$.

**Closure.** Every singular matrix $A$ is a limit of invertible matrices: $A+\varepsilon I$ is invertible for small $\varepsilon>0$. So $\overline{GL_n}=M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

**Boundary.** $\partial GL_n=\overline{GL_n}\setminus\mathring{GL_n}=M_n(\mathbb{R})\setminus GL_n$, the set of singular matrices.

**Conclusion.** Interior $=GL_n$; boundary $=$ singular matrices.

@@@ topology-MnR-distance-to-groups.md
---
title: Distance to $O_n$ and polar decomposition (statement)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: distance to O_n, singular values, polar decomposition
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Show that for any $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$, the distance to $O_n$ for $\|\cdot\|_2$ is
$$\operatorname{dist}_2(A,O_n)=\max_i\bigl|\sigma_i(A)-1\bigr|,$$
where $\sigma_i(A)$ are the singular values of $A$.

## Hint

Use the polar decomposition $A=U\Sigma V^T$ with $U,V\in O_n$, $\Sigma$ diagonal nonnegative.

## Solution

**Polar decomposition.** Every real $A$ is $U\Sigma V^T$ with $U,V$ orthogonal and $\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_1,\ldots,\sigma_n)\ge0$.

**Lower bound.** For any $Q\in O_n$, $\|A-Q\|_2=\|\Sigma-V^TQU\|_2=\|\Sigma-R\|_2$ where $R=V^TQU\in O_n$. Since $R$ is orthogonal, its singular values are all $1$, so $\|\Sigma-R\|_2\ge\max_i|\sigma_i-1|$.

**Upper bound.** Setting $Q=UV^T\in O_n$, one has $\|A-Q\|_2=\|\Sigma-I\|_2=\max_i|\sigma_i-1|$.

**Conclusion.** $\operatorname{dist}_2(A,O_n)=\max_i|\sigma_i(A)-1|$.

@@@ topology-MnR-matrix-exponential.md
---
title: Continuity of the matrix exponential
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: matrix exponential, continuity, series
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Show that $\exp:M_n(\mathbb{R})\to M_n(\mathbb{R})$, $\exp(A)=\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{A^k}{k!}$, is continuous.

## Hint

Bound $\|\exp(A)-\exp(B)\|$ using the series and the inequality $\|A^k-B^k\|\le\|A-B\|\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\|A\|^j\|B\|^{k-1-j}$. Or use local boundedness and uniform convergence on bounded sets.

## Solution

**Local bound.** Fix $R>0$ and $A,B$ with $\|A\|,\|B\|\le R$. For each $k\ge1$:
$$\|A^k-B^k\|\le\|A-B\|\cdot kR^{k-1}.$$
Summing: $\|\exp(A)-\exp(B)\|\le\|A-B\|\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{kR^{k-1}}{k!}=\|A-B\|\,e^R$.

**Conclusion.** $\exp$ is locally Lipschitz on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$, hence continuous.

@@@ topology-MnR-eigenvalue-continuity.md
---
title: Continuity of eigenvalues (statement)
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: eigenvalues, continuity, roots of polynomial
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Show that the multiset of eigenvalues of a matrix depends continuously on the matrix entries.

## Hint

Eigenvalues are roots of the characteristic polynomial $\chi_A(\lambda)=\det(\lambda I-A)$, whose coefficients depend continuously on $A$; the roots of a monic polynomial depend continuously on its coefficients.

## Solution

**Characteristic polynomial.** $A\mapsto\chi_A$ is continuous (composition of $\det$ and affine maps).

**Root continuity.** A monic polynomial's roots (with multiplicity) depend continuously on the coefficients in the Hausdorff metric on finite multisets of $\mathbb{C}$.

**Conclusion.** Eigenvalues depend continuously on the matrix.

@@@ topology-MnR-separability.md
---
title: Separability of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: separability, dense countable subset, rational matrices
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Show that $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is separable.

## Hint

Consider matrices with rational entries.

## Solution

**Countable dense subset.** Let $M_n(\mathbb{Q})$ be the set of matrices with rational entries. It is countable (finite product of countable sets).

**Density.** For any $A\in M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and $\varepsilon>0$, approximate each entry $a_{ij}$ by a rational $q_{ij}$ with $|a_{ij}-q_{ij}|<\varepsilon$. The matrix $Q=(q_{ij})$ satisfies $\|A-Q\|_\infty<\varepsilon$.

**Conclusion.** $M_n(\mathbb{Q})$ is countable and dense in $M_n(\mathbb{R})$, which is separable.

@@@ topology-MnR-norm-submultiplicative.md
---
title: Submultiplicativity of matrix norms
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: submultiplicative norms, operator norm, matrix norm
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Define $\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\sup_{\|x\|=1}\|Ax\|$ (for $\|\cdot\|$ the Euclidean norm on $\mathbb{R}^n$). Show that $\|AB\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|B\|_{\mathrm{op}}$.

## Hint

Write $\|ABx\|\le\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|Bx\|\le\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|B\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|x\|$.

## Solution

**Inequality.** For every $x$:
$$\|ABx\|\le\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|Bx\|\le\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|B\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|x\|.$$
Taking the supremum over $\|x\|=1$: $\|AB\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|B\|_{\mathrm{op}}$.

**Conclusion.** $\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ is a submultiplicative matrix norm.

@@@ topology-MnR-continuity-determinant.md
---
title: Continuity of the determinant
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: determinant, continuity, polynomial
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Show that $\det:M_n(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}$ is continuous.

## Hint

The determinant is a polynomial in the entries.

## Solution

**Polynomial in entries.** By the Leibniz formula,
$$\det(A)=\sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^n a_{i,\sigma(i)}.$$
Each term is a product of continuous coordinate functions; the sum is continuous.

**Conclusion.** $\det$ is continuous, even polynomial in the entries.

@@@ topology-MnR-homeomorphism-quadratic.md
---
title: A homeomorphism of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: homeomorphism, matrix map, inverse map
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Consider $\Phi:M_n(\mathbb{R})\to S_n(\mathbb{R})\times A_n(\mathbb{R})$ defined by $\Phi(A)=(A+A^T,A-A^T)$, where $S_n$ (resp. $A_n$) is the set of symmetric (resp. antisymmetric) matrices. Show $\Phi$ is a homeomorphism.

## Hint

Explicitly find the inverse; both maps are polynomial.

## Solution

**Inverse.** Given $(S,A)$ with $S$ symmetric and $A$ antisymmetric, set $M=\frac{S+A}{2}$. Then $M+M^T=S$, $M-M^T=A$; indeed $M=\frac{S+A}{2}$. So $\Phi^{-1}(S,A)=\frac{S+A}{2}$.

**Continuity.** Both $\Phi$ and $\Phi^{-1}$ are linear maps between finite-dimensional spaces, hence continuous.

**Conclusion.** $\Phi$ is a homeomorphism.

@@@ topology-MnR-summary.md
---
title: Summary of the topology of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$
category: topology/topology-of-mnr
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: summary, matrix topology, M_n(R)
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Summarize the main topological properties of $M_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Review convergence, completeness, compactness, groups.

## Solution

**Identification.** $M_n(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$ via entries; all norms are equivalent.

**Convergence.** Entrywise convergence $=$ convergence for any matrix norm.

**Completeness.** $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is a Banach space.

**Compactness.** Heine–Borel: compact $\iff$ closed and bounded. $O_n$ is compact (closed + bounded); $GL_n$, $SL_n$, $S_n$ are closed or open subsets as appropriate.

**Subgroups.** $GL_n$ is open; $SL_n$, $O_n$, $SO_n$ are closed; $O_n$, $SO_n$ are compact.

**Matrix exponential.** $\exp$ is continuous, even locally Lipschitz.

**Eigenvalues.** Depend continuously on the matrix entries.

**Separability.** $M_n(\mathbb{Q})$ is a countable dense subset.

**Conclusion.** $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ inherits from $\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$ all its metric properties; the classical matrix groups add rich geometric structure.

@@@ metric-definition.md
---
title: Metric spaces
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 1
tags: metric, definition, axioms
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define a metric space and give two examples.

## Hint

A metric $d:X\times X\to[0,\infty)$ satisfies positivity, symmetry, and the triangle inequality.

## Solution

**Definition.** A metric on $X$ is a map $d:X\times X\to[0,+\infty[$ such that for all $x,y,z\in X$:
1. $d(x,y)=0\iff x=y$;
2. $d(x,y)=d(y,x)$;
3. $d(x,z)\le d(x,y)+d(y,z)$.

**Examples.**
- Euclidean metric on $\mathbb{R}^n$: $d(x,y)=\|x-y\|_2$.
- Discrete metric: $d(x,y)=0$ if $x=y$, $d(x,y)=1$ otherwise.

**Conclusion.** A metric space is a pair $(X,d)$; the metric encodes distance and topology.

@@@ metric-examples-classical.md
---
title: Classical metrics on $\mathbb{R}^n$
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: Euclidean, Manhattan, sup norm
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define the Euclidean, Manhattan, and sup metrics on $\mathbb{R}^n$ and compare them.

## Hint

$\|x\|_2\le\|x\|_1\le n\|x\|_2$ and $\|x\|_\infty\le\|x\|_2\le\sqrt n\|x\|_\infty$.

## Solution

**Metrics.**
- Euclidean: $d_2(x,y)=\sqrt{\sum(x_i-y_i)^2}$.
- Manhattan: $d_1(x,y)=\sum|x_i-y_i|$.
- Sup: $d_\infty(x,y)=\max_i|x_i-y_i|$.

**Comparison.** For every $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$:
$$\|x\|_\infty\le\|x\|_2\le\|x\|_1\le n\|x\|_\infty.$$

**Conclusion.** These metrics define the same topology (they are equivalent).

@@@ metric-open-ball.md
---
title: Open balls in a metric space
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 1
tags: open ball, metric, definition
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define the open ball $B(a,r)$ in a metric space and show it is open.

## Hint

For $x\in B(a,r)$, use $r-\|x-a\|>0$.

## Solution

**Definition.** $B(a,r)=\{x\in X:d(x,a)<r\}$.

**Openness.** Let $x\in B(a,r)$ and $\rho=r-d(x,a)>0$. For $y\in B(x,\rho)$: $d(y,a)\le d(y,x)+d(x,a)<\rho+d(x,a)=r$.

**Conclusion.** $B(x,\rho)\subseteq B(a,r)$: open balls are open sets.

@@@ metric-convergence.md
---
title: Convergence in metric spaces
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: convergence, sequences, metric
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define convergence of a sequence in a metric space and show that limits are unique.

## Hint

Suppose $x_n\to x$ and $x_n\to y$ with $x\neq y$; take $\varepsilon=d(x,y)/2$.

## Solution

**Definition.** $x_n\to x$ iff $d(x_n,x)\to0$.

**Uniqueness.** If $x_n\to x$ and $x_n\to y$, then $d(x,y)\le d(x,x_n)+d(x_n,y)\to0$, so $d(x,y)=0$, hence $x=y$.

**Conclusion.** Limits in metric spaces are unique.

@@@ metric-continuity.md
---
title: Continuity in metric spaces
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: continuity, epsilon-delta, metric
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define continuity of $f:X\to Y$ between metric spaces.

## Hint

For all $x_0$, $\forall\varepsilon>0,\exists\delta>0:\ d_X(x,x_0)<\delta\Rightarrow d_Y(f(x),f(x_0))<\varepsilon$.

## Solution

**Definition.** $f$ is continuous at $x_0$ iff $\forall\varepsilon>0,\exists\delta>0:\ d_X(x,x_0)<\delta\Rightarrow d_Y(f(x),f(x_0))<\varepsilon$.

**Global.** $f$ is continuous iff continuous at every point.

**Equivalence.** Equivalent to: $f^{-1}(U)$ open in $X$ for every open $U\subseteq Y$; and to sequential continuity.

**Conclusion.** Continuity has multiple equivalent characterizations in metric spaces.

@@@ metric-compact.md
---
title: Compactness in metric spaces
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: compactness, open cover, sequential
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State the equivalence between compactness by open covers and sequential compactness in metric spaces.

## Hint

In metric spaces, compact $\Leftrightarrow$ sequentially compact.

## Solution

**Theorem.** For a metric space $X$: compact (every open cover has a finite subcover) $\iff$ every sequence has a convergent subsequence (sequential compactness).

**Key steps.** Sequential compactness implies the space is totally bounded; a totally bounded complete space is compact.

**Conclusion.** Both compactness notions coincide in metric spaces.

@@@ metric-complete.md
---
title: Complete metric spaces
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: completeness, Cauchy sequences, examples
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define completeness and give two examples of complete and two non-complete metric spaces.

## Hint

Complete = every Cauchy sequence converges in the space.

## Solution

**Definition.** A metric space is complete if every Cauchy sequence has a limit in the space.

**Complete.** $\mathbb{R}$, $\mathbb{R}^n$ with Euclidean metric, closed subsets of $\mathbb{R}^n$.

**Not complete.** $\mathbb{Q}$ (with usual metric: $1,1.4,1.41,\ldots$ converges to $\sqrt2\notin\mathbb{Q}$); $]0,1]$ (sequence $1/n$ is Cauchy but converges to $0\notin]0,1]$).

**Conclusion.** Completeness depends both on the metric and the underlying set.

@@@ metric-equivalence.md
---
title: Equivalent metrics
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: equivalent metrics, same topology, norms
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the three classical metrics on $\mathbb{R}^n$ are equivalent: they define the same topology.

## Hint

Two metrics are topologically equivalent iff each open ball for one contains an open ball for the other.

## Solution

**Bounds.** $\|x\|_\infty\le\|x\|_2\le\sqrt n\|x\|_\infty$, and similar for $\|\cdot\|_1$.

**Consequence.** $B_\infty(a,r/\sqrt n)\subseteq B_2(a,r)\subseteq B_\infty(a,r)$; similarly for $\|\cdot\|_1$.

**Conclusion.** $d_1,d_2,d_\infty$ are topologically equivalent on $\mathbb{R}^n$.

@@@ metric-norm-definition.md
---
title: Normed spaces
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: norm, definition, axioms
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define a norm on a vector space and show that the induced distance is a metric.

## Hint

$\|x\|=0\iff x=0$; $\|\lambda x\|=|\lambda|\|x\|$; triangle inequality.

## Solution

**Axioms.** A norm $\|\cdot\|:E\to[0,+\infty[$ satisfies:
1. $\|x\|=0\iff x=0$;
2. $\|\lambda x\|=|\lambda|\|x\|$;
3. $\|x+y\|\le\|x\|+\|y\|$.

**Induced metric.** $d(x,y)=\|x-y\|$ is a metric: positivity from (1), symmetry from $\|\lambda x\|$ with $\lambda=-1$, triangle from (3).

**Conclusion.** Every normed space is a metric space.

@@@ metric-norm-equivalent.md
---
title: Equivalent norms
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: equivalent norms, topology, finite dimension
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that in finite-dimensional spaces, any two norms are equivalent.

## Hint

Work in a basis; the closed unit ball of one norm is compact for the other.

## Solution

**Setup.** Let $\|\cdot\|_1,\|\cdot\|_2$ be two norms on $\mathbb{R}^n$. By compactness of the unit sphere of $\|\cdot\|_1$ (for $\|\cdot\|_2$), the continuous function $\|\cdot\|_1/\|\cdot\|_2$ has a positive minimum $m$ and finite maximum $M$.

**Consequence.** $m\|x\|_2\le\|x\|_1\le M\|x\|_2$.

**Conclusion.** In finite dimension, all norms are equivalent.

**Note.** Fails in infinite dimension (e.g. $\|\cdot\|_\infty$ vs $\|\cdot\|_1$ on $C([0,1])$).

@@@ metric-compact-closed.md
---
title: Compact sets are closed and bounded
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: compact, closed, bounded, metric
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that in a metric space, every compact set is closed and bounded.

## Hint

Sequential argument: a limit point outside would contradict compactness; a sequence with no convergent subsequence gives unboundedness.

## Solution

**Closed.** If $x_n\in K$ converges to $x$, sequential compactness gives a convergent subsequence in $K$, so $x\in K$; hence $K$ is closed.

**Bounded.** If $K$ were unbounded, there would exist $(x_n)$ in $K$ with $d(x_0,x_n)\to+\infty$; such a sequence has no convergent subsequence, contradiction.

**Conclusion.** In metric spaces, compact $\Rightarrow$ closed and bounded.

**Note.** The converse holds in $\mathbb{R}^n$ (Heine–Borel) but not in general metric spaces.

@@@ metric-Cauchy.md
---
title: Cauchy sequences
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: Cauchy, sequences, metric
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define a Cauchy sequence and show that a convergent sequence is Cauchy.

## Hint

For $\varepsilon>0$, use $d(x_n,x)\to0$ and the triangle inequality.

## Solution

**Definition.** $(x_n)$ is Cauchy if $\forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists N,\ \forall m,n\ge N,\ d(x_m,x_n)<\varepsilon$.

**Convergent $\Rightarrow$ Cauchy.** If $x_n\to x$, then for $m,n\ge N$, $d(x_m,x_n)\le d(x_m,x)+d(x,x_n)<\varepsilon$.

**Conclusion.** Every convergent sequence is Cauchy.

**Note.** The converse characterizes complete spaces.

@@@ metric-product.md
---
title: Product metrics
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: product spaces, metric, sup
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define a metric on $X\times Y$ in terms of metrics on $X$ and $Y$.

## Hint

$d((x_1,y_1),(x_2,y_2))=\max(d_X(x_1,x_2),d_Y(y_1,y_2))$.

## Solution

**Definition.** $d((x_1,y_1),(x_2,y_2))=\max(d_X(x_1,x_2),d_Y(y_1,y_2))$.

**Properties.** Positivity, symmetry, triangle: all inherited component-wise.

**Conclusion.** $X\times Y$ is a metric space; convergence is component-wise.

@@@ metric-Banach.md
---
title: Banach fixed point theorem
category: topology/metric
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: Banach, fixed point, contraction
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $(X,d)$ be complete and $f:X\to X$ be a contraction ($d(f(x),f(y))\le kd(x,y)$ with $k<1$). Show that $f$ has a unique fixed point and that iterates converge to it.

## Hint

Show $(x_n)$ with $x_{n+1}=f(x_n)$ is Cauchy; use $d(x_{n+1},x_n)\le k^n d(x_1,x_0)$.

## Solution

**Cauchy.** $d(x_{n+1},x_n)\le k^nd(x_1,x_0)$; summing, $d(x_n,x_m)\le\frac{k^n}{1-k}d(x_1,x_0)\to0$.

**Convergence.** Complete: $x_n\to x^*$. Continuity: $f(x^*)=x^*$.

**Uniqueness.** If $f(x^*)=x^*$ and $f(y^*)=y^*$, $d(x^*,y^*)\le kd(x^*,y^*)$, so $d(x^*,y^*)=0$.

**Conclusion.** Banach fixed point theorem holds; iterates converge geometrically.

@@@ metric-Baire.md
---
title: Baire category theorem
category: topology/metric
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Baire, category, complete
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State the Baire category theorem for complete metric spaces.

## Hint

In a complete metric space, a countable intersection of dense open sets is dense.

## Solution

**Theorem (Baire).** Let $(X,d)$ be complete. If $(U_n)$ is a sequence of dense open sets, then $\bigcap_nU_n$ is dense.

**Consequence.** A complete metric space is not a countable union of nowhere dense sets.

**Conclusion.** Baire's theorem is a powerful tool in functional analysis (open mapping, closed graph, uniform boundedness).

@@@ metric-completion.md
---
title: Completion of a metric space
category: topology/metric
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: completion, Cauchy, dense embedding
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State the completion theorem for metric spaces.

## Hint

Every metric space embeds isometrically as a dense subset of a complete metric space.

## Solution

**Theorem.** Given a metric space $(X,d)$, there exists a complete metric space $(\hat X,\hat d)$ and an isometric embedding $i:X\to\hat X$ with dense image. This $(\hat X,\hat d)$ is unique up to isometry.

**Construction.** Take equivalence classes of Cauchy sequences of $X$ (with two sequences equivalent if their pointwise distance tends to $0$).

**Example.** $\mathbb{Q}$ completes to $\mathbb{R}$.

**Conclusion.** Completion fills in "missing limits" in a canonical way.

@@@ metric-norm-examples.md
---
title: Classical normed spaces
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: normed spaces, Banach, examples
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

List the classical normed spaces $\ell^p$ and $C([0,1])$.

## Hint

$\ell^p$ sequences with $\sum|x_n|^p<\infty$; $C([0,1])$ with $\sup$-norm.

## Solution

**$\ell^p$ for $1\le p<\infty$.** $\|x\|_p=(\sum|x_n|^p)^{1/p}$.

**$\ell^\infty$.** $\|x\|_\infty=\sup_n|x_n|$.

**$C([0,1])$.** $\|f\|_\infty=\sup_{[0,1]}|f|$.

**Completeness.** All these spaces are Banach (complete normed) spaces.

**Conclusion.** Classical spaces provide examples and counterexamples in functional analysis.

@@@ metric-compact-image.md
---
title: Continuous image of compact
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: compact image, continuity, compactness
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the continuous image of a compact metric space is compact.

## Hint

Sequential: extract a subsequence from a sequence in the image.

## Solution

**Proof.** Let $K$ compact and $f:K\to Y$ continuous. Given a sequence $(f(x_n))$ in $f(K)$, extract a subsequence $x_{n_k}\to x\in K$. Then $f(x_{n_k})\to f(x)\in f(K)$.

**Conclusion.** $f(K)$ is compact; if $Y=\mathbb{R}$, extrema are attained.

@@@ metric-uniform-continuity.md
---
title: Heine–Cantor theorem
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Heine-Cantor, uniform continuity, compact
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State and prove that continuous maps from a compact metric space are uniformly continuous.

## Hint

Contradiction via sequences.

## Solution

**Theorem.** Continuous $f:K\to Y$ with $K$ compact is uniformly continuous.

**Proof.** Suppose not: $\exists\varepsilon>0$ with $x_n,y_n\in K$, $d(x_n,y_n)\to0$ but $d(f(x_n),f(y_n))\ge\varepsilon$. Compactness of $K$ gives a subsequence $x_{n_k}\to x$; then $y_{n_k}\to x$. Continuity: $f(x_{n_k}),f(y_{n_k})\to f(x)$, contradiction.

**Conclusion.** Heine–Cantor holds in general metric spaces.

@@@ metric-isometry.md
---
title: Isometries between metric spaces
category: topology/metric
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: isometry, metric, distance-preserving
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define an isometry and show it is injective.

## Hint

If $f(x)=f(y)$, apply $d(f(x),f(y))=d(x,y)$.

## Solution

**Definition.** $f:X\to Y$ is an isometry if $d_Y(f(x),f(y))=d_X(x,y)$ for all $x,y$.

**Injectivity.** $f(x)=f(y)\Rightarrow d_Y(f(x),f(y))=0\Rightarrow d_X(x,y)=0\Rightarrow x=y$.

**Conclusion.** Every isometry is injective; if surjective, it is an isometric isomorphism.

@@@ metric-non-compact-closed-bounded.md
---
title: Closed bounded sets need not be compact
category: topology/metric
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: counterexample, compactness, infinite dimension
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Give an example of a closed bounded set that is not compact in a metric space.

## Hint

In $\ell^2$, the closed unit ball is closed and bounded but not compact.

## Solution

**Counterexample.** In $\ell^2$ (space of square-summable sequences with $\|\cdot\|_2$), the closed unit ball $\overline B(0,1)$ is closed and bounded. But the sequence $(e_n)$ of standard basis vectors is at pairwise distance $\sqrt2$, so has no convergent subsequence.

**Conclusion.** In infinite dimension, closed bounded $\not\Rightarrow$ compact.

**Note.** Riesz's theorem: the closed unit ball of a normed space is compact iff the space is finite-dimensional.

@@@ topology-Rn-continuity-method-system.md
---
title: Solving a non-linear system via the continuity method
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: continuity method, implicit function theorem, connectedness, nonlinear system
date: 2026-11-06
---

## Statement

Prove that the nonlinear system
$$\begin{cases}
x+\tfrac{1}{2}\sin(y)=3,\\[2mm]
y+\tfrac{1}{2}\cos(x)=4
\end{cases}$$
has at least one solution $(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^2$.

## Hint

For $t\in[0,1]$, consider the deformed system
$$\begin{cases}
x+\tfrac{t}{2}\sin(y)=3,\\[2mm]
y+\tfrac{t}{2}\cos(x)=4.
\end{cases}$$
Let $S\subseteq[0,1]$ be the set of parameters $t$ for which the system is solvable. Show that $S$ is nonempty, closed, and open. Conclude by connectedness of $[0,1]$.

## Solution

**Setup.** Define $F:[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$ by
$$F(t,x,y)=\bigl(x+\tfrac{t}{2}\sin(y)-3,\ y+\tfrac{t}{2}\cos(x)-4\bigr).$$
This map is continuous ($C^\infty$ in all variables). Let
$$S=\{t\in[0,1]:\exists(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^2,\ F(t,x,y)=(0,0)\}.$$
