**Nonemptiness.** For $t=0$, the system reduces to $x-3=0$ and $y-4=0$, giving the unique solution $(3,4)$. Hence $0\in S$, so $S\neq\emptyset$.

**Closedness.** Let $(t_k)\subseteq S$ with $t_k\to t^*\in[0,1]$, and choose $(x_k,y_k)$ with $F(t_k,x_k,y_k)=0$. From the equations,
$$|x_k|=\Bigl|3-\tfrac{t_k}{2}\sin(y_k)\Bigr|\le3+\tfrac12=\tfrac72,\qquad |y_k|=\Bigl|4-\tfrac{t_k}{2}\cos(x_k)\Bigr|\le4+\tfrac12=\tfrac92.$$
Thus $(x_k,y_k)$ lies in the compact rectangle $[-\tfrac72,\tfrac72]\times[-\tfrac92,\tfrac92]$. By Bolzano–Weierstrass, extract a convergent subsequence $(x_{k_j},y_{k_j})\to(x^*,y^*)$. By continuity of $F$, passing to the limit gives $F(t^*,x^*,y^*)=0$, so $t^*\in S$. Thus $S$ is closed.

**Openness.** Let $t_0\in S$ with a solution $(x_0,y_0)$, so $F(t_0,x_0,y_0)=0$. The Jacobian of $F$ with respect to $(x,y)$ at $(t_0,x_0,y_0)$ is
$$J_{(x,y)}F=\begin{pmatrix}1&\tfrac{t_0}{2}\cos(y_0)\\[2mm]-\tfrac{t_0}{2}\sin(x_0)&1\end{pmatrix}.$$
Its determinant is
$$\det J_{(x,y)}F=1+\tfrac{t_0^2}{4}\sin(x_0)\cos(y_0)\ge 1-\tfrac14=\tfrac34>0.$$
Therefore $J_{(x,y)}F$ is invertible, and the implicit function theorem gives a neighbourhood $]t_0-\delta,t_0+\delta[\,\cap[0,1]$ on which $F(t,x,y)=0$ has a solution. Hence $S$ is open in $[0,1]$.

**Conclusion.** $S$ is a nonempty subset of the connected set $[0,1]$ that is both open and closed in $[0,1]$. Therefore $S=[0,1]$. In particular $1\in S$: the original system $x+\tfrac12\sin(y)=3,\ y+\tfrac12\cos(x)=4$ has a real solution.

@@@ topology-Rn-open-ball.md
---
title: Open balls in $\mathbb{R}^n$
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 1
tags: open ball, Euclidean norm, definition
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define the open ball $B(a,r)$ in $\mathbb{R}^n$ and show it is an open set.

## Hint

For $x\in B(a,r)$, take $\rho=r-\|x-a\|>0$ and use the triangle inequality.

## Solution

**Definition.** $B(a,r)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^n:\|x-a\|<r\}$.

**Openness.** Let $x\in B(a,r)$ and $\rho=r-\|x-a\|>0$. For $y\in B(x,\rho)$: $\|y-a\|\le\|y-x\|+\|x-a\|<\rho+\|x-a\|=r$. So $B(x,\rho)\subseteq B(a,r)$.

**Conclusion.** Open balls are open sets.

@@@ topology-Rn-open-definition.md
---
title: Open sets in $\mathbb{R}^n$
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 1
tags: open sets, definition, ball
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State the definition of an open set in $\mathbb{R}^n$ and give two examples.

## Hint

$U$ is open iff every point of $U$ has a small ball contained in $U$.

## Solution

**Definition.** $U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$ is open iff for all $x\in U$ there is $r>0$ with $B(x,r)\subseteq U$.

**Examples.** $\mathbb{R}^n$ itself, open balls, $\{x:x_1>0\}$ (open half-space).

**Counterexample.** The closed ball $\overline B(a,r)$ is not open (its boundary points fail the condition).

**Conclusion.** Openness is defined pointwise via balls.

@@@ topology-Rn-closed.md
---
title: Closed sets in $\mathbb{R}^n$
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 1
tags: closed sets, complement, examples
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define a closed set in $\mathbb{R}^n$ and give two examples.

## Hint

Closed = complement open.

## Solution

**Definition.** $F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$ is closed iff $\mathbb{R}^n\setminus F$ is open.

**Examples.** Closed ball $\overline B(a,r)=\{x:\|x-a\|\le r\}$; spheres $\{x:\|x-a\|=r\}$; hyperplanes.

**Conclusion.** Closed sets are complements of open ones.

@@@ topology-Rn-union-intersection.md
---
title: Stability of open and closed sets
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: unions, intersections, stability
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that arbitrary unions and finite intersections of open sets in $\mathbb{R}^n$ are open; dual properties for closed sets.

## Hint

For finite intersections, take the minimum of radii. For closed sets, use De Morgan's laws.

## Solution

**Union.** If $x\in\bigcup U_i$, pick $i_0$ with $x\in U_{i_0}$; use the ball around $x$ inside $U_{i_0}$.

**Finite intersection.** With $x\in U_1\cap\cdots\cap U_n$, take $r=\min_i r_i>0$ where $B(x,r_i)\subseteq U_i$.

**Closed sets.** By De Morgan: arbitrary intersections and finite unions of closed sets are closed.

**Conclusion.** The topology is stable under the expected operations.

@@@ topology-Rn-interior-closure.md
---
title: Interior, closure, boundary
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: interior, closure, boundary
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define the interior, closure and boundary of $A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$, and compute them for $A=B(a,r)$ (open ball).

## Hint

$\overline{A}$ is the smallest closed set containing $A$; boundary $=\overline A\setminus\mathring A$.

## Solution

**Definitions.**
- Interior: union of open subsets of $A$.
- Closure: intersection of closed sets containing $A$; equivalently $A$ plus its limit points.
- Boundary: $\overline A\setminus\mathring A$.

**For $A=B(a,r)$.**
- Interior: $A$ itself (it is open).
- Closure: $\overline B(a,r)=\{x:\|x-a\|\le r\}$.
- Boundary: sphere $\{x:\|x-a\|=r\}$.

**Conclusion.** For an open ball, boundary is the sphere, closure is the closed ball.

@@@ topology-Rn-limit-points.md
---
title: Limit points in $\mathbb{R}^n$
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: limit points, sequences, closure
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $x$ is in the closure of $A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$ iff there is a sequence $(x_k)$ in $A$ with $x_k\to x$.

## Hint

Use $\varepsilon$-balls and sequential characterisation.

## Solution

**$\Leftarrow$.** If $x_k\to x$ with $x_k\in A$, every ball around $x$ contains a $x_k$. So $x$ is a limit point of $A$; $x\in\overline A$.

**$\Rightarrow$.** If $x\in\overline A$, for each $k$ pick $x_k\in A\cap B(x,1/k)$; such a ball always meets $A$ by definition of closure.

**Conclusion.** Closure equals the set of limits of sequences in $A$.

@@@ topology-Rn-compact-heine-borel.md
---
title: Heine–Borel in $\mathbb{R}^n$
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: compactness, Heine-Borel, closed bounded
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State the Heine–Borel theorem in $\mathbb{R}^n$ and give examples.

## Hint

In $\mathbb{R}^n$, compact = closed + bounded.

## Solution

**Theorem.** $K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$ is compact iff $K$ is closed and bounded.

**Examples.**
- Compact: closed ball $\overline B(0,1)$, the cube $[0,1]^n$, spheres.
- Non-compact: open ball $B(0,1)$ (not closed), hyperplane (not bounded).

**Conclusion.** Compactness in $\mathbb{R}^n$ = closed and bounded.

@@@ topology-Rn-sequential-compact.md
---
title: Sequential compactness in $\mathbb{R}^n$
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Bolzano-Weierstrass, sequential compactness
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State the Bolzano–Weierstrass theorem in $\mathbb{R}^n$.

## Hint

Every bounded sequence has a convergent subsequence.

## Solution

**Theorem.** Every bounded sequence in $\mathbb{R}^n$ has a convergent subsequence.

**Link to compactness.** If $K$ is compact, every sequence in $K$ has a subsequence converging in $K$ (sequential compactness).

**Conclusion.** Compactness $\Leftrightarrow$ sequential compactness in $\mathbb{R}^n$.

@@@ topology-Rn-connected.md
---
title: Connectedness in $\mathbb{R}^n$
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: connectedness, path-connectedness
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define connectedness and path-connectedness of subsets of $\mathbb{R}^n$, and show that any convex set is path-connected.

## Hint

For convex $C$, the segment between any two points lies in $C$.

## Solution

**Definitions.**
- $A$ is connected if no separation $A=U\cup V$ with $U,V$ nonempty open, disjoint.
- $A$ is path-connected if any two points are joined by a continuous path in $A$.

**Convex $\Rightarrow$ path-connected.** For $x,y\in C$ convex, the segment $t\mapsto(1-t)x+ty$, $t\in[0,1]$, lies in $C$.

**Conclusion.** Convex sets are path-connected (hence connected).

@@@ topology-Rn-convex-connected.md
---
title: Convex sets are connected
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: connectedness, convexity, segments
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that a convex set of $\mathbb{R}^n$ is connected.

## Hint

Path-connected sets are connected; convex sets are path-connected.

## Solution

**Path-connectedness.** As shown, every pair of points of a convex set is joined by a segment inside the set.

**Connectedness.** Path-connected sets are connected.

**Conclusion.** Convex sets are connected.

@@@ topology-Rn-continuity-open.md
---
title: Continuity and open preimages
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: continuity, open preimages, multivariable
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $f:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^m$ is continuous iff $f^{-1}(U)$ is open for every open $U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^m$.

## Hint

Copy the one-dimensional proof, replacing intervals by balls.

## Solution

**$\Leftarrow$.** Given $x_0$ and $\varepsilon>0$, the ball $U=B(f(x_0),\varepsilon)$ is open. So $f^{-1}(U)$ is open; it contains $x_0$, hence $B(x_0,\delta)\subseteq f^{-1}(U)$. So $\|f(x)-f(x_0)\|<\varepsilon$ for $\|x-x_0\|<\delta$.

**$\Rightarrow$.** Given open $U$ and $x_0\in f^{-1}(U)$, $f(x_0)\in U$ gives $\varepsilon>0$ with $B(f(x_0),\varepsilon)\subseteq U$; continuity gives $\delta$ with $f(B(x_0,\delta))\subseteq U$.

**Conclusion.** Continuity is equivalent to the preimage of open sets being open.

@@@ topology-Rn-boundary-sphere.md
---
title: Boundary of a ball
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: boundary, sphere, ball
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the boundary of the closed ball $\overline B(a,r)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$.

## Hint

Boundary = closure $\setminus$ interior. The closed ball is its own closure; its interior is the open ball.

## Solution

**Closure.** $\overline B(a,r)$ is closed, so its closure is itself.

**Interior.** $\mathring{\overline B}=B(a,r)$: any boundary point fails to have a ball inside the closed ball.

**Boundary.** $\overline{B(a,r)}\setminus B(a,r)=\{x:\|x-a\|=r\}$.

**Conclusion.** The boundary is the sphere $S(a,r)=\{x:\|x-a\|=r\}$.

@@@ topology-Rn-limit-not-continuity.md
---
title: Continuity versus iterated limits
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: iterated limits, continuity, subtleties
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $f(x,y)=\dfrac{xy}{x^2+y^2}$ (with $f(0,0)=0$) has iterated limits $0$ along both axes but no limit at the origin.

## Hint

Compute limits along $y=0$ and $x=0$, then along $y=x$.

## Solution

**Iterated limits.** $f(x,0)=0$, $f(0,y)=0$: so $\lim_{x\to0}\lim_{y\to0}f=\lim_{y\to0}\lim_{x\to0}f=0$.

**Diagonal.** Along $y=x$: $f(x,x)=\frac12$, so the limit along this path is $1/2$.

**Conclusion.** Iterated limits exist and coincide, but the two-dimensional limit does not: $f$ is not continuous at the origin.

@@@ topology-Rn-compactness-image.md
---
title: Continuous image of a compact set
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: compact, continuous image, extrema
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that if $K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$ is compact and $f:K\to\mathbb{R}$ is continuous, then $f$ attains its maximum and minimum on $K$.

## Hint

$f(K)$ is compact in $\mathbb{R}$, hence closed and bounded; contains its supremum and infimum.

## Solution

**Image compact.** $f(K)$ is compact in $\mathbb{R}$ (continuous image of compact is compact).

**Extrema.** $f(K)$ is closed and bounded in $\mathbb{R}$: it contains its supremum $M$ and infimum $m$. So there exist $x_{\max}, x_{\min}\in K$ with $f(x_{\max})=M$, $f(x_{\min})=m$.

**Conclusion.** Continuous functions on compact sets attain their extrema.

@@@ topology-Rn-uniform-continuity.md
---
title: Heine–Cantor in $\mathbb{R}^n$
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: uniform continuity, Heine-Cantor, compactness
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State Heine–Cantor theorem for $\mathbb{R}^n$ and outline its proof.

## Hint

Argue by contradiction using sequential compactness.

## Solution

**Theorem.** A continuous $f:K\to\mathbb{R}^m$ on a compact $K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$ is uniformly continuous.

**Proof.** Assume not: $\exists\varepsilon>0$ with $\|x_k-y_k\|\to0$ but $\|f(x_k)-f(y_k)\|\ge\varepsilon$. Compactness gives a subsequence $x_{k_j}\to x$. Then $y_{k_j}\to x$ too. Continuity: $f(x_{k_j})\to f(x)$ and $f(y_{k_j})\to f(x)$, contradiction.

**Conclusion.** Heine–Cantor holds in $\mathbb{R}^n$.

@@@ topology-Rn-baire.md
---
title: Baire category theorem for $\mathbb{R}^n$
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Baire, complete metric, category
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State Baire's theorem for $\mathbb{R}^n$ and explain its use for the existence of functions continuous nowhere differentiable.

## Hint

$\mathbb{R}^n$ is a complete metric space; the set of continuous functions is meager in the space of all functions, but the set of nowhere-differentiable ones is residual.

## Solution

**Baire's theorem.** In a complete metric space, a countable intersection of dense open sets is dense.

**Application.** In the space of continuous functions on $[0,1]$ with the uniform norm, the set of nowhere-differentiable functions is a dense $G_\delta$ (residual) — hence non-empty (in fact, "typical").

**Conclusion.** Baire's theorem forces the existence of highly irregular but continuous functions.

@@@ topology-Rn-boundary-open.md
---
title: Boundary of an open set
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: boundary, open set, disjointness
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that an open set $U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$ is disjoint from its boundary.

## Hint

Boundary $=\overline U\setminus\mathring U$.

## Solution

**Compute.** Since $U$ is open, $\mathring U=U$. Thus $\partial U=\overline U\setminus U$.

**Disjointness.** $\partial U\cap U=\emptyset$.

**Conclusion.** Open sets do not meet their boundary.

@@@ topology-Rn-isometry.md
---
title: Isometries of $\mathbb{R}^n$
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: isometry, Euclidean, rigid motion
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that every isometry $f:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^n$ is of the form $f(x)=Qx+b$ with $Q$ orthogonal.

## Hint

Translate to assume $f(0)=0$; then $Q=f$ preserves the norm; use the polarization identity to show $Q$ is linear.

## Solution

**Norm preservation.** Since $f$ is an isometry and $f(0)=0$, $\|f(x)\|=\|x\|$.

**Linear.** Polarization identity: $\langle f(x),f(y)\rangle=\langle x,y\rangle$. This linearises $f$: for $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$, $f(\lambda x)=\lambda f(x)$ and $f(x+y)=f(x)+f(y)$.

**Orthogonal.** $Q=f$ satisfies $Q^TQ=I$.

**Conclusion.** Any isometry of $\mathbb{R}^n$ is an orthogonal transformation plus a translation.

@@@ topology-Rn-homeomorphism-ball-cube.md
---
title: Ball and cube are homeomorphic
category: topology/topology-of-rn
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: homeomorphism, ball, cube
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the unit ball $B(0,1)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^n$ and the open cube $]-1,1[^n$ are homeomorphic.

## Hint

Use a scaling by the sup-norm: $T(x)=x/\|x\|_\infty\cdot\|x\|_2$ (or directly $T(x)=\frac{\|x\|_2}{\|x\|_\infty}x$ when $x\neq0$).

## Solution

**Map.** Define $T(x)=\frac{\|x\|_2}{\|x\|_\infty}x$ for $x\neq0$, $T(0)=0$. This maps the cube $\|x\|_\infty<1$ onto the ball $\|x\|_2<1$.

**Inverse.** The inverse is $T^{-1}(y)=\frac{\|y\|_\infty}{\|y\|_2}y$, continuous.

**Conclusion.** Ball and cube are homeomorphic.

@@@ topology-R-open-interval.md
---
title: Open intervals are open sets
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 1
tags: open sets, open intervals, definition
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that any open interval $]a,b[$ (with $a<b$) is an open set of $\mathbb{R}$.

## Hint

For any $x\in]a,b[$, take $r=\min(x-a,b-x)>0$; then $]x-r,x+r[\subseteq]a,b[$.

## Solution

**Radius.** Let $x\in]a,b[$ and $r=\min(x-a,b-x)>0$. Then for any $y\in]x-r,x+r[$: $y<x+r\le b$ and $y>x-r\ge a$. So $y\in]a,b[$.

**Conclusion.** Every $x\in]a,b[$ has a neighbourhood inside $]a,b[$; the interval is open.

@@@ topology-R-open-ball.md
---
title: Open balls in $\mathbb{R}$
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 1
tags: open ball, interval, topology
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For $a\in\mathbb{R}$ and $r>0$, describe the open ball $B(a,r)$ and show it is an open set.

## Hint

$B(a,r)=\{x:|x-a|<r\}=]a-r,a+r[$.

## Solution

**Identification.** $B(a,r)$ is the open interval $]a-r,a+r[$.

**Openness.** Every point of $]a-r,a+r[$ has a small ball around it contained in $]a-r,a+r[$ (previous exercise).

**Conclusion.** Open balls are open sets; they are the building blocks of the topology of $\mathbb{R}$.

@@@ topology-R-union-open.md
---
title: Arbitrary unions of open sets
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: unions, open sets, arbitrary
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that an arbitrary union of open sets of $\mathbb{R}$ is open.

## Hint

For $x$ in the union, it belongs to at least one open set $U_i$; use the open ball around $x$ inside $U_i$.

## Solution

**Proof.** Let $(U_i)_{i\in I}$ be open and $x\in\bigcup_i U_i$. Pick $i_0$ with $x\in U_{i_0}$. Since $U_{i_0}$ is open, there is $r>0$ with $B(x,r)\subseteq U_{i_0}\subseteq\bigcup_i U_i$.

**Conclusion.** $\bigcup_i U_i$ is open.

**Remark.** Finite intersections of open sets are open, but infinite intersections may fail: $\bigcap_{n\ge1}]-\frac1n,\frac1n[=\{0\}$, not open.

@@@ topology-R-intersection-open.md
---
title: Finite intersections of open sets
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: intersections, open sets, finite
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the intersection of finitely many open sets of $\mathbb{R}$ is open.

## Hint

For $x\in U_1\cap\cdots\cap U_n$, take the minimum of the radii from each $U_i$.

## Solution

**Proof.** Let $U_1,\ldots,U_n$ be open and $x\in\bigcap U_i$. For each $i$, there is $r_i>0$ with $B(x,r_i)\subseteq U_i$. Let $r=\min(r_1,\ldots,r_n)>0$. Then $B(x,r)\subseteq\bigcap U_i$.

**Conclusion.** The intersection is open.

@@@ topology-R-closed-interval.md
---
title: Closed intervals are closed
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: closed sets, closed intervals, complement
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $[a,b]$ is closed in $\mathbb{R}$.

## Hint

Its complement $]-\infty,a[\cup]b,+\infty[$ is a union of open sets.

## Solution

**Complement.** $\mathbb{R}\setminus[a,b]=]-\infty,a[\cup]b,+\infty[$, a union of open sets, hence open.

**Conclusion.** $[a,b]$ is closed.

@@@ topology-R-closed-definition.md
---
title: Closed sets
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: closed sets, complement, definition
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define a closed set in $\mathbb{R}$ and give two examples and one counterexample.

## Hint

Closed = complement is open.

## Solution

**Definition.** $F\subseteq\mathbb{R}$ is closed iff $\mathbb{R}\setminus F$ is open.

**Examples.** $\emptyset$, $\mathbb{R}$, $[0,1]$, singletons $\{a\}$.

**Counterexample.** $]0,1]$ is neither open nor closed: it is not open (0 is a boundary point) and its complement $]-\infty,0]\cup]1,+\infty[$ is not open.

**Conclusion.** Some sets are neither open nor closed.

@@@ topology-R-boundary.md
---
title: Interior, closure, boundary
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: interior, closure, boundary
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For $A=]0,1]\cup\{2\}$, compute the interior, closure, and boundary of $A$.

## Hint

$\mathring A$ = union of open subsets of $A$; $\overline A$ = smallest closed set containing $A$; $\partial A=\overline A\setminus\mathring A$.

## Solution

**Interior.** $\mathring A=]0,1[$: $1$ is not interior (every ball around $1$ hits the complement), $2$ is not interior (isolated).

**Closure.** $\overline A=[0,1]\cup\{2\}$: the limit points of $]0,1]$ are in $[0,1]$; $2$ is already in $A$.

**Boundary.** $\partial A=\{0,1,2\}$.

**Conclusion.** Interior $]0,1[$, closure $[0,1]\cup\{2\}$, boundary $\{0,1,2\}$.

@@@ topology-R-limits.md
---
title: Limit points
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: limit points, accumulation, closure
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define a limit point of a set $A\subseteq\mathbb{R}$ and describe the limit points of $A=\{1/n:n\ge1\}$.

## Hint

$x$ is a limit point if every neighbourhood of $x$ meets $A\setminus\{x\}$.

## Solution

**Definition.** $x$ is a limit point of $A$ if $\forall\varepsilon>0$, $(x-\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)\cap(A\setminus\{x\})\neq\emptyset$.

**Limit points of $A$.** Only $0$: any $\varepsilon$-neighbourhood of $0$ contains infinitely many $1/n$; other points are isolated.

**Conclusion.** $A'=\{0\}$; the closure is $\overline A=A\cup\{0\}$.

@@@ topology-R-compact.md
---
title: Compact sets in $\mathbb{R}$
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: compact sets, Heine-Borel, closed bounded
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State the Heine–Borel theorem and give two examples of compact and two non-compact sets.

## Hint

A subset of $\mathbb{R}$ is compact iff it is closed and bounded.

## Solution

**Theorem (Heine–Borel).** $K\subseteq\mathbb{R}$ is compact iff $K$ is closed and bounded.

**Compact.** $[0,1]$, $\{0\}\cup\{1/n:n\ge1\}$.

**Non-compact.** $]0,1[$ (not closed), $[0,+\infty[$ (not bounded).

**Conclusion.** Compactness = closed + bounded in $\mathbb{R}$.

@@@ topology-R-open-cover.md
---
title: The Borel–Lebesgue property
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: compactness, open cover, finite subcover
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Recall the definition of compactness by open covers and show that $[0,1]$ has the Borel–Lebesgue property.

## Hint

Standard proof: assume a cover with no finite subcover, bisect, iterate, get contradiction.

## Solution

**Definition.** $K$ is compact if every open cover of $K$ admits a finite subcover.

**Proof sketch for $[0,1]$.** Suppose $(U_i)$ is an open cover with no finite subcover. Bisect $[0,1]$: one half also has no finite subcover. Iterate to get nested intervals $I_n$ of length $2^{-n}$, each requiring infinitely many $U_i$. Their intersection is a single point $x\in[0,1]$, which lies in some $U_{i_0}$. For $n$ large, $I_n\subseteq U_{i_0}$ — contradiction.

**Conclusion.** $[0,1]$ is compact.

@@@ topology-R-connected.md
---
title: Connected subsets of $\mathbb{R}$
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: connectedness, intervals, IVT
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the connected subsets of $\mathbb{R}$ are exactly the intervals.

## Hint

If $A$ has a gap, split $A\cap]-\infty,c[$ and $A\cap]c,+\infty[$. Conversely, if $A$ is an interval, any separation would contradict the IVT for the identity.

## Solution

**Interval connected.** If $A$ is an interval and $A=U\cup V$ with $U,V$ disjoint nonempty open in $A$, take $u\in U$, $v\in V$, WLOG $u<v$. Since $A$ is an interval, $[u,v]\subseteq A$. The sets $U\cap[u,v]$ and $V\cap[u,v]$ give a separation of $[u,v]$, contradiction with connectedness of intervals.

**Non-interval disconnected.** If $a<b<c$ with $a,c\in A$, $b\notin A$, then $A\cap]-\infty,b[$ and $A\cap]b,+\infty[$ separate $A$.

**Conclusion.** Connected subsets of $\mathbb{R}$ = intervals.

@@@ topology-R-continuity-characterisation.md
---
title: Continuity via open preimages
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: continuity, open preimages, topological definition
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}$ is continuous iff $f^{-1}(U)$ is open for every open $U$.

## Hint

$(\Leftarrow)$: apply to $U=]f(x_0)-\varepsilon,f(x_0)+\varepsilon[$. $(\Rightarrow)$: use the $\varepsilon$-$\delta$ definition.

## Solution

**$\Leftarrow$.** Given $x_0$ and $\varepsilon>0$, the ball $U=]f(x_0)-\varepsilon,f(x_0)+\varepsilon[$ is open, so $f^{-1}(U)$ is open. It contains $x_0$, hence contains $B(x_0,\delta)\subseteq f^{-1}(U)$. So $|f(x)-f(x_0)|<\varepsilon$ for $|x-x_0|<\delta$.

**$\Rightarrow$.** Given open $U$ and $x_0\in f^{-1}(U)$, $f(x_0)\in U$ gives $\varepsilon>0$ with $B(f(x_0),\varepsilon)\subseteq U$. Continuity provides $\delta>0$ with $B(x_0,\delta)\subseteq f^{-1}(U)$.

**Conclusion.** Continuity is topological.

@@@ topology-R-cantor-set.md
---
title: The Cantor set is compact
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Cantor set, compactness, closed
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the Cantor set $K$ (intersection of nested triadic sets) is compact.

## Hint

$K$ is the intersection of closed bounded sets.

## Solution

**Construction.** $K=\bigcap_n K_n$ with $K_0=[0,1]$, $K_{n+1}$ removing the middle thirds of each interval of $K_n$.

**Closed.** Each $K_n$ is a union of closed intervals, hence closed; the intersection is closed.

**Bounded.** $K\subseteq[0,1]$.

**Conclusion.** $K$ is closed and bounded, hence compact (Heine–Borel).

**Remark.** $K$ is also uncountable, perfect, and has empty interior.

@@@ topology-R-open-dense.md
---
title: Dense sets
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: dense, rationals, irrationals
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $\mathbb{Q}$ and $\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}$ are both dense in $\mathbb{R}$.

## Hint

Between any two reals there is a rational, and between any two reals there is an irrational.

## Solution

**$\mathbb{Q}$ dense.** For $a<b$ reals, pick $n$ with $1/n<b-a$; some $k/n\in]a,b[$.

**$\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}$ dense.** Given $a<b$, take a rational $q\in]a,b[$; then $q+\frac{\sqrt2}{n}\in]a,b[$ for large $n$ is irrational.

**Conclusion.** Both $\mathbb{Q}$ and $\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}$ are dense in $\mathbb{R}$.

@@@ topology-R-baire.md
---
title: Baire category theorem for $\mathbb{R}$
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: Baire, category, dense G-delta
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State and prove that $\mathbb{R}$ is not a countable union of nowhere dense sets.

## Hint

Assume $\mathbb{R}=\bigcup F_n$; construct nested intervals avoiding each $F_n$.

## Solution

**Statement (Baire).** $\mathbb{R}$ is not a countable union of nowhere dense sets.

**Proof.** Suppose $\mathbb{R}=\bigcup_n F_n$ with each $F_n$ nowhere dense. Construct nested closed intervals $I_n$ with $I_n\cap F_n=\emptyset$ and $\mathrm{diam}(I_n)\le2^{-n}$. Their intersection is a point $x\notin\bigcup F_n$ — contradiction.

**Conclusion.** $\mathbb{R}$ is of second Baire category, in particular uncountable.

**Remark.** A dense $G_\delta$ (e.g. irrationals) is residual; its complement is meager.

@@@ topology-R-limit-superior.md
---
title: Limit superior and limit inferior
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: limit superior, limit inferior, sequences
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\limsup$ and $\liminf$ of $u_n=(-1)^n\left(1+\frac1n\right)$.

## Hint

Consider even and odd subsequences.

## Solution

**Even.** $u_{2k}=1+\frac{1}{2k}\to1$.

**Odd.** $u_{2k+1}=-(1+\frac{1}{2k+1})\to-1$.

**Conclusion.** $\limsup u_n=1$, $\liminf u_n=-1$; the sequence has no limit.

@@@ topology-R-compact-char.md
---
title: Sequential compactness in $\mathbb{R}$
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: sequential compactness, Bolzano-Weierstrass
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State the Bolzano–Weierstrass theorem and explain its link with compactness of $[0,1]$.

## Hint

Every bounded sequence has a convergent subsequence.

## Solution

**Theorem (Bolzano–Weierstrass).** Every bounded sequence of reals has a convergent subsequence.

**Link.** If $K\subseteq\mathbb{R}$ is compact (closed and bounded), every sequence in $K$ is bounded, so has a convergent subsequence; its limit lies in $K$ since $K$ is closed.

**Conclusion.** Compactness implies sequential compactness in $\mathbb{R}$; the converse also holds.

@@@ topology-R-accumulation.md
---
title: Accumulation points of $\mathbb{Q}$
category: topology/topology-of-r
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: accumulation, rationals, density
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that every real number is an accumulation point of $\mathbb{Q}$.

## Hint

Use density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$.

## Solution

**Proof.** Let $x\in\mathbb{R}$ and $\varepsilon>0$. Since $\mathbb{Q}$ is dense, there is $q\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(x-\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)$. If $q\neq x$, done; if $q=x$, take another rational in the smaller ball $(x-\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)\setminus\{x\}$.

**Conclusion.** Every real number is an accumulation point of $\mathbb{Q}$; $\overline{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathbb{R}$.
